# Supplement Register

Sixty-six products, each with a verdict, an evidence grade, the dose that was actually tested, its pros and cons listed separately, the foods that supply it with quantities, and whether your body already makes it.

> **Not medical advice.** It cannot replace a clinician who knows your history, medication list and blood results.

| | |
|---|---|
| Products | 66 |
| Worth taking | 10 |
| Evidence of harm | 14 |
| Food composition rows | 517 |
| The body cannot make | 32 |
| The body makes fully | 9 |
| Fabricated references found | 0 |

## 1. How to read this

| Axis | Question it answers |
|---|---|
| **1 Verdict** | Should you take it? Always per group, never universal. |
| **2 Evidence grade** | How sure is the literature? A confident answer can be no. |
| **3 In food** | Does the substance occur in ordinary food? |
| **4 Dose from food** | Could eating reach the tested dose? |
| **5 Body makes it** | Can you synthesise it yourself? |
| **6 If you lack it** | Is there a deficiency state at all, and does the damage reverse? |

> **Worked example.** Vitamin E is rich in food, so axis 3 reads Rich. But the harm-trial dose of 400 IU is 268 mg of alpha-tocopherol, about 18 times the reference intake, so axis 4 reads Out of reach. And the body cannot make tocopherol at all, so axis 5 reads Cannot. All three are true at once.

### Nine ways this material gets misread

1. A high grade does not mean it works. It means we are confident about the answer, and the answer can be no. Selenium is Grade A in this document because Cochrane established with HIGH certainty that it does NOT prevent cancer. Read the Grade column together with the Verdict column, never on its own.
2. A grade attaches to one specific claim, not to the substance in general. Vitamin D3 is Grade A for correcting documented deficiency and Grade D for preventing cancer or cardiovascular disease. Those are the same molecule and two different questions.
3. An official statement of insufficient evidence is not an endorsement. The USPSTF gives multivitamins an I statement. That means the trials could neither prove benefit nor rule out harm. The industry quotes it as vindication.
4. No upper limit set means nobody has assessed it. It is a gap in the science, not a finding of safety. Most botanicals and every probiotic in this document fall into that gap.
5. Risk ratios below 1 mean fewer events, above 1 mean more events. When the confidence interval crosses 1, no effect was demonstrated. That is the entire logic of Figure 5.
6. Relative reductions are not absolute reductions. REDUCE-IT is quoted everywhere as a 25% reduction. The absolute reduction was 4.8 percentage points over 4.9 years, meaning about 21 people take the drug for one to benefit.
7. The number on a mineral label is usually the compound, not the mineral. Magnesium oxide is about 60% magnesium, magnesium citrate about 16%. A capsule labelled 500 mg magnesium citrate delivers roughly 80 mg of magnesium.
8. The safety record of a food does not transfer to a concentrated extract of it. Drinking green tea is fine. Green tea extract has caused more than 100 published cases of liver injury, 8% of which went to transplant.
9. A certification mark says nothing about whether the product works. USP Verified, NSF and Informed Sport all certify content and purity. None of them assesses efficacy, and USP Verified does not screen for substances banned in sport.

## 3. Take it

Strong evidence, but only for the group named under Who. None of these is for everyone.

### Folic acid

**Verdict:** Take it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 400 µg/d, from at least one month before conception. 4 mg/d if a previous pregnancy was affected.
- **Who it is for:** Anyone capable of becoming pregnant.

**Pros**

- Neural tube defects RR 0.31 (95% CI 0.17-0.58) across 5 trials and 6,708 births. This is the strongest single result in the whole supplement literature.
- The MRC Vitamin Study (n=1,817 high-risk women) showed a 72% protective effect at 4 mg/d.
- Population fortification works at scale: US grain fortification prevents about 1,326 affected births per year.
- Costs a few pounds a year. No meaningful downside at 400 µg.

**Cons**

- Never take it alone when B12 status is unknown. Folic acid corrects the anaemia of B12 deficiency while the neurological damage silently progresses. This is the stated basis of the upper limit.
- High-dose folic acid plus B12 taken for cardiovascular reasons raised cancer incidence (HR 1.21) and cancer mortality (HR 1.38) in pooled Norwegian trials. That is a different product at a different dose for a different purpose, and it should not be confused with the 400 µg periconceptional dose.
- Upper limit 1,000 µg/d of synthetic folate, which applies to supplements and fortified food but not to folate from whole food.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Supplies the one-carbon units cells need to build DNA and divide.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** Folate is reduced to tetrahydrofolate, which carries one-carbon groups for thymidylate and purine synthesis and for the remethylation of homocysteine to methionine by methionine synthase (a B12-dependent enzyme). Tissues that divide fastest depend on it most: bone marrow, gut mucosa, and the closing neural tube of an early embryo. Folic acid is the synthetic, fully oxidised form; it must be reduced by dihydrofolate reductase, whose capacity in humans is limited, so unmetabolised folic acid appears in plasma at intakes above roughly 200 to 400 micrograms in one dose. The natural circulating form is 5-methyltetrahydrofolate.
- **Deficiency disease:** Megaloblastic (macrocytic) anaemia. In early pregnancy, low folate status raises the risk of neural tube defects (anencephaly, spina bifida, encephalocele); periconceptional folic acid prevents a large proportion but not all of them.
- **First signs:** Serum folate falls first, then red cell folate. Raised mean corpuscular volume appears before anaemia, with hypersegmented neutrophils on the film. Symptoms are non-specific: tiredness, breathlessness on exertion, sore tongue, mouth ulcers, mild diarrhoea. In pregnancy there are no maternal warning signs at all; the first evidence of a neural tube defect is usually the anomaly scan.
- **Untreated:** Marrow failure with pancytopenia, severe anaemia, mucosal ulceration and malabsorptive diarrhoea. In pregnancy the severe endpoint is structural: anencephaly is incompatible with life, and open spina bifida causes lifelong paralysis below the lesion, hydrocephalus, and bowel and bladder impairment.
- **Does it reverse:** The haematological and mucosal effects reverse completely: reticulocytosis begins within a week and the blood count normalises in about two months, with no residual deficit. A neural tube defect that has already formed is permanent and cannot be reversed by any later intake. The neural tube closes 26 to 28 days after conception, so supplementation started after a positive pregnancy test is too late for that specific endpoint, which is the whole argument for taking it before conception.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Women who could become pregnant, particularly with an unplanned pregnancy. Coeliac disease and other malabsorption. Heavy alcohol use. Drugs that block folate handling: methotrexate, sulfasalazine, trimethoprim, phenytoin and other anticonvulsants. High-turnover states such as chronic haemolysis and exfoliative skin disease. Dialysis.
- **How common:** In the fortified United States, 18.6% of women aged 12 to 49 had red cell folate below the threshold associated with elevated neural tube defect risk (748 nmol/L) in 2011 to 2016, down from 23.2% in 2007 to 2010 (PMID 31504109). Frank folate-deficiency anaemia is now uncommon where fortification is mandatory and is seen more often where it is not.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** None
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Folic acid (pteroylmonoglutamic acid) is manufactured by chemical synthesis, a Waller-type condensation of a triaminopyrimidinone with a three-carbon unit and p-aminobenzoyl-L-glutamic acid, giving a fully oxidised molecule carrying a single glutamate that does not occur in unfortified food. Ordinary food supplies reduced folate polyglutamates instead, chiefly 5-methyltetrahydrofolate, and USDA's component structure keeps 'folic acid' and 'folate, food' as separate entries: beef liver, lentils, spinach, chickpeas, asparagus and boiled broccoli all read folic acid 0 with folate food equal to folate total. Two distinct answers therefore sit inside one word, so they are separated here: the nutrient class folate is rich in ordinary food, while the marketed molecule folic acid enters the food supply only through fortification and enrichment, which is what every food row below is. Enriched pasta and enriched white rice are the other mandated US enrichment vehicles; their contents are label-dependent per product and no verified figure was available, so they get no row.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ready-to-eat breakfast cereal, folic-acid fortified (brand-specific) *[unconfirmed]* | Label-dependent. A product declaring 400 ug folic acid per serving works out near 1,000 ug/100 g at a 40 g serving, but serving weights differ by brand. | 1 serving (about 40 g, per label) | 400 ug folic acid (680 ug DFE) for a product fortified at that level | 100% of a 400 ug periconceptional folic acid dose in one bowl | Indicative and unconfirmed: category figure for US cereals fortified to 400 ug folic acid per serving, quoted from the verification report and not traceable to a single FoodData Central record. Current US labels declare folate in ug DFE against a 400 ug DFE DV, so the folic acid content has to be read off the specific product. |
| Yeast extract spread (Marmite type), folic-acid fortified (single analysed sample, brand-specific) | 2,990 ug folic acid (plus 793 ug food folate; 5,880 ug DFE) | 1 tsp (6 g) | 179 ug folic acid (353 ug DFE) | 45% of a 400 ug periconceptional folic acid dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 167717, SR Legacy. Single analysed profile: fortification level is set per product, so this figure does not carry across brands or across the UK, AU and US formulations of the same brand. USDA's own components do not reconcile exactly (3,790 total minus 2,990 folic acid = 800, printed food folate 793). |
| Instant oatmeal, flavoured, dry, fortified (QUAKER maple and brown sugar) | 114 ug folic acid (208 ug DFE) | 1 packet (43 g) | 49.0 ug folic acid (89 ug DFE) | 12% of a 400 ug folic acid dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173907 (folic acid 114 ug, folate food 14 ug, folate total 128 ug, DFE 208 ug) |
| Bread, wheat, commercially prepared from enriched flour | 20 ug folic acid + 65 ug food folate (99 ug DFE) | 1 slice (29 g) | 5.8 ug folic acid (28.7 ug DFE) | 1.5% of a 400 ug folic acid dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172686, SR Legacy |

- **To match the dose:** Two routes with different answers. As folic acid, the 400 ug periconceptional dose is reachable in one sitting only from fortified products: one bowl of ready-to-eat cereal fortified to 400 ug folic acid per serving is the entire dose, whereas matching it from yeast extract spread takes about 13 g (a bit over two teaspoons), from instant fortified oatmeal 8.2 packets, and from enriched wheat bread about 69 slices. As natural food folate the equivalent target is 680 ug DFE (400 x 1.7), and this is where the verification pass forced a correction: the submission's cheapest option was 376 g of cooked lentils, but simmered chicken liver at 578 ug food folate per 100 g (FDC 171061) needs only 118 g, under three 44 g livers, which is an ordinary portion, and pan-fried lamb liver at 400 ug DFE per 100 g (FDC 172533) needs 170 g. The plant routes stay awkward: 376 g cooked lentils (1.9 cups), 219 g prepared edamame (1.4 cups), 300 g dried sunflower kernels, 351 g raw spinach (about twelve 30 g cups), 395 g cooked chickpeas, 456 g cooked asparagus (about thirty spears) or 630 g boiled broccoli. So the plain answer is that a person can eat their way to the folate equivalent of the 400 ug dose through liver or through fortified food, and cannot realistically do it through leafy greens; the UK high-risk 5 mg dose (8,500 ug DFE, 1.47 kg of chicken liver or 4.7 kg of cooked lentils every day) is unreachable by any route. Liver carries its own pregnancy restriction on preformed vitamin A grounds, which is why it is rarely offered as the answer to the population that needs the dose.
- **Food vs supplement:** Folic acid is not bioidentical to food folate and the difference cuts both ways. It must be reduced by dihydrofolate reductase before entering one-carbon metabolism, and human DHFR capacity is low: unmetabolised folic acid appears in serum above a threshold of about 266 ug of folic acid per meal (Kelly et al., Am J Clin Nutr 1997;65:1790-5, PMID 9174474), which a 400 ug tablet exceeds. That is why the adult UL of 1,000 ug/day applies to folic acid from supplements and fortified food with no UL on food folate: high folic acid can correct the megaloblastic anaemia of B12 deficiency while neurological damage continues, masking the diagnosis. Food folate has no toxicity ceiling and the synthetic form does. Running the other way, the quantity of food folate is not the only obstacle. Cuskelly, McNulty and Scott randomised 62 women to an extra 400 ug/day as a folic acid supplement, as folic-acid-fortified food, as natural food folate, or as dietary advice: red cell folate rose only in the supplement and fortified-food arms and did not move in the food-folate arms despite documented increases in intake (Lancet 1996;347:657-9, PMID 8596381). The two trials that established the neural-tube-defect effect used synthetic folic acid, 4 mg/day in the MRC Vitamin Study (Lancet 1991;338:131-7, PMID 1677062) and 0.8 mg/day in a multivitamin (Czeizel and Dudas, N Engl J Med 1992;327:1832-5, PMID 1307234). Substituting leafy greens for the tablet before pregnancy has never been tested against that endpoint. On L-5-methyltetrahydrofolate (calcium methylfolate), the marketed 'MTHFR-friendly' alternative: EFSA's 2022 derivation of DFE factors for it judged 5-MTHF more bioavailable than folic acid at supplement doses of 400 ug/day and above (2.0 versus 1.7; PMC9399872), so the case against it is not absorption. It is that no periconceptional trial has been run on it, and the neural-tube-defect evidence base is specifically folic acid (Czeizel, Ann Nutr Metab 2011, PMID 21865678).
- **Absorption:** Food folate is roughly 50% available against about 85% for folic acid eaten with food and close to 100% fasting, which is where the 1.7 and 2.0 DFE multipliers come from (Suitor and Bailey, J Am Diet Assoc 2000;100:88-94, PMID 10646010). Food folate is a polyglutamate that must be deconjugated at the brush border before absorption, and it is chemically fragile. USDA Table of Nutrient Retention Factors Release 6 (2007) gives folate retention of 45% for legumes boiled 45-75 min and drained (code 0521), 30% for legumes boiled 2-2.5 h (code 0541), 60% for greens boiled in water to cover and drained (code 3005) and 90% for potatoes baked in the skin (code 3301). A cup of dried lentils simmered for an hour loses over half its folate, so a composition table value describes the cooked food only when the cooking matches. Folic acid in a fortified food or a tablet is stable and its dose is what the label says.
- **Does the body make it:** Humans have no folate biosynthetic pathway, so every microgram is dietary or supplemental, and NONE here does mean required, with megaloblastic anaemia as the described deficiency state. Colonic bacteria do synthesise folate, and this dataset counts bacterial output as exogenous supply because the human genome carries no such pathway; colonic absorption is limited and the contribution to human status is unquantified. Supplementation therefore fills a real gap when intake is low, and the strongest indication is periconceptional, where folic acid 400 micrograms/day prevents neural tube defects with outcome evidence behind it.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for synthesis. Human enzymology handles absorption and use only: PCFT (SLC46A1) and the reduced folate carrier bring folate in, and 5-methyl-THF is the methyl donor for MTR, which remethylates homocysteine to methionine using vitamin B12. Bacterial folate synthesis in the colonic lumen sits outside the human genome and counts as exogenous supply in this dataset.
- **Amount made:** 0 µg/d synthesised by human cells. The contribution of colonic microbial folate synthesis to human vitamin status is NOT ESTABLISHED in µg/d. Reference point: folate RDA 400 µg DFE/d.
- **Still matters when:** Preconception and pregnancy, where folic acid 400 micrograms/day prevents neural tube defects and the outcome evidence is the strongest recovered for folate. Also documented deficiency with megaloblastic anaemia, malabsorption, and low intake. Broad-spectrum antibiotics and colectomy reduce whatever microbial contribution exists, which was never quantified in the first place.

*Refs. Cochrane CD007950.pub3 (PMID 26662928); MRC PMID 1677062; Williams PMID 25590678; Ebbing PMID 19920236*

### Vitamin D3 (cholecalciferol)

**Verdict:** Take it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 800-2,000 IU/d, taken daily. Never as an annual or monthly bolus.
- **Who it is for:** People with serum 25(OH)D below about 30 nmol/L: dark skin at high latitude, covered clothing, housebound, northern-European winter.

**Pros**

- Correcting genuine deficiency is real medicine: it treats rickets and osteomalacia, and no other intervention does.
- With calcium, it cuts hip fracture in frail institutionalised elderly, RR 0.84 (0.74-0.96) across 9 trials and 49,853 people.
- Deficiency is genuinely common in the target groups: 17.5% of non-Hispanic Black Americans are below 30 nmol/L versus 2.1% of white Americans, and 17.7% of Europeans are below it in the October to March window.
- Costs almost nothing. Generic D3 at 1,000 IU is a few pounds a year.

**Cons**

- It does not prevent cancer or cardiovascular disease. VITAL (n=25,871, 5.3 years): invasive cancer HR 0.96 (0.88-1.06), major cardiovascular events HR 0.97 (0.85-1.12). D-Health (n=21,315): all-cause mortality HR 1.04. ViDA (n=5,108): cardiovascular disease HR 1.02.
- Vitamin D alone does nothing for fractures. Cochrane: hip fracture RR 1.12 (0.98-1.29) without calcium. The calcium is doing the work.
- More is actively worse, and this is the least-marketed finding in the field. Annual 500,000 IU increased falls (IRR 1.15) and fractures (IRR 1.26). Monthly 60,000 IU raised the proportion of people falling from 47.9% to 66.9%. Three years at 4,000 or 10,000 IU/d caused dose-dependent LOSS of radial bone density versus 400 IU/d.
- The 30 ng/mL threshold that drives most deficiency marketing came from the Endocrine Society, and that society walked it back in 2024. Its current guideline suggests against routine testing in healthy adults and against empiric supplementation above the reference intake in healthy under-75s.
- Contraindicated in hypercalcaemia, primary hyperparathyroidism and granulomatous disease such as sarcoidosis or tuberculosis. Upper limit 4,000 IU/d.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Lets the gut absorb calcium and phosphate so that bone can mineralise properly.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** Cholecalciferol is hydroxylated in the liver to 25-hydroxyvitamin D, the circulating storage form measured on a blood test, and then in the kidney to 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D (calcitriol), a steroid hormone acting through the nuclear vitamin D receptor. Calcitriol drives active intestinal absorption of calcium and phosphate, suppresses parathyroid hormone, and maintains the calcium-phosphate product that osteoblasts need to mineralise newly laid osteoid. Vitamin D receptors are present in immune cells, muscle and many other tissues, but the only role with an established human requirement is the calcium and bone axis. Skin synthesis from UVB normally supplies most of it, which is why dietary requirement figures depend heavily on latitude, season and sun exposure.
- **Deficiency disease:** Rickets in growing children, osteomalacia in adults. Severe infant deficiency also causes hypocalcaemic seizures and dilated cardiomyopathy.
- **First signs:** Serum 25(OH)D falls first, then parathyroid hormone rises (secondary hyperparathyroidism) while serum calcium is still normal or low-normal. At this stage a person notices little or nothing: vague aching in the hips, ribs and shins, and proximal muscle weakness showing up as difficulty rising from a low chair or climbing stairs. A blood test shows low 25(OH)D, raised PTH and often raised alkaline phosphatase before any X-ray change.
- **Untreated:** In children, growth plates fail to mineralise: bowed legs or knock knees, widened wrists and ankles, the rachitic rosary at the costochondral junctions, delayed walking, poor growth and fractures. Infants can present first with hypocalcaemic tetany, seizures or dilated cardiomyopathy, which can be fatal. In adults, unmineralised osteoid accumulates as osteomalacia, giving bone pain, proximal myopathy with a waddling gait, and Looser zones (pseudofractures) that progress to complete fractures of the pubic rami, femoral neck or ribs.
- **Does it reverse:** Biochemistry, bone pain, myopathy, hypocalcaemic seizures and the radiological signs of active rickets correct completely with vitamin D plus adequate calcium, usually over weeks to a few months. Skeletal deformity already established in long-standing rickets often persists, and marked bowing, rachitic dwarfism or pelvic deformity may need surgical correction.
- **Who becomes deficient:** People with little effective sun exposure: housebound or institutionalised older adults, those who cover the skin fully, and anyone living at high latitude through winter. Darker skin at high latitude (more melanin means less UVB synthesis). Exclusively breastfed infants not given a supplement, since breast milk is low in vitamin D. Obesity (sequestration in adipose tissue). Fat malabsorption from coeliac disease, cystic fibrosis, cholestasis or bariatric surgery. Chronic kidney or liver disease, and long-term anticonvulsant or glucocorticoid therapy.
- **How common:** NHANES 2011-2014: 5.0% of the US population aged 1 year and over had serum 25(OH)D below 30 nmol/L (at risk of deficiency) and 18.3% fell between 30 and 49.9 nmol/L; the below-30 figure was 17.5% in non-Hispanic Black participants against 2.1% in non-Hispanic White participants (PMID 31076739). Clinical nutritional rickets is uncommon in high-income countries but has not disappeared, and remains common in parts of the Middle East, Africa and South Asia (PMID 26741135).

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** Low
- **Body makes it:** Partly

Oily fish, egg yolk and UV-treated mushrooms supply measurable vitamin D, and fortified fat spreads, milk, breakfast cereals and juices add small amounts, so this is a genuine dietary nutrient rather than a manufactured one. The RICH label needs qualifying on two counts: the food base is narrow (oily fish, egg yolk, UV-treated mushrooms), and two of the stronger rows below are not ordinary foods, since cod liver oil behaves as a supplement and fortified orange juice is a manufactured delivery vehicle. Population evidence points the same way, because SACN concluded the 10 µg/day RNI is difficult to achieve from natural food sources and set it as a year-round RNI for everyone aged 4 and over; UK mean intakes from food are around a third of that (the often-quoted NDNS figures of 2.8 µg/day at ages 19 to 65 and 3.3 µg/day in over-65s are carried here as uncorroborated, since the full SACN report could not be retrieved to confirm them).

- **To match the dose:** 1,000 IU (25 µg), the low end of ordinary supplement doses, is about 150 g of cooked sockeye salmon every day, or 155 g to 463 g of grilled herring depending on which herring value holds. 4,000 IU (100 µg) is roughly 600 g of sockeye salmon, 621 g to 1.85 kg of herring, about 109 large egg yolks, or about 890 g of UV-treated portabella mushrooms daily. A 50,000 IU (1,250 µg) weekly loading dose has no food equivalent: 7.5 kg of sockeye salmon, or 7.8 to 23.1 kg of grilled herring. One food gets close on mass alone, UV-treated maitake at the top of its measured range, where 178 g would carry 4,000 IU, and that content is unlabelled, spans 0.1 to 56.1 µg/100 g by growing method and is D2 rather than D3. Above roughly 50 degrees north, cutaneous synthesis is effectively zero from October to March, so there is no sunlight to close the winter shortfall.
- **Food vs supplement:** Mushroom vitamin D is D2 (ergocalciferol), a different molecule from the D3 in fish, egg yolk and almost all supplements, and D3 raises serum 25(OH)D more effectively, with the gap widest for intermittent bolus dosing (Tripkovic et al., Am J Clin Nutr 2012;95:1357-64, PMID 22552031, DOI 10.3945/ajcn.111.031070, mean difference 34.10 nmol/L for bolus dosing, P = 0.0002; the advantage is lost with daily dosing, P = 0.10). UV mushroom content is wildly variable and usually unlabelled: USDA measured UV-treated portabella at 3.4 µg/100 g from one producer and 25.6 µg/100 g from another, maitake from 0.1 to 56.1 µg/100 g by growing method, and untreated white mushrooms at 0.1 to 0.2 µg/100 g, so buying ordinary mushrooms because 'mushrooms have vitamin D' gets you essentially nothing. Species and provenance matter as much as the food name: the salmon row is wild sockeye, while farmed Atlantic salmon, which is what most shoppers buy, runs several-fold lower, so 'salmon' on a pack does not deliver the sockeye figure. Cod liver oil is nominally a food but behaves as a supplement and carries 18,000 µg retinol per 100 g (CoFID 17-488, unconfirmed), so a tablespoon delivers about 8,160 IU of preformed vitamin A alongside the D3, which rules it out in pregnancy. Several real UK vehicles are missing from the table for want of a verifiable figure and should not be forgotten: fortified margarine and fat spreads (a statutory UK vitamin D vehicle for decades, and a larger contributor to UK intake than the US fortified orange juice listed), fortified breakfast cereals, kippers and other smoked herring, canned red sockeye (higher than the canned pink listed), herring and cod roe, and sun-dried shiitake for D2. Oily fish delivers D3 inside a fat matrix with EPA/DHA, protein, iodine and selenium; a capsule delivers D3 alone.
- **Absorption:** Vitamin D from both food and capsules is fat-soluble and absorbed better with a meal containing fat, which oily fish and egg yolk supply themselves. Form matters more than source: D2 from mushrooms is less effective per microgram than D3 (PMID 22552031). Egg yolk additionally contains 25-hydroxycholecalciferol, an already-hydroxylated metabolite that supplements do not normally provide. Composition tables bundle 25-OH-D3 into the headline total under the convention total = cholecalciferol + 5 x 25-OH-D3, so a 'vitamin D' number for eggs, trout or canned salmon is not strictly comparable to the D3 in a capsule; the canned pink salmon (12.1 + 5 x 0.30 = 13.6), trout (7.19 + 5 x 0.20 = 8.19) and whole egg (2.30 + 5 x 0.18 = 3.20) totals here all follow it.
- **Does the body make it:** Skin makes vitamin D3 photochemically: UVB at 290 to 315 nm opens 7-dehydrocholesterol to previtamin D3, which isomerises thermally, after which CYP2R1 in liver makes 25(OH)D and renal CYP27B1 makes the hormone 1,25(OH)2D. Because the first step needs photons rather than an enzyme, production collapses with latitude, season, time of day, skin pigmentation, sunscreen, clothing, indoor living and age, which is why deficiency is common despite a functioning pathway. The recovered entries split on the label, one scoring it FULL conditional on UVB exposure and another calling it the one true CONDITIONAL vitamin, and PARTIAL is taken here because the pathway routinely fails to cover requirement under real exposure conditions; supplementing is therefore genuine repletion of a synthesis shortfall, and serum 25(OH)D makes it targetable and monitorable.
- **Pathway:** 7-dehydrocholesterol (a mevalonate/cholesterol pathway intermediate and the DHCR7 substrate) plus UVB at 290-315 nm gives previtamin D3 by non-enzymatic photolysis in the epidermis, followed by thermal isomerisation to vitamin D3; DBP (GC) transports it to liver for 25-hydroxylation by CYP2R1 (and CYP27A1), then to kidney and extrarenal sites for 1-alpha-hydroxylation by CYP27B1 under PTH and FGF23 control; inactivation by CYP24A1 (24-hydroxylation); action through the nuclear receptor VDR.
- **Amount made:** Cutaneous synthesis has no fixed daily figure because it is UVB-dependent, so NOT ESTABLISHED as a constant. Whole-body exposure to one minimal erythemal dose of UVB equates to roughly 10,000-25,000 IU (250-625 µg) cholecalciferol, and IOM cites an estimate that average summer cutaneous synthesis at northern latitudes equates to about 2,000 IU/d. Dietary reference values: RDA 600 IU (15 micrograms)/day for ages 1 to 70 and 800 IU/day above 70, UL 4,000 IU/day (IOM 2011).
- **Still matters when:** Rickets and osteomalacia, exclusively breastfed infants, dark skin at high latitude, housebound or fully covered individuals, obesity (sequestration in adipose tissue), fat malabsorption and bariatric surgery, anticonvulsant or glucocorticoid therapy that induces catabolism, and chronic kidney disease, where activated forms are needed because CYP27B1 activity is lost. Excess causes hypercalcaemia, so mega-dosing is not a stronger version of adequacy, and intermittent very high doses performed worse than daily dosing in fracture and fall trials. Redundant for someone with regular summer sun on bare skin at low latitude.

*Refs. VITAL PMID 30415629; D-Health PMID 35026158; ViDA PMID 28384800; Cochrane PMID 24729336; Herrick PMID 31076739; Cashman PMID 26864360; Endocrine Society PMID 38828931; Sanders PMID 20460620; Bischoff-Ferrari PMID 26747333; Burt PMID 31454046*

### Vitamin B12 (cobalamin)

**Verdict:** Take it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 50-250 µg/d, or 1,000-2,000 µg once or twice weekly. Deficiency treatment: 1,000-2,000 µg/d orally.
- **Who it is for:** Vegans and long-term vegetarians, metformin users, people on a proton pump inhibitor for over two years, post-bariatric patients, pernicious anaemia.

**Pros**

- The deficiency is real and the neurological damage is irreversible once established, so correcting it early genuinely matters.
- Oral works as well as injection. Cochrane found 1,000 µg/d oral matched intramuscular, and at 2,000 µg/d oral was better by 680 pg/mL. Injections are a business model.
- Risk in the named groups is well quantified: metformin lowers B12 by 19% over 4.3 years; two or more years of PPI use gives OR 1.65.
- No tolerable upper intake level has been needed, because no adverse effect has been found from excess.

**Cons**

- It does nothing for energy, mood or cognition in people who are not deficient. That is the entire marketing claim and it has no support.
- Do not treat with folate alone if B12 status is unknown, for the reason given under folic acid.
- Cyanocobalamin should be avoided in Leber hereditary optic neuropathy and in renal failure. Use hydroxocobalamin instead.
- High-dose biotin in the same B-complex product interferes with immunoassays including troponin, which can mimic or mask a heart attack on a blood test.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Needed by two enzymes: one for making new blood cells, one for nerve insulation.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Permanent damage
- **In detail:** Cobalamin is the cofactor for exactly two human enzymes. Methionine synthase remethylates homocysteine to methionine using 5-methyltetrahydrofolate, which both regenerates usable folate and supplies S-adenosylmethionine for the methylation reactions that maintain myelin. Methylmalonyl-CoA mutase converts methylmalonyl-CoA to succinyl-CoA in the mitochondrion during breakdown of odd-chain fatty acids and branched-chain amino acids. Absorption is elaborate: gastric acid and pepsin release B12 from food protein, intrinsic factor from parietal cells carries it, and receptors in the terminal ileum take it up. Outside vegan diets the usual cause of deficiency is failure somewhere along that route rather than low intake.
- **Deficiency disease:** Pernicious anaemia (autoimmune intrinsic factor failure) and subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord; megaloblastic anaemia is the haematological presentation.
- **First signs:** Methylmalonic acid and homocysteine rise before serum B12 drops into the clearly deficient range, which is why borderline results are often followed with those metabolites. Symmetrical tingling and numbness in the feet and hands, loss of vibration sense and joint position sense, unsteadiness that is worse in the dark. Macrocytosis with hypersegmented neutrophils, sore smooth tongue, fatigue, mild cognitive slowing and low mood. Neurological signs can be present with a completely normal full blood count.
- **Untreated:** Subacute combined degeneration: demyelination of the dorsal columns and lateral corticospinal tracts producing sensory ataxia, spasticity, extensor plantar responses, and bladder and bowel disturbance, progressing untreated to spastic paraparesis and loss of independent walking. Optic atrophy occurs in a minority, as do dementia and psychosis. Severe megaloblastic anaemia with pancytopenia can occur, but it is the neurology that disables.
- **Does it reverse:** The blood corrects fully (reticulocytosis within a week, counts normal in roughly eight weeks) while the neurology may not. In the largest series, 153 episodes in 143 patients, 47.1% had complete resolution of neurological abnormalities on treatment and 6.3% were left with moderate-to-severe residual disability; so most treated patients recover fully or nearly so and a minority are left disabled. What survives treatment is inversely related to how severe the deficits were and how long they had run before treatment started. Recovery is fastest in the first two months and continues to about six months. This is exactly why folate must not be given blind for an unexplained macrocytic anaemia: folic acid repairs the blood picture and removes the visible signal while the cord damage carries on.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Adults over 60 with atrophic gastritis and food-bound B12 malabsorption. Autoimmune pernicious anaemia. Gastrectomy or gastric bypass, ileal resection, Crohn's disease. Long-term metformin. Long-term proton pump inhibitors or H2 blockers. Strict vegans and, importantly, their exclusively breastfed infants. Repeated nitrous oxide exposure, recreational or occupational, which inactivates methionine synthase directly.
- **How common:** About 6% of people aged 60 and over in large US and UK surveys have plasma B12 below 148 pmol/L, and close to 20% are in the marginal range of 148 to 221 pmol/L; prevalence rises with age and is considerably higher in low-income countries across the whole life span (PMID 19116323).

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Rich in animal foods and effectively absent from plant foods, because cobalamin is synthesised only by bacteria and archaea: every reliable source is an animal that accumulated bacterial B12, or a bacterially fermented or fortified product. The single word RICH is population-dependent and hides a reversal, so state it plainly: for an omnivore B12 is one of the easiest nutrients in the food supply, while for someone on an unfortified plant-based diet the same evidence makes it effectively absent, the only credible source being dried purple laver at 2.33 ug per 3 g sheet from a product whose content varies about elevenfold by origin and degrades with toasting and storage. On the contested plant candidates: spirulina is not usable, since 83% of its cobamide is pseudovitamin B12 (adeninylcobamide), inactive in humans, with only 17% real B12, and microbiological assays cannot tell them apart, which is why the labels claim high B12 (Watanabe et al., J Agric Food Chem 1999;47:4736-41, PMID 10552882); nori is usable and is the only credible plant source, carrying five biologically active cobalamin forms with adenosyl- and methylcobalamin about 60% of the total, and it corrected B12 status in deficient rats (Takenaka S et al., Br J Nutr 2001;85:699-703, PMID 11430774); tempeh is unreliable at 0.7-8.0 ug/100 g depending on which bacteria happened to contaminate the Rhizopus fermentation; mushrooms are not usable, with dried shiitake averaging 5.61 ug/100 g dry weight (range 1.3-12.7), black trumpet and golden chanterelle 1.09-2.65 ug/100 g dry weight and most other species about 0.09 ug/100 g or trace; chlorella ranges from zero to several hundred ug/100 g between products and cannot be relied on (all from Watanabe et al., Nutrients 2014;6:1861-73, PMID 24803097). Two further claims in the original submission, that kimchi and fermented vegetables sit below 0.1 ug/100 g and that lion's mane contains the inactive B12[c-lactone] analogue, could not be traced to the cited review and are recorded here as indicative and unsourced.

Dry-weight basis, which is how the product is sold and eaten. |
| Milk, whole, 3.25% milkfat | 0.45 ug | 1 cup (244 g) | 1.10 ug | 46% of the 2.4 ug RDA; 0.11% of a 1,000 ug tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171265, SR Legacy (14 samples, range 0.27-0.53) |
| Egg, whole, raw, fresh | 0.89 ug | 1 large egg (50 g) | 0.45 ug | 19% of the 2.4 ug RDA on content, and 1.7% on absorbed B12 (0.445 ug x under 9% = 0.040 ug) | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171287, SR Legacy (range 0.65-1.20); absorption from Watanabe, Exp Biol Med 2007;232:1266-74, PMID 17959839 |
| Fortified nutritional yeast, fortified plant milks and fortified breakfast cereals | Set by the fortification, not by the food; content varies per product and must be read off the label | Per label serving | Per label serving | Products are commonly fortified to deliver at or above the 2.4 ug RDA in one serving, which is the practical route for an unfortified plant-based diet | Category row with no numeric figure, listed unranked because no traceable composition value exists. Named because this item's own conclusion is that fortified food or a tablet is the answer for the one population with a real gap. Cite the specific product label. |

- **To match the dose:** Against the 2.4 ug/day RDA there is no gap for anyone eating animal food: one 3 oz portion of cooked clams is 35 times the RDA, a slice of beef liver 20 times, a can of sardines 3.4 times. Against the supplement dose the gap cannot be closed. Oral B12 products are typically 500-1,000 ug, and 1,000-2,000 ug for treating deficiency. To have 1,000 ug physically present in a meal takes 1.01 kg of cooked clams, 1.17 kg of pan-fried lamb liver, 1.42 kg of braised beef liver, 4.17 kg of cooked blue mussels, 11.2 kg of canned sardines, 35.7 kg of cooked salmon, or 222 kg of whole milk, which is about 216 litres (the submission's '222 litres' confused grams with litres; 1,000/0.0045 = 222,222 g, and at about 1.03 kg/L that is 216 L). No one could eat any of those. The comparison is also the wrong one, because intrinsic-factor-mediated absorption saturates at about 1.5-2.0 ug per meal (Watanabe, Exp Biol Med 2007;232:1266-74, PMID 17959839), so everything above roughly 2 ug in a single sitting is absorbed only by passive diffusion at on the order of 1%. A 1,000 ug tablet works because 1% of 1,000 ug bypasses the receptor route, which no food can imitate and none needs to unless that route is broken. For a strict vegan the picture inverts: nothing reliably supplies even the 2.4 ug RDA except dried nori, and Watanabe puts the requirement at about 4 g/day of dried purple laver, which is about one and a third 3 g sheets every day (the submission said one and a half) from a product varying about elevenfold by origin. Fortified foods or a tablet are the honest answer.
- **Food vs supplement:** Supplements use cyanocobalamin, a form not found in food, where the cyanide ligand is an artefact of the purification step and is removed intracellularly. Food B12 is protein-bound and needs gastric acid and pepsin to release it before intrinsic factor can take it up, so proton pump inhibitors, atrophic gastritis, gastric surgery and metformin knock out food B12 while leaving free crystalline B12 in a tablet or fortified cereal largely usable. Here the supplement is the mechanistically correct choice, and not merely the convenient one. The form risk with algae products runs opposite to the usual concentration worry: a spirulina capsule sold as a B12 source delivers mostly inactive pseudovitamin B12, and the microbiological assay behind the label number could not distinguish it from the real thing, so the label overstates delivered B12 rather than the dose being too strong. Note also that USDA gives yeast extract spread only 0.50 ug B12/100 g (FDC 167717), which is 0.03 ug per 6 g teaspoon: yeast does not make B12, so any yeast product that is a real source has been fortified and the number belongs to the fortification.
- **Absorption:** About 50% of dietary B12 is assumed absorbed from a normal meal, but it varies sharply by food: fish 42%, sheep meat 56-89%, chicken 61-66% and eggs under 9% (Watanabe 2007, PMID 17959839). The egg figure matters because eggs are routinely offered to vegetarians as a B12 food; on absorbed B12 a large egg delivers about 0.04 ug, which is 1.7% of the RDA rather than the 19% the content figure implies. Cooking costs real B12. USDA Table of Nutrient Retention Factors Release 6 (2007) gives 70% for roasted beef (code 0601), 60% for beef braised or simmered without the drippings (0651/0653), 65% for roasted chicken (0805), 60% for liver simmered without drippings (1152), 90% for baked lean finfish (2301), 60% for shellfish boiled in the shell (2701) against 90% steamed (2702), and 30% for milk held at heat for an hour (2153). Keeping the pan juices recovers much of it, which is why the with-drippings factors run exactly 15 points higher. One methodological point earned by the corrected mussel row: where USDA publishes a cooked composition entry, use it, because a hand-applied retention factor ignores the moisture loss that concentrates B12 and in this case halved the figure for the food people actually eat.
- **Does the body make it:** Humans have no cobalamin biosynthetic pathway, and the deficiency states are well described, megaloblastic anaemia and subacute combined degeneration. Bacteria in the human colon do make cobalamin and it cannot be absorbed there, because intrinsic factor dependent uptake by cubilin and amnionless is confined to the ileum, upstream of where the bacteria produce it, so colonic B12 synthesis contributes nothing to B12 status. That leaves supplementation as the clearest indication in nutrition for anyone whose dietary or absorptive route fails.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for synthesis. Host handling only: haptocorrin binding, release by gastric acid and pepsin, intrinsic factor from gastric parietal cells, cubilin and amnionless mediated uptake in the ileal epithelium, transcobalamin transport in blood and CD320 uptake by tissues, with no colonic step anywhere in the sequence. Inside cells B12 serves MTR, which remethylates homocysteine using 5-methyl-THF.
- **Amount made:** 0 µg/d synthesised by human cells, and effectively 0 µg/d absorbable from colonic bacterial production. Reference point: vitamin B12 RDA 2.4 µg/d.
- **Still matters when:** Vegans and long-term vegetarians, pernicious anaemia, after gastrectomy or ileal resection, with metformin and long-term proton pump inhibitor use, and in older adults with atrophic gastritis; colonic bacterial production never substitutes for any of these. B12 is usually an absorption problem rather than a pure intake problem outside vegan diets, so the route and dose should follow the mechanism of failure. Broad-spectrum antibiotics and colectomy remove whatever microbial output exists, which was never absorbable anyway.

*Refs. Cochrane PMID 29543316; de Jager BMJ 2010 PMID 20488910; Lam JAMA 2013 PMID 24327038; Pawlak review*

### Iron

**Verdict:** Take it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** Test ferritin first. Treatment 60-120 mg elemental iron. Ferrous sulfate 325 mg delivers 65 mg elemental.
- **Who it is for:** Menstruating women with ferritin at or below about 50 µg/L, pregnancy, blood donors, post-bariatric patients.

**Pros**

- Grade A for treating iron-deficiency anaemia and for pregnancy outcomes, across 57 trials and 48,971 women.
- Fatigue improves in non-anaemic iron-deficient women, but only below a ferritin threshold of about 50 µg/L. Above that, nothing happens.
- Cheap and generic. Ferrous sulfate is the reference standard and there is no evidence that premium salts work better.

**Cons**

- Alternate-day dosing is oversold. The absorption physiology is real (21.8% versus 16.3% fractional absorption) but the only adequately sized randomised trial with haemoglobin as the endpoint found the same final ferritin either way. Treat it as a tolerability strategy, not a superiority one.
- Never supplement anaemia that has not been shown to be iron-deficient. Anaemia of inflammation and haemochromatosis both get worse. Unexplained anaemia in an adult needs investigation, not a pill.
- Contraindicated in hereditary haemochromatosis (HFE C282Y homozygotes) and in transfusion-dependent anaemias such as thalassaemia and myelodysplastic syndrome.
- Acute iron overdose was historically a leading cause of fatal poisoning in children under six, which is why unit-dose packaging exists. Keep it locked away.
- Gastrointestinal side effects are the main reason people stop. Upper limit 45 mg/d (US); EFSA sets no limit but a safe level of 40 mg/d.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Carries oxygen in the blood and powers the enzymes that turn food into usable energy.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** Iron sits at the centre of the haem group in haemoglobin and myoglobin, where it binds and releases oxygen, and in the iron-sulfur clusters of mitochondrial complexes I to III, where it moves electrons during ATP synthesis. It is also the cofactor for ribonucleotide reductase, the rate-limiting enzyme of DNA synthesis, which is why dividing tissues (marrow, gut lining) fail first. In the developing brain it is required for tyrosine hydroxylase (dopamine synthesis), for oligodendrocyte myelination, and for hippocampal maturation, which is the mechanism behind the developmental findings in infants. Absorption is governed by hepcidin, and the direction runs opposite to what is often assumed: when stores are low hepcidin is suppressed, ferroportin survives on enterocytes and macrophages, and fractional absorption rises several-fold, from roughly 10% of intake to 20 to 30%. What hepcidin sets is the ceiling on rate, and there is no regulated route for excreting iron, so a large deficit still takes months to refill and oral iron works slowly. During inflammation hepcidin stays high and blocks absorption despite empty stores, which is why the anaemia of inflammation does not respond to oral iron.
- **Deficiency disease:** Iron deficiency anaemia (microcytic, hypochromic anaemia). Iron deficiency without anaemia is a recognised earlier stage of the same process.
- **First signs:** Serum ferritin falls long before haemoglobin does, so a blood test shows depleted stores while the full blood count is still normal. What a person notices at that stage: fatigue disproportionate to activity, breathlessness on stairs, cold hands, hair shedding, restless legs at night, and sometimes pica (craving ice or chalk). Ferritin is an acute-phase protein, so inflammation or infection can mask true depletion.
- **Untreated:** Haemoglobin falls with small pale red cells, producing pallor, tachycardia, exertional dyspnoea, and in extreme cases high-output cardiac failure. In pregnancy it raises the risk of preterm delivery, low birth weight, and poor infant iron stores. In infancy it impairs motor and cognitive development. In adults over 50 a new iron deficiency is a red flag for occult gastrointestinal bleeding and needs investigation rather than a supplement.
- **Does it reverse:** In adults, anaemia and its symptoms correct fully once iron is replaced and the cause is fixed; haemoglobin typically rises within 4 to 8 weeks and stores refill over 3 to 6 months. Iron deficiency during infancy is the exception. Children with severe chronic iron deficiency in infancy still scored worse on cognitive, motor and behavioural measures more than 10 years after full iron treatment, so the developmental window matters more than the correction.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Menstruating women, especially with heavy periods; pregnant women (requirement roughly doubles); infants 6 to 24 months, particularly preterm or low-birth-weight; frequent blood donors; endurance athletes; people with coeliac disease, inflammatory bowel disease, H. pylori gastritis or after bariatric surgery; vegetarian and vegan diets high in phytate; and anyone with chronic gastrointestinal blood loss.
- **How common:** Anaemia affects about 33.7% of women aged 15 to 49 worldwide on GBD 2021 figures (age-standardised prevalence 33,716.77 per 100,000); WHO's 2019 estimates are lower, near 30% in non-pregnant women and 36 to 37% in pregnant women of that age band, about 29.9% overall. Iron deficiency is the single largest cause. In the US, the reported prevalence of iron deficiency in women depends on both the age band and the ferritin cut-off, which is why published figures disagree so widely: in females aged 12 to 21, ferritin below 25 micrograms/L gives about 38 to 39% (Weyand et al., JAMA 2023), while in a 62,685-woman US and Canadian sample of mean age 49.6 the same cut-off gives 15.33% and ferritin below 15 gives 7.43%; restricted to ages 25 to 54, three definitions span 4.46% to 21.23%.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Iron is genuinely abundant in ordinary food, in two chemically distinct forms that behave differently. Haem iron (offal, red meat, shellfish, blood sausage) is absorbed by a dedicated transporter and is largely indifferent to the rest of the plate; non-haem iron (legumes, seeds, greens, fortified flour) is the bulk of most people's intake and is absorbed at a fraction of the rate, heavily modified by vitamin C, polyphenols, phytate and calcium. Haem accounts for 77 percent of total iron in raw lean beef, 81 percent in lamb, 88 percent in pork and 74 percent in chicken (PMID 26433293), higher than the 40 percent assumed in the older Monsen equation; those fractions were measured in raw lean meat, so the haem share of a braised product is probably slightly lower than the calculations below imply. Reference point used in the table: EFSA PRI 16 mg/d for premenopausal women, 11 mg/d for men (EFSA, Dietary Reference Values for nutrients Summary report, approved 4 December 2017, doi 10.2903/sp.efsa.2017.e15121, Tables 5 and 7; underlying opinion EFSA Journal 2015;13(10):4254).

- **To match the dose:** Two supplement doses need two answers. A nutritional dose (14 to 18 mg elemental iron, the EU labelling reference level) is comfortably reachable by eating: 1 cup of boiled lentils (6.6 mg) plus 85 g of braised lamb's liver (7.0 mg) plus 1 oz of pumpkin seeds (2.3 mg) is 15.9 mg in one day, and a single 85 g portion of braised pig's liver is 15.2 mg on its own. A therapeutic dose is out of reach. The absorption and treatment trials gave 60 mg and 120 mg of elemental iron as labelled ferrous sulfate, which is a salt weight of about 325 mg for 65 mg of iron (PMID 29032957). Matching 65 mg of iron by total content takes roughly 363 g of braised pig's liver, 785 g of lamb's liver, 3.3 kg of sirloin steak, 1.95 kg (about 10 cups) of boiled lentils, or 2.4 kg (about 80 cups) of raw spinach in a single day. By absorbed iron the food burden is about 1.6 times larger than the submission claimed, because the trial it cites measured cumulative fractional absorption of 16.3 percent on consecutive days and 21.8 percent on alternate days at 60 mg/d, and 11.8 percent for a single 120 mg dose, rather than the 10 percent assumed. At the source's own 16.3 percent a 65 mg dose delivers about 10.6 mg absorbed. Absorbing 10.6 mg as haem iron at 25 percent requires 42.4 mg of haem iron, about 1.94 kg of braised lean lamb shoulder (2.7 mg total iron per 100 g, 81 percent haem in raw lean; FDC 174331 and PMID 26433293). Absorbing the same 10.6 mg from non-haem food at 5 percent requires 212 mg of non-haem iron, about 6.4 kg of cooked lentils per day. Correcting a diagnosed deficiency cannot be done with food; preventing one can.
- **Food vs supplement:** Three traps, and the first is narrower than usually stated. (1) The retinol hazard belongs to liver, not to offal as a class. Braised lamb liver carries 7,490 ug retinol activity equivalents per 100 g, which is 6,367 ug in an 85 g portion, and braised beef liver 9,440 ug RAE per 100 g (raw beef liver 4,970 ug RAE; FDC 172532, 168626, 169451). That is why liver is contraindicated in pregnancy in most national guidance. The hazard separates cleanly by organ: braised lamb kidney is 12.4 mg iron with 137 ug RAE (FDC 174355) and braised pork kidney 5.29 mg iron with 78 ug RAE (FDC 167860), so kidney beats every muscle-meat row on iron without the vitamin A problem. The earlier claim that the iron benefit and the vitamin A hazard travel together and cannot be separated is withdrawn. (2) Liver's iron is largely ferritin and haemosiderin iron, so its headline 8.28 mg is less absorbable than the haem fraction of plain muscle meat implies, and muscle meat with a smaller number can deliver more absorbed iron. (3) Supplement iron behaves unlike food iron: a 60 mg dose raises hepcidin and suppresses absorption of the next dose, which is why alternate-day dosing absorbed 21.8 percent against 16.3 percent on consecutive days (PMID 29032957). Food iron arriving in small amounts across the day never triggers that. On spinach, the correction runs both ways. Isotope work found that potassium oxalate added to a low-oxalate kale meal did not change non-haem iron absorption (11.5 percent against 10.7 percent, P=0.86), so the textbook oxalate explanation does not survive testing. But the spinach meal in the same study absorbed 24 percent less than the matched kale meal without reaching significance (P>0.16), and although the paper reports spinach as significantly higher in calcium and polyphenols it does not attribute the absorption difference to them. The honest reading is that spinach iron was numerically lower in a single small study, the oxalate mechanism is refuted, and no replacement mechanism is established. Either way a 30 g bowl of raw spinach leaves contains 0.81 mg of iron and yields a rounding error.
- **Absorption:** Haem iron is absorbed at roughly 15 to 35 percent and non-haem at 2 to 20 percent, with iron status the dominant variable. Across whole diets rather than single meals, bioavailability is 14 to 18 percent for mixed diets and 5 to 12 percent for vegetarian diets in people with no iron stores, and the authors note that iron status generally has a greater effect than diet composition (PMID 20200263). Vitamin C: the source concludes that about 50 mg of ascorbic acid in each main meal is desirable for optimum effect, with the most pronounced effects in meals high in inhibitory ligands such as phytates and tannins (PMID 3700141). The '25 mg' lower bound and the 'several-fold' multiplier claimed earlier are not in that source and have been dropped. When the same amount of ascorbic acid is given across a complete diet over days, the facilitating effect is far less pronounced (PMID 11124756): squeezing lemon on your lentils helps, and it does not turn plant iron into meat iron. Tea and coffee inhibition, with numbers: a cup of coffee cut non-haem absorption from a hamburger meal by 39 percent and tea by 64 percent; instant coffee at double strength drove absorption from 5.88 percent to 0.53 percent; coffee one hour before the meal did no harm, while coffee one hour after inhibited as much as coffee with the meal (PMID 6402915). Black tea inhibited non-haem absorption by 79 to 94 percent, peppermint tea by 84 percent, cocoa by 71 percent and chamomile by 47 percent, dose-dependently on total polyphenols, and adding milk made no difference (PMID 10999016). That is polyphenol inhibition; phytate is a separate inhibitor sitting inside the wholegrains and legumes themselves. Practical consequence: the highest-yield single change for someone with low iron stores is moving tea and coffee to between meals rather than with or after them.
- **Does the body make it:** Iron is chemical element 26, and no enzyme can build an element out of anything else, so every iron atom in haemoglobin arrived in food, water or a pill. The body compensates by hoarding: macrophages strip iron from dying red cells and return roughly 20-25 mg/d to plasma, while only about 1-2 mg/d is absorbed to cover obligatory losses. Recycling only reuses atoms already present, so iron supplements are never redundant on this axis and stand or fall entirely on measured status and losses, with no regulated route for excreting the surplus.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE (chemical element, zero biosynthetic route). Downstream use only: haem is built in eight enzymatic steps split between two compartments, starting from glycine plus succinyl-CoA with ALAS2 in the erythroblast mitochondrion, continuing with ALAD, HMBS, UROS and UROD in the cytosol, then CPOX, PPOX and ferrochelatase (FECH) back in the mitochondrion, where the iron atom FECH inserts is entirely dietary. Absorption: duodenal cytochrome b (CYBRD1) reduces Fe3+, DMT1 (SLC11A2) carries Fe2+ into the enterocyte, and basolateral ferroportin (SLC40A1) exports it under hepcidin control, with ferritin as the store.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/d synthesised. For scale, internal recycling returns about 20-25 mg Fe/d to plasma against obligatory losses of roughly 1-2 mg/d in adults; RDA 8 mg/d men, 18 mg/d premenopausal women.
- **Still matters when:** NEVER ON THIS AXIS as a redundancy argument, since synthesis is zero. Justification comes from intake or loss instead: menstrual or gastrointestinal blood loss, pregnancy, frequent blood donation, infant and adolescent growth, high-phytate or plant-based diets, malabsorption or post-bariatric surgery, documented low ferritin or transferrin saturation. Not justified in iron-replete adults, where surplus iron is stored and cannot be excreted.

*Refs. Cochrane 2024; Stoffel PMID 29032957; von Siebenthal PMID 38021373; Verdon BMJ 2003*

### Iodine

**Verdict:** Take it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 150 µg/d as potassium iodide. 250 µg/d in pregnancy and lactation (WHO).
- **Who it is for:** Preconception, pregnancy and lactation, vegans, and anyone avoiding both dairy and fish.

**Pros**

- Universal salt iodisation is the single most successful micronutrient intervention in public health, and it works because iodine is the sole substrate for thyroid hormone.
- The deficiency has quietly returned in the UK and parts of Europe. UK schoolgirls had a median urinary iodine of 80.1 µg/L against a WHO adequacy threshold of 100, with 51% mildly deficient.
- The requirement rises about 50% in pregnancy, and the window for fetal brain development does not reopen.

**Cons**

- Correcting status during pregnancy may be too late. MITCH randomised 832 mildly deficient pregnant women to 200 µg/d and found a verbal IQ difference of -0.7 in their children at age 5-6. Fix it before conception.
- Avoid kelp and bladderwrack entirely. Content is wildly variable and can deliver many times the upper limit in a single dose.
- An iodine load can precipitate iodine-induced hyperthyroidism in autonomous thyroid nodules or multinodular goitre, and can cause hypothyroidism in autoimmune thyroid disease. Anyone with a thyroid history needs medical advice first.
- Upper limit 1,100 µg/d (US) but only 600 µg/d (EFSA).

**What it does, and what happens without it**

The raw material for thyroid hormone, which sets metabolic rate and builds the fetal brain.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Permanent damage
- **In detail:** Iodine has exactly one job: it is the substrate thyroid peroxidase attaches to tyrosine residues on thyroglobulin to make thyroxine (T4) and triiodothyronine (T3). There is no alternative pathway and no substitute element. T3 acting on nuclear thyroid hormone receptors drives basal metabolic rate and thermogenesis in adults. In the fetus it governs neuronal migration, dendritic branching, synaptogenesis and myelination, on a fixed developmental timetable. The fetal thyroid does not begin producing hormone until roughly week 12 to 16, so first-trimester brain development runs entirely on maternal T4 crossing the placenta. Maternal requirement rises to about 250 micrograms per day in pregnancy and lactation.
- **Deficiency disease:** Iodine deficiency disorders: endemic goitre and hypothyroidism in adults, and endemic cretinism as the severe congenital form.
- **First signs:** The thyroid enlarges (goitre) as TSH rises to extract more iodine from a falling supply. On testing, a population median urinary iodine concentration below 100 micrograms/L (below 150 in pregnancy) and a raised serum thyroglobulin signal inadequacy before any clinical change. A pregnant woman with mild to moderate deficiency usually notices nothing at all, which is what makes this dangerous.
- **Untreated:** Endemic cretinism: severe intellectual disability, deaf-mutism, spastic diplegia and stunted growth in a child whose mother was severely deficient during pregnancy. Adults develop overt hypothyroidism with cold intolerance, weight gain, bradycardia and, in extreme untreated cases, myxoedema. Even mild to moderate maternal deficiency is associated with lower verbal IQ and reading scores in the child, on the order of a few points, which is small individually and large across a population.
- **Does it reverse:** Goitre, adult hypothyroidism and impaired thyroid function correct with iodine repletion. Brain damage sustained in utero and in the first two years of life does not. The window is narrower than the whole pregnancy but far wider than the moment a pregnancy is confirmed: in Cao's trial, iodine given in the first or second trimester left 2% of infants with moderate or severe neurologic abnormality, against 9% when it was given in the third trimester or after birth, so treatment up to the end of the second trimester still protects the fetal brain. Beyond that window iodine given to an affected child undoes nothing; only iodine reaching the mother in time prevents the damage. The practical reading is to start immediately, not to conclude it is too late. This is the strongest irreversibility case among common micronutrients, and the reason salt iodisation is a public health programme rather than an individual choice.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Pregnant and lactating women anywhere, since requirements rise sharply. Populations in iodine-poor soil regions (mountainous and flood-plain areas) without salt iodisation. People who avoid iodised salt while also avoiding dairy, eggs and seafood, including strict vegans without a deliberate iodine source. Users of non-iodised speciality salts (pink, sea, kosher), which supply essentially no iodine.
- **How common:** 21 countries still had insufficient population iodine intake as of 2020-2021, down from 113 in 1993, thanks to salt iodisation (IGN Global Scorecard of Iodine Nutrition). Mild to moderate deficiency in pregnant women persists in countries whose general populations are replete, including the UK, Norway and Australia. Iodine deficiency remains the leading preventable cause of intellectual disability worldwide.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Iodine occurs in ordinary food, and the answer rests on a narrow set of foods: dairy, white fish, eggs, shellfish and, where a country iodises it, table salt. Nothing else contributes meaningfully. The RICH classification carries a jurisdictional qualifier: for a UK reader it rests on dairy, white fish and eggs alone, because the UK has no salt iodisation programme, so the US iodised salt row below is not transferable and is marked as such. Iodine in milk is not intrinsic to the cow, it arrives from fortified cattle feed and iodophor teat disinfectants, which is why it is seasonal (higher in winter housing) and why organic milk is lower. Reference point used in the table: EFSA AI 150 ug/d for adults, 200 ug/d in pregnancy and lactation (EFSA, Dietary Reference Values for nutrients Summary report, approved 4 December 2017, doi 10.2903/sp.efsa.2017.e15121, Tables 5 and 7; underlying opinion EFSA Journal 2014;12(5):3660).
