| Soya drink, unsweetened, calcium- and vitamin-fortified, not iodine-fortified | 0.0 ug | 1 cup (240 g) | 0 ug | 0% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 1999630 (Foundation Foods). Iodine 0.0 ug confirmed; the record carries Ca 101 mg and 58 ug RAE per 100 g. |

- **To match the dose:** There is essentially no gap. A standard iodine supplement or prenatal multivitamin supplies 150 ug, exactly the EFSA adult AI. A single 120 g UK haddock fillet delivers 390 ug, two and a half times the dose. One 115 g cod fillet delivers 131 ug. About 400 ml of milk delivers roughly 156 ug at milk density (412 g); the 151 ug figure quoted previously is what 1.6 USDA cups (398 g) gives, and the two should not be equated. Six large eggs deliver 147 ug. Half a teaspoon of iodised salt (3 g) delivers 152 ug where salt is iodised. Iodine belongs in the small category of supplements a normal eater does not need. The gap opens the moment those specific foods leave the diet, and the UK numbers are stark. Substituting three dairy portions a day (milk, yoghurt, cheese) with plant alternatives cuts the iodine those portions supply from 124 ug to 2.6 ug, a 97.9 percent loss, taking the contribution to the 150 ug recommendation from 83 percent down to 1.8 percent (PMID 35373723). The same market survey identified 146 milk alternatives and 76 yoghurt alternatives, excluded 48 organic products, and found 29 milk alternatives and 4 yoghurt alternatives iodine-fortified; that is 28 percent and 6 percent of the non-organic subset, or 19.9 percent and 5.3 percent of all products identified. The 28 and 6 percent figures quoted earlier against the full denominators were wrong. Against those, 88 percent and 73 percent were fortified with calcium; no cheese alternative and no fish alternative was iodine-fortified; and organic plant alternatives cannot legally be fortified. This shows up in real people: in NDNS years 7 to 9, adults and children consuming milk alternatives exclusively had an iodine intake of 94 ug/d against 129 ug/d for cows' milk consumers, and a median urinary iodine concentration of 79 ug/l against 132 ug/l, which classifies the milk-alternative group as iodine deficient by the WHO criterion while the milk drinkers were sufficient (PMID 32993817). Unfortified milk-alternative concentrations were a median 7.3 ug/kg, 1.7 percent of winter conventional cows' milk; the three fortified products found in 2015 sat at 266 to 287 ug/kg (PMID 28946925).
- **Food vs supplement:** Kelp is the dangerous answer, and the danger is dosing variability rather than iodine itself. Across 12 species of commercially sold edible seaweed, iodine ranged from 16 ug/g in nori to over 8,165 ug/g in processed kelp granules from Laminaria digitata sold as a salt substitute, a 510-fold spread, and within a single species it varied with preharvest conditions (514 ug/g in sun-bleached blades, 6,571 ug/g in fresh juvenile blades) (PMID 15588380). One gram of those granules is 54 times the adult AI. A 2021 survey of 96 commercial macroalgae products found 128 to 62,400 ug of iodine per portion in wholefood kelp products and 5 to 5,600 ug per supplement dose, that for 54 of the 96 products a single serving or dose would exceed the tolerable upper intake level, and that species labelling and declared iodine content were frequently inadequate or inaccurate (PMID 33889064). A kelp tablet is therefore an unlabelled dose of a potent thyroid-active substance, and excess iodine causes both hypothyroidism and hyperthyroidism. If iodine is the goal, potassium iodide at a known 150 ug, or milk, fish and eggs, are the correct answers and kelp is not. Second, milk: iodine there comes from feed and dairy hygiene, so it moves with farming practice. Organic milk was 42.1 percent lower in iodine than conventional in a 172-sample UK retail survey (median 144.5 against 249.5 ng/g; PMID 21781365), and winter milk runs higher than summer. Someone switching to organic milk has quietly cut their main iodine source by nearly half without changing anything visible. Third, iodised salt: the USDA entry in the table is a US product, the UK has no iodisation programme, and that is precisely why the Bath group repeatedly identify milk, dairy and fish as the main UK iodine sources. Fourth, the plant drinks in the table are fortified products, with calcium and vitamin A, that skip iodine. That is the mechanism behind the 97.9 percent substitution loss rather than an absence of fortification technology.
- **Absorption:** Iodine bioavailability is the simple case. Iodide from milk, iodised salt and potassium iodide supplements is almost completely absorbed, and urinary iodine excretion is used directly as the intake biomarker on the standard assumption that about 90 percent of ingested iodine is excreted in urine (PMID 25274294). There is no meaningful absorption advantage to any form and no matrix effect worth chasing: 150 ug from a fillet of cod, a pot of yoghurt or a tablet are close to interchangeable systemically. The one real form difference sits at the top end. Organically bound iodine in seaweed is released variably during digestion and cooking, and boiling kelp leaches a large fraction of its iodine into the water, so a seaweed dose is uncertain in both directions. Two further practical points: iodine and selenium interact, because the deiodinase enzymes that convert thyroxine to the active hormone are selenoproteins, so iodine repletion in a selenium-poor person behaves differently; and goitrogens in raw brassicas and cassava compete with iodide uptake, which matters only when iodine intake is already marginal.
- **Does the body make it:** Iodine is chemical element 53, so there is no synthetic route to it and thyroid hormone output falls when dietary supply stops. What the body does make is the hormone itself: thyroid peroxidase attaches dietary iodide to tyrosine residues on thyroglobulin to build T4 and T3, and deiodinases salvage iodide when hormone is degraded. The enzymatic machinery is endogenous while every iodine atom is dietary, so iodine supplementation is genuinely useful wherever intake is low and pointless once intake is adequate.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE (chemical element). Downstream use only: the sodium-iodide symporter NIS (SLC5A5) traps iodide at the basolateral membrane of thyroid follicular cells, pendrin (SLC26A4) moves it across the apical membrane, DUOX2 with its maturation factor DUOXA2 supplies the H2O2 that thyroid peroxidase needs, and TPO iodinates thyroglobulin tyrosines in the follicular lumen. Salvage runs at two levels: iodotyrosine dehalogenase (IYD, also called DEHAL1) recovers iodide from MIT and DIT inside the follicular cell, while peripherally DIO1 is the main iodide-recycling deiodinase and DIO2 is the hormone-activating one.
- **Amount made:** 0 µg/d synthesised. Reference points: thyroid iodine stores 15-20 mg in an iodine-sufficient adult; RDA 150 µg/d adults, 220 µg/d in pregnancy and 290 µg/d in lactation (IOM); WHO, UNICEF and ICCIDD recommend 250 µg/d in pregnancy and lactation; adult UL 1100 µg/d (IOM).
- **Still matters when:** NEVER ON THIS AXIS. Justification is intake-based: pregnancy and lactation, diets excluding iodised salt, dairy and seafood (including many vegan diets), regions of endemic deficiency and goitre. Excess is actively harmful rather than merely wasteful, since high intakes can precipitate hypothyroidism or thyrotoxicosis (adult UL 1100 µg/d, IOM).

*Refs. Vanderpump Lancet 2011 PMID 21640375; MITCH PMID 29030199; Cochrane PMID 28260263*

### Creatine monohydrate

**Verdict:** Take it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 3-5 g/d. Loading is optional: 20 g/d for 6 days and 3 g/d for 28 days reach the same muscle saturation.
- **Who it is for:** Anyone doing resistance training, and older adults in particular. Also vegetarians and vegans, whose baseline stores are lower.

**Pros**

- The best-evidenced ergogenic aid on the market. In older adults doing resistance training, +1.37 kg lean tissue mass (95% CI 0.97-1.76) over training alone, across 22 randomised trials.
- Extremely cheap. Monohydrate costs pennies per dose and is the form used in essentially every trial.
- Long safety record: no signal of harm in healthy kidneys across years of use in trials.

**Cons**

- The cognition claim is weaker than the marketing. Pooled memory effect SMD 0.31 (0.18-0.44), and EFSA formally concluded in 2024 that a cause-and-effect relationship with cognitive function has NOT been established.
- Do not pay for HCl, buffered, liquid or micronised premium forms. They are price increases with no comparative evidence behind them.
- Existing chronic kidney disease or dialysis requires nephrology supervision, because the safety data are in healthy kidneys only.
- Expect 1-2 kg of water weight in the first weeks. That is intracellular water, not fat, but it matters if you compete in a weight class.
- No official upper limit exists. The commonly quoted safety envelope comes from an industry-affiliated position stand, and the actual sentence is absence of evidence of harm rather than demonstrated safety.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Recycles ATP inside muscle and brain cells, buffering energy during short bursts of intense effort.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Creatine is phosphorylated by creatine kinase to phosphocreatine, a rapid-turnover phosphate reservoir that regenerates ATP from ADP during high-intensity contraction and in neurons. Roughly half of the daily turnover (about 1 to 3 g) is synthesized endogenously from arginine, glycine and methionine by AGAT and GAMT in liver, kidney and pancreas; the remainder comes from meat and fish. There is no dietary requirement. Vegetarians carry intramuscular creatine roughly 10 to 15 percent below omnivores and remain healthy, they simply respond more strongly to supplementation. That range comes from two different designs: Blancquaert et al. 2018 is an intervention trial in 40 omnivorous women in which vastus lateralis total creatine fell 15 percent over 3 months in the vegetarian-plus-placebo arm (against +9.7 percent vegetarian-plus-creatine and +6.8 percent omnivorous control), while cross-sectional comparisons of habitual vegetarians report a smaller gap of around 10 percent. Separately from anything to do with intake, three inborn errors of metabolism produce the cerebral creatine deficiency syndromes: GAMT deficiency, AGAT deficiency and X-linked creatine transporter (SLC6A8) deficiency. These are genetic defects of synthesis or transport, not consequences of eating too little creatine, and no dietary pattern produces them. Onset is in infancy or early childhood with global developmental delay, expressive speech delay out of proportion to other skills and hypotonia, plus early epilepsy and a movement disorder in GAMT deficiency; brain magnetic resonance spectroscopy showing an absent or near-absent creatine peak is the diagnostic finding. Untreated GAMT deficiency causes intractable seizures, autistic features, extrapyramidal movement disorder and severe intellectual disability, with accumulated guanidinoacetate contributing to the neurotoxicity, and creatine transporter deficiency causes lifelong moderate to severe intellectual disability and seizures in affected males. AGAT and GAMT deficiency respond to oral creatine (with arginine restriction and ornithine in GAMT), and 7 of 8 infants treated before 6 months of age had normal developmental outcomes, which is what makes early detection decisive; creatine transporter deficiency does not respond to oral creatine at all, because the transporter that would carry it into the brain is the broken protein. On frequency, GeneReviews gives an estimated GAMT incidence of 1:2,640,000 to 1:250,000; prospective newborn screening in Utah and New York found 1:405,655; a single-centre referral series of 5 patients seen between 2001 and 2011 produced a much higher outlier estimate of 1:114,000. Creatine transporter deficiency is reported in 0.4 to 1.4 percent of cohorts of males with intellectual disability, and separately in 2.64 percent of a more recent cohort of individuals with neurodevelopmental disorders that was neither male-restricted nor restricted to intellectual disability of unknown cause.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Partly

Creatine is a genuine dietary constituent, confined to the skeletal muscle of animals: Harris 1997 measured about 30 mmol/kg (393 mg/100 g) in uncooked beef, chicken and rabbit muscle. Organ distribution is the trap, because ox heart holds 295 mg/100 g while ox liver holds only 30 mg/100 g despite the liver being where creatine is synthesised. Plant foods contain none, so for a vegetarian or vegan the dietary status is effectively zero rather than RICH; baseline muscle total creatine was 117 mmol/kg dry mass in vegetarians against 130 in non-vegetarians.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Pork loin, raw, lean *[unconfirmed]* | 500 mg creatine | 1 loin steak, 200 g raw | 1,000 mg | 20% of a 5 g dose | Balsom PD, Soderlund K, Ekblom B, Sports Med 1994;18(4):268-80. PMID 7817065; DOI 10.2165/00007256-199418040-00005 |
| Herring, Atlantic, raw *[unconfirmed]* | 650-1,000 mg creatine | 1 fillet, 100 g raw | 650-1,000 mg | 13-20% of a 5 g dose | Balsom 1994, PMID 7817065; DOI 10.2165/00007256-199418040-00005 |
| Beef, raw, lean (skeletal muscle) | 393 mg creatine (30 mmol/kg) | 1 steak, 200 g raw | 787 mg | 16% of a 5 g dose | Harris RC, Lowe JA, Warnes K, Orme CE. The concentration of creatine in meat, offal and commercial dog food. Res Vet Sci 1997;62(1):58-62. PMID 9160426; DOI 10.1016/s0034-5288(97)90181-8 (value confirmed against the paper) |
| Chicken, raw, breast muscle | 393 mg creatine (30 mmol/kg) | 1 skinless breast, 170 g raw | 668 mg | 13% of a 5 g dose | Harris RC et al., Res Vet Sci 1997;62(1):58-62. PMID 9160426; DOI 10.1016/s0034-5288(97)90181-8 (confirmed) |
| Salmon, raw *[unconfirmed]* | 450 mg creatine | 1 fillet, 140 g raw | 630 mg | 13% of a 5 g dose | Balsom 1994, PMID 7817065; DOI 10.2165/00007256-199418040-00005 |
| Rabbit, raw muscle | 393 mg creatine (about 30 mmol/kg, same as beef and chicken muscle) | 1 serving, 150 g raw | 590 mg | 12% of a 5 g dose | Harris RC et al., Res Vet Sci 1997;62(1):58-62. PMID 9160426 (rabbit muscle measured at the same ~30 mmol/kg; added because the original text named rabbit but showed no row) |
| Ox heart, uncooked | 295 mg creatine (22.5 mmol/kg) | 1 serving, 150 g | 443 mg | 9% of a 5 g dose | Harris RC et al., Res Vet Sci 1997;62(1):58-62. PMID 9160426 (22.5 mmol/kg = 295.0 mg/100 g, confirmed) |
| Cod, raw *[unconfirmed]* | 300 mg creatine | 1 fillet, 140 g raw | 420 mg | 8% of a 5 g dose | Balsom 1994, PMID 7817065; DOI 10.2165/00007256-199418040-00005 |
| Ox liver, uncooked (the textbook answer that fails) | 30 mg creatine (2.3 mmol/kg) | 1 serving, 150 g | 45 mg | 0.9% of a 5 g dose | Harris RC et al., Res Vet Sci 1997;62(1):58-62. PMID 9160426 (2.3 mmol/kg = 30.2 mg/100 g, confirmed; the paper also found no phosphocreatine in any sample and degradation to creatinine in dried or processed products) |
| Cow's milk *[unconfirmed]* | 10 mg creatine | 1 glass, 200 mL (206 g) | 21 mg | 0.4% of a 5 g dose | Balsom 1994, PMID 7817065; DOI 10.2165/00007256-199418040-00005 |

- **To match the dose:** On the verified Harris figure of 393 mg/100 g, 3 g/day of creatine takes 763 g of raw lean beef and 5 g/day takes 1,272 g, about six 200 g steaks every day, carrying roughly 280 g of protein and its full fat and calorie load. On Balsom's slightly higher 4.5 g/kg for beef it is 667 g and 1,111 g. Balsom's herring figure is the highest on the list and would still need 500-770 g of raw herring daily, and that figure is indicative only. Cooking makes the plate-equivalent worse, since creatine converts to inert creatinine, so the raw-basis numbers are the optimistic case. Nobody eats this way.
- **Absorption:** Creatine monohydrate is close to completely absorbed and dietary creatine from meat is also efficiently absorbed, so absorption is not the limiting step; dose size is. The responsive variable is baseline muscle store: vegetarians started at 117 mmol/kg dry mass versus 130 in non-vegetarians and showed larger increases in total creatine, phosphocreatine, lean tissue and work performance after supplementation (Burke DG et al., Med Sci Sports Exerc 2003;35(11):1946-55, PMID 14600563). Habitual omnivore intake is roughly 1-2 g/day against a whole-body turnover of about 2 g/day in a 70 kg male, with the balance made endogenously (PMID 7817065), which is why a supplement of 3-5 g/day is a two- to five-fold multiple of anything a diet delivers. Cooking losses are the one real bioavailability penalty for the food form: in cooked beef, creatine and taurine are the compounds most reduced, partly by conversion to creatinine and partly by loss into the cooking juices (Purchas RW, Rutherfurd SM, Pearce PD, Vather R, Wilkinson BHP, Meat Sci 2004;68(2):201-7, PMID 22062229).
- **Does the body make it:** Your body builds creatine every day, on the order of 1 g in a young man (an inference from creatinine loss averaging about 2 g/day in 70 kg men aged 20 to 39, roughly half of it replaced by dietary creatine in omnivores), and a normal omnivorous diet covers the rest of the 1 to 3 g/day lost as creatinine. The catch is that this combined supply leaves muscle stores only around 60 to 80 percent saturated and synthesis is feedback-inhibited, so it will not rise to close the gap, while the synthesis that does run costs about 40 percent of the labile methyl groups carried by S-adenosylmethionine and 20 to 30 percent of the amidino groups of arginine. So 3 to 5 g/day adds to existing supply instead of duplicating it, which makes creatine one of the few endogenous compounds where this axis supports supplementing.
- **Pathway:** Glycine plus the amidino group of arginine gives guanidinoacetate via AGAT (GATM), mainly in kidney and pancreas. Guanidinoacetate is methylated by GAMT using S-adenosylmethionine (methyl group from dietary methionine), mainly in liver. Creatine is exported and taken up against a gradient by muscle, heart and brain through the transporter CRT1 (SLC6A8), phosphorylated by creatine kinase isoforms and lost non-enzymatically as creatinine. The brain expresses AGAT and GAMT itself and makes most of its own creatine behind the blood-brain barrier, which is why the SLC6A8 transporter defect does not respond to oral creatine.
- **Amount made:** Roughly 1 g/day of endogenous synthesis against about 2 g/day of total turnover in a 70 kg adult, with 1.5 to 2 percent of the creatine pool converted to creatinine daily (PMID 10893433). Creatinine loss averages about 2 g (14.6 mmol)/day in 70 kg men aged 20 to 39 and about half of that loss is replaced by dietary creatine in omnivores, implying endogenous synthesis on the order of 1 g/day (PMID 21387089); women and older people lose less with lower muscle mass and synthesise proportionally less, and that paper does not give the 0.75 g/day figure often quoted for women.
- **Still matters when:** Vegetarians and vegans, who start with measurably lower muscle total creatine (117 versus 130 mmol/kg dry mass) and gain most from loading (PMID 14600563); repeated high-intensity and resistance work; probably older adults; and inborn errors of AGAT (GATM), GAMT or SLC6A8, where dosing is clinical and the response differs by defect, since the two synthetic defects respond to oral creatine and the transporter defect largely does not. Rising creatinine on supplementation reflects the enlarged creatine pool and carries no implication of kidney injury.

*Refs. Kreider PMID 28615996; Hultman PMID 8828669; Chilibeck PMID 29138605; Xu Front Nutr 2024; EFSA PMID 39564533*

### Protein powder

**Verdict:** Take it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** Whatever brings total daily protein to about 1.6 g/kg. The powder itself has no special property.
- **Who it is for:** People doing resistance training who are currently falling short of 1.6 g/kg/d from food.

**Pros**

- The nutrient is Grade A. Protein supplementation on top of resistance training adds 2.49 kg to 1RM strength and 0.30 kg of fat-free mass across 49 randomised trials and 1,863 people.
- The dose-response is precisely mapped: fat-free mass gains plateau at 1.62 g/kg/d, so you know exactly when to stop paying for more.
- Convenient and shelf-stable, which is a real advantage for people who cannot cook three protein-containing meals.
- Kidney fear is unfounded in healthy people. The reassuring data are solid, though they apply to healthy kidneys only.

**Cons**

- The benefit is real and small. Three hundred grams of extra muscle over months of training is what the meta-analysis actually found. Food protein does the same job.
- Branched-chain amino acids sold alongside are redundant and mechanistically incoherent. No human study has ever quantified muscle protein synthesis from oral BCAAs alone, and both intravenous studies found BCAAs DECREASED it. A 25 g whey serving already contains 5-6 g of BCAAs plus the six essential amino acids BCAAs lack.
- Heavy-metal loading is a genuine issue in plant and organic powders, which carry more cadmium and lead than whey. Third-party testing matters here more than in most categories.
- Chronic kidney disease stage 3 or above requires a renal dietitian to set the protein prescription.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Concentrated food protein supplying the amino acids used to build and repair muscle and every tissue.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** Protein powder is isolated food protein (whey, casein, soy, pea, rice, egg) dried and concentrated. It has no function of its own; the function is that of dietary protein, which supplies the nine indispensable amino acids plus nitrogen for synthesis of muscle, enzymes, albumin, immunoglobulins, haemoglobin, hormones and connective tissue. An adult turns over roughly 250 to 300 g of body protein daily, most of it recycled, with dietary intake covering obligatory oxidative losses. The US RDA is 0.8 g/kg/day. The PROT-AGE group recommends 1.0 to 1.2 g/kg/day for healthy older adults and 1.2 to 1.5 g/kg/day in acute or chronic illness, because the muscle protein synthetic response to a given protein dose is blunted with age (PMID 23867520).
- **Deficiency disease:** Protein-energy malnutrition, presenting as marasmus and as wasting. Two conditions often listed here do not belong to dietary protein. Kwashiorkor is a form of severe acute malnutrition of uncertain aetiology, not the consequence of eating too little protein: affected children often have protein intakes comparable to marasmic children, some are still breastfed, and high-protein repletion does not preferentially resolve it. Sarcopenia is a muscle disease of ageing, inactivity, inflammation, anabolic resistance and comorbidity under the EWGSOP2 definition, and it occurs in older adults meeting the RDA; low protein intake is one modifiable contributor to it, not the deficiency state itself.
- **First signs:** Unintentional weight loss, falling mid-upper arm circumference, loss of subcutaneous fat and muscle bulk, thinning brittle hair, delayed wound healing and more frequent infections. Serum albumin falls late and is heavily confounded by inflammation, so it is a poor early marker. In older adults, declining grip strength and slower gait speed usually appear before visible muscle loss.
- **Untreated:** Marasmus is severe wasting: weight-for-height far below normal, visible loss of muscle and fat, an aged appearance in a young child. Kwashiorkor adds pitting oedema, an enlarged fatty liver, flaky-paint skin desquamation and hair depigmentation. Both carry high case fatality from hypoglycaemia, hypothermia, electrolyte disturbance and overwhelming infection; without structured refeeding the endpoint is death. In older adults, untreated sarcopenia ends in falls, fracture, loss of independence and raised mortality.
- **Does it reverse:** Acute wasting reverses with careful staged refeeding, and weight-for-height recovers. What does not fully recover is linear growth and neurocognitive development when the episode falls within the first 1000 days: stunting established before age two is largely permanent, and the associated cognitive deficits persist into adulthood. Sarcopenia is partly reversible, since resistance training with adequate protein rebuilds strength and some mass, but muscle lost over decades is not fully regained.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Children in food-insecure settings, especially around weaning and following infection. Hospitalised, post-surgical and critically ill patients. Older adults with poor appetite, dental problems, dysphagia or social isolation. People with malabsorption, cirrhosis, or cachexia from cancer, advanced kidney or heart failure. Anorexia nervosa. Healthy adults in wealthy countries eating adequate calories almost never fall short of protein.
- **How common:** In 2024, 42.8 million children under five were wasted (6.6%) and 12.2 million severely wasted (1.9%); 150.2 million were stunted (UNICEF/WHO/World Bank Joint Malnutrition Estimates, 2025 edition). Pooled sarcopenia prevalence in older adults is 17.7% under EWGSOP1 and 11% under EWGSOP2, with study-level ranges of 6.2% to 35.3% and 3.2% to 26.3% respectively (Fernandes LV et al., Aging Clin Exp Res. 2022;34:505-514).

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Partly

Protein powder is itself a food fraction, so there is no molecule here that food lacks: whey and casein are dairy by-products, soya and pea isolates are legume fractions. Ordinary single portions deliver 5-53 g each, with grilled turkey breast fillet at 35.0 g/100 g and fresh Parmesan at 36.2 g/100 g topping the table. This is the one item on the list where everyday food comfortably beats the supplement.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Beef rump steak, grilled, lean | 31.0 g protein | 1 steak, 170 g cooked | 52.7 g | 47% of 112 g/day (1.6 g/kg at 70 kg) | McCance and Widdowson's Composition of Foods Integrated Dataset (CoFID) 2021, food code 18-474 (code and value verified against the dataset) |
| Turkey breast fillet, grilled, meat only | 35.0 g protein | 1 fillet, 150 g cooked | 52.5 g | 47% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021, food code 18-356 (added: richer per 100 g than anything in the original list) |
| Chicken breast, grilled without skin, meat only | 32.0 g protein | 1 breast, 130 g cooked | 41.6 g | 37% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021, food code 18-323 (verified) |

| Mackerel, grilled | 20.3 g protein | 1 fillet, 150 g cooked | 30.5 g | 27% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021 (value identified by the verification report; food code not stated there) |

| Greek style yogurt, plain (UK whole milk type) | 5.7 g protein | 1 pot, 170 g | 9.7 g | 9% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021, food code 12-555 (verified). Note this is the traditional product; modern strained pots and skyr run roughly 10-12 g/100 g. |
| Milk, semi-skimmed, pasteurised | 3.5 g protein | 1 large glass, 250 mL (258 g) | 9.0 g | 8% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021, food code 12-313 (verified) |
| Tofu, soya bean, steamed | 8.1 g protein | 1 serving, 100 g | 8.1 g | 7% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021, food code 13-570 (verified) |
| Cheddar cheese, English | 25.4 g protein | 1 matchbox piece, 30 g | 7.6 g | 7% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021, food code 12-346 (verified) |
| Pumpkin seeds | 24.4 g protein | 1 handful, 30 g | 7.3 g | 7% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021 (value identified by the verification report; food code not stated there) |
| Peanut butter, smooth | 22.8 g protein | 1 tbsp, 20 g | 4.6 g | 4% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021, food code 14-892 (verified) |

- **To match the dose:** A 1.6 g/kg target is 112 g/day for a 70 kg adult and food covers it without powder. Worked day: 2 boiled eggs (14.1 g) plus a 170 g pot of Greek style yogurt (9.7 g) plus a 130 g grilled chicken breast (41.6 g) plus 200 g cooked red lentils (16.2 g) plus 250 mL semi-skimmed milk (9.0 g) plus 30 g Cheddar (7.6 g) totals 98.2 g; add one 100 g can of tuna in brine and it reaches 123 g, over target. A 100 kg athlete at 1.6 g/kg (160 g) gets there by adding a 150 g grilled turkey breast fillet (52.5 g) or a 170 g rump steak (52.7 g). A shopper does better than the table suggests on dairy, since modern strained pots and skyr run roughly 10-12 g/100 g against CoFID's traditional Greek style yogurt at 5.7 g. The gap appears only at very high targets combined with poor appetite, a low-calorie diet or exclusively plant sources, where food volume becomes the binding constraint. Powder solves convenience and calorie density.
- **Absorption:** Absorption differences between food and powder are about speed and amino acid pattern, not quantity absorbed. Animal-source protein is more digestible and more leucine-dense per gram than most plant sources: roast chicken breast is 31.0 g protein and 2.33 g leucine per 100 g, that is 7.5% of protein as leucine (USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171477, SR Legacy NDB 5064, both values verified). Whey is faster and more leucine-rich per gram than whole food, casein and whole-food meals are slower, and for total daily protein this difference is small. One genuine food-form penalty: composition tables report protein as nitrogen multiplied by a conversion factor, so figures for plant foods with non-protein nitrogen slightly overstate usable protein, and cooking method changes yield per gram bought (raw beef mince is 19.7 g/100 g versus 21.8 g/100 g stewed as water is lost, CoFID 18-469 and 18-470, both verified).
- **Does the body make it:** The roll-up is PARTIAL and it hides three tiers: nine amino acids have no human pathway to their carbon skeletons (histidine, isoleucine, leucine, lysine, methionine, phenylalanine, threonine, tryptophan, valine) and score NONE, five are built freely from central intermediates (alanine, aspartate, asparagine, glutamate, serine) and score FULL, and six sit in the IOM's conditionally indispensable middle tier (arginine, cysteine, glutamine, glycine, proline, tyrosine), made in health and needed from the diet in prematurity, trauma, sepsis or specific enzyme defects. Roughly 250 to 300 g of body protein is degraded and resynthesised per day, several times typical intake, so reutilisation dominates supply and the powder functions as a convenience format for hitting total daily protein. It therefore makes sense only when total intake sits below target, and above roughly 1.62 g/kg/day in resistance trainees it adds nothing.
- **Pathway:** Dispensable amino acids come from central metabolism: alanine from pyruvate, glutamate and glutamine from alpha-ketoglutarate, aspartate from oxaloacetate, asparagine from aspartate via asparagine synthetase (ASNS), serine from 3-phosphoglycerate, glycine from serine via SHMT, proline from glutamate, cysteine from methionine sulfur plus the serine carbon skeleton by transsulfuration, tyrosine from phenylalanine, and arginine through the intestinal-renal citrulline axis. There is no human route to the carbon skeletons of the nine indispensable amino acids, so dietary protein is a genuine requirement that no supply of glucose or fat substitutes for.
- **Amount made:** Roughly 250 to 300 g of body protein is degraded and resynthesised per day in an adult, several times typical Western intake, so amino acid reutilisation dominates supply (FAO/WHO/UNU 2007; IOM DRI 2005, standard turnover figures 3 to 4 g/kg/day). The adult dietary requirement is 0.8 g protein/kg/day, about 56 g for a 70 kg adult (IOM DRI 2005). Net synthesis of the nine indispensable amino acids is 0.
- **Still matters when:** Total daily protein below target, energy deficit, older adults with anabolic resistance, vegan diets low in leucine and lysine, clinical malnutrition, burns and recovery. For resistance trainees the meta-regression breakpoint is about 1.62 g/kg/day (95% CI 1.03 to 2.20), beyond which extra protein produced no further fat-free mass gain (PMID 28698222); above that intake the powder is redundant.

*Refs. Morton PMID 28698222; Jager PMID 28642676; Wolfe PMID 28852372; Kato PMID 27322029*

### Psyllium husk (ispaghula)

**Verdict:** Take it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** About 10 g/d, always with a full glass of fluid. Separate from other oral drugs by about two hours.
- **Who it is for:** People with raised LDL cholesterol, irritable bowel syndrome of either subtype, or chronic constipation.

**Pros**

- LDL cholesterol falls 0.33 mmol/L (95% CI -0.38 to -0.27) at a median 10.2 g/d across 28 trials, and the apolipoprotein B evidence is graded HIGH quality, which is rare anywhere in this field.
- Irritable bowel syndrome: relative risk of persistent symptoms 0.83 (0.73-0.94), NNT 7. Very few gut products have a number this good.
- Cheap, generic and unglamorous, which is why it is under-sold rather than over-sold.
- Works in both constipation-predominant and diarrhoea-predominant IBS, because it is a gel-forming fibre rather than a stimulant.

**Cons**

- No hard-outcome trial exists. The LDL reduction is a surrogate endpoint, so nobody has shown psyllium prevents a heart attack.
- Contraindicated in dysphagia and in known or suspected bowel obstruction, stricture, impaction or ileus. This is an explicit FDA contraindication, not a formality.
- It must be taken with enough fluid. Bed-bound patients with poor fluid intake should not use it.
- It delays absorption of other oral drugs taken at the same time, which matters for narrow-therapeutic-index medicines.
- Wheat bran, sold beside it under identical digestive-health signage, is a different product and is null in IBS (RR 0.90) and often makes it worse. Do not substitute.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A gel-forming fibre that holds water in stool, making it softer, bulkier and easier to pass.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Psyllium is the milled seed husk of Plantago ovata, largely a highly branched arabinoxylan that is gel-forming and, unusually among viscous fibres, poorly fermented by colonic bacteria. Because it survives intact to the rectum it retains its water there, producing bulky soft stool with far less gas than fermentable fibres. It normalises stool form in both directions, softening hard stool and firming loose stool. The same gel raises the viscosity of small intestinal contents, which slows glucose absorption and traps bile acids; the resulting loss of bile acids drives hepatic synthesis from cholesterol and lowers LDL. In IBS it is the fibre with actual trial support: ispaghula gave a relative risk of IBS not improving of 0.83 (95% CI 0.73 to 0.94), number needed to treat 7 (95% CI 4 to 25), and is the fibre recommended by the ACG guideline (PMID 33315591).
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Psyllium husk is itself a food ingredient, the milled seed coat of Plantago ovata, so the supplement and the food are the same material and nothing is extracted or concentrated. Among ordinary foods, the genuine analogues for viscous gel-forming fibre are oat and barley beta-glucan and the mucilage of ground flaxseed and chia; konjac glucomannan and guar gum are also sold as foods and are more viscous still, though no confirmed per-portion figure was available for them here. Legumes are not in the same class and have been dropped from that claim: their soluble fibre measures 0.41-1.36 g/100 g and, as this entry's own bioavailability section says, it ferments proximally and behaves differently at the same gram intake. Beta-glucan is also chemically distinct from psyllium's arabinoxylan gel.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Psyllium husk powder, plain (food-grade, unflavoured) | 67 g soluble fibre and 11 g insoluble fibre as declared on the label record; the 78 g total is the sum of those two declarations rather than a measured value. Independent analyses of psyllium husk put total dietary fibre at roughly 71-85 g/100 g. | 1 tbsp (9 g declared serving in the same record) | 6.0 g soluble fibre; about 7.0 g total fibre on the summed figure | 9 g of husk is 90% of the 10 g/day husk dose in the Bijkerk IBS trial (PMID 19713235), computed on husk mass; the 7.0 g of total fibre is 23% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake, computed on fibre mass | USDA FoodData Central branded record 1742558 (NOW Psyllium Husk Powder). This is a manufacturer label declaration, not a laboratory analysis, and the record's own 'Fiber, total dietary' field reads 0 g, so the 78 g total is arithmetic on the two declared fractions. The 71-85 g/100 g range comes from independent analyses of psyllium husk. |
| Oat bran, raw | 15.4 g total dietary fibre; oat bran beta-glucan is roughly 5.5-8% w/w | 1 cup dry (94 g) | 14.5 g total dietary fibre, of which roughly 5.2-7.5 g is beta-glucan | 48% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake; the beta-glucan alone is 1.7-2.5x the 3 g/day beta-glucan target | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 168872 (total fibre, verified). Beta-glucan share from Stevenson et al., Int J Food Sci Nutr 2012;63(8):1001-13, DOI 10.3109/09637486.2012.687366 (oat soluble fibre 3-7% of dry weight; oat bran about 5.5-8% beta-glucan). The submission's Hughes and Grafenauer 2021 citation has been removed: that paper is a packaged-product label audit and contains no grain composition data. |
| Chia seed, dried | roughly 27-34 g total dietary fibre, a substantial part of it viscous mucilage (the mucilage fraction is not quantified in the source used here) | 1 oz (28 g), conventional serving | 7.6-9.5 g total dietary fibre | 25-32% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake; no soluble-fibre comparison against the psyllium dose is possible because the mucilage fraction is unquantified here | Fibre range as supplied in the verification report for ground flaxseed and chia (roughly 27-34 g/100 g); indicative and not traced to a specific composition record in this pass. Soluble and insoluble split from Li BW, Andrews KW, Pehrsson PR, J Food Compos Anal 2002;15(6):715-723, DOI 10.1006/jfca.2002.1096. The paper is genuine and correctly cited but its per-food table could not be retrieved, so these fractions are |
| Flaxseed (linseed), ground | roughly 27-34 g total dietary fibre with a large viscous mucilage fraction (fraction not quantified in the source used here) | 2 tbsp ground (about 14 g), conventional serving | 3.8-4.8 g total dietary fibre | 13-16% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | Fibre range as supplied in the verification report for ground flaxseed and chia (roughly 27-34 g/100 g); indicative and not traced to a specific composition record in this pass. Portion weight is a conventional serving, not from a source record. |
| Barley, pearled, cooked | 3.8 g total dietary fibre (cooked, hydrated weight) | 1 cup cooked (157 g) | 6.0 g total dietary fibre | 20% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 170285, verified. Barley soluble fibre of 3-11% w/w (Stevenson et al., DOI 10.3109/09637486.2012.687366) is a dry-grain figure and is not applied to this cooked weight. |
| Oatmeal, regular, cooked with water *[unconfirmed]* | 0.42 g soluble fibre, 1.23 g insoluble fibre, 1.65 g total (AOAC 991.43) | 1 cup cooked (234 g) | 0.98 g soluble fibre; 3.9 g total fibre | 15% of the roughly 6.7 g of soluble fibre in a 10 g psyllium husk dose | Portion weight USDA FoodData Central 173905. Soluble and insoluble fractions from Li BW et al., DOI 10.1006/jfca.2002.1096; the data table could not be retrieved, so these fractions are |

- **To match the dose:** The 10 g/day husk dose that beat placebo in primary-care IBS (Bijkerk 2009, BMJ 339:b3154, PMID 19713235) is about one heaped tablespoon of a product sold as food, and 9 g of husk powder already supplies 6.0 g of soluble fibre, so for psyllium itself the gap is zero. Matching that 6-7 g of soluble fibre from other ordinary foods is a different question: at 0.42 g/100 g you would need roughly 1.6 kg of cooked porridge (about 6.8 cups), and canned kidney beans at 1.36 g/100 g would take about 493 g, which is 2.8 cups at 177 g per cup, corrected from the submission's 3 cups. Both of those soluble-fibre fractions come from one study table that could not be retrieved, so treat the kilogram figures as indicative. The separate and easier target of 3 g/day of oat or barley beta-glucan is reachable by eating: about 67-75 g of dry rolled oats at 4-4.5% w/w, about 40-55 g of oat bran at 5.5-8%, or about 75 g of barley at 4%. The submission's 40-50 g of dry oats applied an oat-bran-level percentage to whole oats and has been corrected.
- **Food vs supplement:** There is no extract-versus-food problem because psyllium husk is unprocessed plant material, but three real caveats apply. Physical: the husk must be taken with a full glass of fluid, since a gel-forming dose swallowed dry can obstruct the oesophagus or bowel. Compositional: 'soluble fibre' on a label is not one substance. Psyllium's arabinoxylan gel is viscous and poorly fermented, while inulin carries the same soluble-fibre label and ferments fast with gas production, which is the opposite of what an IBS patient wants, so gram-for-gram substitution is a mistake. Processing: oat beta-glucan's benefit tracks viscosity, which depends on molecular weight, and milling, extrusion and prolonged heating degrade it, so a heavily processed oat product with an identical gram figure on the label is not equivalent to whole rolled oats.
- **Absorption:** Nothing here is absorbed, so bioavailability means viscosity, water-holding and fermentability. Psyllium holds water through the whole colon with a low gas yield, which is why it helps both constipation and diarrhoea; legume soluble fibre and inulin are largely fermented in the proximal colon and behave quite differently at the same gram intake. Analytical method is a genuine source of confusion: AOAC 991.43 gravimetric soluble-fibre values run systematically higher than the enzymatic-chemical (Englyst/NSP) values used in UK tables, so soluble-fibre numbers from different tables should not be mixed. Head-to-head evidence supports the distinction between fibre classes: at the same 10 g dose, psyllium beat placebo at one and two months while wheat bran did not (PMID 19713235).
- **Does the body make it:** Psyllium is a highly branched arabinoxylan that no human enzyme can cleave, which is the operational meaning of dietary fibre. Being non-digestible is the mechanism: the husk holds water into a viscous gel and the gel does the work on stool form and transit. So the axis has nothing to say against it, and psyllium is the one fibre with reasonable randomised support in IBS.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Dietary fibre is defined by resistance to human digestive enzymes; humans lack the glycoside hydrolases that would depolymerise the arabinoxylan backbone.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway; intake is the only variable)
- **Still matters when:** Every gram must be eaten, so intake is the whole question. In primary care IBS (n=275: 85 psyllium, 97 bran, 93 placebo, ages 18 to 65), 10 g/day psyllium gave adequate symptom relief in 57 percent versus 35 percent on placebo at month 1 (RR 1.60, 95 percent CI 1.13 to 2.26), also favoured psyllium at month 2 (59 percent versus 41 percent, RR 1.44, 95 percent CI 1.02 to 2.06), and reduced the symptom severity score by 90 points at 3 months against 49 for placebo (P=0.03). Start low and titrate, since gas and bloating are dose-related.

*Refs. Jovanovski PMID 30239559; Moayyedi PMID 25070054; Bijkerk BMJ 2009 PMID 19713235; Reynolds Lancet 2019 PMID 30638909*

### Caffeine

**Verdict:** Take it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 3-6 mg/kg body mass, about 60 minutes before exercise. For a 70 kg person that is 210-420 mg.
- **Who it is for:** Almost anyone training, trained or untrained. Also shift workers and the sleep-deprived, for the separate alertness effect.

**Pros**

- The most reliably ergogenic legal substance in this document. An umbrella review of 11 systematic reviews containing 21 meta-analyses, all of moderate or high quality, found benefit for aerobic endurance, strength, muscle endurance, power, jumping and speed.
- Cheap, well characterised, and the dose-response is known.
- The mechanism is central rather than metabolic, so it works by reducing perceived effort and pain, which is why it helps across so many different tasks.

**Cons**

- Genotype changes the sign of the effect. CYP1A2 AA carriers gain (SMD 0.30), AC carriers gain a little (0.16), and CC carriers get WORSE (SMD -0.22). Most people do not know their genotype.
- 9 mg/kg brings a high incidence of side effects with no added benefit. More is not better past 6 mg/kg.
- The fat-burner claim is the oversold part of the category. Thermogenesis is real and trivial.
- Contraindicated or needing care in uncontrolled hypertension, arrhythmia, anxiety and panic disorder. EFSA caps pregnancy at 200 mg/d, and derives no safe intake for children.
- Fluvoxamine is a potent CYP1A2 inhibitor and can raise caffeine exposure severalfold. Taken late, it damages the sleep that training depends on.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Blocks the brain's sleepiness signal, so you feel more alert and effort feels easier.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Caffeine is an adenosine receptor antagonist, principally at A1 and A2A receptors. Blocking A1 lifts the inhibitory brake that accumulating adenosine places on arousal circuits, and blocking striatal A2A disinhibits dopaminergic signalling, which produces the alertness and the reduced perception of effort. It is a plant alkaloid and a xenobiotic. No human enzyme, receptor or pathway requires it, and there is no intake below which anything fails. Caffeine withdrawal is not a deficiency state: habitual intake upregulates adenosine receptors, so abrupt cessation unmasks adenosine signalling and produces headache, fatigue and low mood for roughly 2 to 9 days, with headache in about half of regular users. That is physical dependence on a drug, and it resolves completely without resuming the drug.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Caffeine in ordinary drinks sits at the same scale as the supplement dose: USDA gives home brewed coffee 40 mg/100 g and restaurant espresso 212 mg/100 g, and the Brazilian caffeine table gives espresso 279 mg/100 mL. Guarana seed powder at roughly 2-6 g/100 g and instant coffee powder on a dry basis at roughly 3-4 g/100 g overshoot a full dose from a spoonful, though neither figure was traced to a primary analysis in this review. Tea, matcha, cola and dark chocolate all contribute at tens of milligrams per serving, and yerba mate belongs in the same group though no figure was available to place it.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Guarana seed powder *[unconfirmed]* | roughly 2,000-6,000 mg caffeine (2-6 g/100 g), about 25-75 times brewed coffee per gram | 1 tsp, 3 g | 60-180 mg | 30-90% of a 200 mg dose | Approximate literature range supplied by the verification report as the single richest natural caffeine source |
| Espresso (Brazilian caffeine content table, BraCaffT) | 279 mg per 100 mL | 1 cup, 40 mL | 112 mg | 56% of a 200 mg dose | Rocha PLA, Lima ALC, Saunders B, Reis CEG. Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417. PMID 36297100; DOI 10.3390/nu14204417, Table 1 (value confirmed) |
| Coffee, brewed from grounds, prepared with tap water | 40 mg caffeine | 1 mug, 240 mL (240 g) | 96 mg | 48% of a 200 mg dose; 46% of 210 mg (3 mg/kg at 70 kg) | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171890 (SR Legacy, NDB 14209); FDC ID, NDB number, description and value all verified |
| Energy drink | 30 mg per 100 mL | 1 can, 250 mL | 75 mg | 38% of a 200 mg dose | Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, Table 1. PMID 36297100 (confirmed) |
| Instant coffee powder, dry basis *[unconfirmed]* | roughly 3,000-4,000 mg caffeine (3-4 g/100 g) | 1 rounded tsp, 2 g | 60-80 mg | 30-40% of a 200 mg dose | Approximate range supplied by the verification report |
| Coffee, brewed espresso, restaurant-prepared | 212 mg caffeine | 1 single shot, 30 mL (30 g) | 64 mg (double shot, 60 g: 127 mg) | 32% of a 200 mg dose per single shot | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171891 (SR Legacy, NDB 14210); all identifiers and the value verified |
| Matcha, green tea powder | 1,890-4,440 mg per 100 g (18.9-44.4 mg/g) | 1 tsp, 2 g | 38-89 mg | 19-44% of a 200 mg dose | Kochman J, Jakubczyk K, Antoniewicz J, Mruk H, Janda K. Molecules 2020;26(1):85. PMID 33375458; DOI 10.3390/molecules26010085 (range confirmed verbatim, citation year defensible) |
| Dark chocolate (Brazilian table, higher value) | 114 mg per 100 g | 1 serving, 45 g | 51 mg | 26% of a 200 mg dose | Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, Table 1. PMID 36297100 (confirmed) |
| Tea, green, brewed | 20 mg per 100 mL | 1 cup, 200 mL | 40 mg | 20% of a 200 mg dose | Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, Table 1. PMID 36297100 (confirmed) |
| Tea, black, brewed | 18 mg per 100 mL | 1 cup, 200 mL | 36 mg (measured range across brewed white, green and black teas 14-61 mg per 6-8 oz serving) | 18% of a 200 mg dose | Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417 (DOI 10.3390/nu14204417), confirmed; range from Chin JM et al., J Anal Toxicol 2008;32(8):702-4, DOI 10.1093/jat/32.8.702 |
| Cola soft drink | 9 mg per 100 mL | 1 can, 350 mL | 32 mg | 16% of a 200 mg dose | Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, Table 1. PMID 36297100 (confirmed) |
| Chocolate, dark, 70-85% cacao solids | 80 mg caffeine (plus 802 mg theobromine) | 4 squares, 25 g | 20 mg | 10% of a 200 mg dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170273 (SR Legacy, NDB 19904); both values verified |

- **To match the dose:** An ergogenic 3-6 mg/kg is 210-420 mg for a 70 kg adult, which is two to four mugs of home brewed coffee at 96 mg each; a 200 mg pre-workout dose is about two mugs or three single espresso shots. What food cannot deliver is a known dose. Measured caffeine in caffeinated specialty coffees ranged 58-259 mg per dose, mean 188 mg (SD 36) for a 16 oz cup, and the same beverage bought from the same outlet on six consecutive days ranged 259-564 mg, a 2.2-fold swing in what the customer thought was an identical drink. Decaffeinated versions still contained up to 17.7 mg per dose. Matcha spans 18.9-44.4 mg/g, so one teaspoon is anywhere from 38 to 89 mg. Reaching the dose from drinks is trivial; hitting it within plus or minus 20% is not, which is why trials use capsules.
- **Food vs supplement:** The caveat is inverted here. Anhydrous caffeine powder is the same molecule and is dangerous precisely because it is concentrated: a level teaspoon is several grams, in the range associated with fatal poisoning, whereas the same amount as brewed coffee would be 30 to 50 mugs and physically self-limiting. Whole-drink forms carry matrix effects that change the experience and the risk profile. Coffee brings chlorogenic acids and, when unfiltered, diterpenes that raise LDL; tea and matcha bring L-theanine and catechins that alter the subjective stimulation; energy drinks bring sugar, taurine and often glucuronolactone, so a caffeine dose from a can is never only caffeine. Guarana concentrates the same molecule in a seed powder, which is how many energy products reach their potency without looking like a caffeine tablet. Tea and coffee polyphenols also inhibit non-haem iron absorption when taken with meals, a cost the capsule does not impose. Energy drink and specialty coffee servings are large and easy to stack, and label values are nominal rather than analysed.
- **Absorption:** Caffeine is essentially completely absorbed from every form tested, so there is no meaningful absorption penalty for the food route; the differences are in rate and in dose certainty. Liquids reach peak plasma concentrations faster than capsules, chewing gum faster still, and a hot drink sipped over 20 minutes produces a flatter curve than a capsule swallowed at once, which matters for timing a pre-exercise dose and not for total exposure. Analytical values differ between databases for the same drink because brewing dominates: USDA lists brewed coffee at 40 mg/100 g and espresso at 212 mg/100 g (FDC 171890, 171891), while the Brazilian table lists espresso at 279 mg/100 mL (DOI 10.3390/nu14204417). Both are correct for what they measured, which is the whole point: the food is the variable, and the absorption is not.
- **Does the body make it:** Humans have no caffeine biosynthetic pathway: there is no caffeine synthase and no route from purine nucleotides to caffeine in any animal, so every milligram in your blood was eaten or drunk, and the human enzymology is purely disposal, dominated by hepatic CYP1A2. Note what NONE does and does not imply here, since caffeine is a drug and not a nutrient, so there is no deficiency to correct and no redundancy to worry about. This axis neither supports nor undermines it, and the decision rests entirely on pharmacology and dose.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE in humans. In Coffea and Camellia, xanthosine is methylated by 7-methylxanthosine synthase (XMT), the ribose is then removed by an N-methylnucleosidase to give 7-methylxanthine, which is methylated to theobromine by theobromine synthase (MXMT) and then to caffeine by caffeine synthase (DXMT), a bifunctional enzyme. Human enzymology covers disposal only, dominated by hepatic CYP1A2.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/day, no endogenous synthesis. On the disposal side, CYP1A2 metabolises about 95 percent of ingested caffeine (PMID 29514871).
- **Still matters when:** Whenever acute adenosine receptor antagonism is the goal: alertness, reduced perceived effort, endurance and sprint performance, typically 3 to 6 mg/kg. The endogenous axis is silent on caffeine; the real constraints are habitual tolerance, CYP1A2 genotype, sleep disruption and cumulative daily dose.

*Refs. Guest ISSN PMID 33388079; Grgic PMID 30926628; Barreto PMID 37844569; EFSA J 2015;13(5):4102*

### Omega-3 (EPA and DHA)

**Verdict:** Take it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 4 g/d of prescription-grade product if fasting triglycerides are at or above 500 mg/dL. Otherwise eat oily fish.
- **Who it is for:** People with severe hypertriglyceridaemia. Separately, pregnant women with genuinely low baseline DHA, and people who eat essentially no fish.

**Pros**

- Triglyceride lowering is genuine pharmacology: 4 g/d cuts triglycerides by at least 30% in severe hypertriglyceridaemia, and the AHA endorses this specific indication.
- REDUCE-IT was strongly positive in statin-treated patients with established cardiovascular disease: primary composite 17.2% versus 22.0%, HR 0.75 (0.68-0.83), an absolute reduction of 4.8 percentage points over 4.9 years.
- Pregnancy: Cochrane found preterm birth before 37 weeks RR 0.89 (0.81-0.97) and before 34 weeks RR 0.58 (0.44-0.77), both graded high quality, across 70 trials and 19,927 women.

**Cons**

- It does not prevent cardiovascular disease at supplement doses. Cochrane, 86 trials and 162,796 people: all-cause mortality RR 0.97 (0.93-1.01) at HIGH certainty, stroke RR 1.02. VITAL and ASCEND both missed their primary endpoints at 1 g/d.
- The two big high-dose trials contradict each other. STRENGTH tested 4 g/d of EPA plus DHA against corn oil in 13,078 people and was flatly null (HR 0.99), stopped for futility. REDUCE-IT used a mineral-oil placebo that raised LDL, apolipoprotein B and hs-CRP in the control arm, so part of its 25% may be placebo-arm harm. Quote both trials or neither.
- Atrial fibrillation risk rises with dose: HR 1.25 (1.07-1.46), dose-dependent across 7 trials. Prior atrial fibrillation is a reason to stay away.
- Bleeding risk on warfarin, direct oral anticoagulants, aspirin and clopidogrel. REDUCE-IT showed serious bleeding 2.7% versus 2.1%.
- Product quality is poor. Half of North American products exceeded voluntary oxidation limits, and 38% of Australian products exceeded primary oxidation limits, with premium pricing giving no advantage. Buy encapsulated, unflavoured and in date.
- Pregnancy is not straightforward either: ORIP (n=5,486, 900 mg/d) was null and showed excess very-large-for-gestational-age infants. Benefit concentrates in women with low baseline DHA.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Long-chain fats built into brain, retina and heart cell membranes; they also help resolve inflammation.

- **If you lack it:** Low status causes dysfunction
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** EPA (20:5n-3) and DHA (22:6n-3) are the long-chain products of omega-3 metabolism. DHA is the dominant structural fatty acid of retinal photoreceptor outer segments and neuronal synaptic membranes, where it sets membrane fluidity and supports the function of embedded proteins such as rhodopsin. EPA is the substrate for series-3 prostaglandins and series-5 leukotrienes, and both are precursors of the resolvins and protectins that actively terminate inflammation. Because the body can make them from alpha-linolenic acid, they are classed as conditionally essential rather than strictly essential; the conversion is inefficient enough that tissue levels track intake of preformed EPA and DHA closely.
- **Deficiency disease:** NONE. No named clinical deficiency disease exists for EPA and DHA specifically, because they can be synthesised from ALA.
- **First signs:** Nothing a person would notice. The first measurable change is biochemical: a falling omega-3 index, meaning EPA plus DHA as a percentage of red cell fatty acids. Values at or below about 4% define the low band and 8% or above was the proposed target (Harris and von Schacky, PMID 15208005). Symptoms sometimes attributed to low omega-3 status, such as dry skin or low mood, are non-specific and have not been shown to track the index.
- **Untreated:** There is no severe clinical syndrome attributable to low EPA and DHA alone in an adult who is otherwise fed. Frank neurological deficiency signs belong to total omega-3 deprivation, which is an ALA problem (see that entry). The clearest documented consequence of low long-chain omega-3 supply is developmental: preterm infants cut off from third-trimester placental DHA transfer accrete far less retinal and brain DHA, with fetal accretion roughly doubling in the last five weeks of gestation.
- **Does it reverse:** In adults repletion is complete. Red cell EPA and DHA rise over roughly three to four months, approximately the erythrocyte lifespan, and normalise fully with sustained intake. The partial designation covers the perinatal window only: in rhesus monkeys deprived of n-3 before birth, brain phospholipids had normalised by three years but retinal DHA reached only 84% of controls. Behavioural visual acuity thresholds were normal at 4, 8 and 12 weeks, but cone and rod ERG a-wave amplitudes stayed persistently reduced, so the residue after prenatal deprivation is functional as well as biochemical, and the study answers its own title question in the negative (Anderson GJ et al., Pediatr Res. 2005;58:865-72).
- **Who becomes deficient:** People who eat no oily fish and take no supplement. Vegans and vegetarians, who have no direct dietary source of preformed EPA or DHA. Preterm infants. Carriers of FADS1/FADS2 minor alleles, who have lower desaturase activity and convert ALA less efficiently.
- **How common:** Not a disease rate. As a status distribution, omega-3 index values at or below 4% are typical across North America, Central and South America, Europe, the Middle East, Southeast Asia and Africa, while levels above 8% occur mainly around the Sea of Japan, in Scandinavia and in some indigenous coastal populations, based on 298 studies (Stark et al., PMID 27216485).

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Partly

Oily fish supply EPA and DHA in gram quantities per ordinary portion, so this is one of the few supplement categories where food competes with capsules at physiological doses. One 143 g herring fillet or one 130 g salmon portion delivers 1.8-2.9 g EPA+DHA, roughly seven to twelve times a single day's EFSA Adequate Intake of 250 mg (EFSA Journal 2010;8(3):1461, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2010.1461; EFSA DRVs summary 2017). The lower end of that range is deliberate: the USDA farmed-salmon entry reflects older analyses, and in over 3,000 Scottish farmed salmon EPA+DHA fell from 2.74 g to 1.36 g per 100 g wet weight between 2006 and 2015 as feed shifted from fish oil to vegetable oil (Sprague 2016, DOI 10.1038/srep21892), giving 1.8 g for the same 130 g portion. Treat the farmed-salmon figures below as an upper bound and roughly halve them for current farmed fish. The richest sources in the USDA database are not fillets at all: caviar carries 6,540 mg EPA+DHA per 100 g and mixed fish roe 2,343 mg, both above cooked herring.

Eaten as a 100 g portion, which is how cod roe and herring roe are normally served, the same food supplies 2,343 mg, above cooked Atlantic herring | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175132 (SR Legacy), composition and 14 g tablespoon supplied and confirmed by the verification pass |

- **To match the dose:** Two doses with two different answers. The physiological dose (EFSA AI 250 mg/day, evidence range 250-500 mg/day) is easily eaten: one 143 g herring fillet a week averages 410 mg/day, one 130 g farmed salmon portion 399 mg/day, one wild salmon portion 342 mg/day. Two mackerel fillets, two 92 g tins of sardines or two 45 g tins of anchovies a week give 258-302 mg/day. In portion terms 250-500 mg/day is 0.6-1.3 salmon portions, 0.6-1.2 herring fillets, 1.7-3.3 mackerel fillets, 1.9-3.9 tins of sardines, 1.9-3.8 tins of anchovies, 2.8-5.6 trout fillets, or 1.2-2.4 USDA 172 g cans of albacore per week. Tinned light tuna is the weakest realistic route at 370 mg per 165 g can, which is 5 cans a week for 250 mg/day and 9 cans a week for 500 mg/day. The pharmacological dose of 4 g/day used in trials and in prescription products (AHA Science Advisory, Circulation 2019, DOI 10.1161/CIR.0000000000000709; REDUCE-IT used 4 g icosapent ethyl) cannot be eaten. It requires 199 g/day of herring (1.4 kg/week), 186 g/day of farmed salmon on 2006-era composition or 294 g/day on 2015 composition (1.3-2.1 kg/week), 288 g/day of pickled herring, 333 g/day of mackerel (3.8 fillets a day), 407 g/day of sardines (4.4 tins a day), 195 g/day of anchovies (4.3 tins a day, with the sodium load that implies), 61 g/day of caviar (about four tablespoons a day), or 464 g/day of tinned albacore. Above roughly 1 g/day of EPA+DHA, supplements are the only realistic route.
- **Food vs supplement:** Whole fish and fish oil are not interchangeable in the evidence base. VITAL randomised 25,871 adults to a 1 g/day capsule supplying 840 mg EPA+DHA (460 mg EPA, 380 mg DHA) and found no reduction in major cardiovascular events (HR 0.92, 95% CI 0.80-1.06) or cancer incidence (HR 1.03) over 5.3 years (Manson 2019, PMID 30415637). The benefit at 4 g/day in REDUCE-IT came from purified prescription EPA rather than a food-equivalent mixture, and high-dose omega-3 carries an atrial fibrillation signal that eating fish does not. On contaminants, the direction of the tradeoff is clear but the numbers are weaker than they look: the FDA/EPA per-species mercury means quoted in the earlier draft (1.45 ppm Gulf of Mexico tilefish, 1.00 swordfish, 0.98 shark, 0.73 king mackerel, 0.35 albacore, 0.13 canned light tuna, 0.01-0.08 for sardine, salmon, anchovy, Atlantic mackerel, trout and herring) could not be retrieved from any live FDA or EPA page, so treat every one of them as indicative and unconfirmed. The tilefish figure in particular should be re-checked, since FDA's published Gulf of Mexico mean is usually cited as 1.123 ppm. What the surviving EPA page does confirm is the qualitative placement: anchovies, Atlantic mackerel, sardines and trout are all 'Best Choices'. Independent synthesis in Karimi 2012 (PMID 22732656) gives grand means of 0.882 ppm for shark and 0.883 ppm for tilefish, and 0.450 ppm for fresh or frozen tuna, which is not the canned product and should not be read as such. The earlier claim that high-mercury predators are also mediocre omega-3 sources has been withdrawn: it rested on a swordfish figure of 108 mg EPA per 85 g with no FDC ID that could not be traced, and an EPA-only figure understates a DHA-dominant fish. Dioxins run the other way, concentrating in the fat of oily fish. EFSA cut the tolerable weekly intake to 2 pg TEQ/kg body weight and reported it exceeded across all European age groups with fatty fish among the top contributors (EFSA Journal 2018;16(11):5333, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2018.5333, document confirmed; the '5 to 15 fold' exceedance figure inside it was not independently verified). Refined fish oils must meet 1.75 pg WHO-TEQ/g fat for dioxins and 6.0 pg/g fat for dioxins plus dioxin-like PCBs under Regulation (EU) 2023/915 (limits not re-verified in this pass), so a purified concentrate can carry a lower contaminant load per gram of EPA+DHA than the fish it came from. One processing detail: draining oil-packed tuna pours away omega-3, and it takes both fatty acids with it. Canned white tuna in oil retains 66 mg EPA and 178 mg DHA per 100 g after draining, a total of 244 mg, against 233 mg EPA and 629 mg DHA (862 mg total) for the water-packed product, a 72% loss of total EPA+DHA (USDA FDC 175157 versus 175158, both confirmed).
- **Absorption:** Chemical form changes absorption measurably. Using natural triglyceride fish oil as the 100% reference, the bioavailability index was 124% for re-esterified triglyceride and 73% for ethyl ester (Dyerberg 2010, PMID 20638827, both values confirmed). The 91% figure for free fatty acid and the 3.1-3.6 g/day matched dose range are consistent with the paper as usually cited but were not independently confirmed. Most cheap concentrates are ethyl esters, which also depend more on a fat-containing meal for absorption, so a 1,000 mg ethyl-ester capsule taken on an empty stomach does not deliver what the label implies. Fish is delivered as natural triglyceride inside a fat and protein matrix and needs no such allowance. Preparation matters more than cooking method: canning in oil and then draining removes omega-3, whereas canning in water and draining does not.
- **Does the body make it:** Humans can make EPA and DHA, yet only by remodelling a fatty acid they cannot make at all, the plant-derived precursor ALA, and the final steps are the bottleneck. In stable-isotope tracer work the fraction of ingested ALA reaching EPA is 21% in young women, while DHA reaches 9% in young women and stays undetectable in young men for 21 days after dosing, and FADS1 and FADS2 genotype is the largest single modifier between individuals. Because the pathway cannot be driven harder, tissue DHA tracks intake of preformed DHA and barely responds to ALA, so preformed EPA and DHA is one of the few supplements the endogenous route genuinely cannot make redundant.
- **Pathway:** ALA 18:3n-3 → FADS2 (delta-6 desaturase) → 18:4n-3 → ELOVL5 → 20:4n-3 → FADS1 (delta-5 desaturase) → EPA 20:5n-3 → ELOVL5 and ELOVL2 → DPA 22:5n-3 → ELOVL2 → 24:5n-3 → FADS2 again (delta-6, the same enzyme reused) → 24:6n-3 → one round of peroxisomal beta-oxidation (ACOX1, D-bifunctional protein) → DHA 22:6n-3, then re-esterified in the endoplasmic reticulum. Mainly hepatic; linoleic acid competes for the same FADS2 step.
- **Amount made:** Established as fractional conversion of ALA rather than as mg/d, and the figures are not commensurable because the denominators differ. Burdge and Wootton 2002: net fractional interconversion estimated within the plasma tracer pool (phosphatidylcholine, TAG and non-esterified fatty acids) over 21 days after a single 700 mg dose of 13C-ALA, young women EPA 21%, DPA 6%, DHA 9%. Burdge, Jones and Wootton 2002: in young men EPA and DPA appear while DHA stays undetectable to 21 days. Absolute mg/d of endogenous EPA and DHA: NOT ESTABLISHED.
- **Still matters when:** Justified for anyone not eating oily fish, and specifically in pregnancy and lactation for fetal and infant DHA accretion, in vegans and vegetarians, in adult men whose measured ALA to DHA conversion is effectively nil, and at pharmacological doses for hypertriglyceridaemia. Partial synthesis does not cover requirement here, so the supplement is doing real work.

*Refs. Cochrane PMID 32114706; REDUCE-IT PMID 30415628; STRENGTH PMID 33190147; Gencer PMID 34612056; Cochrane pregnancy PMID 30480773; ORIP PMID 31509674; AHA PMID 31422671*

## 4. Consider it

Defensible in one specific situation. Outside it there is no case.

### Magnesium

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 365 mg/d elemental for blood pressure. Note magnesium oxide is 60% elemental, citrate 16%, bisglycinate 14%.
- **Who it is for:** People with documented low magnesium, chronic proton pump inhibitor or diuretic users, alcohol use disorder, and treated hypertensives.

**Pros**

- Blood pressure falls by a real amount in the right people: -5.97 mmHg systolic in the hypomagnesaemic and -7.68 mmHg in treated hypertensives.
- Proton-pump-inhibitor-induced hypomagnesaemia is an established, under-recognised clinical problem that this genuinely fixes.
- Cheap, and dietary intake is genuinely low in many Western diets.

**Cons**

- In replete normotensive people the pooled blood pressure effect did not reach significance. The general-population heart-health pitch is unsupported.
- The sleep claim, now the dominant marketing angle, rests on 3 trials and 151 older adults, all at moderate to high risk of bias, and the review authors called the literature substandard for making recommendations.
- The 600 mg/d migraine dose exceeds the US supplemental upper limit of 350 mg and is 2.4 times the EFSA limit of 250 mg. Diarrhoea is the dose-limiting effect.
- Contraindicated without supervision in chronic kidney disease stage 4-5 and dialysis, where hypermagnesaemia causes bradycardia, hypotension and respiratory depression. Also in myasthenia gravis.
- Chelates tetracyclines, fluoroquinolones and bisphosphonates. Separate by 2-4 hours.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A cofactor in hundreds of reactions; every molecule of ATP works bound to magnesium.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Fully reversible
- **In detail:** The biologically active form of ATP is Mg-ATP, so magnesium is an obligate participant in every kinase reaction, every ATPase and both DNA and RNA polymerases, more than 300 enzymes in total. It acts as a physiological calcium antagonist: it blocks the NMDA receptor channel at rest and dampens calcium entry in vascular smooth muscle and myocardium, which is why depletion raises neuromuscular excitability and arrhythmia risk. Magnesium is also required both for parathyroid hormone secretion and for end-organ response to it, so depletion produces a hypocalcaemia that will not correct until magnesium is replaced. About 60% of body magnesium is locked in bone and most of the rest is intracellular, leaving only about 1% in serum. Serum magnesium is therefore a poor guide to total body status and can be normal in a genuinely depleted person.
- **Deficiency disease:** Symptomatic hypomagnesaemia (ICD-10 E83.4). There is no eponymous deficiency disease; the severe presentation is hypomagnesaemic tetany with refractory hypocalcaemia and hypokalaemia. Inherited renal magnesium wasting (Gitelman syndrome, familial hypomagnesaemia with hypercalciuria) is a genetic tubular disorder rather than a dietary deficiency.
- **First signs:** Usually nothing at all. Low dietary intake shows up as intake below the estimated average requirement with a normal serum magnesium, because the kidney conserves aggressively. The first things a person notices are muscle cramps, eyelid or hand tremor, and irritability or poor sleep, all of which are non-specific. In practice a low serum magnesium is most often spotted incidentally on a metabolic panel run for unexplained hypokalaemia or hypocalcaemia.
- **Untreated:** Tetany with positive Chvostek and Trousseau signs, paraesthesiae, seizures, and cardiac arrhythmias including atrial fibrillation and torsades de pointes. Accompanying hypokalaemia and hypocalcaemia resist correction until magnesium itself is replaced, which is the clinically important teaching point. Severe cases are almost always driven by drug or gastrointestinal or renal losses rather than by diet alone.
- **Does it reverse:** Repletion corrects the neuromuscular and cardiac features and allows the linked potassium and calcium abnormalities to correct, with no residual deficit once the underlying cause is addressed. The one caveat is timing rather than permanence: serum magnesium normalises before body stores do, so bone and intracellular pools take days to weeks to refill and stopping too early lets the deficit return.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Chronic alcohol use, one of the leading causes. Gastrointestinal losses from chronic diarrhoea, malabsorption, short bowel and after bariatric surgery, together with renal losses, account for a large share of clinically encountered hypomagnesaemia. Long-term proton pump inhibitor use, which can cause profound hypomagnesaemia. Loop and thiazide diuretics, cisplatin, amphotericin, and calcineurin inhibitors; drug causes dominate in hospital populations. Poorly controlled diabetes with osmotic diuresis. Refeeding syndrome. In someone with normal kidneys and a normal gut, diet alone rarely produces symptomatic deficiency.
- **How common:** About 48% of Americans of all ages consume less magnesium from food and beverages than their estimated average requirement (NHANES 2013-2016). Clinically significant hypomagnesaemia is less common than that figure suggests: roughly 2.5% to 15% in the general population, rising to about 10% to 20% of hospitalised patients and higher in intensive care. The gap between the intake figure and the clinical one is why low intake is often overstated as widespread deficiency.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Magnesium sits in the chlorophyll molecule and in the outer layers of seeds and grains, so every unrefined plant food carries some; nuts, seeds, cocoa, wholegrains, legumes and dark leafy greens are all meaningful contributors, and a mixed diet built on them reaches the adult RDA (420 mg/day men 31+, 320 mg/day women 31+, Institute of Medicine 1997) with no supplement. Refining is what strips it, and the honest like-for-like comparison is CoFID wholemeal bread at 66 mg/100 g against white bread at 22 mg/100 g (11-981 and 11-1145), a three-fold gap. The wheat bran figure of 389 mg/100 g (CoFID 11-906) is a dry milling fraction, so setting it against a roughly 40%-water baked loaf inflates the effect of refining to eighteen-fold and should not be used that way.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Spinach, boiled, drained, without salt | 87 mg magnesium | 1 cup cooked (180 g) | 157 mg | 37% of the 420 mg RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168463. The oxalate write-down applied to this row in the original submission was wrong in direction: Bohn et al. (Br J Nutr 2004;91:601-606) measured fractional magnesium absorption of 26.7% from a spinach meal against 36.5% from kale and concluded the penalty is partly offset by spinach's higher native magnesium, which leaves roughly 42 mg absorbed per cooked cup against roughly 11 mg for a cooked cup of kale. Spinach is one of the better absorbed-magnesium portions on this list, and the 5.1% logic that disqualifies it for calcium does not transfer to magnesium |
| Pumpkin (pepita) seed kernels, roasted, without salt | 550 mg magnesium | 1 oz small handful (28.35 g) | 156 mg | 37% of the 420 mg RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170557 (SR Legacy); 1 oz = 28.35 g is USDA's own portion entry |

Pine nuts are usually eaten as a 1-2 tbsp sprinkle rather than by the ounce, so a real-world portion is often a third of this; 1 oz is used for comparability with the other nuts |

Milk chocolate is far lower at 57 mg/100 g (CoFID 17-648), and 45-59% cacao is 146 mg/100 g (FDC 170271), so cacao percentage drives the number |

The 10 g tablespoon is a conventional weight rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
