| Bread, wholemeal / whole-wheat, commercially prepared | 75 mg magnesium | 1 slice (32 g) | 24 mg | 6% of the 420 mg RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172688; CoFID 11-981 gives 66 mg/100 g |

- **To match the dose:** Supplements are sold at 200 to 400 mg elemental magnesium, which is the whole daily requirement, and ordinary food covers that range easily. A worked day: 1 oz pumpkin seeds (156 mg) plus 1 cup cooked black beans (120 mg) plus 1 oz 70-85% dark chocolate (65 mg) is 341 mg before anything else on the plate is counted. Single-food routes are absurd by comparison: 400 mg from dark chocolate alone means 175 g of bar, roughly 1,000 kcal, and 400 mg from wholemeal bread alone means 533 g, about 17 slices. Nobody needs a single-food route, because the mixed-diet route is three normal portions. The safety asymmetry matters more than the arithmetic: the Institute of Medicine set the 350 mg/day upper limit for supplemental magnesium only, and it explicitly excludes magnesium naturally present in food and water, so 700 mg of magnesium from seeds and beans carries no upper limit while 400 mg from tablets is already over the supplemental ceiling. A comparable 250 mg/day EU limit for magnesium added through supplements or fortified foods is normally attributed to the SCF 2001 opinion; the source text could not be retrieved during verification, so that figure is carried here as indicative and unconfirmed.
- **Food vs supplement:** The difference is dose kinetics rather than chemistry. Food delivers magnesium in small amounts across a day inside a matrix that slows release, so the unabsorbed fraction stays small and osmotic diarrhoea does not happen. A 400 mg bolus of a soluble magnesium salt leaves a large unabsorbed load in the gut lumen, and that load is the mechanism of the laxative effect that set the upper limit in the first place. Magnesium citrate and magnesium hydroxide are marketed as laxatives at doses close to supplement doses. In anyone with reduced renal clearance (CKD stage 4-5, elderly patients on multiple drugs) supplements can cause symptomatic hypermagnesaemia, while food magnesium is handled by renal excretion across the normal intake range. There is no situation where the food form is the risky one.
- **Absorption:** Net absorption of dietary magnesium is 40 to 60% at an intake averaging 380 mg/day, falling to 15-36% at higher daily intakes of 550 to 850 mg (Institute of Medicine DRI report 1997, Chapter 6, NCBI Bookshelf NBK109816). NIH ODS quotes 30-40% for public guidance, so the working figure should be the lower half of the DRI range rather than the 50% midpoint. Phytate is the main food inhibitor and can cut fractional absorption by up to 60% in a dose-dependent way, so the magnesium in bran, seeds and legumes is not fully realised. Oxalate matters much less than the textbook version implies: Bohn et al. (Br J Nutr 2004;91:601-606) measured 26.7% fractional absorption from a spinach meal against 36.5% from kale and concluded the spinach penalty is at least partly counterbalanced by its higher native magnesium content, so a cooked cup of spinach delivers roughly 42 mg absorbed against roughly 11 mg from a cooked cup of kale. Supplement forms differ sharply among themselves: Firoz and Graber (Magnesium Research 2001;14(4):257-262, PMID 11794633) measured fractional absorption of about 4% for magnesium oxide against 9-11% for the lactate, aspartate and chloride forms, and magnesium oxide is the cheapest and most widely sold form. A 400 mg magnesium oxide tablet can therefore deliver less absorbed magnesium than a handful of pumpkin seeds while producing the loose stools.
- **Does the body make it:** Magnesium is chemical element 12 and cannot be synthesised, so all of the roughly 25 g in an adult body came from food. Bone holds 50 to 60% of it and the kidney adjusts reabsorption tightly, which buffers short-term intake gaps and is also why serum magnesium is a poor guide to total body stores. With synthesis at zero, the only question is whether habitual intake, drug use or renal and gastrointestinal losses justify supplementing, and for a large share of the population intake alone does.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE (chemical element). Handling only: TRPM6 and TRPM7 channels for intestinal and distal tubular uptake, with TRPM6 activity regulated by EGF acting through EGFR, efflux by the CNNM proteins (CNNM2 in kidney and brain, CNNM4 in intestine) and SLC41, paracellular reabsorption through claudin-16 and claudin-19 in the thick ascending limb, and bone as the exchangeable reservoir.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/d synthesised. Reference points: adult body content about 25 g, 50 to 60% of it in bone; RDA 400-420 mg/d men and 310-320 mg/d women; UL 350 mg/d for supplemental magnesium only, with food magnesium uncapped.
- **Still matters when:** Justified by intake or loss: habitual intake below the EAR, proton pump inhibitors, loop and thiazide diuretics, chronic alcohol use, type 2 diabetes, Crohn disease or coeliac disease, refeeding, documented hypomagnesaemia. Not justified as a routine add-on on an already adequate diet, where the main effect of surplus oral magnesium salts is osmotic diarrhoea.

*Refs. Argeros Hypertension 2025 PMID 41000008; Mah & Pitre PMID 33865376; Peikert PMID 8792038*

### Zinc lozenges

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 75-100 mg/d elemental, split into 6-8 lozenges, started within 24 hours of symptom onset and stopped when symptoms resolve.
- **Who it is for:** Someone at the very start of a cold who will actually follow the lozenge schedule.

**Pros**

- Cold duration shortens. Hemila found -33% (21-45%) across lozenge trials, with no additional benefit above 100 mg/d.
- Cheap, and the treatment window is short enough that the copper risk does not apply.
- Zinc for acute childhood diarrhoea in deficient populations is a separate and much stronger indication, used as standard of care in low-income settings.

**Cons**

- The evidence is genuinely contested, and honest reporting means saying so. Cochrane 2024 rated duration LOW certainty at -2.37 days, found prevention null and severity null. Hemila published a methodological rebuttal alleging Cochrane pooled a 190 mg/d lozenge trial with a 0.046 mg/d nasal trial, a 4,300-fold dose difference.
- Chronic intake above 40 mg/d causes copper depletion, which produces sideroblastic anaemia, neutropenia and an irreversible myelopathy that mimics subacute combined degeneration. This is the reason the treatment must be short.
- Formulation matters and most retail lozenges are wrong. Citric acid and sorbitol bind zinc and stop it being released.
- Chelates fluoroquinolones and tetracyclines. Nasal zinc caused permanent anosmia and should never be used.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Zinc runs hundreds of enzymes; the lozenge form targets cold symptoms in the throat.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** Zinc is a structural or catalytic component of more than 300 enzymes and of the roughly 2,000 zinc-finger transcription factors that bind DNA, so it is required for gene expression, DNA and RNA synthesis, and cell division. Tissues with fast turnover fail first: gut mucosa, skin, growth plate cartilage, and lymphocytes. Thymic function and T-cell development are zinc-dependent, which is the basis of the immune effects. The lozenge specifically is a different proposition from nutrition: it works (where it works) by releasing ionic zinc onto the oropharyngeal mucosa, where it appears to interfere with rhinovirus replication and ICAM-1 binding locally. That is a short-course pharmacological use in someone whose zinc status is usually normal. Lozenges are a poor way to correct systemic deficiency. On the toxicity side, sustained intake above the 40 mg/day tolerable upper limit induces intestinal metallothionein and progressively impairs copper status; the upper limit itself rests on a marginal fall in erythrocyte superoxide dismutase at around 60 mg/day, a biochemical rather than a clinical endpoint. Frank copper-deficiency anaemia and myeloneuropathy are reported at prolonged intakes several times higher, in the region of 100 to 300 mg/day, typically from high-dose supplements or zinc-containing denture cream.
- **Deficiency disease:** Nutritional zinc deficiency (growth failure, chronic diarrhoea, impaired immunity, characteristic dermatitis). Acrodermatitis enteropathica is the inherited form, caused by SLC39A4 mutations impairing zinc uptake, and is a genetic disorder rather than a consequence of diet.
- **First signs:** Blunted taste and smell, poor appetite, slow wound healing, and infections that are more frequent and last longer. In children, growth velocity crosses centiles downward before any skin change appears. There is no good early biomarker: plasma zinc falls only late, drops during any inflammatory episode, and varies with time of day and recent meals, so a normal plasma zinc does not exclude deficiency.
- **Untreated:** Stunting and delayed sexual maturation in children, persistent diarrhoea that worsens the deficiency in a loop, alopecia, and a distinctive bullous, crusted dermatitis around the mouth, eyes, genitals and on the hands and feet. Cell-mediated immunity is impaired, with thymic atrophy and reduced T-cell counts. In young children in low-income settings, zinc deficiency contributes measurably to deaths from diarrhoeal disease and pneumonia.
- **Does it reverse:** Dermatitis, taste disturbance, diarrhoea and immune function recover within days to a few weeks of repletion, and the response is often dramatic. Linear growth is the part that does not fully catch up: height lost during a prolonged deficiency spanning a growth period is only partly recovered, and final adult stature can remain reduced.
- **Who becomes deficient:** People on cereal-based diets high in phytate with little animal protein, which is the dominant global pattern. Children in low-income settings. Malabsorption from Crohn's disease, coeliac disease or short bowel; bariatric surgery. Chronic alcohol use, which causes urinary zinc loss. Chronic diarrhoea, sickle cell disease, and older adults with restricted intake. High-dose supplemental iron taken at the same time competes for the same absorptive route.
- **How common:** An estimated 17.3% of the world's population is at risk of inadequate zinc intake, based on national food-supply zinc availability (Wessells and Brown, 2012). Clinically overt nutritional zinc deficiency in healthy adults in high-income countries is uncommon.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Two separate questions share a mineral name here, and they get different answers. Zinc the nutrient is abundant in ordinary food: oysters are the outlier of the entire composition table, red meat and offal follow, and wheat germ, seeds and legumes carry useful amounts, so on the question of whether the substance is in food the answer is RICH. Zinc lozenges are not a nutritional product: the trials used more than 75 mg/day of elemental zinc, most commonly 80 to 92 mg/day and in two trials 192 to 207 mg/day, delivered as slowly dissolving zinc acetate or gluconate lozenges taken roughly every two hours while awake (PMID 28515951), and the mechanism depends on free zinc ions released in the mouth and throat over 20 to 30 minutes in contact with the pharyngeal mucosa. Food zinc is swallowed and absorbed in the small intestine, so no amount of it reproduces the intended local effect, which is why dose_from_food is NO while food_status is RICH. Reference point used in the table: EFSA PRI 7.5 mg/d for adult women and 9.4 mg/d for adult men at a phytate intake of 300 mg/d (EFSA, Dietary Reference Values for nutrients Summary report, approved 4 December 2017, doi 10.2903/sp.efsa.2017.e15121, Tables 5 and 7; underlying opinion EFSA Journal 2014;12(10):3844).

- **To match the dose:** The lozenge dose fails on arithmetic and then fails again on route. Arithmetically, hitting the 80 mg/day of elemental zinc used in the trials (PMID 28515951) every day for the duration of a cold takes 204 g of raw oyster meat, roughly 14 to 15 medium oysters; or 102 g of cooked oysters; or 88 g of canned oysters, about 11 of them; or 479 g of toasted wheat-germ cereal; or 989 g of braised lean beef chuck; or 1.10 kg of braised lamb shoulder; or 1.05 kg of pumpkin seed kernels; or 5.23 kg of boiled chickpeas; or 6.30 kg of boiled lentils. Only oysters make it look even arithmetically plausible, and 14 raw oysters a day for a week is a Vibrio and norovirus decision rather than a nutrition one. Closing the arithmetic would still not reproduce the product, because the lozenge trials work through free Zn2+ released in the oropharynx and held against the mucosa, and swallowed zinc from food or from a capsule bypasses that route entirely. On scale, 80 to 92 mg/day is roughly 11 to 12 times the EFSA female PRI of 7.5 mg/d (10.7 and 12.3 times); the 'eight times' figure quoted earlier corresponds to the male PRI of 9.4 mg/d, not the female one named in the sentence. Separately, and this is the part food answers easily, the nutritional dose is trivially reachable: 85 g of braised lean beef chuck (6.9 mg) or 6 medium raw oysters (33.4 mg) meets or vastly exceeds the 7.5 to 9.4 mg PRI in one portion.
- **Food vs supplement:** Zinc gluconate lozenges shortened colds by 28 percent and zinc acetate lozenges by 40 percent, a 12-point difference that was not statistically significant (95% CI -12 to +36), and there is no evidence that doses above 100 mg/day do better than 80 to 92 mg/day (PMID 28515951). The salt matters less than people claim and the lozenge format matters more. Zinc capsules, tablets, syrups and food are not substitutes in this indication, because the local contact is the point. Two safety notes follow from the numbers: 80 to 92 mg/day is roughly 11 to 12 times the EFSA female PRI and well above nutritional upper levels, defensible only for a few days because sustained high zinc antagonises copper absorption; and this is a short pharmacological course with an actual end date, unlike a nutrient. One earlier claim about the oyster records has to be withdrawn. Cooking does concentrate oyster zinc by moisture loss (raw 89.0 g water, cooked 78.2 g; dry matter 11.0 g against 21.8 g, so solids exactly double and zinc exactly doubles from 39.3 to 78.6 mg per 100 g). Canning does not work that way: canned oysters hold 85.1 g water and 14.9 g dry matter, only 1.35 times the raw solids, yet 2.3 times the zinc at 91.0 mg, which is 1.7 times richer per gram of solids (611 mg zinc per 100 g dry matter against 357 raw and 361 cooked). The canned entry reflects different material or different analyses rather than moisture loss. Per-100 g comparisons across preparation states remain misleading either way unless the yield is tracked.
- **Absorption:** Oysters are the outlier of the entire food composition table. At 39.3 mg/100 g raw and up to 91.0 mg/100 g canned they carry four to twelve times the zinc of red meat, which is itself the next best category, and they are why oysters answer every zinc question. Beyond oysters, absorption efficiency does most of the work and phytate is the variable. Human whole-body-retention studies of matched cereal meals found fractional zinc absorption of 26.8 percent from a rye bread meal containing 100 umol of phytic acid, falling to 8.4 percent from oatmeal porridge containing 600 umol, a threefold loss driven by phytate alone (PMID 2824731). Fractional absorption is not the whole story, and the earlier version of this section had the direction of the bread comparison backwards. A related study found that a lower absolute amount of zinc was absorbed from white bread than from the same amount of wholemeal bread, because wholemeal supplies more zinc and the phytate penalty on fractional absorption did not overturn that content advantage; white bread only won once both breads were enriched with zinc chloride. Adding milk products did improve zinc absorption from the wholemeal meal (PMID 7361691). Replacing 25 percent of the beef protein in a composite meal with defatted soy flour lowered both the zinc content and the zinc absorbed, while the same substitution in a chicken meal changed neither (PMID 6893252). EFSA builds phytate straight into the requirement: the adult male PRI rises from 9.4 mg/d at 300 mg phytate/day to 11.7, 14.0 and 16.3 mg/d at 600, 900 and 1,200 mg, and the female PRI from 7.5 to 9.3, 11.0 and 12.7 mg/d across the same range, ratios of 1.73 and 1.69. A high-legume, high-wholegrain diet therefore needs about 70 percent more zinc on the label to deliver the same absorbed zinc. On mitigations, the evidence is narrower than previously claimed: the cited review covers added Aspergillus niger phytase (12 iron studies, 5 zinc studies) and states that for zinc not enough information is available to determine optimal activities (PMID 24050000), so added phytase is supported while soaking, sprouting and sourdough fermentation need a fermentation-specific source. Animal protein and milk in the same meal do raise absorption, and vegetarians consequently sit at the top of the EFSA PRI range rather than the bottom (PMID 12936958).
- **Does the body make it:** Zinc is chemical element 30 and cannot be synthesised, so the many enzymes and transcription factors that depend on it require a continuous dietary supply, and there is no rapidly mobilisable store analogous to ferritin. Lozenges are a special case: they release ionic zinc into the mouth and throat for a local effect during a cold, so they act as a short-course topical drug and do not correct a systemic shortfall. With synthesis at zero zinc is never redundant on this axis, while long-term use at lozenge doses antagonises copper absorption and is harmful in its own right.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE (chemical element). Transport and buffering only: ZIP family uptake transporters (SLC39, with ZIP4/SLC39A4 as the apical intestinal route lost in acrodermatitis enteropathica), ZnT efflux transporters (SLC30) and metallothionein binding, across intestine, liver and all tissues.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/d synthesised. Reference points: adult body content about 1.5-2.5 g, roughly 60% in skeletal muscle and 30% in bone; RDA 11 mg/d men, 8 mg/d women; UL 40 mg/d elemental zinc for adults, which most lozenge regimens exceed.
- **Still matters when:** NEVER ON THIS AXIS. Justification is use-case or intake-based: documented deficiency, high-phytate or vegetarian diets, chronic diarrhoea, inflammatory bowel disease or post-bariatric malabsorption, sickle cell disease, acrodermatitis enteropathica (ZIP4/SLC39A4 loss of function, which needs systemic zinc salts and cannot be treated with lozenges), pregnancy and lactation. The common cold use case is contested: the Cochrane review Zinc for the common cold was retracted (PMID 25924708) and later reviews have been publicly challenged (Hemilä and Chalker, PMID 39478818 and PMID 40680659), so the evidence should be described as unsettled.

*Refs. Cochrane Nault PMID 38719213; Hemila PMID 28515951; Hemila & Chalker PMID 39478818*

### Melatonin

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 0.5-5 mg. Timing is the active ingredient, not dose. For phase advance, 0.5 mg taken 2-4 hours before dim-light melatonin onset.
- **Who it is for:** Jet lag eastward across five or more time zones, delayed sleep-wake phase disorder, shift work, and blind people with free-running rhythms.

**Pros**

- Circadian phase-shifting is real pharmacology with a mapped phase-response curve. For jet lag across five or more zones the NNT is 2, and 9 of 10 trials were positive.
- In delayed sleep-wake phase disorder, sleep onset advanced by 0.67 hours and endogenous melatonin onset by 1.18 hours.
- Short-term safety is good and it is not habit-forming in the way sedatives are.

**Cons**

- For ordinary insomnia it barely does anything: 7 minutes off sleep latency and 8 minutes more total sleep across 19 trials and 1,683 people. The AASM guideline explicitly suggests clinicians NOT use it for insomnia.
- Retail dose accuracy is the worst documented in this document. 22 of 25 gummy products were inaccurately labelled, at 74-347% of the declared amount, and one product labelled 3 mg contained no melatonin at all but 31.3 mg of CBD. A separate analysis of 31 products found -83% to +478% of label and serotonin contamination in 8 of them.
- Not for children. The gummy format plus up to 347% overdosing plus palatability is a bad combination.
- Retail doses of 5-10 mg are far above what the pharmacology needs and can shift the clock the wrong way if taken at the wrong time.
- Cochrane specifically flagged caution in people on warfarin and people with epilepsy. Fluvoxamine markedly raises melatonin exposure via CYP1A2 inhibition.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A hormone the pineal gland releases after dark to tell the body clock that night has begun.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Melatonin is synthesised from serotonin in the pineal gland on a schedule set by the suprachiasmatic nucleus, rising roughly two hours before habitual sleep onset and clearing by morning. It acts through MT1 and MT2 receptors on the clock itself, so it works as a timing signal rather than a sedative, which is why small evening doses shift sleep phase more reliably than large doses deepen sleep. There is no dietary requirement at all: the body makes every molecule it uses. Nocturnal peak concentrations do fall with age (pooled maxima around 49 pg/mL in study populations averaging 65 to 70 years and around 28 pg/mL in those 75 and over), and ordinary evening room light below 200 lux delays melatonin onset in 99% of people and shortens the nocturnal melatonin window by about 90 minutes, so the common problem is a mistimed or blunted signal rather than an absent nutrient.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Trace
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Body makes it

Melatonin is genuinely present across a wide range of plant and animal foods, and the richest of them are not the fruits and nuts usually named: roasted coffee, red wine and germinated legumes carry far more than tart cherries or walnuts. Even so, every realistic portion sits one to four orders of magnitude below a 0.5-5 mg supplement dose. The supplement is a separate article from the dietary substance: commercial melatonin is chemically synthesised rather than extracted from food, so a product sold as natural melatonin is a manufactured molecule and would be labelled SYNTHETIC if this field described the pill instead of the substance.

J Agric Food Chem 2001;49(10):4898-4902, PMID 11600041. Value and the 6.5-fold cultivar difference against Montmorency (13.46/2.06) both verified in the same paper. |

| Walnut kernels (Juglans regia), raw | 0.35 ug melatonin (3.5 +/- 1.0 ng/g) | 30 g, about 15 halves | 0.105 ug (105 ng) | 0.011% of a 1 mg dose | Reiter RJ, Manchester LC, Tan DX. Nutrition 2005;21(9):920-924, PMID 15979282, DOI 10.1016/j.nut.2005.02.005 (exact match, PMID added). Method caveat the submission omitted: this used HPLC plus radioimmunoassay, which tends to read high; the independent bracket of 1.19-3.30 ng/g across cultivars (Verde et al. J Food Compos Anal 2022;105:104180) is consistent and slightly lower. Portion count corrected: 14 halves is the 1 oz (28 g) count, so 30 g is about 15 halves. |
| Pistachio kernels, raw (LC-MS/MS value) | 0.26 ug melatonin (2609 +/- 3096 pg/g) | 30 g, about 52 kernels | 0.078 ug (78 ng) | 0.008% of a 1 mg dose | Paroni et al., Bioactive phytochemicals of tree nuts: melatonin and sphingolipid content in almonds and pistachios. J Food Compos Anal 2019, DOI 10.1016/j.jfca.2019.05.010 (value and DOI confirmed exactly). Caveat the submission omitted: the SD (3096 pg/g) exceeds the mean, so 2.609 ng/g is not a stable point estimate. Portion count corrected: 49 kernels is the 1 oz (28.35 g) count, so 30 g is about 52 kernels. |
| Hen egg, whole, boiled | 0.15 ug melatonin (about 1.5 ng/g fresh weight) | 1 large egg (50 g) | 0.077 ug (77 ng) | 0.008% of a 1 mg dose | Meng X et al., Nutrients 2017;9(4):367, PMC5409706: the 6.1 +/- 0.95 ng/g figure the submission used is for EGG DRIED SOLIDS. Whole egg is about 75% water, giving a fresh-weight equivalent near 1.5 ng/g. Applying the dry-solids value to a fresh 50 g egg overstated melatonin roughly four-fold; older HPLC work reporting 1.54 ng/g for fresh egg, which the submission mentioned then discarded, is consistent with the corrected value. The submission's original citation was CUME+, J Hum Nutr Diet 2026, PMID 41588582. |
| Almond kernels, raw (LC-MS/MS value, same paper and method as pistachio) | 0.12 ug melatonin (1222 +/- 500 pg/g) | 30 g, about 24 kernels | 0.037 ug (37 ng) | 0.0037% of a 1 mg dose | Paroni et al., J Food Compos Anal 2019, DOI 10.1016/j.jfca.2019.05.010 (verified). Added because the submission cited this paper for pistachios but dropped its direct same-method comparator, removing the reader's only method-matched reference point. |
| Cow's milk, whole (daytime-milked) | 0.0015 ug melatonin (0.015 ng/g) | 1 glass, 200 mL (206 g) | 0.0031 ug (3.1 ng) | 0.0003% of a 1 mg dose | CUME+ study, J Hum Nutr Diet 2026, PMID 41588582, DOI 10.1111/jhn.70193; 0.015 ng/g = 15 pg/g sits inside the reviewed 14.45-18.41 pg/mL range (PMC5409706) and corresponds to daytime-milked milk. Night-milked milk runs several-fold higher and is sold commercially on that basis, so one value understates the range. |

- **To match the dose:** 1 mg requires roughly 104 g of roasted coffee grounds, the closest any food comes, and grounds are not consumed whole (brewing transfers an uncharacterised fraction to the cup); or 7.7 L of red wine; or about 918 g of dry-matter germinated lentils; or 1.9 kg dry-matter germinated kidney beans; or 5.3 kg of white mustard seed; or 9.7 kg of dried goji at the review value of 103 ng/g dry weight; or 23 kg of fenugreek seed, 34 kg of sunflower seed, 42 kg of tomato, 74 kg of Montmorency cherries, 89 kg of strawberries, 286 kg of walnuts, 485 kg of Balaton cherries, 818 kg of almonds, about 655-670 kg of egg (13,100-13,400 eggs) and about 64,500 L of milk. Pistachios cannot be given a single number: the point estimate is 383 kg but the reported SD (3096 pg/g) exceeds the mean (2609 pg/g), so the true requirement is uncertain by roughly an order of magnitude in either direction. Halve all of these for a 0.5 mg dose, multiply by five for 5 mg. The submission's headline claim that goji berries are the one food a human could theoretically eat to a dose does not survive correction, because it rested on an unreplicated outlier; at the authoritative value no food in the table reaches a supplement dose in an edible quantity.
- **Food vs supplement:** Food melatonin is not a smaller version of a tablet. Tart cherry juice concentrate measurably raises urinary 6-sulfatoxymelatonin and improved actigraphy-measured sleep efficiency at 2 x 30 mL/day (Howatson et al. Eur J Nutr 2012;51(8):909-916, PMID 22038497, verified), yet no melatonin content figure for the concentrate could be sourced here, so the one preparation with a positive human sleep outcome remains uncosted; the melatonin delivered is a fraction of a microgram, and most of the effect probably comes from the polyphenol matrix and tryptophan availability. Cultivar swamps everything: Montmorency and Balaton are the same species and differ 6.5-fold. Two melatonin-rich botanicals bear on this list for context: Scutellaria (huang-qin) at about 7,110 ng/g dry weight and St John's wort at about 4,490 ng/g dry weight, the latter classified NOT_FOOD in its own entry while being among the richest melatonin-bearing plants on record. On the pistachio dispute: one 2014 spectrofluorimetric paper reported roughly 233 ug/g dry weight, about 89,300 times the LC-MS/MS value, and it carries a published Expression of Concern (original PMID 24878439; EoC Spectrochim Acta A 2019;217:322, PMID 31027610). At that disputed figure 4.3 g of pistachios, about seven kernels, would supply 1 mg, which is exactly why the number should be treated as unconfirmed rather than as a food-based melatonin strategy. The Expression of Concern record lists only the four original authors and states no replication failure, so the submission's attribution of it to a German federal risk-assessment body has been removed. On the supplement side, doses commonly sold at 3-10 mg are ten to thirty times a physiological replacement dose, independent testing of commercial products has repeatedly found large deviations from label content plus occasional serotonin contamination, and melatonin is a prescription-only medicine in the UK and much of the EU while sold freely as a supplement in the US.
- **Absorption:** Absolute oral bioavailability of melatonin is low and highly variable, averaging about 15% (reported range roughly 9-33%, with individual values spanning a five-fold spread), because up to 85% is removed on first pass, mainly by hepatic CYP1A2. CYP1A2 modifiers therefore matter more than the source: caffeine raises plasma melatonin from a given dose, smoking lowers it. Nothing is known about whether the nanogram to low-microgram quantities in a food matrix follow the same kinetics, and at those quantities the question is close to academic. Anyone comparing natural melatonin in pistachios with a tablet is comparing a sub-microgram intake against a supra-physiological one.
- **Does the body make it:** The pineal gland makes melatonin from tryptophan through TPH1, aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase, AANAT and ASMT, under suprachiasmatic nucleus control and suppressed by light, and nocturnal output is small. In a dose-ranging trial in adults over 50, 0.3 mg restored sleep efficiency and raised plasma melatonin to normal nocturnal levels, while 3.0 mg raised it above the physiological range, so common 3 to 10 mg capsules sit one to two orders of magnitude above the nightly physiological amount. There is no melatonin deficiency disease, so dose and timing are the whole discussion and repletion never enters into it.
- **Pathway:** Tryptophan to 5-hydroxytryptophan by TPH1, to serotonin by aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase (DDC), to N-acetylserotonin by AANAT (the rhythm-generating and light-suppressed step, driven by noradrenergic input from the suprachiasmatic nucleus via the superior cervical ganglion), then to melatonin by ASMT (HIOMT). Pinealocytes are the main source, with extrapineal synthesis in retina, gut and other tissues contributing little to circulating levels. Clearance is mainly hepatic CYP1A2 6-hydroxylation; receptors are MT1 and MT2.
- **Amount made:** The dedicated row records total endogenous nightly output as NOT ESTABLISHED as a verified figure in this pass, with the dose-response as the usable sourced anchor: 0.1, 0.3 and 3.0 mg given orally 30 minutes before bedtime in adults over 50, where 0.3 mg restored sleep efficiency and raised plasma melatonin to normal levels and 3.0 mg produced supraphysiological levels (PMID 11600532). The merged rows quote roughly 10 to 80 micrograms secreted per night in adults, falling with age, against supplement doses of 0.5 to 10 mg, and about 38.5 microg over a night for a 70 kg adult.
- **Still matters when:** Circadian timing problems where melatonin acts as a chronobiotic: jet lag, delayed sleep-wake phase disorder, shift work, free-running or non-24-hour rhythms in some blind people, neurodevelopmental conditions in children under supervision, and older adults, where prolonged-release 2 mg is licensed in the EU and UK. All of these depend on low doses given at the right time, and none of it is repletion, since no deficiency state exists. Timing errors and self-selected multi-milligram doses are the two practical failure modes.

*Refs. Burgess PMID 20410229; van Geijlswijk PMID 21120122; Ferracioli-Oda PMID 23691095; Cohen JAMA 2023 PMID 37097362; Erland & Saxena PMID 27855744; Sateia AASM PMID 27998379*

### Probiotics, named strain only

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** Antibiotic-associated diarrhoea in children: at least 5 billion CFU/d. C. difficile prevention: 10-50 billion CFU/d. Infant colic: L. reuteri DSM 17938 at 100 million CFU/d.
- **Who it is for:** Children on a course of antibiotics, adults on antibiotics with elevated C. difficile risk, and exclusively breastfed infants with colic.

**Pros**

- Paediatric antibiotic-associated diarrhoea: 8% versus 19%, RR 0.45 (0.36-0.56), NNT 9 at the higher dose. This is a genuinely useful number.
- C. difficile prevention during antibiotics: RR 0.50 (0.38-0.64), 1.6% versus 3.2%.
- Infant colic in exclusively breastfed babies: NNT 2.6 for treatment success at day 21, which is a striking number for a benign intervention with a defined stop point.
- Pouchitis maintenance after antibiotic-induced remission has a real randomised result: remission maintained in 17 of 20 versus 1 of 16.

**Cons**

- The category claim is unfalsifiable marketing. Evidence attaches to a named strain at a defined dose. Disaggregate 82 trials and 10,332 IBS patients by strain and GRADE certainty collapses to low or very low almost everywhere.
- C. difficile prevention has NNT 65 at LOW certainty, so 64 of 65 people get nothing. The absolute benefit scales with baseline risk.
- The largest trial ever run was null. PLACIDE randomised 2,981 hospitalised patients over 65 and found no effect on either antibiotic-associated diarrhoea (RR 1.04) or C. difficile.
- The AGA recommends AGAINST probiotics for irritable bowel syndrome outside clinical trials.
- There is a real harm signal in the critically ill. In predicted severe acute pancreatitis, mortality was 16% versus 6% (RR 2.53) with 9 cases of bowel ischaemia, 8 fatal, versus zero on placebo. The FDA also warned providers after a preterm infant died of probiotic-associated sepsis.
- Absolute contraindications: central venous catheter, neutropenia, severe immunocompromise, critical illness, and preterm infants outside a supervised protocol.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Live bacteria given in defined doses, where each named strain acts as its own drug.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** The accepted definition is live micro-organisms which, when administered in adequate amounts, confer a health benefit on the host, and the operative unit of evidence is the strain, written as genus, species and an alphanumeric designator (for example Lacticaseibacillus rhamnosus GG or Saccharomyces boulardii CNCM I-745). Strain-level genetics determine adhesion proteins, bacteriocin production, bile salt hydrolase activity and short-chain fatty acid output, so a trial result for one strain carries no information about a different strain of the same species. Most administered strains transit the gut without colonising, and the effect ceases when dosing stops. This is a therapeutic product class, not a nutrient class, so there is nothing a person can be short of.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** None
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** n/a

SYNTHETIC here means no dietary source, not an artificial molecule: a named probiotic strain is a live organism, a single clonal isolate held in a culture bank, grown in industrial fermenters, freeze-dried and encapsulated. No food contains it. Fermented foods do carry large numbers of live lactic acid bacteria, but the species and strains present are whichever organisms grow in that food and its starter, never the deposited trial strain. Because probiotic efficacy is demonstrated strain by strain, a food supplying a different strain of the same species is a different product. Live-organism loads for fermented foods are given under the generic multi-strain entry.

- **To match the dose:** The gap is categorical and no quantity of food closes it. Named-strain doses in trials run from about 1x10^8 to 1x10^10 CFU/day; Bifidobacterium longum 35624 (formerly classified as infantis) was effective at 1x10^8, a figure the source trial expressed as CFU/mL rather than CFU/day (PMID 16863564). A single cup of kefir at 2.5x10^8 CFU/mL supplies roughly 6x10^10 live lactic acid bacteria (DOI 10.3390/foods15101616), so the raw CFU total is easily exceeded by food. The fraction of those cells belonging to the trialled strain is zero, which makes the CFU comparison meaningless for a strain-specific claim.
- **Food vs supplement:** This is the most important form caveat in the group. Clinical evidence for probiotics is strain-specific, and often formulation-specific and indication-specific on top of that. Yoghurt, kefir, sauerkraut, kimchi, miso, natto and tempeh do not contain the strains tested in the trials, so evidence generated with a named strain does not transfer to any fermented food, and does not transfer to a different strain of the same species either. Eating fermented food is a reasonable dietary habit with its own emerging evidence base, and it is a separate thing from taking a named strain at a defined dose for a specific clinical effect. Two practical points: heat kills the organisms, so a pasteurised or cooked ferment (canned sauerkraut, miso stirred into boiling soup, baked tempeh) delivers no live cells; and species-level labelling on a supplement such as 'contains Lactobacillus acidophilus' without a strain designation carries no more transferable evidence than a food does.
- **Absorption:** Survival of gastric acid and bile is strain-dependent, and the delivery matrix changes it: the fat and buffering capacity of a dairy food improves survival relative to a bare capsule, while enteric coating or microencapsulation is used to achieve the same in supplements. Dose is not monotonic, which is the clearest evidence that formulation matters as much as CFU count. In a 362-patient dose-ranging trial in primary-care IBS, B. infantis 35624 at 1x10^8 was significantly superior to placebo and to all other doses, while 1x10^6 and 1x10^10 were not; the paper attributes the failure of the highest dose to significant formulation problems (Whorwell et al., Am J Gastroenterol 2006;101:1581-1590, PMID 16863564). Two generalisability caveats the submission omitted: the trial enrolled women only, and doses were expressed as CFU/mL in the paper rather than CFU/day. A higher label CFU is therefore not better, and label CFU at manufacture is not the same as viable CFU at end of shelf life.
- **Does the body make it:** The synthesis axis does not fit here at all, because these are living organisms and not molecules, which is why the label is NA: forcing NONE would make a live organism look like a plant alkaloid on a screening table. Human cells cannot manufacture a bacterium, and the resident microbiome is acquired at birth and from the environment and then renews itself, so nobody is deficient in a strain the way someone is deficient in a vitamin. The honest test for a probiotic is strain-level trial evidence for a named indication, since effects do not transfer between strains, let alone between species.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Microorganisms are acquired, not synthesised. The resident microbiota is self-renewing and is defined by colonisation history rather than by any host biosynthetic capacity.
- **Amount made:** 0 (human cells cannot synthesise a microorganism). No daily requirement exists; the resident microbiota replaces itself.
- **Still matters when:** Where a specific, fully identified strain has randomised evidence for a specific indication, and in settings of documented disruption such as after antibiotics. Ingested strains are generally transient passengers and are cleared within days to weeks of stopping, so any benefit lasts only as long as dosing.

*Refs. Cochrane Guo PMID 31039287; Cochrane CD006095.pub5 PMID 40931979; Sung PMID 29279326; PLACIDE PMID 23932219; Goodoory & Ford PMID 37541528; AGA PMID 32531291; PROPATRIA PMID 18279948*

### Berberine

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 500 mg two or three times daily with meals. Note dihydroberberine and phytosome forms are more bioavailable and are not milligram-equivalent.
- **Who it is for:** Adults with type 2 diabetes or prediabetes not reaching glycaemic targets, as an add-on, with a prescriber informed.

**Pros**

- The effect is real and clinically sized, which is unusual in this document: fasting glucose -0.82 mmol/L, HbA1c -0.63%, across 37 trials and 3,048 patients. That HbA1c reduction is in the range of a real drug.
- Total adverse events were lower than comparators in the pooled analysis (RR 0.73), and it does not cause hypoglycaemia on its own.

**Cons**

- The marketing understates the pharmacology rather than overstating it, and that is the danger. This behaves like a drug and should be treated as one.
- The most dangerous interaction in this document: 600 mg/d for three months raised ciclosporin trough concentrations by 88.9% in 52 renal transplant recipients. Note that this interaction dose is BELOW the typical glycaemic dose.
- Berberine alone showed no statistical advantage over lifestyle change. The benefit appears as an add-on to an existing oral hypoglycaemic.
- The trial base is dominated by small Chinese trials that meta-analysts repeatedly describe as having low methodological quality.
- No upper limit exists. Toxicology review reports dose-dependent gastrointestinal ulceration, immunotoxicity, phototoxicity, neurotoxicity and cardiotoxicity. Contraindicated in pregnancy and in neonates (kernicterus risk).

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A plant alkaloid that lowers blood sugar and cholesterol; the body has no need for it.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Berberine is an isoquinoline alkaloid extracted from Berberis, Coptis and Hydrastis species. Its metabolic effects are usually attributed to activation of AMP-activated protein kinase, and the upstream trigger appears to be mild inhibition of mitochondrial complex I that raises the cellular AMP to ATP ratio (Turner et al., Diabetes 2008;57:1414-1418), giving it a mechanistic family resemblance to metformin. AMPK is not the whole account: Xu et al. confirmed the complex I effect but concluded that berberine promotes glucose consumption independently of AMPK activation. Downstream it suppresses hepatic gluconeogenesis, and it upregulates hepatic LDL receptor expression through ERK-dependent stabilisation of LDL receptor mRNA rather than through AMPK (Kong et al., Nat Med 2004;10:1344-1351). Oral absorption is very poor, with absolute bioavailability under 1% in rats, so a substantial share of any effect is probably exerted in the gut lumen and on the microbiota. It is a xenobiotic: no human tissue synthesises it and no physiological process depends on its presence.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Not food
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Berberine is an isoquinoline alkaloid from the roots, rhizomes and stem bark of medicinal plants: Berberis vulgaris (common barberry), B. aristata, B. asiatica and B. lycium, Coptis chinensis (huang lian) rhizome, Hydrastis canadensis (goldenseal), Mahonia aquifolium (Oregon grape) and Phellodendron amurense. None of these organs is eaten as food. Accumulation is highest in root tissue, 1.6-4.3% of dry weight across Berberis species, and B. vulgaris bark carries over 8% total alkaloids of which about 5% is berberine (Neag et al., Front Pharmacol 2018, PMC6111450, verified). Barberry fruit is the one part genuinely eaten, as zereshk in Iranian rice dishes, and it is the wrong organ: the whole-plant survey reporting up to 26,482 ug/g berberine in root and no detectable alkaloids in fruit pulp across 96 accessions was done on Berberis darwinii, the Chilean michay, which is a congener of the zereshk species rather than the same plant, and in that study leaves and seeds did carry detectable (lower) alkaloid levels, so only the pulp was non-detect (Molecules 2025;30(8):1849, PMID 40333914). The species question is closed by a separate group working directly on B. vulgaris fruit, which reports that the flowers, fruit pulp and seeds have little or no significant alkaloid content and describes their fruit extract as nearly devoid of berberine (Iran J Pharm Res 2011;10(1):97-104, PMID 24363687).

- **To match the dose:** Trials use 500 mg two or three times daily, 1000-1500 mg/day. At the 5% berberine content of B. vulgaris root bark that is 20-30 g of dried root bark per day, which is a herbal decoction rather than a meal. Via the edible fruit the gap is not a large number, it is undefined: berberine is below the limit of detection in barberry pulp, so no portion size reaches the dose at all. A generous serving of zereshk polow uses perhaps 30-50 g of dried berries spread across several people and contributes no measurable berberine. Coptis rhizome and goldenseal root would need 1-3 g of dried herb and neither is eaten as food.
- **Food vs supplement:** This is the clearest case in the set where a food and its supplement share only a plant name. Eating zereshk supplies vitamin C, anthocyanins and organic acids, with no pharmacological berberine effect. The isolated alkaloid at 1000-1500 mg/day behaves like a drug: it inhibits CYP3A4 and CYP2D6 and is a P-glycoprotein substrate and inhibitor, so it can raise levels of co-administered medicines; it commonly causes constipation, cramping and diarrhoea; and it is contraindicated in pregnancy and in neonates because berberine displaces bilirubin from albumin and has been linked to kernicterus risk. Whole-plant root extracts also carry berbamine, palmatine and, in goldenseal, hydrastine, so a barberry root extract and a purified berberine HCl capsule are not the same product. Goldenseal is additionally a conservation concern and a frequent target of species adulteration.
- **Absorption:** Berberine's oral bioavailability is under 1%, measured at 0.36-0.68% in rats, with roughly 99.5% eliminated during gastrointestinal and hepatic first pass through poor solubility, self-aggregation in gastric acid, P-glycoprotein efflux and CYP-mediated intestinal metabolism (Expert Opin Drug Metab Toxicol 2023;19(3), PMID 37057922). Much of berberine's metabolic effect is thought to be exerted in the gut lumen and on the microbiome rather than systemically, so low bioavailability does not mean inactive. It does mean trace amounts from any food would be pharmacologically irrelevant, and it means formulation comparisons (plain salt, phytosome, micelle) can change exposure by an order of magnitude at the same labelled dose.
- **Does the body make it:** Berberine is a protoberberine isoquinoline alkaloid made by plants such as Coptis chinensis, Berberis species and Hydrastis canadensis. Human cells do form the upstream benzylisoquinolines, since norlaudanosoline, 1(S)-reticuline and morphine were identified in human SH-SY5Y neuroblastoma and DAN-G pancreatic carcinoma cells and shown by labelled-precursor feeding to be of biosynthetic origin (PMID 15383669), but humans lack berberine bridge enzyme and the protoberberine steps beyond reticuline, so every milligram of berberine in the blood came from a plant or a factory. The endogenous axis can neither argue for nor against taking it, and the judgement falls to trial data, poor oral bioavailability and interaction burden.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for berberine itself. The plant benzylisoquinoline route runs from L-tyrosine through (S)-norcoclaurine and (S)-reticuline, then berberine bridge enzyme to (S)-scoulerine and on to berberine. Humans reach reticuline as part of the endogenous morphine pathway (PMID 15383669) but have no berberine bridge enzyme and none of the protoberberine steps downstream of it.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway for berberine)
- **Still matters when:** Every dose is exogenous by definition, so this axis is silent. Screen instead on clinical effect size, the low and variable oral bioavailability, and drug interactions through P-glycoprotein and CYP inhibition.

*Refs. Xie PMID 36467075; Lan PMID 25498346; Dong PMID 23118793; ciclosporin interaction PMID 16133554*

### Vitamin C

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 200 mg/d if taken regularly. Higher doses do not shorten colds further and cause osmotic diarrhoea.
- **Who it is for:** Smokers, heavy alcohol users, people on very restricted diets, and those under extreme physical stress such as marathon runners.

**Pros**

- It cures scurvy, which is an unambiguous deficiency disease, and scurvy still occurs in food-insecure and alcohol-dependent populations.
- Under extreme physical stress such as marathons and sub-arctic exercise, prophylactic vitamin C halved cold incidence. That subgroup effect is real.
- Smokers genuinely need 35 mg/d more than non-smokers because of higher turnover.

**Cons**

- It does not prevent colds in the general population: RR 0.97 (0.94-1.00) across 29 comparisons and 11,306 people. This is the founding claim of the modern supplement industry and it is false.
- Duration falls only 8% in adults and 14% in children, which is roughly half a day.
- Intravenous vitamin C in sepsis is HARMFUL. The LOVIT trial (n=863) found more death or persistent organ dysfunction in the treatment arm.
- Avoid with a history of calcium oxalate kidney stones or hyperoxaluria, and in renal impairment, because ascorbate is metabolised to oxalate.
- Upper limit 2,000 mg/d, set on osmotic diarrhoea.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Keeps collagen strong and wounds healing; without it blood vessels and gums break down.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Fully reversible
- **In detail:** Ascorbate is the reducing cofactor that keeps the iron atom in a family of dioxygenases in its active state. The best known are prolyl-4-hydroxylase and lysyl hydroxylase, which hydroxylate collagen so the triple helix is stable and can be cross-linked; without them the collagen laid down is defective, which explains bleeding, poor wound healing and the reopening of old scars. The same chemistry serves dopamine beta-hydroxylase (noradrenaline synthesis), peptidylglycine alpha-amidating monooxygenase (peptide hormone amidation), two carnitine biosynthesis enzymes, and the HIF prolyl hydroxylases and TET and JmjC demethylases in gene regulation. Ascorbate also reduces dietary non-haem iron to the absorbable ferrous form. Humans cannot synthesise it because the gulonolactone oxidase gene is inactive.
- **Deficiency disease:** Scurvy
- **First signs:** After roughly one to three months of intake below about 10 mg/day, plasma ascorbate falls and body stores drop under about 300 mg. The first phase is fatigue, irritability and diffuse aching in the legs, with nothing visible. Then come the specific skin signs: perifollicular hyperkeratosis with corkscrew-shaped body hairs on the thighs and shins, perifollicular haemorrhage, easy bruising, and swollen, spongy, bleeding gums in anyone who still has teeth.
- **Untreated:** Bleeding into skin, muscle and joints, haemarthrosis, and subperiosteal haemorrhage, which in children causes severe limb pain and refusal to move (pseudoparalysis). Old wounds and scars break down, teeth loosen and are lost, anaemia and oedema develop. Untreated scurvy is fatal, usually from haemorrhage or cardiac failure, though it very rarely reaches that stage in current practice because treatment is trivially available.
- **Does it reverse:** Complete and unusually fast. Fatigue and pain begin improving within 24 to 48 hours of treatment, bleeding gums and bruising settle over one to two weeks, and full recovery takes around three months on 100 to 300 mg/day. No permanent deficit remains. The only exception is teeth already lost, which do not grow back.
- **Who becomes deficient:** People whose diets contain almost no fruit or vegetables: highly restrictive or selective eaters including some autistic children, people living alone with severe mental illness or dementia, alcohol and drug dependence, homelessness and food insecurity. Also smokers (requirement about 35 mg/day higher), people on dialysis, malabsorption, and some patients after bariatric surgery. Scurvy is a disease of dietary pattern, not of insufficient supplementation; roughly 10 mg/day prevents it and a single orange comfortably exceeds that.
- **How common:** 7.1% (SE 0.9) of the US population aged 6 and over had serum ascorbate below 11.4 micromol/L in NHANES 2003 to 2004, down from about 13% in NHANES III, with the highest rates in smokers and low-income groups (PMID 19675106).

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Genuinely rich and cheap in ordinary food, and the verification pass challenged this classification and upheld it as the strongest in the set: one 119 g red pepper (152 mg) or one 55 g guava (125 mg) exceeds the 90 mg adult male RDA on its own, with no trace-amount inflation anywhere in the list. Synthetic ascorbic acid is manufactured (Reichstein or two-step fermentation from glucose), but it is the identical molecule, so the synthetic label carries no penalty here. Percentages below are given against both DRI values, since the RDA is 90 mg for men and 75 mg for women and quoting only the male figure understates every food's contribution for women by 20%. Rose hips, camu-camu, chilli peppers, papaya and Brussels sprouts are further rich sources for which no verified figure was available, so they get no row.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

- **To match the dose:** This is the one item in the set where the supplement dose is physically reachable by eating. A 1,000 mg tablet equals 60 g of raw acerola, about 12 small fruits, or 439 g of guava (8 fruits), or 552 g of blackcurrants, or 781 g of raw red pepper (about 6.6 medium peppers), or 1.07 kg of raw kale, or 1.70 kg of strawberries, or 1.88 kg of orange (14 medium oranges), or 2.0 kg of fresh orange juice, which is about 1.9 litres (the submission's '2.0 litres' confused grams with litres). A 500 mg dose is 3.3 red peppers or 7 oranges. Eccentric for most of that list, and trivially easy through acerola, so a person genuinely could eat this dose. The real gap is that the dose above roughly 200 mg is pointless rather than unreachable. Levine et al. gave 30-2,500 mg/day under depletion-repletion control: bioavailability was complete at a single 200 mg dose and fell off at 500 mg and above with the absorbed excess appearing in urine, while leucocytes saturated at 100 mg/day and plasma at 1,000 mg/day (PNAS 1996;93:3704-9, PMID 8623000, DOI 10.1073/pnas.93.8.3704). Four or five servings of ordinary fruit and vegetables reach the physiologically useful plateau, and the 1,000 mg tablet mostly funds expensive urine. The number that should temper any composition table is what survives handling: see cooking and storage below.
- **Food vs supplement:** Effectively NONE on the molecule. Synthetic and food-derived vitamin C are chemically identical and all steady-state human comparative bioavailability studies show no difference, with some acute pharmacokinetic studies showing minor transient differences of no physiological consequence (Carr and Vissers, Nutrients 2013;5:4284-304, PMID 24169506, DOI 10.3390/nu5114284). That kills the claim that natural vitamin C is better absorbed, and it means acerola, camu-camu and rose hip products carry no absorption advantage; most such products are in any case standardised by adding synthetic ascorbic acid, so the buyer gets the synthetic molecule with fruit powder attached. Note that acerola still earns a food row on content alone, at 1,680 mg/100 g the richest verified source in the database, which is a separate matter from the debunked absorption claim. What the whole food genuinely adds is the rest of the matrix, potassium, folate, carotenoids, flavonoids and fibre, none of which is in the tablet and none of which the vitamin C evidence is about. Where a caveat does bite is the textbook recommendation to eat spinach: its vitamin C is modest at 28.1 mg/100 g, a realistic 30 g cup gives 8.4 mg, and boiling extracts most of what is there, moving 60% of the vitamin C and 50% of the flavonoids of fresh-cut spinach into the cooking water (Gil, Ferreres and Tomas-Barberan, J Agric Food Chem 1999;47:2213-7, PMID 10794612). Recommending boiled spinach for vitamin C comes close to recommending the cooking water. Raw kale at 93.4 mg/100 g is the green that actually delivers, three times the spinach figure.
- **Absorption:** Absorption is near-complete up to about 200 mg per dose via the sodium-dependent transporter SVCT1, declines above 500 mg, and plasma saturates at 1,000 mg/day (PMID 8623000). Absorption is therefore not the variable that matters; retention before eating is. Cooking and storage losses, all from USDA Table of Nutrient Retention Factors Release 6 (2007), ascorbic acid column: greens boiled in water to cover and drained retain 55% (code 3005), greens boiled in a little water and drained 60% (3004), greens stir-fried 85% (3015), greens cooked from frozen and drained 60% (3019); other vegetables boiled in water to cover and drained 75% (3775), steamed 85% (3784); potatoes baked in the skin 80% (3301), boiled pared and drained 75% (3308), and only 25% when boiled after storage and browned (3311); canned fruit 50% (0154) and dried fruit 20% (0158). Steaming beats boiling and draining consistently: in broccoli every method except steaming caused significant loss of vitamin C and chlorophyll (Yuan et al., J Zhejiang Univ Sci B 2009;10:580-8, PMID 19650196, DOI 10.1631/jzus.b0920051). Storage compounds it: 15 days at 4 C across 19 fruits and vegetables lowered vitamin C and cut total phenolics by up to 29.67%, though total anthocyanins showed minimal change in that study despite the title (Galani et al., Antioxidants 2017;6(3):59, PMID 28737734). Practical upshot: a week-old boiled vegetable can deliver a quarter of its table value, so the 27% loss between raw broccoli at 89.2 mg/100 g and boiled broccoli at 64.9 mg/100 g is the optimistic case rather than the worst one.
- **Does the body make it:** Humans carry the terminal enzyme L-gulonolactone oxidase (GULO) only as a non-functional pseudogene, so synthesis is zero and the loss is genetic in origin (PMID 8175804); rats and dogs, which retain GULO, do not develop scurvy and we do. This is the reference case for what NONE means, and here the endogenous axis argues for intake, because a deficiency disease exists with a defined clinical picture. Every milligram is dietary, so supplementation is never redundant on this axis, and the case for a given dose rests on intake, losses and the plateau of tissue saturation.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE in humans, because the terminal enzyme is a pseudogene. In species that retain it: glucose to UDP-glucuronate to D-glucuronate to L-gulonate to L-gulono-1,4-lactone, then GULO to L-ascorbate, hepatic in mammals and renal in birds and reptiles. Humans retain only downstream handling: SVCT1 (SLC23A1) for intestinal and renal uptake, SVCT2 (SLC23A2) for tissue uptake, with dehydroascorbate regenerated to ascorbate by glutathione and thioredoxin reductase. Ascorbate is the required reducing cofactor for the Fe(II)/2-oxoglutarate-dependent prolyl and lysyl hydroxylases of collagen maturation.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/day endogenous synthesis. Dietary requirement instead: scurvy is prevented by roughly 10 mg/d; RDA 90 mg/day for men and 75 mg/day for women, plus 35 mg/day for smokers; plasma and neutrophil pools approach saturation near 200 mg/d; UL 2,000 mg/day (IOM DRI 2000, vitamin C chapter).
- **Still matters when:** Scurvy and populations at risk of it: very low fruit and vegetable intake, alcohol use disorder, restrictive diets, institutionalised or isolated older people, smoking, haemodialysis and some malabsorption patients, where the response to repletion is rapid and unambiguous. Also relevant to collagen synthesis, which is why collagen peptide products depend on adequate vitamin C status. Gram doses for cold prevention in the general population are not requirement-based, and surplus is renally cleared with a rise in urinary oxalate; high-dose intravenous ascorbate in cancer treatment is a separate pharmacological question for the treating team.

*Refs. Cochrane Hemila PMID 23440782; LOVIT PMID 35704292*

### Coenzyme Q10 / ubiquinol

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 100 mg three times daily, the Q-SYMBIO regimen. Ubiquinol is more bioavailable and doses do not transfer 1:1 from ubiquinone.
- **Who it is for:** Patients with heart failure with reduced ejection fraction, already on guideline therapy, with cardiology input.

**Pros**

- Q-SYMBIO is the only clinical-endpoint result in the whole longevity and metabolic category: n=420, 2 years, major adverse cardiovascular events 15% versus 26%, HR 0.50 (0.32-0.80).
- Genuine primary CoQ10 deficiency is a real, rare condition where replacement is the treatment.

**Cons**

- Q-SYMBIO is a single unreplicated trial. The NIHR health technology assessment covering 26 trials and 2,250 participants flagged important concerns about risk of bias, plausibility of effect sizes and applicability, and found all-cause mortality RR 0.68 (0.45-1.03), not significant.
- For statin-associated muscle symptoms, which is where most retail CoQ10 is actually sold, it does nothing: weighted mean difference -0.42 (-1.47 to 0.62) and adherence RR 0.99 across 7 trials.
- Warfarin interaction: CoQ10 is structurally similar to vitamin K, and one cross-sectional analysis of patients presenting with anticoagulant overdose found an association with bleeding.
- Expensive relative to everything else in this document, and the 1,200 mg observed safe level comes from a risk assessment whose authors were affiliated with an industry trade body.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Carries electrons in the mitochondrial energy chain and protects cell membranes from oxidation.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Coenzyme Q10 is a lipid-soluble benzoquinone with a ten-unit isoprenoid tail. In the inner mitochondrial membrane it shuttles electrons from complexes I and II to complex III, and it is also the electron acceptor for dihydroorotate dehydrogenase in pyrimidine synthesis and for fatty acid beta-oxidation through ETF-QO. Its reduced form, ubiquinol, is the main lipid-phase antioxidant of membranes and regenerates vitamin E. Humans synthesize it through the mevalonate pathway, the same pathway statins inhibit, and food contributes only a few milligrams a day. It is not a vitamin, there is no dietary requirement, and no dietary CoQ10 deficiency state exists. What does exist is primary coenzyme Q10 deficiency (COQ10D1 to COQ10D10), an autosomal recessive inborn error of CoQ10 biosynthesis caused by variants in PDSS1, PDSS2, COQ2, COQ4, COQ5, COQ6, COQ7, COQ8A, COQ8B or COQ9. That is a genetic disorder of synthesis, not a consequence of low dietary intake, and it is ultra-rare: GeneReviews notes that prevalence data are limited by the small number of affected individuals reported, ranging from 2 to just over 10 families per causative gene. Presentation spans the neonatal period to the seventh decade: steroid-resistant nephrotic syndrome with proteinuria, cerebellar ataxia, exercise intolerance and muscle weakness, or encephalopathy with seizures, and diagnosis rests on CoQ10 measurement in muscle or fibroblasts plus genetic testing. Severe neonatal-onset forms cause fatal multisystem disease with encephalopathy, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and renal failure, while later-onset forms progress to cerebellar atrophy with disabling ataxia or to end-stage kidney disease. High-dose oral CoQ10 can block progression of the kidney disease and improve some myopathic and encephalopathic features, and works best when started before damage is established; established severe neurological or renal damage does not reverse, and neonatal-onset disease responds poorly, partly because CoQ10 penetrates the blood-brain barrier badly. Separately and far more commonly, statins lower circulating CoQ10. Marcoff and Thompson's narrative systematic review of 9 observational studies and 6 randomised trials reports reductions of 16 to 54 percent, with the largest single trial (n=1,049) finding 38 percent with atorvastatin and 27 percent with lovastatin; pooled meta-analysis puts the effect at a weighted mean difference of -0.44 micromol/L (95% CI -0.52 to -0.37). That is a change in a blood concentration, not the disease described above, and trials of CoQ10 for statin-associated muscle symptoms have given conflicting results.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Body makes it

Organ meats and oily fish are genuine, substantial sources, so the substance question and the dose question separate cleanly here. Pork heart at 118.1-282 mg/kg is the richest food in the literature, beef heart is 113 mg/kg, chicken heart 92.3-192 mg/kg, chicken liver 116.2-132.2 mg/kg and mackerel dark flesh 43-67 mg/kg. Total habitual dietary intake is only 3.8 mg/day in women and 5.4 mg/day in men (Mattila & Kumpulainen 2001), so a single 100 g portion of heart is two to five days of ordinary intake. That is what RICH means in this dataset. It does not mean the supplement dose is reachable, which it is not. Supplement CoQ10 is produced by microbial fermentation or chemical synthesis, not extracted from beef.
