- **To match the dose:** A 300 mg/day dose needs 1.06 kg of pork heart at its richest measured value, rising to 2.54 kg at its low end; 1.6 to 3.3 kg of chicken hearts; 2.65 kg of beef heart; or 4.7 kg of the very richest sardines, about 51 tins, rising past 60 kg if the tins sit at the low end of the measured range. The submission's '2.7 kg of beef heart' was presented as the floor only because pork heart, the richest source of all, was missing. Against a whole day's ordinary dietary CoQ10 of 3.8-5.4 mg, a 300 mg capsule is about 56 to 79 days of eating in one dose and a 100 mg capsule about 19 to 26 days. The one boundary case worth stating: 355 g of pork heart at its richest measured value does reach 100 mg, which is a real meal in some cuisines but not a diet anyone sustains. Anyone whose reason for taking CoQ10 is a claimed pharmacological effect at 100-300 mg cannot get there by eating; anyone whose reason is a low-CoQ10 diet can move the needle with a weekly portion of heart, liver or oily fish.
- **Food vs supplement:** Significant. CoQ10 is a large lipophilic quinone (MW 863) and crystalline ubiquinone powder in a dry tablet absorbs very poorly; commercial products use oil suspensions, solubilisates or reduced-form ubiquinol precisely because the raw molecule does not dissolve. Absorption is saturable, so a 300 mg dose does not deliver proportionally more than a 100 mg dose. Food has one genuine advantage: CoQ10 in meat and fish comes packaged with fat, and supplemental CoQ10 taken with a fatty meal absorbs materially better, so a capsule swallowed on an empty stomach may deliver less than the label implies. Cooking costs some of the food content, with reported losses of roughly 15-32% frying pork cutlets and about 15% grilling beef. Most body CoQ10 is made endogenously and the biosynthetic pathway shares its early steps with cholesterol synthesis, which is the basis of the statin argument; neither food nor supplement changes that pathway.
- **Absorption:** Low and formulation-dependent for the supplement, which is the honest headline. Ubiquinol (reduced) and solubilised formulations achieve higher plasma levels than plain crystalline ubiquinone, and all forms depend on dietary fat and bile for uptake. In the food matrix CoQ10 sits in mitochondrial membranes and is co-ingested with fat, so uptake per milligram is probably as good as or better than a dry capsule, but the milligram totals are 10 to 80 times too small: the richest single portion in the table is 28.2 mg against a 300 mg dose. The reduced-to-oxidised split also differs between foods: a Japanese survey of 70 food items found ubiquinol-10 above 20 ug/g in pork shoulder, bovine liver, chicken heart, horse mackerel, young yellowtail and soybean oil (Kubo H, Fujii K, Kawabe T, Matsumoto S, Kishida H, Hosoe K, 'Food content of ubiquinol-10 and ubiquinone-10 in the Japanese diet', J Food Compos Anal 2008;21(3):199-210, DOI 10.1016/j.jfca.2007.10.003), so ubiquinol is not an exclusively supplemental form.
- **Does the body make it:** Every human cell makes ubiquinone on the mevalonate pathway, attaching a decaprenyl tail made by PDSS1/PDSS2 to a 4-hydroxybenzoate ring via COQ2, then finishing the molecule with the COQ3 to COQ9 enzymes and reducing it to ubiquinol. Diet supplies only 3 to 5 mg a day, a small contribution next to the tissue pools that endogenous synthesis maintains, so a healthy person is not short of CoQ10 and this is the clearest case in the group of a supplement filling a gap that does not exist. The statin link is real at the level of plasma measurement, where CoQ10 falls by about 0.44 micromol/L on treatment, and its interpretation is limited because plasma CoQ10 is carried on LDL, so part of the fall simply tracks the lipoprotein concentration and tissue or muscle depletion has not been demonstrated; on the endogenous axis nothing here exposes a dietary deficiency to correct.
- **Pathway:** Acetyl-CoA to HMG-CoA to mevalonate to farnesyl-PP to decaprenyl-PP (PDSS1/PDSS2); condensation with 4-hydroxybenzoate derived from tyrosine/phenylalanine by COQ2, and the tyrosine-to-4-hydroxybenzoate transamination steps require pyridoxal 5'-phosphate, so vitamin B6 status feeds the ring supply; ring modification by COQ3, COQ5, COQ6, COQ7 and COQ9 with the COQ8A and COQ8B kinases at the inner mitochondrial membrane, then reduction to ubiquinol. HMG-CoA reductase, the statin target, sits upstream of the polyprenyl tail.
- **Amount made:** Endogenous synthesis rate in humans NOT ESTABLISHED, though it proceeds in all tissues and is highest in heart, kidney and liver. Sourced comparators: average dietary intake 3-5 mg/day, about 64% of it from meat and poultry, with 14-32% destroyed by frying and no detectable destruction by boiling; total body content of roughly 0.5 to 1.5 g and plasma of roughly 0.6 to 1.9 micromol/L; no RDA, AI or UL exists. Across 8 pooled placebo-controlled treatment arms, statin therapy lowered plasma CoQ10 by -0.44 micromol/L versus placebo. Supplement doses are 100-300 mg/day, and 5-50 mg/kg/d in primary CoQ10 deficiency.
- **Still matters when:** Primary CoQ10 deficiency caused by COQ2, COQ4, COQ6, COQ8A, COQ8B, COQ9, PDSS1 or PDSS2 variants, where high-dose supplementation is standard care, dosing is time-critical and early treatment changes outcome. Also specific secondary deficiencies in mitochondrial disease and mitochondrial myopathies, adjunctive use in heart failure (Q-SYMBIO), and clinician-supervised trials of statin-associated muscle symptoms, where results remain inconsistent and mostly negative. Absorption is poor and lipid-dependent, so any trial or personal trial should dose it with fat.

*Refs. Q-SYMBIO PMID 25282031; Kennedy PMID 32179207; Claxton NIHR HTA PMID 35076012*

### Alpha-lipoic acid

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 600 mg/d orally. The strong evidence is for the intravenous route at the same dose, which consumers cannot access.
- **Who it is for:** People with symptomatic diabetic distal symmetric polyneuropathy, specifically positive sensory symptoms such as burning and paraesthesia.

**Pros**

- Licensed as a drug for diabetic polyneuropathy in Germany, which is a higher bar than any supplement in this document clears.
- The intravenous meta-analysis (4 trials, 1,258 patients) found consistent relief of positive neuropathic symptoms.
- Secondary endpoints in the long oral trial did improve, including neuropathy impairment scores.

**Cons**

- The definitive oral trial failed. NATHAN 1 randomised 460 patients to 600 mg/d for 4 years and missed its primary endpoint (P=0.105).
- Serious adverse events in that trial were 38.1% on alpha-lipoic acid versus 28.0% on placebo, an excess that is rarely mentioned in marketing.
- It is a leading trigger of insulin autoimmune syndrome (Hirata disease) in people carrying HLA-DRB1*04 alleles, producing anti-insulin antibodies and severe hypoglycaemia in non-diabetics. Almost nobody knows their HLA status.
- No upper limit has been set by any authority.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A cofactor mitochondria build and clip onto the enzymes that burn carbohydrate and amino acids.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Lipoic acid functions as a covalently bound prosthetic group (lipoamide) on the E2 subunits of pyruvate dehydrogenase, alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase, branched-chain ketoacid dehydrogenase and the glycine cleavage system, where its dithiolane ring carries acyl groups and reducing equivalents. Humans make it de novo inside mitochondria via lipoic acid synthase and attach it enzymatically to apoproteins; free lipoate from food or capsules is never used for this. Rare biallelic LIAS, LIPT1 or LIPT2 variants cause a severe neonatal encephalopathy with lactic acidosis and hyperglycinaemia, and oral lipoic acid does not rescue the lipoylation defect in patients or in patient fibroblasts, which is precisely why that genetic disorder is not a deficiency of the supplement molecule. Supplemental alpha-lipoic acid therefore acts only as a free redox-active compound, unrelated to the cofactor role, and its best evidence is symptomatic relief in diabetic polyneuropathy.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Trace
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Low
- **Body makes it:** Body makes it

TRACE was challenged and upheld, and the qualitative point matters more than the numbers: alpha-lipoic acid exists in food only as lipoyllysine, covalently attached to lysine residues in the E2 subunits of mitochondrial keto-acid dehydrogenases. Free racemic ALA, which is what a capsule contains, does not occur in food at all. The measured food quantities are in the range 0.003 to 0.40 mg per 100 g, four to five orders of magnitude below a 600 mg capsule. Supplemental ALA is chemically synthesised, so the supplement is best read as a synthetic compound with a bound, chemically different trace analogue in food.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Heart (turkey, pork, calf, cow, chicken), raw *[unconfirmed]* | 0.21-0.40 mg lipoyllysine (2.11-3.99 ug/g fresh weight: turkey 3.99, pork 3.46, calf 3.32, cow 3.12, chicken 2.11) | 1 serving (100 g) | 0.21-0.40 mg | 0.04-0.07% of a 600 mg dose | Kaminska A & Chwatko G 2020, 'Estimation of lipoyllysine content in meat and its antioxidative capacity', J Agric Food Chem 68(39):10992-10999, PMID 32886880, DOI 10.1021/acs.jafc.0c03778 |
| Kidney (beef/pork), raw *[unconfirmed]* | 0.07-0.14 mg lipoyllysine (0.71-1.40 ug/g fresh weight) | 1 serving (100 g) | 0.07-0.14 mg | 0.01-0.02% of a 600 mg dose | Kaminska & Chwatko 2020, PMID 32886880 |
| Liver (beef/pork), raw *[unconfirmed]* | 0.06-0.12 mg lipoyllysine (0.56-1.17 ug/g fresh weight) | 1 serving (100 g) | 0.06-0.12 mg | 0.01-0.02% of a 600 mg dose | Kaminska & Chwatko 2020, PMID 32886880 |
| Broccoli, raw | about 0.010 mg lipoyllysine (0.94 +/- 0.33 ug/g DRY weight; about 89% water, giving 10.3 ug/100 g fresh) | 1 cup chopped (91 g) | about 0.0094 mg (9.4 micrograms) | 0.0016% of a 600 mg dose | Lodge JK et al. 1997, J Appl Nutr 49:3-11, reproduced by Moini et al. 2002 and tabulated by the Linus Pauling Institute (dry-weight value and the dry-to-fresh conversion both confirmed); fresh/dry basis from USDA FDC 170379, ID not confirmed this session |
| Spinach, raw | about 0.028 mg lipoyllysine (3.15 +/- 1.11 ug/g DRY weight; spinach is about 91% water, giving 27-28 ug per 100 g fresh) | 1 cup raw leaves (30 g) | about 0.0085 mg (8.5 micrograms) | 0.0014% of a 600 mg dose | Lodge JK et al. 1997, J Appl Nutr 49:3-11, reproduced by Moini et al. 2002 and tabulated by the Linus Pauling Institute (highest plant value; dry-weight figure and conversion both confirmed); fresh/dry basis from USDA FDC 168462, ID not confirmed this session |
| Tomato, raw | about 0.003 mg lipoyllysine (about 0.56 ug/g DRY weight; about 94.5% water) | 1 medium tomato (123 g) | about 0.0038 mg (3.8 micrograms) | 0.0006% of a 600 mg dose | Lodge JK et al. 1997, J Appl Nutr 49:3-11, as tabulated by the Linus Pauling Institute. The spinach and broccoli dry-weight values from this dataset were confirmed; the tomato value is plausible but was not directly confirmed, so treat it as indicative |

- **To match the dose:** Using the confirmed plant figures, a 600 mg dose is about 2,143 kg of raw spinach a day, roughly 71,000 cups, and broccoli is similar. Using the richest meat value (turkey heart at 0.40 mg/100 g) it is 150 kg of heart a day, though that meat figure is itself unconfirmed. The arithmetic understates the problem. What food contains is lipoyllysine, lipoic acid amide-bonded to protein-bound lysine, and human lipoamidase activity releases very little free lipoic acid from that bond, so the free-ALA yield from eating spinach is close to unmeasurable rather than merely small. A 600 mg fasting capsule produces micromolar plasma peaks of free racemic lipoic acid; no quantity of food produces a detectable free-ALA peak at all. This is the clearest food-supplement discontinuity in the group.
- **Food vs supplement:** Large, and it cuts against the supplement. Supplemental ALA is chemically synthesised as a 50:50 racemate of R- and S-lipoic acid, while nature makes only the R enantiomer and only protein-bound, so the capsule contains a molecule (S-lipoic acid) that does not exist in food. Absorption differs by design as well: capsules are dosed fasting because food reduces ALA absorption by roughly 20-30%, whereas the trace food form is unavoidably eaten with food. On safety, the whole food is unremarkable and the concentrated free acid is not. High-dose free ALA has been linked to insulin autoimmune syndrome with severe hypoglycaemia in genetically susceptible people, and there is a paediatric accidental-overdose literature with seizures and death. Spinach has done none of that. If you want the antioxidant-vegetable benefit, eat the vegetables; if you want the 600 mg neuropathy dose from the ALADIN and SYDNEY trials, understand that it is a synthetic drug-dose racemate with its own risk profile.
- **Absorption:** Free supplemental ALA is absorbed reasonably well but cleared very fast, with a plasma half-life on the order of half an hour, and the R enantiomer reaches higher plasma levels than S. Food lipoyllysine is a different question: it is not a bioavailable source of free lipoic acid, because the lipoyl group stays bound to the enzyme protein and is largely digested along with it rather than released as the free dithiol. The measurement literature reflects this, noting that free lipoic acid in tissues is low if present at all and that essentially all of it is protein-bound. Spinach carries the usual oxalate caveat for other purposes, but oxalate is not why spinach fails as a lipoic acid source; it fails because there is almost none there and what is there is chemically locked up.
- **Does the body make it:** Human mitochondria make lipoic acid de novo: an octanoyl chain from the mitochondrial fatty acid synthase route is transferred onto a lysine residue of the target protein, and lipoyl synthase (LIAS) then inserts two sulfur atoms while the chain is already attached. The functional cofactor pool is protein-bound R-lipoyllysine on pyruvate dehydrogenase, alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase, BCKDH and the glycine cleavage H protein, and free lipoate is not a physiological cofactor form, although mammalian lipoamidase, the same protein as serum biotinidase, does hydrolyse lipoyl substrates (PMID 8484735, PMID 2225462), an activity present in human serum and milk (PMID 3168970, PMID 2127386). The FULL label applies to the endogenous cofactor and does not carry over to the capsule: swallowed free ALA is never loaded onto the dehydrogenase complexes, so a supplement works as a short-acting redox drug and not as repletion of a cofactor.
- **Pathway:** Mitochondrial FAS II generates octanoyl-ACP; LIPT2 transfers the octanoyl group onto the H protein lysine; LIAS, a radical-SAM iron-sulfur enzyme, inserts sulfur at C6 and C8; LIPT1 then relays lipoyl groups to the E2 subunits of the 2-oxo acid dehydrogenase complexes. LIAS activity depends on iron-sulfur cluster assembly machinery (NFU1, BOLA3, GLRX5, ISCA2, IBA57), the commonest genetic cause of human lipoylation failure and usually omitted from supplement-side accounts. The reactions sit in the mitochondrial matrix and run in all tissues.
- **Amount made:** NOT ESTABLISHED. No measured human de novo lipoate synthesis rate exists. Food provides only protein-bound lipoyllysine in trace amounts, orders of magnitude below the free ALA content of a typical 200-600 mg supplement.
- **Still matters when:** Diabetic peripheral neuropathy, where 600 mg oral or intravenous ALA has trial support of mixed quality; that is a pharmacological indication, unrelated to correcting a deficiency. Inherited LIAS, LIPT1 and LIPT2 defects, and the Fe-S cluster defects (NFU1, BOLA3, GLRX5) that cause the same lipoylation failure, produce severe multisystem disease; in patient fibroblasts, treatment with lipoate and with mitochondria-targeted lipoate failed to correct the defect (PMID 24334290), and no registered clinical trial of oral lipoate exists in these patients, so that evidence is cell-level. Watch for reports of insulin autoimmune syndrome in susceptible HLA genotypes.

*Refs. NATHAN 1 PMID 21775755; Ziegler IV meta-analysis PMID 14984445; BMJ Case Rep PMID 42493219*

### Beta-alanine

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 4-6 g/d for at least 2-4 weeks, split into doses of about 1.6 g to limit paraesthesia. Total accumulated dose matters more than daily dose.
- **Who it is for:** Athletes whose limiting factor is acidosis in the one to four minute window: middle distance, rowing, combat sports, high-repetition lifting.

**Pros**

- The mechanism is sound and confirmed: it raises intramuscular carnosine, which buffers protons.
- The IOC consensus statement places buffering agents on its short list of supplements with robust performance evidence.
- Cheap, and paraesthesia aside, no established contraindication or drug interaction exists.

**Cons**

- The overall pooled effect is SMD 0.18 (0.08-0.28) across 40 studies and 1,461 people. Statistically significant and practically tiny.
- In the 0.5 to 10 minute window where it should work best, exercise CAPACITY improved (SMD 0.50) but exercise PERFORMANCE did not (SMD 0.11, 95% CI -0.20 to 0.42, non-significant). Capacity is a laboratory measure; performance is what a race is.
- The meta-analysis is routinely quoted for the flattering subgroup and never for the null one.
- No long-term safety data beyond a few months, and no upper limit set by any authority.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

The raw material for carnosine, which buffers acid build-up in muscle during hard efforts.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Beta-alanine is the rate-limiting substrate for carnosine synthase, which joins it to histidine to form carnosine in skeletal muscle. Carnosine acts as an intracellular pH buffer across the physiological range and also chelates metals and scavenges reactive carbonyls. Supplementation raises muscle carnosine by up to 64 percent at 4 weeks and up to 80 percent at 10 weeks, and produces a small performance gain concentrated in efforts of roughly 0.5 to 10 minutes, with no benefit below 30 seconds and only a non-significant moderate effect beyond 10 minutes (Saunders et al. 2017, 40 studies, 1,461 subjects); the ISSN position stand places the most pronounced benefit in open end-point tasks and time trials lasting 1 to 4 minutes. The body forms beta-alanine from uracil degradation in the liver and also obtains it from meat-derived carnosine and anserine. It is a non-essential amino acid with no recommended intake.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Partly

Free beta-alanine, the molecule in the tub, is manufactured and no food supplies it in that form, which is a question about form rather than about dietary presence. What food supplies is beta-alanine bound into the histidine dipeptides carnosine and anserine, hydrolysed after ingestion, at hundreds of milligrams of beta-alanine equivalent per 100 g of white muscle: 346 mg/100 g in commercial broiler breast and 767 mg/100 g in the native Korean strain measured in the same study. Per portion that is 589 mg and 1,304 mg, 18% and 41% of a 3.2 g daily dose, at or above the per-portion contribution creatine makes, and isomolar oral L-carnosine raised muscle carnosine as much as free beta-alanine did (65.8% versus 64.2%), so the dietary route is biologically real. The earlier TRACE label was wrong by the dataset's own yardstick; poultry dark meat is the weak part of the picture, at 19.51-36.19 mg carnosine per 100 g.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Chicken breast, native Korean strain HH, raw (carnosine 511.04 plus anserine 1,525.80 mg/100 g) | 767 mg beta-alanine equivalent (carnosine x 0.394 plus anserine x 0.371) | 1 skinless breast, 170 g raw | 1,304 mg beta-alanine equivalent | 41% of a 3.2 g/day dose | Ali M, Lee SY, Park JY, Jung S, Jo C, Nam KC. Comparison of Functional Compounds and Micronutrients of Chicken Breast Meat by Breeds. Food Sci Anim Resour 2019;39(4):632-642. PMID 31508593; DOI 10.5851/kosfa.2019.e54 (values confirmed in full text). Added because the original cited this paper but used only its lowest strain. |
| Chicken breast, commercial broiler, raw (carnosine 257.94 plus anserine 660.38 mg/100 g) | 346 mg beta-alanine equivalent (carnosine x 0.394 plus anserine x 0.371) | 1 skinless breast, 170 g raw | 589 mg beta-alanine equivalent (439 mg carnosine, 1,123 mg anserine) | 18% of a 3.2 g/day dose | Ali M et al., Food Sci Anim Resour 2019;39(4):632-642. DOI 10.5851/kosfa.2019.e54 (both dipeptide values and the 346 mg conversion re-derived and confirmed) |
| Chicken breast, Thai native and black-bone breeds, raw | 428.08-553.93 mg carnosine, i.e. 169-218 mg beta-alanine equivalent from carnosine alone | 1 skinless breast, 170 g raw | 728-942 mg carnosine (287-371 mg beta-alanine equivalent) | 9-12% of a 3.2 g/day dose (carnosine only; anserine not reported) | Khumpeerawat P, Duangjinda M, Phasuk Y. Animals (Basel) 2021;11(7):1987. PMID 34359114; DOI 10.3390/ani11071987 (range confirmed) |
| Beef muscle, raw (Japanese Black/Wagyu steers, 21 muscles) | 187.5 mg carnosine (8.291 umol/g wet tissue, range across the 21 muscles 0.27-418.7 mg/100 g) plus 26.5 mg anserine (1.104 umol/g) = 84 mg beta-alanine equivalent | 1 steak, 200 g raw | 375 mg carnosine plus 53 mg anserine = 167 mg beta-alanine equivalent | 5% of a 3.2 g/day dose | Mashima D et al., Anim Sci J 2019;90(4):604-609. PMID 30811817; DOI 10.1111/asj.13185, Table 2 (means confirmed). This is one specialty breed, so it should not be read as beef generally. |
| Chicken thigh, fresh (dark meat, the poor relation) | 19.51-36.19 mg carnosine = 7.7-14.3 mg beta-alanine equivalent; the ordinary commercial broiler sits at the top of that range at 36.19 | 2 boneless thighs, 150 g | 29-54 mg carnosine (12-21 mg beta-alanine equivalent) | 0.4-0.7% of a 3.2 g/day dose | Barido FH et al., Foods 2022;11(19):3006. PMID 36230082; DOI 10.3390/foods11193006, Table 4 (all four breed values confirmed) |

- **To match the dose:** Carnosine is 39.4% beta-alanine by mass (89.09/226.23) and anserine 37.1% (89.09/240.26). The lowest dose shown to work is 3.2 g/day for 4 weeks, which raised muscle carnosine 42.1%. From commercial broiler breast at 346 mg beta-alanine equivalent per 100 g, counting both dipeptides, that is 923 g of raw chicken breast every day for the whole loading month, roughly 210 g of protein from one food; counting carnosine alone it is 3.15 kg/day. The carnosine-dense native strain at 767 mg/100 g cuts the requirement to 417 g/day, a large but conceivable portion, except that the breed is not what a supermarket sells. Beef takes 3.8 kg of raw steak per day and chicken thigh over 20 kg. No food delivers free beta-alanine at all.
- **Food vs supplement:** MAJOR, and it cuts both ways. Ingested carnosine does not reach muscle intact: after histidine dipeptides were given in chicken broth at 40 mg/kg beta-alanine equivalent, carnosine was undetectable in plasma while plasma beta-alanine peaked at 428 uM, because serum carnosinase hydrolyses the dipeptide. So the food form works by liberating beta-alanine, and it does so at roughly half the peak concentration of an equal weight of free beta-alanine (833 uM at the same 40 mg/kg dose). Isomolar oral L-carnosine nonetheless raised muscle carnosine as much as 6.4 g/day of beta-alanine (65.8% versus 64.2%), so the dipeptide route is biologically equivalent and only the volume of food is impossible (Harris RC et al., Amino Acids 2006;30(3):279-89, PMID 16554972, DOI 10.1007/s00726-006-0299-9, every figure confirmed). Two further points: cooking significantly reduces meat carnosine, measured in lamb longissimus and semimembranosus after 90 min at 70 C (Purchas RW et al., Meat Sci 2004;66(3):629-37, PMID 22060873), and the paraesthesia that makes people split beta-alanine doses is a property of a large free-amino-acid bolus that food never produces.
- **Absorption:** Quantified head to head. At 40 mg/kg body weight, plasma beta-alanine peaked at 833 uM from free beta-alanine and at 428 uM from an equivalent load of histidine dipeptides in chicken broth, with both returning to baseline by 2 h and urinary loss of beta-alanine under 5% (PMID 16554972). Muscle carnosine responses after 4 weeks: 42.1% at 3.2 g/day, 64.2% at 6.4 g/day, and 65.8% for isomolar L-carnosine. The food matrix costs about half the peak plasma concentration per gram and does not block the pathway; the binding constraint is that meat holds only 0.08-0.77% beta-alanine equivalent by weight. Content varies severalfold with muscle type and breed: carnosine across 21 beef muscles ranged 0.012-18.508 umol/g wet tissue, that is 0.27-418.7 mg/100 g (PMID 30811817), and glycolytic white muscle holds far more than red oxidative muscle, so 'chicken' is not one number.
- **Does the body make it:** Your liver does make beta-alanine as a by-product of breaking down the pyrimidine uracil (cytosine reaches the same route by deamination to uracil), and GADL1 makes some from aspartate in certain tissues, while the parallel thymine branch runs the same three enzymes and yields 3-aminoisobutyrate, so thymine contributes none. The amounts are small relative to what muscle could use, and beta-alanine availability is what limits muscle carnosine, since carnosine synthase (CARNS1) is not saturated at resting beta-alanine levels and the enzyme capacity is not the bottleneck. So this is a compound the body makes yet never accumulates to a functionally saturated level, which is why 4 to 6 g/day in divided doses raises muscle carnosine and why supplementing is not redundant.
- **Pathway:** Uracil (and cytosine via deamination to uracil) is reduced by dihydropyrimidine dehydrogenase (DPYD) to dihydrouracil, hydrolysed by dihydropyrimidinase (DPYS) to 3-ureidopropionate, then cleaved by beta-ureidopropionase (UPB1) to beta-alanine, mainly in liver; the parallel thymine branch uses the same three enzymes and ends at 3-aminoisobutyrate. Beta-alanine plus L-histidine is condensed to carnosine by carnosine synthase (CARNS1) in skeletal muscle, and ingested carnosine or anserine is hydrolysed first by serum carnosinase (CNDP1), which is why the effective oral form is beta-alanine itself. GADL1 supplies some beta-alanine from aspartate in certain tissues, and gut bacteria contribute part of the supply, which this dataset counts as exogenous.
- **Amount made:** NOT ESTABLISHED. No validated figure exists for human daily endogenous beta-alanine production; the literature characterises it only as small relative to demand. The sourced comparison is on the intake side: 4 to 6 g/day in divided doses of no more than 2 g, for at least 2 to 4 weeks, reliably raises muscle carnosine, and 3.2 to 6.4 g/day raises muscle carnosine by 40 to 80 percent over 4 to 10 weeks.
- **Still matters when:** High-intensity efforts lasting more than about 60 s, with the clearest effects in the 1 to 4 min range and in open-end-point tasks, where intracellular buffering limits performance; vegetarians and vegans, who carry lower muscle carnosine; older adults, in whom muscle carnosine declines. Histidine availability can co-limit carnosine synthesis, so very low protein intake blunts the response, and paraesthesia is dose-related and controllable with divided or sustained-release dosing. Endogenous synthesis exists here but sits far below the buffering ceiling, so supplementation is not redundant.

*Refs. Saunders PMID 27797728; Trexler ISSN PMID 26175657; IOC PMID 29540367*

### Dietary nitrate / beetroot

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** At least 6 mmol/d (372 mg) of nitrate, taken for more than 3 days, 2-3 hours before exercise. Read the label in mmol, not millilitres.
- **Who it is for:** Recreationally active people rather than elite athletes, and separately anyone using it for blood pressure.

**Pros**

- Time to exhaustion improves (SMD 0.33), as do total distance (0.42), muscular endurance (0.48) and peak power (0.25), across 180 primary studies and 2,672 participants.
- The blood pressure effect is more consistent than the performance effect: systolic -3.55 mmHg (-4.55 to -2.54) across 22 controlled trials.
- Non-athletes and recreationally active people benefit most, which is unusual and makes it accessible.

**Cons**

- Time trial performance, the one outcome that maps onto racing, is NULL. So is VO2max. The umbrella review is blunt about this.
- Antibacterial mouthwash abolishes the effect entirely, because the pathway depends on commensal bacteria on the tongue.
- Ergogenic doses of 6-8 mmol exceed the EFSA and JECFA acceptable daily intake for nitrate of 3.7 mg/kg body weight per day in a single serving. That is a real regulatory tension, not a technicality.
- Additive hypotension with organic nitrates such as glyceryl trinitrate and isosorbide mononitrate, and with PDE5 inhibitors such as sildenafil and tadalafil. Care with any antihypertensive.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Not a nutrient. Gut and mouth bacteria turn it into nitric oxide, which widens blood vessels.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Inorganic nitrate from beetroot and leafy greens is absorbed in the proximal small intestine, and roughly a quarter of the circulating pool is actively concentrated by the salivary glands. Commensal bacteria on the posterior tongue reduce it to nitrite, and swallowed nitrite is further reduced to nitric oxide in the acidic stomach and in hypoxic tissue by deoxyhaemoglobin, deoxymyoglobin, xanthine oxidoreductase and other metalloproteins. This enterosalivary route is a supplementary supply line that runs in parallel with the body's own synthesis of nitric oxide from L-arginine by nitric oxide synthase, which is the primary and always-available pathway. That distinction is what makes nitrate a usable substrate rather than a required nutrient: NOS-derived nitric oxide continues normally on a nitrate-free diet. Measured effects are pharmacological and dose-dependent: vasodilation with a modest fall in blood pressure, and a reduced oxygen cost of submaximal exercise. Antibacterial mouthwash abolishes the effect entirely by killing the nitrate-reducing oral flora, which is the clearest demonstration that the mechanism is microbial rather than nutritional.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Partly

Vegetables are the dietary source and several reach supplement-level concentrations. EFSA pooled 25,306 leafy vegetable samples: rocket had the highest median nitrate of any vegetable at 4,800 mg/kg, with rhubarb at 2,808, amaranth 2,660, lamb's lettuce 2,130, mixed lettuce 1,878, beet leaves 1,770, curled lettuce 1,628, oak-leaf lettuce 1,553, chard 1,510, Belgian endive 1,475, white radish 1,256, black radish 1,245, beetroot root 1,100 and cos lettuce 1,097, ahead of spinach at 785. This is the one entry on the list where a normal portion of a normal food matches the dose used in trials.

| Spinach, raw | 78.5 mg nitrate (1.27 mmol); median 785 mg/kg, mean 1,066, P95 3,048, n=6,657 | 1 large bowl raw leaves, 100 g | 78.5 mg (1.27 mmol) | 21% of a 6 mmol dose (474 g reaches 6 mmol) | EFSA Journal 2008;689:1-79, Table 7. DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2008.689 (all statistics verified) |
| Fennel bulb, raw | 78.3 mg nitrate (1.26 mmol); median 783 mg/kg | 1 serving, 100 g | 78.3 mg (1.26 mmol) | 21% of a 6 mmol dose (475 g reaches 6 mmol) | EFSA Journal 2008;689:1-79, nitrate tables. Added on the verification report's finding; individual row not re-checked here. |
| Iceberg lettuce, raw | 84.4 mg nitrate (1.36 mmol); median 844 mg/kg, mean 875, n=1,980 | 1 side salad, 80 g | 67.5 mg (1.09 mmol) | 18% of a 6 mmol dose (441 g reaches 6 mmol) | EFSA Journal 2008;689:1-79, Table 7. DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2008.689 (all statistics verified) |
| Celery, raw | 69.3 mg nitrate (1.12 mmol); median 693 mg/kg, mean 1,103, P95 3,319, n=387 | 2 sticks, 80 g | 55.4 mg (0.89 mmol) | 15% of a 6 mmol dose (537 g reaches 6 mmol) | EFSA Journal 2008;689:1-79, Table 9. DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2008.689 (all statistics verified) |
| Radish, raw | 73.5 mg nitrate (1.19 mmol); median 735 mg/kg, mean 967, n=788 | 5 radishes, 50 g | 36.8 mg (0.59 mmol) | 10% of a 6 mmol dose | EFSA Journal 2008;689:1-79, Table 10. DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2008.689 (all statistics verified) |

- **To match the dose:** At 62 mg per mmol a 6 mmol target is 372 mg of nitrate. At EFSA median concentrations that is 78 g of rocket, 132 g of rhubarb, 140 g of amaranth or 175 g of lamb's lettuce, all portions a person would actually eat, or exactly 100 mL of concentrated beetroot shot, since 70 mL measured 4.2 mmol and 140 mL 8.4 mmol. Beetroot root itself takes 338 g, butterhead lettuce 188 g, iceberg 441 g, raw spinach 474 g and celery 537 g. Wylie's dose-response data put the useful target higher than 6 mmol: 4.2 mmol changed no exercise response, 8.4 mmol reduced steady-state oxygen uptake 1.7% and extended time to task failure 14%, and 16.8 mmol gave no further gain. An 8 mmol target is 496 mg of nitrate, which is about 103 g of rocket or 133 mL of concentrate.
- **Food vs supplement:** SUBSTANTIAL, mostly about dose uncertainty and a regulatory conflict. Vegetable nitrate varies enormously: rocket ran from 1,528 mg/kg at the 5th percentile to 7,340 at the 95th, and the highest single leafy sample EFSA saw was 19,925 mg/kg in an oak-leaf lettuce grown under cover in Norway. Beetroot's mean (1,379 mg/kg) is well above its median (1,100) because of a long tail, so a salad or a home-pressed juice cannot be dosed. The regulatory tension is concrete: EFSA calculated that eating more than 47 g of rocket at the median nitrate concentration would on its own exceed the acceptable daily intake for nitrate, and a rocket-heavy salad pattern pushed modelled intake to 330 mg/person/day. So the food route to 6 mmol means routinely exceeding the nitrate ADI, which EFSA judged acceptable in the context of overall vegetable benefits and which is still not nothing. Processing matters too: nitrate in red beet and kohlrabi was reduced by up to 50% by fermentation, and boiling leaches nitrate into the water, so pickled beetroot and boiled-and-drained spinach are weaker than the raw figures suggest. The pathway also depends on oral bacteria reducing nitrate to nitrite, so antibacterial mouthwash blunts the effect regardless of source. Spinach is the classic textbook nitrate answer and a mediocre one: its median is six times lower than rocket, and it is separately a bad iron recommendation because its oxalate binds the iron.
- **Absorption:** Nitrate from vegetables and from a juice concentrate behave the same because it is a small inorganic anion, absorbed essentially completely in the upper small intestine, then concentrated in saliva by the enterosalivary circulation and reduced to nitrite by oral bacteria before any nitric oxide is formed. Wylie's dose-response work showed plasma nitrite rising dose-dependently with peak change at 2-3 h after 4.2, 8.4 and 16.8 mmol, while the physiological response saturated: 8.4 and 16.8 mmol produced similar improvements, so more nitrate is not more effect. The practical differences are therefore in the food rather than the gut: concentration varies severalfold with species, season, greenhouse versus open-field growing and region (EFSA 2008), juice concentrates are standardised while salad is not, and cooking water discards nitrate. A whole-food route is entirely viable; it wants rocket, rhubarb or amaranth rather than beetroot to be efficient.
- **Does the body make it:** Humans do produce nitrate in quantity: about 1 mmol/day appears from the oxidation of NO, roughly 37 percent of the total body nitrate pool against a dietary intake of about 1700 micromol/day, and that output is no inert waste, since it re-enters the enterosalivary circuit, is concentrated in saliva and is reduced back to nitrite and NO by the same route a beetroot dose exploits. Two limits carry the practical conclusion: endogenous appearance cannot be up-regulated on demand, and raising plasma nitrite enough to lower the O2 cost of exercise needs dietary loading well above endogenous turnover. Keep the two routes separate as well, since NO made from arginine by nitric oxide synthase is independent of the dietary nitrate to nitrite to NO route, so on this axis a nitrate dose is not redundant.
- **Pathway:** Endogenous nitrate arises from oxidation of NOS-derived NO through nitrite, with essentially all NOS output ending as nitrate, which is why endogenous nitrate appearance and NOS-dependent NO production are the same order of magnitude. Nitrate, ingested or endogenous, is concentrated in saliva by sialin (SLC17A5), with roughly 25 percent of an ingested dose entering the enterosalivary circuit and being reduced to nitrite by oral bacterial nitrate reductase; nitrite is then reduced to NO in the acid stomach and in hypoxic tissue by deoxyhaemoglobin and other nitrite reductases. That first reduction step is bacterial, which this dataset counts as exogenous supply.
- **Amount made:** Endogenous nitrate appearance from NO oxidation is about 1 mmol/day (900 to 1280 micromol/day, roughly 56 to 79 mg) in participants held below 180 micromol nitrate intake, about 37 percent of body nitrate against a dietary contribution of about 1700 micromol/day.
- **Still matters when:** Endurance and repeated-sprint work at roughly 6 to 13 mmol nitrate (about 400 to 800 mg), lowering the O2 cost of exercise; hypoxia; blood pressure reduction; older adults and others with impaired NOS function. Antibacterial mouthwash blunts the effect because oral bacteria do the bulk of the first reduction. Nitrite is a separately supplemented species with different kinetics and a narrower margin (methaemoglobinaemia), so the two are not interchangeable, and despite the PARTIAL label supplementation is not redundant, because endogenous production cannot be raised on demand.

*Refs. Poon umbrella review PMID 40085422; Bahadoran PMID 29141968; Govoni PMID 18793740*

### St John's wort

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 900 mg/d of standardised extract, usually 300 mg three times daily. Trials ran 4-12 weeks.
- **Who it is for:** Adults with mild to moderate unipolar depression who take NO other medication whatsoever and no hormonal contraception.

**Pros**

- It genuinely works for mild to moderate depression. Cochrane pooled 29 trials and 5,489 patients: response-rate ratio 1.28 (1.10-1.49) in the 9 larger trials.
- Side-effect burden is lower than standard antidepressants in the trials, which is the main reason patients seek it out.
- In the EU it is handled as a herbal medicinal product rather than a food supplement, so product quality is better controlled than for most botanicals.

**Cons**

- This has the most dangerous interaction profile in this entire document. Hyperforin is a potent pregnane X receptor ligand and therefore a strong inducer of CYP3A4, CYP2C9, CYP1A2 and P-glycoprotein.
- Documented consequences: oral contraceptive failure with breakthrough bleeding and unintended pregnancy; loss of ciclosporin and tacrolimus levels leading to transplant rejection; antiretroviral failure with indinavir; reduced digoxin and warfarin effect; and serotonin syndrome when combined with SSRIs.
- The efficacy estimate is unstable. The response-rate ratio was 1.28 in the 9 larger trials but 1.87 in the 9 smaller ones, and there is a strong country-of-origin effect concentrated in German-speaking countries.
- Photosensitivity, and it should be stopped before elective surgery.
- Practically speaking this is suitable for almost nobody, because almost everyone is on something.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A herbal antidepressant that also speeds up how the liver destroys many prescription drugs.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Hypericum perforatum extracts contain hypericin, pseudohypericin and hyperforin; hyperforin is the antidepressant-relevant constituent and inhibits reuptake of serotonin, noradrenaline and dopamine non-selectively. Hyperforin is also a potent ligand for the pregnane X receptor (Ki 27 nM), the nuclear receptor that drives transcription of CYP3A4 and P-glycoprotein, so the extract induces the clearance of a very wide range of co-administered drugs. Because CYP3A4 handles the oxidative metabolism of more than half of all medicines, this produces documented therapeutic failures with ciclosporin, tacrolimus, indinavir, warfarin and combined oral contraceptives, and serotonin toxicity when combined with SSRIs. Hypericin and hyperforin are plant products with no role in human physiology and no dietary requirement.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Not food
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

St John's wort is the dried flowering aerial parts of Hypericum perforatum, a European meadow plant with no culinary use anywhere. It is consumed only as a medicine: tea, tincture, oil macerate or standardised dry extract. The two constituents that define it, hypericin (a naphthodianthrone) and hyperforin (a prenylated phloroglucinol), are confined to Hypericum species and are absent from the food supply, so there is no food route at any dose. Pharmacopoeial and commercial standards specify 0.3% hypericin, and the well-studied extract WS 5570 is an 80% ethanolic extract at a drug-to-extract ratio of 3:1 to 7:1 containing 0.12-0.28% hypericin and 5-6% hyperforin. One point worth carrying across from the melatonin entry: Hypericum perforatum is itself among the richest melatonin-bearing botanicals on record at roughly 4,490 ng/g dry weight, which is a curiosity about the plant rather than a reason to eat it.

- **To match the dose:** Standard antidepressant dosing is 300 mg of standardised extract three times daily, 900 mg/day, delivering roughly 1.1-2.5 mg of hypericin (900 mg x 0.12-0.28%) and 45-54 mg of hyperforin. At a 3:1 to 7:1 drug-to-extract ratio that corresponds to about 2.7-6.3 g of dried herb per day. There is no food equivalent to compute at any portion size, because hypericin and hyperforin do not occur in foods. This is a complete absence rather than a large multiplier.
- **Food vs supplement:** The most consequential caveat in the whole set, and it runs opposite to the usual pattern: here the concentrated product is the dangerous one and there is no safe whole-food version to fall back on. Hyperforin is a potent activator of the pregnane X receptor and therefore a strong inducer of CYP3A4, CYP2C19 and P-glycoprotein. It lowers plasma levels of combined oral contraceptives (breakthrough bleeding and contraceptive failure are documented), ciclosporin and tacrolimus (with reported transplant rejection, including graft loss), warfarin, digoxin, HIV protease inhibitors and non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors, irinotecan and imatinib, and several anticonvulsants. Combined with SSRIs, SNRIs, triptans or tramadol it can precipitate serotonin syndrome. Hypericin causes dose-dependent photosensitisation. Preparation type changes the risk profile substantially: hyperforin is lipophilic and unstable, so a water infusion delivers far less of it than an ethanolic extract, and low-hyperforin extracts were developed specifically to reduce interactions. A tea, a tincture and a capsule labelled with the same herb weight are not interchangeable, and treating this plant as a gentle food-adjacent botanical is the central error.
- **Absorption:** Hyperforin is well absorbed from oil-containing or ethanolic preparations, has a long elimination half-life on the order of nine hours and accumulates with three-times-daily dosing, which is why enzyme induction builds over one to two weeks and takes a similar period to wash out after stopping. Hypericin absorption is slow, low and variable, with a much longer half-life still. Both are lipophilic, so an aqueous tea extracts a small and inconsistent share, particularly of hyperforin, and hypericin is light-sensitive and degrades in poorly stored material. Since there is no food form, the practical absorption question is preparation rather than matrix, and the answer is that interaction risk tracks hyperforin exposure rather than the labelled hypericin percentage most products still advertise.
- **Does the body make it:** The relevant constituents are hypericin, a naphthodianthrone, and hyperforin, a prenylated phloroglucinol, both made only by Hypericum perforatum. Humans synthesise neither, so nothing here is replacement. On this one the axis being silent matters less than the interaction profile: hyperforin activates PXR and induces CYP3A4, which cuts blood levels of oral contraceptives, ciclosporin, warfarin and HIV protease inhibitors.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Plant polyketide and phloroglucinol pathways in Hypericum perforatum; no human homologue.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway)
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis. The screening question is drug interaction, not deficiency: CYP3A4 induction by hyperforin plus serotonergic additivity with SSRIs and triptans. Also check hyperforin content, since low-hyperforin extracts induce far less.

*Refs. Cochrane Linde PMID 18843608; Nicolussi PMID 31742659; Moore PNAS DOI 10.1073/pnas.130155097*

### Lactase and pancreatic enzymes

**Verdict:** Consider &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** Lactase 3,000-9,000 FCC units with the first bite of dairy. Pancrelipase is prescribed in lipase units per meal and titrated.
- **Who it is for:** Adults with hydrogen-breath-test-confirmed lactose maldigestion, and patients with objectively confirmed exocrine pancreatic insufficiency.

**Pros**

- These are real enzymes replacing real deficiencies. Pancreatic enzyme replacement raises the coefficient of fat absorption from 63.1% to 83.7% in chronic pancreatitis, which is an objective physiological measurement rather than a symptom score.
- Lactase lets people keep dairy in the diet rather than eliminating a whole food group.
- Pancrelipase is under-prescribed rather than over-sold, which is the opposite of the pattern in this document.

**Cons**

- Generic digestive enzyme complexes treat a condition that does not exist. There is no recognised syndrome of generalised enzyme insufficiency in healthy adults, and the pancreas has roughly 90% functional reserve before malabsorption appears.
- Using enzyme blends to self-treat undiagnosed persistent gut symptoms delays the diagnosis of coeliac disease, inflammatory bowel disease, pancreatic disease and colorectal cancer. That is the main harm.
- Consumer enzyme products are dosed in non-comparable proprietary activity units by design, so cross-product comparison is impossible.
- Pancrelipase is porcine-derived, which matters for observant Muslim and Jewish patients and for vegetarians, and cystic fibrosis patients must not exceed the weight-based ceiling because of fibrosing colonopathy risk.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Gut enzymes that split milk sugar and break down fat, protein and starch.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** Lactase-phlorizin hydrolase sits in the small intestinal brush border and cleaves lactose into glucose and galactose; whatever escapes digestion reaches the colon, where bacteria ferment it into hydrogen, methane and short-chain fatty acids, producing the familiar symptoms. The exocrine pancreas secretes lipase and colipase, amylase, and the proteases trypsinogen and chymotrypsinogen, and lipase is the critical one because no other organ can substitute for it. The gland holds a large functional reserve: in the classic secretion studies, steatorrhoea did not appear until lipase output had fallen to 10% of normal or below, and creatorrhoea only below 10% of normal trypsin, which is why malabsorption in chronic pancreatitis declares itself late. The decline of intestinal lactase after weaning is the normal human developmental programme; retaining it into adulthood (lactase persistence) is the derived condition, driven chiefly by the LCT -13910C>T variant upstream of the lactase gene and selected for in dairying populations.
- **Deficiency disease:** Exocrine pancreatic insufficiency is the genuine clinical deficiency in this pairing. Congenital lactase deficiency is a separate, rare autosomal recessive disorder presenting in the newborn. Adult lactase non-persistence is NOT a deficiency disease: it is the ancestral and worldwide-majority human phenotype.
- **First signs:** Exocrine pancreatic insufficiency starts with bloating, flatulence, loose pale stools and unintended weight loss despite preserved appetite; a low faecal elastase-1 and falling fat-soluble vitamin levels (A, D, E, K) usually precede visible steatorrhoea by a long interval. Lactase non-persistence, being a physiological trait rather than a disease, shows only as dose-dependent bloating, cramps, audible bowel sounds and osmotic diarrhoea beginning 30 minutes to 2 hours after a lactose load, with most affected adults tolerating around 12 g of lactose (one glass of milk) without trouble.
- **Untreated:** Untreated exocrine pancreatic insufficiency causes overt steatorrhoea, progressive weight loss and sarcopenia, fat-soluble vitamin deficiency with osteopenia and osteoporosis, and in cystic fibrosis, failure to thrive in infancy; excess mortality in chronic pancreatitis is concentrated in those with untreated malabsorption. Lactase non-persistence has no severe clinical endpoint whatsoever. The only downstream risk is a calcium and vitamin D shortfall in people who eliminate dairy entirely without substituting an alternative source.
- **Does it reverse:** Pancreatic enzyme replacement restores fat and protein absorption, reverses weight loss and corrects fat-soluble vitamin levels while it is being taken, but destroyed acinar tissue does not regenerate, so treatment is lifelong and bone density already lost recovers only in part. Lactase non-persistence is permanent because it is the normal genetic programme rather than a lesion; symptoms resolve completely with lactose restriction, fermented dairy or exogenous lactase, and the underlying trait is not something to correct.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Exocrine pancreatic insufficiency: people with chronic pancreatitis (as many as 8 in 10 eventually), cystic fibrosis (the great majority, most within the first year of life), pancreatic cancer, and those after pancreatic resection or major gastric surgery. Lactase non-persistence: the global adult majority, with the highest frequencies in East and Southeast Asian, sub-Saharan African, Indigenous American and Middle Eastern populations, and the lowest in northern and northwestern Europeans and their descendants.
- **How common:** Roughly two-thirds of the world's adult population is lactase non-persistent, which is the normal phenotype rather than a disease prevalence (PMID 32899182). Exocrine pancreatic insufficiency prevalence is condition-dependent: up to about 80% of adults with chronic pancreatitis and close to 9 in 10 infants with cystic fibrosis within the first year.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** None
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Usually

This item bundles two manufactured preparations with different answers, and the single SYNTHETIC code fits the products rather than the activities. Lactase: graded on the product it is SYNTHETIC, a microbial beta-galactosidase produced by fermentation of Aspergillus oryzae, A. niger or the yeast Kluyveromyces lactis and then purified, and the mammalian brush-border lactase it substitutes for is a membrane enzyme of the small intestinal epithelium present in no food; graded on the enzyme activity it is TRACE, because live yoghurt and kefir cultures carry microbial beta-galactosidase into the gut, which is why lactase-non-persistent people tolerate fermented dairy better than milk. Pancreatic enzyme replacement: NOT_FOOD. Pancreatin and pancrelipase are extracts of porcine pancreas glands, and the source review states that the preparations with demonstrated efficacy are porcine-derived (de la Iglesia et al., eClinicalMedicine 2026;94:103880, PMID 42011215). The nearest food is pancreas eaten as offal (sweetbread), which is not a realistic route.

- **To match the dose:** Pancreatic enzymes: absolute. Recommended dosing is a minimum of 40,000-50,000 units of lipase with main meals and 20,000-25,000 with snacks, taken with every meal for life (PMID 42011215). No food supplies active lipase in those quantities. Raw pancreas is the only tissue that contains the enzymes at all, it is not eaten raw in any normal diet, cooking denatures them, the delivered activity would be unmeasurable and uncontrolled, and without enteric protection gastric acid would destroy most of what survived. Lactase: there is one genuine partial route, since live yoghurt and kefir carry microbial beta-galactosidase into the gut, but that only handles the lactose in the fermented product itself and does not give a dose-adjustable enzyme to take with an arbitrary lactose-containing meal, which is the point of a lactase capsule. No peer-reviewed per-100 g beta-galactosidase activity figure for retail yoghurt was retrieved here, so no number is given; that is a sourcing gap in this dataset rather than an absence in the literature.
- **Food vs supplement:** For pancreatin the caveats are pharmaceutical rather than dietary. Dose must be titrated to steatorrhoea, meal fat content and disease severity; the enzymes must be enteric-coated to survive gastric acid; the capsules are taken during the meal rather than before or after; and the porcine origin is a real issue for vegetarian, vegan, Muslim and Jewish patients and should be disclosed. Nothing sold as a plant-based or vegan pancreatic enzyme has demonstrated equivalence for pancreatic exocrine insufficiency, and treating a diagnosed insufficiency with a supermarket digestive enzyme blend risks continued fat malabsorption, weight loss and fat-soluble vitamin deficiency. For lactase the honest framing is that this is a targeted, cheap, low-risk aid rather than a nutrient: the alternative food strategy of hard cheese, live yoghurt and small lactose doses spread through the day works for many people, and lactase capsules matter mainly when the meal is unavoidable and the lactose dose is large.
- **Absorption:** Both are enzymes, so bioavailability means surviving intact and active to the site of action rather than being absorbed. Lactase works in the lumen of the upper small intestine and is itself digested, so timing relative to the lactose load determines the effect. Pancreatic lipase is irreversibly denatured below about pH 4, which is why uncoated pancreatin is largely wasted, why the field has moved to pH-sensitive enteric microspheres, and why an emerging approach adds bicarbonate to raise local pH around the pancreatin. Coadministered acid suppression is sometimes needed to make the enzymes work at all. None of this can be replicated by eating anything.
- **Does the body make it:** Both enzymes are made by the body, and both have named states in which that synthesis stops being sufficient, which is what makes them genuine replacement therapy. Lactase is made by small-intestinal enterocytes and its decline after weaning is the normal human pattern, with adult retention the derived trait set by regulatory variants upstream of the LCT gene; pancreatic enzymes are made by acinar cells, and replacement exists precisely because that synthesis fails in pancreatic disease. So these products are correct in the person with the named condition and pointless in everyone else.
- **Pathway:** Lactase-phlorizin hydrolase (LCT) synthesised by enterocytes of the jejunal brush border; adult expression is controlled by MCM6 enhancer variants: -13910C>T in Europeans, -13915T>G in Arabian populations, and -13907C>G and -14010G>C in African populations, so genotyping -13910C>T alone misclassifies most lactase-persistent Africans and Middle Easterners. Pancreatic lipase, colipase, amylase, trypsinogen and chymotrypsinogen are synthesised by pancreatic acinar cells and secreted into the duodenum under CCK and secretin control.
- **Amount made:** Pancreatic lipase secreted in response to a test meal in healthy volunteers: 253.5 +/- 95.5 mg (liquid meal) and 202.9 +/- 96.1 mg (solid meal), against 21.6 +/- 14.5 mg and 15.2 +/- 5.1 mg respectively for gastric lipase (PMID 11040182). Lactase: NOT ESTABLISHED as a daily amount.
- **Still matters when:** Lactase tablets in lactase non-persistence, which is the majority phenotype worldwide. Pancreatic enzyme replacement in exocrine pancreatic insufficiency, where steatorrhoea appears only once enzyme secretion falls below roughly 10 percent of normal, with AGA advising at least 40,000 USP units of lipase with each meal, half that with snacks, and noting that doses above 120,000 units per meal are seldom needed. For a lactase-persistent person with a normal pancreas, NEVER ON THIS AXIS.

*Refs. de la Iglesia-Garcia PMID 27941156; Gan PMID 29212278; de Vrese PMID 25042846; Munasinghe PMID 20204691*

## 5. Skip it

No good evidence for the marketed claim. Where a genuine narrow signal exists it is listed under Pros, so you can see how thin it is.

### Multivitamin / multimineral

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** One tablet daily. There is no established effective dose because there is no established clinical effect in replete adults.
- **Who it is for:** Genuine deficiency or high-probability deficiency: post-bariatric, malabsorption, strict vegans, long-term metformin users, heavy alcohol use, the housebound.

**Pros**

- For someone with a genuine deficiency it is a cheap way to cover several nutrients at once, and that is a real use.
- Doses are usually at reference-intake level, so a plain multivitamin rarely pushes any single nutrient near its upper limit on its own.
- COSMOS-Web and COSMOS-Mind reported a memory signal, which is the only positive finding in this entry and is weak.

**Cons**

- It does not prevent cardiovascular disease, cancer or death. Physicians Health Study II (n=14,641, 11.2 years): major cardiovascular events HR 1.01 (0.91-1.10).
- The largest dataset points the wrong way. Pooled US cohorts (n=390,124, up to 27 years, 164,762 deaths): daily multivitamin users had HIGHER all-cause mortality, HR 1.04 (1.02-1.07). This is observational and probably reflects sick people starting supplements, but it is not the direction the marketing implies.
- The USPSTF gives it an I statement, meaning the evidence is insufficient to weigh benefits against harms. The industry reports this as vindication, which it is not.
- The nutrient-gap argument has never converted into a clinical outcome in a healthy replete adult, and this is a roughly 8 billion US dollar per year category resting on it.
- Vitamin K content antagonises warfarin, so switching brands or starting and stopping the product shifts INR. Treat it as a dosing variable.
- Risk comes from stacking. A multivitamin plus a separate high-dose single nutrient is how people cross an upper limit without noticing.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A mixed low-dose top-up of many nutrients at once; it has no function of its own.

- **If you lack it:** Mixed product
- **In detail:** A multivitamin/multimineral is a product category rather than a substance: a fixed combination typically supplying somewhere around the RDA for ten to thirty vitamins and minerals in a single daily dose. It has no physiology of its own, and whatever it does in the body is simply the sum of the individual nutrients, each acting through its own pathway. Formulations differ enormously in which nutrients are included, in what chemical form (iron as sulphate or bisglycinate, folate as folic acid or 5-MTHF, vitamin D as D2 or D3), and at what fraction or multiple of the RDA, so trial evidence about one product transfers poorly to another. Its coherent use is as insurance against gaps in an inadequate diet. A diagnosed deficiency is treated with the specific nutrient at a therapeutic dose, which a multivitamin does not supply.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** n/a

This is a product category rather than a molecule, and almost every nutrient inside it is abundant in ordinary food, which is why the US Preventive Services Task Force concluded in 2022 that the evidence is insufficient to recommend multivitamins for preventing cardiovascular disease or cancer, and recommended against beta-carotene and vitamin E supplements outright. The nutrients are food-abundant while the pill itself is manufactured: folic acid is not a natural food form, and cyanocobalamin occurs in food only in traces, so the accurate statement is that neither is the predominant natural form, and both are present in fortified foods, which this entry treats as a legitimate route for B12. Shortfalls that are real in populations are a short list: the 2020-2025 Dietary Guidelines for Americans name calcium, potassium, vitamin D and dietary fibre as nutrients of public health concern, with iron and folate added in pregnancy, and UK survey data adds magnesium, selenium and iron in younger women, though the specific NDNS below-LRNI percentages carried in the original submission (magnesium 9% of men and 13% of women, selenium 25.8% of males and 50.3% of females, iron about a quarter of younger women) could not be traced to a named survey round, age band or table and are indicative and unconfirmed here.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |

| Clams, mixed species, cooked, moist heat (vitamin B12) | 98.89 ug B12 | 3 oz (85 g) | 84.1 ug | 3,504% of the 2.4 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171975 |
| Beef liver, braised (B12, folate, haem iron) | 70.58 ug B12; 253 ug folate; 6.54 mg iron | 1 slice (68 g) | 48.0 ug B12; 172 ug folate; 4.4 mg iron | 2,000% of the B12 RDA; 43% of the 400 ug folate target; 24% of the 18 mg iron RDA for premenopausal women | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168626, including the 68 g slice. Retinol content is 9,442 ug RAE/100 g, so liver is unsuitable in pregnancy and should not be eaten more than about weekly |
| Herring, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat (vitamin B12 and vitamin D) | 13.14 ug B12; 5.4 ug vitamin D | 1 fillet (143 g) | 18.8 ug B12; 7.7 ug vitamin D | 783% of the 2.4 ug B12 RDA; 51% of the 15 ug vitamin D RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175117, including the 143 g fillet |
| Haddock, grilled (iodine) | 421 ug iodine | 1 fillet (120 g) | 505 ug | 361% of the 140 ug UK RNI (240% of the 150 ug US RDA) | CoFID 16-376. Added on verification: much richer than the cod used in the original entry and omitted from it. The 120 g fillet is a conventional weight rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Oysters, eastern, wild, cooked, moist heat (zinc) | 78.6 mg zinc | 6 medium (42 g) | 33.0 mg | 300% of the 11 mg RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171980, including the '6 medium = 42 g' portion |
| Cod, flesh only, baked (iodine) | 161 ug iodine | 1 fillet (180 g) | 290 ug | 207% of the 140 ug UK RNI (193% of the 150 ug US RDA) | Iodine from CoFID 16-373; fillet weight from USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171956 (1 fillet = 180 g). CoFID cod entries span 110-276 ug/100 g by cooking method, so any single figure is approximate. Percentage recomputed against the 140 ug UK RNI, since the composition is UK data |
| Crimini / brown mushrooms exposed to ultraviolet light, raw (vitamin D2) | 31.9 ug vitamin D | 1 cup diced (86 g) | 27.4 ug | 183% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168557. Added on verification: outranks the portabella chosen in the original entry. The 86 g diced cup is USDA's portabella portion weight applied here for comparability, so the per-portion figure is indicative. Only UV-treated mushrooms carry this, and the pack has to say so |
| Brazil nuts, dried, unblanched (selenium) | 1,917 ug selenium | 1 kernel (5 g) | 96 ug | 174% of the 55 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170569, including the '1 kernel = 5 g' portion. This is a mean, and it is the most soil-dependent number in food composition: nuts from selenium-poor Amazonian soils run an order of magnitude lower and nuts from selenium-rich soils higher, so no individual nut can be assumed to match it. Six kernels (28.35 g) at the USDA mean give 544 ug, above the 400 ug/day US upper limit, so the sensible instruction is one or two nuts rather than a handful |
| Portabella mushrooms exposed to ultraviolet light, raw (vitamin D2) | 28.4 ug vitamin D (1,135 IU) | 1 cup diced (86 g) | 24.4 ug | 163% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170143, including the 86 g diced cup. UV white mushrooms are 26.2 ug/100 g (FDC 169376). Only UV-treated mushrooms carry this; ordinary shop mushrooms have almost none, and the pack has to say so |
| Mackerel, salted (vitamin D) | 25.2 ug vitamin D | 3 oz (85 g) | 21.4 ug | 143% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168149. Added on verification. The 85 g weight is the standard 3 oz serving rather than a USDA portion-table entry, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Salmon, sockeye, canned, drained solids (vitamin D) | 21.0 ug vitamin D | 3 oz (85 g) | 17.9 ug | 119% of the 15 ug RDA; 179% of the 10 ug UK RNI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173693. Added on verification: this row alone disproves the original claim that UV mushrooms are the only food reaching 15 ug in one portion |
| Rainbow trout, farmed, cooked, dry heat (vitamin D) | 19.0 ug vitamin D | 3 oz (85 g) | 16.2 ug | 108% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173718. Added on verification |
| Salmon, sockeye, cooked, dry heat (vitamin D) | 16.7 ug vitamin D (670 IU) | 3 oz fillet portion (85 g); USDA also lists 0.5 fillet = 155 g | 14.2 ug per 3 oz; 25.9 ug per half fillet | 95% of the 15 ug US RDA per 3 oz, 173% per half fillet; 142% of the 10 ug UK RNI per 3 oz | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173692. The half-fillet portion is USDA's own and clears the RDA on its own, which is why the 'unbridgeable gap' framing was dropped |
| Crab, brown meat (iodine) | 333 ug iodine | 40 g, the brown meat from a dressed crab | 133 ug | 95% of the 140 ug UK RNI | CoFID 16-391. Added on verification. The 40 g portion is a conventional weight rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Lentils, mature seeds, boiled without salt (folate) | 181 ug folate | 1 cup cooked (198 g) | 358 ug | 90% of the 400 ug/day target | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172421 (total folate and DFE both 181 ug). Fortified breakfast cereals, chickpeas, asparagus and edamame are also substantial folate sources; verification named them as omissions but supplied no figures, so they are named here rather than tabulated |
| Cod liver oil (vitamin D) | 250 ug vitamin D | 1 tsp | 11.3 ug | 75% of the 15 ug RDA; 113% of the 10 ug UK RNI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173577. Added on verification: the most concentrated vitamin D entry in the whole USDA table and the historical answer to winter deficiency. Retinol content is high, so the same teaspoon carries a preformed vitamin A load that counts toward the 3,000 ug/day upper limit |
| Spinach, boiled, drained (good for folate, the wrong answer for iron) | 146 ug folate; 3.57 mg iron | 1 cup cooked (180 g) | 263 ug folate; 6.4 mg iron on the label | 66% of the 400 ug folate target; 36% of the 18 mg iron RDA nominally, of which roughly 1-2% is absorbed | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168463. The folate figure was added on verification, which noted the original entry used this row solely as an iron warning while ignoring a real folate contribution. The iron warning stands: non-haem iron complexed with oxalate is among the least absorbable iron in the food supply, and spinach should not be offered as an iron food |
| Milk, whole, 3.25% fat, with added vitamin D (iodine and vitamin D) | 31 ug iodine; 1.3 ug vitamin D | 1 cup / 240 ml (244 g) | 76 ug iodine; 3.2 ug vitamin D | 54% of the 140 ug UK iodine RNI; 21% of the 15 ug vitamin D RDA | Iodine from CoFID 12-596 (whole milk, pasteurised, average); vitamin D from USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171265, with the portion weight taken from the same USDA entry. Iodine percentage recomputed against the 140 ug UK RNI in place of the 150 ug figure the original mislabelled as an RNI |
| Fat spread, fortified (vitamin D) | 17.5 ug vitamin D | 1 tbsp (14 g) | 2.5 ug | 16% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171415. Added on verification as the main UK non-fish vitamin D vehicle. The 14 g tablespoon is a conventional weight, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Egg, whole, hard-boiled (vitamin D) | 2.2 ug vitamin D | 1 large egg (50 g) | 1.1 ug | 7% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173424. Eggs are also one of the four main iodine sources the NHS lists |
| Kombu (dried kelp), shown as a hazard rather than a source (iodine) | 440,670 ug iodine | 1 g of dried kombu | 4,407 ug | 3,148% of the 140 ug UK RNI from a single gram, a 31.5-fold overshoot | CoFID 13-339, the highest iodine value in the whole CoFID dataset. One gram clears the EFSA adult upper level of 600 ug/day several times over (that upper level matches the standard SCF 2002 figure but could not be confirmed from the source text during verification). Wakame is 16,830 ug/100 g and nori 1,470 ug/100 g (CoFID 13-341 and 13-340), a 300-fold spread across three seaweeds sold in the same shop, and kelp-induced thyroid dysfunction is documented. Kelp is never a sensible iodine recommendation, which is why it is placed last despite the largest number on the list |

- **To match the dose:** There is no single food equivalent of a multivitamin, because a multivitamin is not a nutrient: one tablet supplies roughly 100% of the reference intake for 20 or more nutrients at once, and the only thing matching that profile is a varied diet, which by definition already supplies them. So the answer has to be given per nutrient, and it splits. For most of the tablet (vitamin C, vitamin A, thiamine, riboflavin, niacin, B6, vitamin E, vitamin K, zinc, selenium, copper, iodine) the food gap for anyone eating a normal mixed diet is zero. Vitamin D is reachable but rarely reached: the original claim that UV-treated mushrooms are the only food to hit 15 ug in one portion is arithmetically false, because 3 oz of canned sockeye salmon gives 17.9 ug (119% of the 15 ug RDA), 3 oz of farmed rainbow trout 16.2 ug (108%), a USDA half-fillet (155 g) of cooked sockeye 25.9 ug (173%), and a teaspoon of cod liver oil 11.3 ug (75%), alongside a cup of UV portabella at 24.4 ug. Reaching 15 ug/day from food needs about 90 g of cooked sockeye every day, or 1.15 litres of fortified milk, or 14 large eggs; the UK 10 ug RNI needs about 60 g of oily fish daily or 770 ml of fortified milk. So the vitamin D case for supplementing is behavioural (few people at temperate latitudes eat oily fish daily through winter) rather than compositional, and it argues for vitamin D alone rather than a 25-ingredient tablet. Periconceptional folic acid is a genuine exception: 400 ug/day is food-achievable in bulk (about 220 g of cooked lentils daily) but the trial evidence for preventing neural tube defects is for supplemental folic acid, and food folate is both less bioavailable and more variable, so food substitution is not supported. B12 on a vegan diet is the second real exception, since there is no reliable unfortified plant source, so a supplement or fortified food is mandatory. Two mismatches run the other way: the potassium in a multivitamin is capped at about 99 mg against a 3,500 mg reference intake, and the calcium is typically 100-200 mg, so the tablet cannot address either of the shortfalls the guidelines name, and fibre, the fourth named shortfall, is absent from every tablet. Iodised salt, the global public-health iodine vehicle, belongs in this list as well; verification named it as a complete omission but supplied no composition figure, so no row is given for it.
- **Food vs supplement:** This category contains the clearest documented case of a whole food being safe while the isolated concentrate is harmful. Carotenoid-rich vegetables are consistently associated with lower lung cancer risk, while beta-carotene as a supplement increased lung cancer incidence in smokers and asbestos-exposed workers in the ATBC and CARET trials, and the USPSTF now recommends against it with moderate certainty that harms exceed benefits. Vitamin E supplements carry the same recommendation against. Preformed vitamin A as retinol in a tablet counts toward the 3,000 ug/day upper limit and is teratogenic at high intake, while beta-carotene in carrots does not convert quickly enough to reach toxicity. Two food rows on this list carry the same warning as the tablet: beef liver at 9,442 ug RAE/100 g and cod liver oil are both concentrated retinol sources, so neither is a free lunch. Folic acid in a tablet can partially mask the haematological signs of B12 deficiency while neurological damage continues, an effect food folate has not been shown to produce. Stacking is the underappreciated risk: a multivitamin alongside fortified breakfast cereal and a fortified plant drink can push folic acid, retinol, niacin and iron past their upper limits in someone who believes they are taking one tablet. Within the tablet itself, iron competes with zinc and copper for shared transporters and calcium interferes with iron uptake, so a combined product delivers less of each than the label states. And no tablet carries the fibre, the food-level potassium, or the polyphenols that account for much of the association between diet quality and outcomes, which is the structural reason a pill is not a diet.
- **Absorption:** Form differences run in both directions, so blanket claims fail here. Iron: haem iron from meat and fish absorbs at roughly 15-35% and is largely insensitive to the rest of the meal, while non-haem iron from plants and from the ferrous salts in tablets ranges from about 2% to 20% depending on phytate, polyphenols, calcium and vitamin C eaten at the same time. Spinach iron sits at the bottom of that range because oxalate binds it, so the textbook answer is the wrong answer. Folate: folic acid from a supplement taken with food is roughly 85% bioavailable against about 50% for naturally occurring food folate, so here the synthetic form genuinely outperforms the food. Vitamin B12: food B12 is protein-bound and needs gastric acid and pepsin to release it, so older adults with atrophic gastritis and long-term proton pump inhibitor or metformin users often absorb crystalline B12 from a tablet better than B12 from a chop. Vitamin D: the D2 in UV-treated mushrooms raises and sustains serum 25(OH)D less efficiently than the D3 in oily fish and most supplements, so 24.4 ug from a cup of portabella is not equivalent to 24.4 ug from salmon, and the mushroom rows should not be read as interchangeable with the fish rows despite ranking near them. Vitamin K: the K1 in leafy greens is poorly released from the plant matrix and cleared fast, while the long-chain MK-7 in natto reaches higher and far longer-lasting serum concentrations, so a portion of natto and a portion of kale are not interchangeable despite sharing the label term. All four fat-soluble vitamins need fat in the same meal, which is why a multivitamin swallowed with black coffee delivers less than the panel claims.
- **Does the body make it:** This is a product category rather than a defined substance, so the axis cannot return one biochemical answer for it and the roll-up is NA. The parts do have answers: every mineral element in the bottle is NONE without exception, because no biological process transmutes elements; vitamin C, B12, folate, thiamine, riboflavin, B6, biotin, pantothenate, vitamin E and preformed vitamin A are NONE because no human biosynthetic pathway exists for any of them; vitamin D3 is PARTIAL through UVB-dependent cutaneous synthesis, vitamin K is PARTIAL through UBIAD1 conversion of dietary K1 to MK-4, and niacin is PARTIAL through the tryptophan route at 60 mg tryptophan per 1 mg niacin equivalent. A multivitamin therefore has a real target for whichever components a given person is short of and no target for the rest, so it has to be judged component by component against the doses on the label.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE as a product category. Component pathways belong in their own rows: for every element human enzymology handles uptake, transport, storage and excretion only, and for the vitamins with no human pathway it handles absorption, activation and transport only. The two components with a genuine human route are vitamin D3 (non-enzymatic UVB photolysis of 7-dehydrocholesterol, then CYP2R1 and CYP27B1) and vitamin K (UBIAD1 prenylation of menadione to MK-4).
- **Amount made:** NOT APPLICABLE for the product as a whole. Component reference points recovered in this dataset: vitamin C RDA 90 mg/day men and 75 mg/day women with UL 2,000 mg/day; folate RDA 400 µg DFE/d; vitamin B12 RDA 2.4 µg/d; biotin AI 30 µg/d; vitamin K AI 120 micrograms/day men and 90 micrograms/day women with no UL; vitamin D RDA 600 IU (15 micrograms)/day for ages 1 to 70 and 800 IU/day above 70 with UL 4,000 IU/day; niacin RDA 16 mg NE/day men and 14 mg NE/day women with UL 35 mg/day for supplemental forms; zinc 11 mg/day men and 8 mg/day women; magnesium 400 to 420 mg/day men and 310 to 320 mg/day women; copper RDA 900 µg/d with UL 10 mg/d.
- **Still matters when:** Where a named component has a documented shortfall or a defined loss: preconception and pregnancy (folic acid 400 micrograms/day), vegan diets and absorptive failure (B12), low UVB exposure (vitamin D), malabsorption, bariatric surgery, dialysis, restrictive diets and refeeding. Above requirement the direction reverses for several components, since zinc over 40 mg a day induces copper deficiency, selenium over 400 micrograms a day is toxic, iron is harmful in men who are not iron deficient, and preformed vitamin A carries teratogenic risk in pregnancy.
