*Refs. USPSTF PMID 35727271; O Connor PMID 35727272; Loftfield PMID 38922615; PHS II*

### Collagen peptides

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 2.5-10 g/d of hydrolysed collagen for skin trials. UC-II is a different molecule dosed at about 40 mg/d and is not interchangeable.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody, on the best available analysis.

**Pros**

- There is a weak, moderate-certainty signal for knee osteoarthritis pain: SMD -0.58 (-0.98 to -0.18) across 4 trials and 507 patients. All included trials had high risk of bias.
- Safety is benign. The problem with collagen is efficacy, not harm.

**Cons**

- This is the cleanest documented case of funding bias in the supplement literature. Myung and Park pooled 23 trials and 1,474 participants. Headline: collagen improved skin hydration, elasticity and wrinkles. Subgroup analysis: studies WITHOUT pharmaceutical-company funding showed no effect on any of the three, and high-quality studies showed no effect. The effect exists only in industry-funded, lower-quality trials.
- Collagen is a poor-quality protein. It lacks tryptophan and is a poor source of essential amino acids, so it displaces better protein in the diet.
- Marine collagen in fish allergy; bovine sourcing matters for religious dietary restriction and BSE-region concerns.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Hydrolysed animal collagen eaten as protein; the body builds its own collagen from ordinary amino acids.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Collagen is the most abundant protein in the body, roughly a third of total body protein, forming the tensile framework of skin, tendon, ligament, bone matrix, cartilage and vessel wall. The body synthesises it from ordinary amino acids as a repeating Gly-X-Y sequence rich in proline; the post-translational hydroxylation that stabilises the triple helix is carried out by prolyl and lysyl hydroxylases, which require vitamin C, iron and 2-oxoglutarate. Ingested collagen is digested like any other protein, though some di- and tripeptides such as prolyl-hydroxyproline do reach the bloodstream intact, which is the proposed mechanism for any effect beyond amino acid supply. As a protein source collagen is nutritionally incomplete: it contains no tryptophan and is low in several other indispensable amino acids.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Body makes it

RICH was challenged and upheld, and this is the one entry whose trial dose is genuinely reachable by eating, because the supplement is simply hydrolysed collagen and several ordinary foods are almost pure collagen. Gelatine is thermally hydrolysed collagen at 85.6 g protein per 100 g with tryptophan recorded as 0.000 g. Plain pork crackling is 61.3 g protein per 100 g with an unmistakable collagen amino-acid signature (glycine 11.9 g, proline 7.3 g per 100 g). Trial doses are 2.5 to 15 g/day over 3 to 18 months (Paul, Leser & Oesser 2019, confirmed). Chicken skin, beef tendon, chicken feet and tinned fish eaten with skin and soft bones all add collagen; chicken feet and cartilage now have published GAG and collagen assays, but none of those sources yields a per-100 g collagen figure I can quote here, so they are named without numbers. Bone broth is the popular food answer and the unreliable one.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Pork skins, plain (pork scratchings / crackling / chicharron) | 61.3 g protein, of which the amino-acid profile indicates roughly 53-55 g is collagen-type protein (glycine 11.9 g and proline 7.3 g per 100 g; glycine is 19.4% of protein mass here against 22.2% in USDA's own gelatine entry); tryptophan only 0.12 g | 1 oz bag (28 g) | about 17 g protein, of which about 15 g is collagen-type protein | about 150% of a 10 g collagen dose; comparable to the 15 g top-end trial dose | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy 'Snacks, pork skins, plain'. Confirmed to the decimal: USDA per 28.35 g gives protein 17 g, glycine 3.378 g, proline 2.059 g, tryptophan 0.033 g, which scale to 11.9, 7.3 and 0.116 g per 100 g |
| Gelatine, dry powder, unsweetened (leaf or powdered gelatine) | 85.6 g protein, effectively all collagen hydrolysate; glycine about 19.0 g (22.2% of protein mass); proline about 12.3 g; tryptophan 0.000 g | 1 sachet/envelope (7 g) | about 6.0 g collagen protein | 60% of a 10 g collagen-peptide dose; 240% of the 2.5 g low-end trial dose | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy 'Gelatins, dry powder, unsweetened' (protein 85.6 g/100 g, corrected from the submission's 85.7; tryptophan 0.000 g). Collagen identity: gelatine is partially hydrolysed collagen, see Paul et al. 2019, Nutrients 11(5):1079, PMID 31096622 |
| Aspic or gelatine-set jelly (made at 1 sachet per 500 mL) | about 1.2 g collagen protein | 1 bowl (150 g) | about 1.8 g collagen protein | about 18% of a 10 g dose; about 72% of the 2.5 g low-end trial dose | Derived arithmetic, not a measured composition value: the gelatine row above (85.6 g protein/100 g) at a stated recipe ratio of one 7 g sachet per 500 mL. Scale it to whatever ratio you actually use |

- **To match the dose:** There is essentially no gap. One 7 g sachet of ordinary supermarket gelatine delivers about 6.0 g of collagen protein, which is 60 per cent of a 10 g dose and 240 per cent of the 2.5 g low-end trial dose; two sachets sit squarely inside the 2.5 to 15 g/day range used in the research. A 28 g bag of plain pork crackling delivers about 15 g of collagen-type protein, matching the top-end trial dose at a fraction of the price of branded peptides. Bone broth is the one route that fails: amino acid content of broths made to a standardised recipe was significantly lower than a 20 g reference collagen dose for hydroxyproline, glycine and proline, and non-standardised preparations varied enormously, with commercially available broths lower than self-prepared ones (Alcock, Shaw & Burke 2019). If you want a defined collagen dose from food, weigh out gelatine rather than simmering bones.
- **Food vs supplement:** Moderate, and worth stating precisely. Collagen contains no tryptophan at all (USDA records gelatine tryptophan as 0.000 g/100 g) and is low in cysteine, methionine and histidine, so it is an incomplete protein with a PDCAAS effectively at zero and must not be counted as general protein intake; modelling suggests up to about 36 per cent of total daily protein can be collagen before indispensable amino acid balance suffers (Paul et al. 2019, PMID 31096622, confirmed). Hydrolysis is a real difference: collagen peptides are enzymatically cut to 2-5 kDa fragments that dissolve in cold liquid and are absorbed partly as di- and tripeptides, whereas gelatine gels and must be dissolved hot, and native collagen in skin and tendon must first be cooked and digested. On the food side, pork crackling brings a large load of saturated fat and salt with its collagen, which a sachet of gelatine does not, so swapping crackling for collagen powder is a trade-off rather than a free win.
- **Absorption:** Genuinely different by form, and here the supplement has a defensible argument. Hydrolysed collagen peptides are absorbed rapidly and hydroxyproline-containing dipeptides such as prolyl-hydroxyproline appear intact in plasma, which is the mechanistic basis of the collagen-peptide literature; plasma amino acid comparisons of dairy against collagen protein confirm the distinctive glycine, proline and hydroxyproline signature of collagen ingestion. Gelatine, already partly hydrolysed, behaves similarly, and a 7 g sachet delivers 6.0 g of that same protein. Native collagen in skin, tendon and bone requires prolonged moist cooking to become digestible at all, which is exactly why bone broth quantities are unpredictable and why standardised broths fell significantly short of a 20 g reference collagen dose. Vitamin C is required for the hydroxylation step in endogenous collagen synthesis, so collagen intake without adequate vitamin C is pointless, and eating hydroxyproline does not bypass that requirement.
- **Does the body make it:** The roll-up is FULL: you build every collagen fibril in your body yourself, from glycine, proline and lysine, with vitamin C and copper as cofactors, and there is no dietary requirement for collagen as a molecule. Nothing you swallow is installed intact, since ingested collagen is digested to amino acids and short peptides before absorption, which makes oral collagen a weak premise as replacement therapy for a molecule you are not short of. The exception sits on the precursor side and carries a contested CONDITIONAL label: if the Melendez-Hevia calculation of a roughly 10 g/day glycine shortfall holds, precursor supply is conditional, and demand for glycine and proline rises in burns, major surgery and wound healing, the same structure that makes glutamine conditional. Two defensible mechanisms remain, supplying amino acids, where plain gelatine or glycine is cheaper, and signalling by absorbed Pro-Hyp and other hydroxyproline-containing dipeptides, which are measurable in plasma after a dose.
- **Pathway:** Fibroblasts, chondrocytes and osteoblasts assemble procollagen alpha chains at the rough ER from glycine (from serine via SHMT, which supplies more than 85 percent of glycine production), proline (from glutamate or ornithine) and lysine. Prolyl-4-hydroxylase (P4HA1-3), prolyl-3-hydroxylase (P3H1) and the lysyl hydroxylases (PLOD1-3) all require Fe2+, ascorbate and 2-oxoglutarate, and are followed by glycosylation, triple-helix assembly, secretion, propeptide cleavage and copper-dependent lysyl oxidase cross-linking. Ingested collagen is digested to amino acids and di- and tripeptides such as prolyl-hydroxyproline.
- **Amount made:** NOT ESTABLISHED for whole-body collagen synthesis in g/day; collagen is roughly a quarter to a third of total body protein and skin and bone turnover proceed continuously. The nearest quantified constraint concerns a precursor: Melendez-Hevia and colleagues calculate that synthesis supplies about 3 g/day of glycine in a 70 kg adult and a typical diet 1.5 to 3.0 g/day, against a total demand including collagen synthesis that leaves the roughly 10 g/day shortfall their paper reports.
- **Still matters when:** Vitamin C deficiency, where hydroxylation genuinely fails (scurvy, and humans depend on dietary ascorbate because GULO is a pseudogene); copper deficiency, where lysyl oxidase cross-linking fails; low total protein or a glycine-poor diet; wound healing, burns and major surgery, when demand for glycine and proline rises; and possibly as a cheap glycine and proline source if the Melendez-Hevia shortfall holds. What the axis rules out is the marketing premise that you are short of collagen itself. If a collagen peptide product is used, judge it on the specific hydrolysate and dose the trials used (2.5 to 10 g/day for skin and joint endpoints) and on how independent those trials were.

*Refs. Myung & Park Am J Med 2025 PMID 40324552; Lin PMID 37717022; PMID 33742704*

### Glucosamine and chondroitin

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** Glucosamine 1,500 mg/d plus chondroitin 1,200 mg/d, the GAIT regimen. Form matters and is routinely ignored.
- **Who it is for:** At most, people with moderate to severe knee osteoarthritis pain, on subgroup data only.

**Pros**

- In the GAIT moderate-to-severe pain stratum the combination beat placebo, 79.2% versus 54.3% response (P=0.002). This was pre-specified but underpowered, and the authors themselves cautioned against over-reading it.
- Cochrane found a short-term chondroitin benefit: 10% absolute pain improvement, NNT 5, at low to moderate quality.
- As safe as placebo in the trials.

**Cons**

- The definitive trial was null. GAIT was NIH-funded, n=1,583, 24 weeks, with a celecoxib active control, designed specifically to remove industry influence. Glucosamine +3.9 percentage points over placebo (P=0.30), chondroitin +5.3 (P=0.17), combination +6.5 (P=0.09). Celecoxib worked; the supplements did not.
- Cochrane found glucosamine benefit ONLY for the Rotta-brand crystalline glucosamine sulfate preparation. Pooled non-Rotta preparations failed to show any pain benefit. Almost nothing sold in shops is the Rotta preparation.
- The industry sells cartilage regeneration. No trial has shown cartilage regeneration.
- This is the best-documented supplement-anticoagulant interaction in this document: 20 FDA MedWatch reports linking glucosamine plus chondroitin to raised INR, bruising and bleeding on warfarin, plus 21 WHO database reports of raised INR.
- Shellfish-derived glucosamine matters in shellfish allergy.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Cartilage building blocks your own cells make from glucose, so eating them is not required.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Glucosamine is an amino sugar produced in every cell from fructose-6-phosphate by glutamine-fructose-6-phosphate amidotransferase, the entry step of the hexosamine biosynthetic pathway that supplies UDP-N-acetylglucosamine for glycosaminoglycans, proteoglycans and O-GlcNAc signalling. Chondroitin sulfate is a sulfated glycosaminoglycan chain assembled on proteoglycan core proteins in the Golgi. Both are made on demand from glucose and glutamine, so neither is a required dietary component and neither has a recommended intake. Orally administered chondroitin is a large polysaccharide with low and variable absorption, and oral glucosamine does not raise synovial fluid concentrations anywhere near those used in cartilage cell experiments. The real question is efficacy rather than nutritional need: the NIH GAIT trial found no significant benefit over placebo for knee osteoarthritis pain in the overall study population.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Mixed
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** Low
- **Body makes it:** Body makes it

The two compounds have different answers and must not share one label. Glucosamine is SYNTHETIC: no ordinary food contains free glucosamine, and glucosamine hydrochloride is manufactured by hydrolysing chitin from crustacean shell or Aspergillus niger fermentation biomass with food-grade concentrated hydrochloric acid at 100 C, then filtering, crystallising, washing and flash-drying. Shell and fungal mycelium are neither digestible nor eaten, so the feedstock is not a food route. Chondroitin is a different case, and the submission's SYNTHETIC label was wrong for it by its own admission that chondroitin sulfate is extracted from cartilage. Chondroitin sulfate in edible chicken keel cartilage has been quantified at 189 +/- 33 mg per gram of cartilage, and GAG content has been quantified in chicken feet and in traditionally stewed pig trotters. Chicken cartilage (yakitori nankotsu), chicken feet, pig trotters and shark fin are ordinary foods in Chinese, Japanese and Korean cuisine, so for chondroitin the honest answer is rich in a small set of real but culturally specific foods.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Chicken keel / soft cartilage (yakitori nankotsu) - CHONDROITIN only | 15.6-22.2 g chondroitin sulfate (189 +/- 33 mg per gram of cartilage) | 2 yakitori nankotsu skewers (about 30 g cartilage) | 4.7-6.7 g chondroitin sulfate as stated; about 1.4-2.3 g if the source figure is per gram of DRIED cartilage and the portion is fresh | 390-555% of a 1,200 mg chondroitin dose as stated; still 117-194% on the more conservative fresh-weight reading. Zero per cent of any glucosamine dose | Br Poult Sci 2014, chicken keel cartilage chondroitin sulfate extraction (189 +/- 33 mg CS per g cartilage), cited via the verification review rather than read directly. Indicative on one specific point: the moisture basis of the per-gram figure is not stated in the material available, which is why both readings are given. Related but non-quantifiable here: GAG content has been assayed in chicken feet (J Food Sci 2020) and in stewed pig trotters (Foods 2022), both expressed as mg GAG per gram of total collagen, which does not convert to a per-portion figure |

- **To match the dose:** Split by compound. Chondroitin: a 1,200 mg daily dose corresponds to roughly 5.4 to 7.7 g of chicken keel cartilage at 189 +/- 33 mg/g, about 6 g at the central value, so two yakitori nankotsu skewers already exceed it several times over. Even if that figure turns out to be per gram of dried cartilage, a 30 g fresh portion would still carry roughly 1.4 to 2.3 g, which is 117 to 194 per cent of the dose, so the conclusion holds on either moisture basis. Glucosamine: there is no food quantity at all, and the informative comparison is endogenous. Human endogenous glucosamine production is 4 to 20 g/day with a median around 14 g/day, so the standard GAIT-trial 1,500 mg adds about 10.7 per cent on top of the median, or 7.5 to 37.5 per cent across the range. Prawn and crab shells are the industrial feedstock but shell is indigestible and not eaten, so eating prawns delivers no useful glucosamine.
- **Food vs supplement:** Several that matter, and they differ by compound. Chemical form is not interchangeable in the trial literature: the European positive trials used a specific patented crystalline glucosamine SULFATE while the large NIH GAIT trial used glucosamine HYDROCHLORIDE, and the results differ, so a shelf product labelled simply 'glucosamine' is not evidence-matched to any particular result. Vegetarian or shellfish-free glucosamine from Aspergillus niger fermentation is the same manufactured molecule from a different feedstock, not a food-derived alternative; EFSA's opinion on the fungal route states that the process is the same as the shellfish process except for the raw material. Shellfish-derived glucosamine is a purified sugar rather than shellfish protein, so it is not the classic tropomyosin allergen route, though labels warn and cautious avoidance is reasonable. Chondroitin product content notoriously deviates from label claim because it is an extracted polydisperse polysaccharide from a bovine or shark by-product, which raises quality-control and sourcing questions. On the food side, cartilage-containing dishes carry chondroitin in a cooked matrix at a concentration that varies with cut and preparation, so a nankotsu skewer is a real chondroitin source rather than a measured dose.
- **Absorption:** Poor and first-pass-limited for glucosamine: oral glucosamine is largely metabolised on first pass and plasma concentrations reach only low micromolar, far below the millimolar concentrations at which glucosamine affects chondrocytes in cell culture, which is the central pharmacological criticism of the category. Absorbed glucosamine enters cells through glucose transporters, a process facilitated by insulin, and is phosphorylated to glucosamine-6-phosphate, the same intermediate the body already makes at 4 to 20 g/day (median about 14 g/day, EFSA ANS Panel 2013 opinion on (6S)-5-methyltetrahydrofolic acid, glucosamine salt; the frequently quoted 230 mg/kg body weight per day is a back-calculation from that median for a 60 kg adult, 14,000/60 = 233, not an EFSA-quoted value). Chondroitin sulfate is a large sulfated polysaccharide of tens of kDa and is absorbed mainly after depolymerisation into smaller fragments and constituent sugars, so what reaches the circulation is not the intact molecule from the capsule. Food-borne chondroitin faces the same depolymerisation step plus the need to liberate the glycosaminoglycan from a cooked cartilage matrix, so a cartilage portion that exceeds the dose on paper is not equivalent to a measured dose in effect.
- **Does the body make it:** Cartilage manufactures both of these on site from glucose and glutamine: glucosamine-6-phosphate comes off the hexosamine branch of glycolysis, and chondroitin sulfate chains are polymerised and sulfated inside the chondrocyte Golgi, so chondroitin sulfate is FULL in the ordinary sense and glucosamine is FULL by substrate sufficiency. The glucosamine label needs one qualification, since what the pathway makes is glucosamine-6-phosphate committed to UDP-GlcNAc and the free glucosamine measurable in fasting plasma is spillover plus lysosomal salvage, so nobody has established what an adequate quantity of free glucosamine would be; the sufficient and correct version is that the chondrocyte is never short of substrate. Neither is a nutrient, neither has a deficiency state, and absorbed material joins the whole-body amino sugar pool instead of being directed to a particular knee, which makes this the clearest case on the axis of a premise that fails at the substrate step.
- **Pathway:** Glucosamine: fructose-6-phosphate plus glutamine gives glucosamine-6-phosphate via GFAT (GFPT1/2), the rate-limiting step of the hexosamine biosynthetic pathway, then N-acetylation and UTP addition to UDP-GlcNAc; a second, GFAT-independent source is lysosomal salvage, where NAGK phosphorylates recovered GlcNAc straight back into the pathway. Chondroitin: UDP-glucose is oxidised by UGDH to UDP-glucuronate, and chondroitin sulfate chains are polymerised and sulfated inside the chondrocyte Golgi.
- **Amount made:** NOT ESTABLISHED as a g/day synthesis figure for either molecule. Sourced anchors instead: the hexosamine pathway diverts roughly 2 to 5 percent of glucose-derived fructose-6-phosphate to UDP-GlcNAc (PMC12847842), endogenous free glucosamine is present in fasting human plasma (PMID 16168682), whole-body hexosamine flux is on the order of grams per day, and the usual oral dose of 1500 mg/day reaches synovial fluid only at low micromolar levels.
- **Still matters when:** Very little on the substrate axis, since any real effect has to be pharmacological rather than nutritional. The largest independent trial (GAIT) found no benefit over placebo across the overall knee osteoarthritis population, with a signal for the glucosamine plus chondroitin combination in its prespecified moderate-to-severe pain subgroup that was exploratory and unconfirmed (PMID 16495392); GAIT used glucosamine hydrochloride while most positive European trials used crystalline glucosamine sulfate, which is the main explanation for the heterogeneity and the reason the two salts cannot be treated as equivalent. What remains defensible is a time-limited personal trial with a defined stopping point in a symptomatic individual, judged on 3 month symptom response, with attention to shellfish sourcing and to blood glucose at high doses.

*Refs. GAIT PMID 16495392; Cochrane chondroitin PMID 25629804; Cochrane glucosamine PMID 15846645*

### NMN (nicotinamide mononucleotide)

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 250-2,000 mg/d in trials, most commonly 250 mg/d for 12 weeks. No dose has improved a clinical outcome.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody demonstrably.

**Pros**

- It reliably raises blood NAD+ two to threefold, so the pill is absorbed and metabolised as claimed.
- One 60-person trial in older adults with impaired physical function reported maintained walking speed and improved subjective sleep at 250 mg/d for 12 weeks. Single small trial.

**Cons**

- This is the purest biomarker-for-outcome substitution in the supplement market. Raising NAD+ is not a health outcome.
- Two independent meta-analyses found nothing. Eight randomised trials in 342 people, and separately 12 studies in 513 people: no effect on fasting glucose, fasting insulin, HbA1c, HOMA-IR or any lipid fraction.
- A systematic review of 10 trials in 437 people found grip strength 29.9 kg before and 30.5 kg after, with skeletal muscle mass index unchanged. Non-significant.
- No contraindications are characterised because no adequately powered safety population exists. No upper limit has been set and EFSA has not authorised it as a novel food.
- Avoid in pregnancy, lactation and active malignancy. NAD+ is a substrate for tumour metabolism and no oncology safety data exist.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A building block cells use to make NAD+, the coenzyme that keeps energy metabolism running.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Nicotinamide mononucleotide is an intermediate in the NAD+ salvage pathway, formed when nicotinamide phosphoribosyltransferase attaches nicotinamide to phosphoribosyl pyrophosphate, then converted to NAD+ by NMN adenylyltransferases. NAD+ is the electron carrier for hundreds of dehydrogenases and the consumed substrate for sirtuins, PARPs and CD38. NMN is thought to be dephosphorylated to nicotinamide riboside before cellular uptake, though the human intestinal route is not established and the proposed direct NMN transporter is contested: Slc12a8-mediated uptake was reported in mouse small intestine (Grozio et al. 2019) and then disputed on methodological grounds (Shats et al. 2020), and no human transporter has been demonstrated in either direction. NMN itself is not an essential nutrient and has no recommended intake. The essential nutrient in this pathway is niacin (vitamin B3), whose deficiency causes pellagra, a real and severe disease that has nothing to do with NMN intake.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Trace
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Body makes it

NMN really is present in ordinary plant and animal foods. Mills et al. 2016 Table 1, confirmed row by row against the primary source, puts every food it measured between 0 and 1.88 mg/100 g, and a 2025 HPLC-DAD/LC-MS-MS plant survey spans 40 to 13,000 ug/100 g, richest in broccoli and green beans. The submission's framing of 'sub-milligram-per-100 g levels, roughly 100 to 1,000 times below a single capsule' is withdrawn as self-contradicting: at the 2025 survey ceiling of 13 mg/100 g one 100 g portion sits about 19 times below a 250 mg capsule, and it is not sub-milligram. TRACE still holds against a 250 to 1,000 mg dose. Cow milk is not a meaningful NMN source, since its NAD+ precursor pool is dominated by nicotinamide and nicotinamide riboside (Trammell et al. 2016). Supplement NMN is manufactured by microbial fermentation or enzymatic/chemical synthesis and is never extracted from broccoli or edamame.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Edamame (green soybeans), shelled, cooked from frozen | 0.47-1.88 mg NMN | 1 cup shelled (155 g) | 0.73-2.91 mg | 0.3-1.2% of a 250 mg dose; 0.07-0.3% of a 1,000 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, Cell Metab 24(6):795-806, PMID 28068222, DOI 10.1016/j.cmet.2016.09.013, Table 1 (value confirmed against the primary table); food identity USDA FDC 168411, ID not confirmed this session |
| Avocado, raw, flesh only | 0.36-1.60 mg NMN | 1 medium avocado, flesh (150 g) | 0.54-2.40 mg | 0.2-1.0% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (confirmed); food identity USDA FDC 171705, ID not confirmed this session |
| Broccoli, raw *[unconfirmed]* | 0.25-1.12 mg NMN (Mills, confirmed). The 2025 plant survey names broccoli one of its two richest NMN foods and reports a whole-survey ceiling of 13 mg/100 g, which if it belongs to broccoli would make this the richest row in the table | 1 cup chopped (91 g) | 0.23-1.02 mg on the confirmed Mills range; up to about 11.8 mg if the 2025 survey ceiling applies to broccoli | 0.09-0.4% of a 250 mg dose on Mills; up to 4.7% on the survey ceiling | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222 (confirmed). Upper figure from Alegre & Pastore 2025, CyTA J Food 23(1), DOI 10.1080/19476337.2025.2458753 |
| Green beans, cooked *[unconfirmed]* | 0.04-13 mg NMN (the whole-survey range, 40-13,000 ug/100 g). Green beans is named with broccoli as one of the two richest NMN plant foods, but no per-food figure is published in the accessible abstract | 1 cup (125 g) | 0.05-16.3 mg across the survey range | 0.02-6.5% of a 250 mg dose | Alegre & Pastore 2025, DOI 10.1080/19476337.2025.2458753 |
| Cabbage, raw | 0.0-0.90 mg NMN | 1 cup shredded (89 g) | 0-0.80 mg | 0-0.32% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (added: this row was dropped from the submission even though its upper bound exceeds tomato and beef, both of which were kept) |
| Mushroom, raw | 0-1.01 mg NMN (some samples below detection) | 1 cup sliced (70 g) | 0-0.71 mg | 0-0.28% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (confirmed) |
| Beef, raw (muscle) | 0.06-0.42 mg NMN | 1 steak (150 g) | 0.09-0.63 mg | 0.04-0.25% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (confirmed) |
| Tomato, raw | 0.26-0.30 mg NMN | 1 medium tomato (123 g) | 0.32-0.37 mg | 0.13-0.15% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (confirmed) |
| Shrimp | 0.22 mg NMN | 1 serving (100 g) | 0.22 mg | 0.09% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (added: second of the two primary-table rows the submission dropped without comment) |
| Cucumber, peel specifically (not the flesh) | 0.65 mg NMN in peel; 0.56 mg in seed | peel pared from 1 medium cucumber (about 30 g of peel) | about 0.20 mg | 0.08% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (confirmed); food identity USDA FDC 168409, ID not confirmed this session |

- **To match the dose:** A 250 mg dose is 13.3 kg of edamame a day at its richest measured value (1.88 mg/100 g) and 53 kg at the low end of the same food (0.47 mg/100 g); a 1,000 mg dose is 53 to 213 kg. Broccoli has to be quoted across both sources: at the 2025 plant survey's 13 mg/100 g ceiling a 250 mg dose is 1.9 kg, about 21 cups, while at Mills' 0.25 to 1.12 mg/100 g it is 22 to 100 kg. The honest span is therefore 1.9 to 100 kg, and the submission's flat '22 to 100 kg' overstated the gap by up to about elevenfold at the high-value end. Avocado: 104 to 463 whole avocados a day. Cucumber peel, the most quoted NMN food, is the least useful of all: 1,282 cucumbers pared, corrected from the 1,250 originally stated. No one eats any of this, and even the corrected best case rests on a survey ceiling that cannot be pinned to broccoli specifically.
- **Food vs supplement:** Substantial. NMN is a phosphorylated nucleotide and is not absorbed intact in any large quantity: it is dephosphorylated in the gut to nicotinamide riboside and then to nicotinamide before or during absorption, so an oral NMN capsule is partly a nicotinamide delivery system. Food NMN arrives as a minor component of a mixed NAD+ vitamer pool (nicotinamide, nicotinic acid, NR, NAD+ itself) inside intact cells and is measured after extraction, which is not the same as being released by chewing and digestion. Supplement material is manufactured by fermentation or enzymatic synthesis, never extracted from broccoli or edamame despite the marketing imagery. Regulatory status differs too: the FDA has taken the position that NMN is precluded from being marketed as a US dietary supplement, while it is sold freely elsewhere, so 'food-derived' framing on a label carries no compositional meaning.
- **Absorption:** Food NMN quantities are too small for bioavailability to matter. For the supplement form, human and mouse tracer work shows the oral route delivers NAD+ precursors largely after conversion to nicotinamide riboside and nicotinamide rather than as intact NMN (Trammell et al. 2016, Nat Commun 7:12948, DOI 10.1038/ncomms12948, for the NR side of the same pathway). Ordinary diets already supply 15-20 mg/day of niacin equivalents feeding the same NAD+ pathway, thousands of times more molar precursor than the NMN in any vegetable portion, so the scarcity premise behind NMN supplementation is not a dietary-shortfall argument.
- **Does the body make it:** Cells make NMN every day as the middle step of the NAD+ salvage pathway: NAMPT joins nicotinamide to 5-phosphoribosyl-1-pyrophosphate to form NMN, and NMNAT enzymes then convert NMN into NAD+. There is no dietary requirement for NMN at any intake, and NAMPT is feedback inhibited by nicotinamide, NAD+ and ATP (PMID 36746631), so the supplement targets the exact step the body already regulates. Buying NMN means adding an internal intermediate to a self-regulating pathway, and the only genuine dietary need upstream of it is the ordinary niacin requirement of 14 to 16 mg NE/day, which a normal diet covers, so the case for supplementing has to rest on outcome trials and not on repletion.
- **Pathway:** Nicotinamide + 5-phosphoribosyl-1-pyrophosphate to NMN by NAMPT (rate-limiting; feedback inhibited by nicotinamide, NAD+ and ATP, PMID 36746631); nicotinamide riboside to NMN by NRK1/NRK2 (NMRK1/2); NMN to NAD+ by NMNAT1 (nucleus), NMNAT2 (cytosol/Golgi) and NMNAT3, whose textbook assignment to mitochondria is disputed. Two further routes feed the same NAD pool: de novo from tryptophan through the kynurenine pathway (TDO2 or IDO1, KMO, KYNU, HAAO) to quinolinate, then QPRT to nicotinic acid mononucleotide and NMNAT1-3 to NAD; and the Preiss-Handler route from nicotinic acid through NAPRT.
- **Amount made:** NOT ESTABLISHED for endogenous NMN production itself, and no human whole-body NAD synthesis rate exists, partly because supply is only interpretable against consumption by PARPs, sirtuins, CD38 and SARM1. Sourced anchors: niacin RDA 16 mg NE/day for men and 14 mg NE/day for women, with dietary tryptophan converting at about 60 mg tryptophan per 1 mg niacin equivalent (IOM 1998). Supplement doses of 250 to 1000 mg/day sit one to two orders of magnitude above that vitamin requirement.
- **Still matters when:** Genuine niacin deficiency states (pellagra, chronic alcohol use, isoniazid therapy, Hartnup disease, carcinoid), where plain nicotinamide or nicotinic acid at vitamin doses is the established and far cheaper correction. Inborn errors of NAD synthesis (HAAO, KYNU, NADSYN1) are a separate clinical situation. Pharmacological NAD raising in ageing or metabolic disease remains experimental and cannot be justified as correcting a deficiency.

*Refs. PMID 39531138; PMID 39116016; Wen PMID 39221308*

### Nicotinamide riboside (NR)

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 250-1,000 mg/d commercially. EFSA assessed 300 mg/d as safe. Trials used up to 2,000 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** People with peripheral artery disease have the single best-supported claim, and it is thin.

**Pros**

- The NICE trial (n=90, 6 months) improved 6-minute walk distance by 17.6 m versus placebo, and by 31.0 m in those taking at least 75% of pills.
- More human trial data exist than for NMN, and EFSA has actually assessed its safety, which is more than most items here can say.

**Cons**

- More trials than NMN and a worse hit rate, which is informative. The pattern is consistent: NAD+ rises two to threefold, the clinical endpoint does not move.
- Long COVID (n=58, 2,000 mg/d, 20 weeks): NAD+ up 2.6 to 3.1-fold, no significant improvement in cognition, fatigue, sleep or mood.
- Obese insulin-resistant men (n=40, 2,000 mg/d, 12 weeks): insulin sensitivity, endogenous glucose production, glucose disposal and oxidation were all unimproved, in the authors own words.
- Mild cognitive impairment trials at 1,000 mg/d were null.
- EFSA caps pregnancy and lactation at 230 mg/d. Sum total nicotinamide exposure if also taking prescription niacin. As with NMN, no oncology safety data exist.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A form of vitamin B3 that cells convert into NAD+, the energy metabolism coenzyme.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Nicotinamide riboside is the riboside of nicotinamide, phosphorylated by nicotinamide riboside kinases to NMN and then to NAD+, bypassing the NAMPT step. It occurs naturally in trace amounts in milk. Single oral doses raise whole-blood NAD+ dose-dependently in humans, which is the basis for marketing it as an NAD+ booster; whether raising NAD+ produces clinical benefit in healthy people remains unsettled. NR is a minor chemical form of vitamin B3 rather than a separate essential nutrient. Vitamin B3 deficiency does exist and is called pellagra (dermatitis, diarrhoea, dementia), but it results from lack of dietary niacin and tryptophan and is treated with nicotinamide or nicotinic acid, not with NR.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Trace
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Body makes it

TRACE was challenged and upheld. NR is a real dietary vitamer, best characterised in cow milk, where Trammell et al. 2016 measured 4.3 +/- 2.6 umol/L across 18 raw samples (5.1 +/- 2.6 umol/L in the subset free of S. aureus, against nicotinamide at 7.3 +/- 1.5 umol/L). That is 1.1 to 1.3 mg per litre. Plant foods carry 62 to 1,600 ug per 100 g, with wild chicory, banana and orange named the highest (Alegre & Pastore 2025). Every one of these is three orders of magnitude below a 250 to 1,000 mg capsule. Commercial NR chloride is synthesised, not extracted from milk.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Orange, raw *[unconfirmed]* | somewhere in the survey-wide plant range of 0.062-1.6 mg NR; named among the three highest plant sources, so probably near the top, but no per-food figure is published | 1 medium orange (131 g) | 0.08-2.1 mg across the survey range | 0.03-0.7% of a 300 mg dose | Alegre & Pastore 2025, CyTA J Food 23(1), DOI 10.1080/19476337.2025.2458753 |
| Banana, raw *[unconfirmed]* | somewhere in the survey-wide plant range of 0.062-1.6 mg NR; named among the three highest plant sources | 1 medium banana (118 g) | 0.07-1.9 mg across the survey range | 0.02-0.6% of a 300 mg dose | Alegre & Pastore 2025, DOI 10.1080/19476337.2025.2458753 |
| Wild chicory greens *[unconfirmed]* | somewhere in the survey-wide plant range of 0.062-1.6 mg NR; named first among the three highest plant sources, so it is the food most likely to carry the 1.6 mg/100 g ceiling | 1 serving cooked greens (100 g) | 0.06-1.6 mg across the survey range | 0.02-0.5% of a 300 mg dose | Alegre & Pastore 2025, DOI 10.1080/19476337.2025.2458753 |
| Cow milk, whole, fresh (pasteurised) | 0.11-0.13 mg NR (4.3-5.1 uM at MW 255.25 = 1.1-1.3 mg/L) | 1 cup (244 g / 237 mL) | 0.26-0.31 mg | 0.09-0.10% of a 300 mg dose; 0.026-0.031% of a 1,000 mg dose | Trammell et al. 2016, J Nutr 146(5):957-963, PMID 27052539, DOI 10.3945/jn.116.230078 (the 4.3 +/- 2.6 uM measurement is Trammell's own, not Ummarino's as the submission stated). Ummarino et al. 2017, Food Chem 221:161-168, PMID 27979136, reports no such figure; its headline finding is that human milk is the richest NMN source. Food identity USDA FDC 171265, ID not confirmed this session |

- **To match the dose:** A 300 mg dose is 231 to 273 litres of milk a day, roughly 970 to 1,150 cups, and a 1,000 mg dose is 769 to 909 litres. One 250 mg capsule holds as much NR as 192 to 227 litres of milk. The submission quoted only the single lowest concentration (1.1 mg/L), which inflated each of these figures by 15 to 20 per cent; the range above spans Trammell's 4.3 umol/L mean and his bacteria-free 5.1 umol/L subset. From the richest plant value in the 2025 survey (1.6 mg/100 g) a 300 mg dose is about 19 kg a day. Milk is genuinely the best dietary source of NR and it is still useless as a route to a trial dose.
- **Food vs supplement:** Important. Commercial NR is nicotinamide riboside chloride, a synthesised salt, while the NR in milk is a fragile glycosidic nucleoside that degrades on storage and is reduced in milk from mastitic (S. aureus positive) cows; Trammell also found conventional milk carried more NR than organic. The food number is a moving target for that reason. More significant, the whole diet already supplies plenty of the pathway's dominant precursor, nicotinamide, at 15-20 mg/day of niacin equivalents, so NR supplementation is not correcting a dietary gap in NAD+ precursors, it is a pharmacological attempt at a different pharmacokinetic profile. High-dose nicotinamide-family precursors also carry methyl-group and flushing or hepatic considerations at gram level that a glass of milk does not.
- **Absorption:** NR is absorbed orally and raises blood NAD+ in humans, which was the point of the original demonstration (Trammell et al. 2016, Nat Commun 7:12948, DOI 10.1038/ncomms12948). Absorption is not the constraint; food quantities are microgram-scale, at 0.26-0.31 mg per cup of milk against a 250-1,000 mg capsule. There is a route difference too: much oral NR is cleaved to nicotinamide in the gut and liver, so a fraction of any dose acts as plain vitamin B3, which is abundant in food. The submission's claim that milk NR is 'free in solution and not bound to milk proteins' inverts the source and has been removed: Trammell's WaterLOGSY NMR gave positive signals from the four aromatic protons of the nicotinamide moiety consistent with association with a skim-milk protein fraction, with stronger binding in conventional than organic milk.
- **Does the body make it:** NR is also an internal NAD intermediate: the body generates it by stripping the phosphate off NMN using CD73 and alkaline phosphatases, and it circulates in human blood and appears in milk without anyone supplementing. The first-pass finding that oral NR is mostly broken down to nicotinamide in the liver comes from mouse tracer work (PMID 27721479 mouse arm; PMID 29685734 in C57BL/6 mice), so the species label matters, and the human evidence is pharmacokinetic. Supplementing does raise blood NAD metabolites measurably, which makes NR a real pharmacological agent, and it still corrects no deficiency in a person eating normally, since NR, NMN, nicotinamide and nicotinic acid all feed the same NAD pool and the two vitamin forms cost a fraction of the price.
- **Pathway:** NR to NMN by nicotinamide riboside kinases NRK1/NRK2 (NMRK1/2), then NMN to NAD+ by NMNAT1-3. NR is formed endogenously by dephosphorylation of NMN (NT5E/CD73, alkaline phosphatases) and degraded to nicotinamide by purine nucleoside phosphorylase. The Preiss-Handler route runs in parallel, with nicotinic acid entering through NAPRT to nicotinic acid mononucleotide and then the shared terminal steps, which is what governs how NAD metabolite biomarkers in trials should be read; the de novo kynurenine route from tryptophan feeds the same pool.
- **Amount made:** NOT ESTABLISHED for endogenous NR turnover. Sourced anchors: niacin requirement 14 to 16 mg NE/day (IOM 1998); in human dosing, a single-subject pilot showed blood NAD+ rising up to 2.7-fold after one oral NR dose, and the first formal pharmacokinetic trial (100, 300 and 1,000 mg) showed dose-dependent rises in blood NAD metabolites. Marketed doses of 250 to 1000 mg/day are one to two orders of magnitude above the vitamin requirement.
- **Still matters when:** The same niacin deficiency and inborn-error settings listed for NMN, where vitamin-dose nicotinamide is the standard fix. Beyond that, use rests on clinical trial endpoints for a named condition, since there is no NR deficiency state to treat.

*Refs. Wu PMID 41357333; Dollerup PMID 29992272; McDermott NICE PMID 38871717; Orr PMID 37994989*

### Resveratrol

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Trials span 150 mg/d to gram doses. The EU caps synthetic trans-resveratrol at 150 mg/d in supplements, adults only.
- **Who it is for:** Possibly people who already have type 2 diabetes. The healthy-ageing consumer is in the null subgroup.

**Pros**

- An 11-trial meta-analysis found significant improvement in glucose control and insulin sensitivity in people who already had diabetes, and explicitly no effect in non-diabetic people.

**Cons**

- It is the cautionary tale the rest of this category has not learned from. Sirtris was acquired by GSK for 720 million US dollars and the development programme was discontinued.
- The best-designed human trial was null: 150 mg/d for 6 months in overweight adults, no effect on insulin sensitivity (adjusted Matsuda index 5.18 versus 5.50 on placebo).
- It can make exercise worse. In aged men undergoing training, resveratrol BLUNTED the training-induced cardiovascular improvements, the opposite of the marketed exercise-mimetic claim.
- Antiplatelet activity, so care with warfarin and antiplatelets. Oestrogen-sensitive conditions warrant caution. Gram doses cause gastrointestinal intolerance, and the oncology programme saw renal adverse events.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A stress compound made by grapevines. It has no established job in human physiology.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Resveratrol is a stilbene phytoalexin produced by grapevines, Japanese knotweed and peanuts in response to injury and fungal attack. In cell and yeast systems it appears to activate SIRT1, an effect later shown to depend substantially on the fluorophore-tagged assay substrate rather than on the enzyme, and it activates AMPK at high concentrations. In humans absorption is good but bioavailability is very low: it is almost completely converted to sulfate and glucuronide conjugates on first pass, leaving free plasma resveratrol far below the concentrations used in cell experiments. It is a plant secondary metabolite. No human transporter, receptor or enzyme requires it, and nothing in the body fails in its absence.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Trace
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Resveratrol occurs in red wine, grapes, peanuts and some berries, at concentrations three to five orders of magnitude below trial doses; red wine averages 1.9 mg trans-resveratrol per litre, so a whole litre holds less than one capsule. Supplement resveratrol is not grape-derived: it is made synthetically or extracted from Japanese knotweed root (Polygonum cuspidatum). Knotweed is not entirely inedible, since young itadori shoots are eaten as a spring vegetable in Japan and foraged in the UK and North America and itadori tea is made from the plant, so the accurate statement is that it is not a food in Western diets, has no composition-table entry, and the root used for extraction is not the part anyone eats. Three sources that belong in the food picture carry no verified figure here and so get no row: peanut skins (the redskin testa, the richest stilbene fraction of the peanut, which is why the roasted-kernel value is explicitly for peanuts without seed coats), red and purple grape juice (the non-alcoholic route the ethanol argument implies), and the wider stilbene foods (muscadine grapes, bilberries, blueberries, cranberries, pistachios, cocoa), so the roughly 5 mg/day dietary ceiling rests on population intake studies rather than on rows built here.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Table grapes, fresh (cv. Napoleon, mature, untreated) | about 0.5 mg resveratrol per 100 g of whole fruit | 1 serving (200 g, roughly a large bunch) | about 1 mg resveratrol, which the authors note is in the range supplied by a glass of red wine | 0.7% of a 150 mg dose per 200 g serving | Cantos E, Garcia-Viguera C, de Pascual-Teresa S, Tomas-Barberan FA, Effect of postharvest ultraviolet irradiation on resveratrol and other phenolics of cv. Napoleon table grapes, J Agric Food Chem 2000;48(10):4606-12, PMID 11052707, DOI 10.1021/jf0002948 (abstract verified: 200 g of mature untreated Napoleon grapes provides approximately 1 mg, rising to 2 mg after UVB and 3 mg after UVC). Berry skin values 0.03 to 29.5 mg/kg of skin across five cultivars: Geana EI et al. 2015, PMID 27904334, citation verified but values not in the abstract |
| Peanuts, boiled (the richest peanut form) *[unconfirmed]* | 0.51 mg per 100 g (5.138 +/- 2.849 ug/g) | 1 cup boiled peanuts (100 g) | 0.51 mg | 0.3% of a 150 mg dose | Sobolev VS, Cole RJ, trans-Resveratrol content in commercial peanuts and peanut products, J Agric Food Chem 1999;47(4):1435-9, DOI 10.1021/jf9809885. Citation verified exactly, but the paper is paywalled and the value could not be read, so it is |
| Red wine | 0.19 mg trans-resveratrol per 100 mL (1.9 +/- 1.7 mg/L mean across a large review of red wines; corroborated sample spread 0.361 to 1.972 mg/L). Trans-piceid, the glucoside, averages about three times higher at up to 29.2 mg/L | 1 standard glass (175 mL) | 0.33 mg trans-resveratrol at the mean (0.06 to 0.35 mg across the corroborated sample spread) | 0.2% of a 150 mg dose per glass | Stervbo U, Vang O, Bonnesen C, A review of the content of the putative chemopreventive phytoalexin resveratrol in red wine, Food Chemistry 2007;101(2):449-57, DOI 10.1016/j.foodchem.2006.01.047, with the mean independently corroborated by Weiskirchen S, Weiskirchen R, Adv Nutr 2016, PMID 27422505, PMC4942868. The previously claimed maximum of 14.3 mg/L and the varietal claim naming St Laurent have been removed: neither could be traced, and the corroborating Geana 2015 study names Pinot Noir and Feteasca Neagra as highest |
| Peanut butter *[unconfirmed]* | 0.032 mg per 100 g (0.324 +/- 0.129 ug/g) | 2 tablespoons (32 g) | 0.010 mg (10 ug) | 0.007% of a 150 mg dose | Sobolev VS, Cole RJ, J Agric Food Chem 1999;47(4):1435-9, DOI 10.1021/jf9809885. Citation verified exactly; the value sits behind a paywall and could not be read, so it is |
| Peanuts, roasted (kernels without seed coats) | 0.0055 mg per 100 g (0.055 +/- 0.023 ug/g). Independent survey of Virginia, runner and Spanish types across four locations: 0.03 to 0.14 ug/g, explicitly for peanuts without seed coats. The redskin testa, removed here, is the richest stilbene fraction of the peanut | 1 oz (28 g), about a small handful | 0.0015 mg (1.5 ug) | 0.001% of a 150 mg dose | Sobolev VS, Cole RJ, J Agric Food Chem 1999;47(4):1435-9, DOI 10.1021/jf9809885; Sanders TH et al., Occurrence of resveratrol in edible peanuts, J Agric Food Chem 2000, DOI 10.1021/jf990737b. The 0.03 to 0.14 ug/g corroborating range is verified (via Weiskirchen 2016, PMC4942868) |

- **To match the dose:** A 150 mg resveratrol dose equals 79 litres of average red wine (150 divided by 1.9 mg/L), about 105 standard 750 mL bottles or roughly 450 glasses, every day. Even at the top of the corroborated sample spread of 0.361 to 1.972 mg/L it is still 76 litres a day. From grapes it is 30 kg daily; from boiled peanuts 29 kg, and from roasted peanuts 2.7 tonnes. Trials commonly use 150 to 1,000 mg/day and the SRT501 cancer studies used up to 5 g/day, all of it manufactured. Measured population intakes sit far lower again (a Spanish study found a median of 100 ug/day and a mean of 933 ug/day for resveratrol plus piceid, and French wine consumption yields about 0.2 mg/day), and no plausible diet gets past about 5 mg/day.
- **Food vs supplement:** Three separate mismatches. First, the supplement is not a food extract: commercial resveratrol is synthetic or comes from Polygonum cuspidatum root, which is not a Western food and not a composition-table food, so the grape imagery on the label is marketing rather than sourcing. Second, wine's stilbenes are mostly the glucoside trans-piceid, at about three times the aglycone concentration, arriving inside a large mixed polyphenol and ethanol matrix, so wine does not behave as a dilute resveratrol capsule. Third, and decisive: recommending wine as a resveratrol source is a bad recommendation regardless of dose, because ethanol is a Group 1 carcinogen and the alcohol harm arrives at one glass while the resveratrol dose would require a hundred bottles. Separately, high-dose resveratrol (2.5 to 5 g/day) causes diarrhoea and nausea and inhibits CYP3A4, CYP2C9 and sulfotransferases, giving it real interaction potential (warfarin, for example) that a glass of wine does not have.
- **Absorption:** Oral resveratrol is well absorbed (about 70% of the dose crosses the enterocyte) but systemic bioavailability of the unchanged aglycone is under 1 to 2% because of near-immediate intestinal and hepatic glucuronidation and sulfation; plasma peaks from dietary quantities sit in the low nanomolar range, while in vitro activity is generally demonstrated at micromolar concentrations. Piceid, the form dominant in grapes and much of wine, must be deglycosylated before absorption, adding another step. Gram doses are needed to reach micromolar plasma levels, which is precisely why the food route is closed.
- **Does the body make it:** Resveratrol is a plant stress compound, a stilbene assembled by stilbene synthase from 4-coumaroyl-CoA and malonyl-CoA in grapevine, peanut and Polygonum. Humans have no stilbene synthase and no phenylpropanoid pathway at all, so every molecule in the body arrived from food or a capsule. The endogenous axis therefore gives resveratrol no free pass and no handicap: the whole case has to rest on clinical outcomes, and the practical obstacle is that absorbed resveratrol is conjugated almost immediately.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Plant route only: phenylalanine to 4-coumaroyl-CoA via phenylalanine ammonia-lyase and downstream enzymes, then condensation with three malonyl-CoA by stilbene synthase. No human homologue of any step exists; human handling is limited to UGT and SULT conjugation and colonic microbial degradation.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/day. Humans possess no biosynthetic route to resveratrol.
- **Still matters when:** Only where trial evidence supports a defined endpoint, and the published trials are mostly small with surrogate endpoints. Bioavailability is the binding constraint: absorption of a dietary-relevant 25 mg oral dose was at least 70% in 6 volunteers, with peak plasma resveratrol plus metabolites at 491 +/- 90 ng/mL (about 2 micromol/L), yet only trace unchanged resveratrol was detectable (under 5 ng/mL) because of rapid glucuronidation and sulfation. Typical supplement doses are 100 to 500 mg, and the absorbed fraction at those doses has not been established.

*Refs. de Ligt PMID 32492138; Liu PMID 24695890; Gliemann J Physiol; EU Decision 2016/1190*

### Spermidine

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 0.9 mg/d was the dose in the definitive trial. Commercial products supply roughly 1-6 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody demonstrably.

**Pros**

- The autophagy mechanism is genuine in yeast, flies, worms and mice, and lifespan is extended in those organisms.

**Cons**

- The one well-conducted human test failed in exactly the target population. SmartAge was 12 months, double-masked, placebo-controlled, in 100 adults aged 60-90 with subjective cognitive decline. Primary outcome difference -0.03 (95% CI -0.11 to 0.05), P=0.47, against a pre-registered primary endpoint.
- The observational case rests on dietary intake epidemiology, and spermidine-rich foods are wheat germ, legumes, mushrooms and aged cheese. That is a dietary pattern, not a molecule.
- Dietary intake from food is typically several-fold higher than most supplements provide, which by itself undermines the supplement premise.
- Wheat-germ-derived products are unsuitable in coeliac disease and wheat allergy. Polyamines are growth-supporting and elevated in many tumours, and polyamine depletion is an active oncology strategy, so avoid during active malignancy.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A polyamine your own cells make, needed to activate a key protein-building factor.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Spermidine is a polyamine synthesized from putrescine by spermidine synthase using decarboxylated S-adenosylmethionine. Its one indispensable and unique function is donating the aminobutyl group for hypusination of eukaryotic translation initiation factor 5A, a modification required to translate polyproline-containing proteins. It also stabilises nucleic acids and induces autophagy in model organisms, which is the basis of the longevity marketing. Humans get it from three simultaneous sources: de novo synthesis in essentially every cell, gut bacterial production, and food such as wheat germ, aged cheese, soy and mushrooms. Because synthesis is ubiquitous and tightly regulated, dietary intake is never limiting and no deficiency state exists.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Body makes it

RICH was challenged and upheld. This is the one entry where ordinary food beats the supplement outright: wheat germ, dried soybeans, mushrooms, green peas, fermented soy and some aged cheeses all carry milligram-per-portion quantities. Measured intakes tabulated by Munoz-Esparza et al. 2019 run from 33.1 umol/day in Turkey to 103.1 umol/day in Spain, with Western populations at 54.7 to 103.1 umol/day, which is 7.9 to 15.0 mg/day. Trial capsules delivered 0.9 mg/day in the 12-month SmartAge trial. The diet supplies roughly ten times the capsule. Atiya Ali et al. 2011 also names chicken liver, corn and shellfish among its high-spermidine foods, and chicken liver in particular would be a useful non-wheat source, but no per-food figure for them could be sourced here, so they are not given rows.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Green peas, frozen, cooked | 5.4 mg spermidine (54.4 mg/kg) | 1 cup (160 g) | 8.7 mg | about 870% of a 1 mg capsule; 58-110% of a whole day's typical dietary spermidine | Munoz-Esparza et al. 2021, Foods 10(8):1752, PMID 34441529, DOI 10.3390/foods10081752 (confirmed); corroborated at 46.6 mg/kg by Atiya Ali et al. 2011, Food Nutr Res 55:5572, PMID 21249159, DOI 10.3402/fnr.v55i0.5572 (cross-check confirmed) |
| Mushrooms, raw (cultivated varieties) | 8.9-10.9 mg spermidine on a FRESH-weight basis | 1 cup sliced (70 g) | 6.2-7.6 mg | about 620-760% of a 1 mg capsule | Atiya Ali et al. 2011, PMID 21249159, measured 88.6 mg/kg fresh, and Munoz-Esparza et al. 2021, PMID 34441529, gives 54-109 mg/kg as the fresh-weight ceiling for the mushroom, legume and pepper group. The submission's 128.5 mg/kg 'fresh' was a back-calculation from the paper's 1,658.9 mg/kg DRY-MATTER figure and exceeded the paper's own fresh-weight ceiling, so it has been dropped |
| Soybeans, dried, raw | 3.3-20.7 mg spermidine (Atiya Ali 33.2-62.1 mg/kg; Munoz-Esparza 1,425 nmol/g = 20.7 mg/100 g) | 1/4 cup dry (46 g) | 1.5-9.5 mg | 150-950% of a 1 mg capsule; roughly 10-120% of a whole day's typical dietary spermidine | Munoz-Esparza et al. 2019, Front Nutr 6:108, PMID 31355206, DOI 10.3389/fnut.2019.00108 (nmol/g conversion confirmed); Atiya Ali et al. 2011, PMID 21249159, which is 3-6x lower and was already cited elsewhere in the submission. Both are given because the two sources genuinely disagree |
| Wheat germ, raw (industrially separated) | 22-35 mg spermidine (2,437 nmol/g = 35.4 mg/100 g; independent LC-MS/MS 221-337 mg/kg = 22.1-33.7 mg/100 g) | 2 tbsp (14 g) | 3.1-5.0 mg | 310-500% of a 1 mg/day supplement capsule | Munoz-Esparza et al. 2019, PMID 31355206 (conversion confirmed); Mohajeri et al. 2023, Food Sci Nutr 11(11), PMID 37970387, DOI 10.1002/fsn3.3650 (confirmed as spermidine-specific values, not the total-polyamine figure) |
| Natto and other fermented soy products | 4.3-10.9 mg spermidine (43.0-108.9 mg/kg, the range reported for legumes and soy derivatives rather than a natto-specific measurement) | 1 pack natto (50 g) | 2.2-5.4 mg | 220-540% of a 1 mg capsule | Munoz-Esparza et al. 2021, PMID 34441529 (group range confirmed; the group-versus-food distinction is stated because the figure is not natto-specific) |
| Cheddar, matured 12 months (aged hard cheese) | 20.0 mg spermidine (199.5 mg/kg) in one Swedish survey; other surveys are far lower, most cheeses under 1 mg/100 g (<69 nmol/g) with one blue cheese at 3.8 mg/100 g (262 nmol/g) | 1 slice (30 g) | 6.0 mg by the high figure, 0.3-1.1 mg by the low figures | 30-600% of a 1 mg capsule, depending which survey you believe | Atiya Ali et al. 2011, PMID 21249159 (matured cheddar 199.5 mg/kg, confirmed); Munoz-Esparza et al. 2019, PMID 31355206 (most cheeses <69 nmol/g, blue cheese 262 nmol/g, both confirmed) |

- **To match the dose:** The gap runs backwards. Two tablespoons of wheat germ (14 g) supply 3.1 to 5.0 mg, three to five times the 0.9 mg/day dose used in the SmartAge trial. A cup of cooked green peas supplies about 8.7 mg and a cup of sliced mushrooms 6.2 to 7.6 mg, each close to an entire ordinary day's intake. Against habitual intakes of 7.9 to 15.0 mg/day, a typical 1 mg capsule adds 6.7 to 12.7 per cent on top of what the person already eats, which is inside the day-to-day variation of a normal diet, and matching a day's dietary spermidine from capsules would take 8 to 15 of them. Anyone who wants more spermidine should buy wheat germ, which costs a fraction of the capsules and delivers several times the dose.
- **Food vs supplement:** Real, in both directions. Most 'spermidine' products are wheat germ extracts standardised to about 1 mg rather than purified spermidine, so the label compound is the same molecule as in the food and there is no extraction advantage. Against that, aged cheese and natto carry biogenic amines (histamine, tyramine, putrescine) that matter for people on MAO inhibitors or with histamine intolerance, so the richest fermented sources are not risk-free foods even though they are ordinary ones. Polyamines are proliferative signalling molecules and concentrated long-term supplementation lacks the observational safety record that food-level intake has. The Bruneck cohort (Kiechl et al. 2018, Am J Clin Nutr 108(2):371-380, PMID 29955838) supports dietary intake, reporting mortality by intake thirds of 40.5, 23.7 and 15.1 deaths per 1,000 person-years; the 9 to 11.5 mg/day figures the submission attributed to it are not retrievable from the paper and have been dropped. Wheat germ also goes rancid quickly, so buy small and refrigerate.
- **Absorption:** Dietary polyamines are absorbed readily in the upper small intestine, gut bacteria synthesise more, and circulating levels are homeostatically buffered, so intake is not the only input. A triple-blind randomised placebo-controlled crossover trial gave 12 healthy volunteers 15 mg/day of spermidine in two 5-day phases, about 15 times a typical capsule and 1.0 to 1.9 times a rich day's diet; plasma spermine rose significantly while spermidine and putrescine did not change, which the authors read as presystemic conversion of spermidine to spermine (Senekowitsch et al. 2023, Nutrients, PMID 37111071). Either way, dosing well above dietary levels did not raise circulating spermidine. Processing of the food matters more than any supplement choice: commercially germinated wheat grain measured 19.22-24.25 mg/kg spermidine against 220.65-337.2 mg/kg for industrially separated wheat germ. Those ranges imply an 89 to 94 per cent drop, about 92 per cent mean to mean, and the paper's own headline figure of 85.52 per cent is inconsistent with its own data.
- **Does the body make it:** Spermidine synthase builds spermidine from putrescine plus an aminopropyl group donated by decarboxylated S-adenosylmethionine, with ornithine decarboxylase as the tightly regulated rate-limiting entry step, while arginine decarboxylase plus agmatinase (AGMAT) provide a second, agmatine-derived route into the putrescine pool. Cells hold polyamines at high micromolar to millimolar concentrations, recycle them continuously through SSAT and polyamine oxidases, and also absorb polyamines produced by gut bacteria, and how that supply splits between host synthesis, diet and microbiota has never been quantified in humans. The FULL label covers the host pathway only, and the practical reading is that a fixed daily dose is an awkward intervention against a homeostatic system the body makes on demand and regulates in both directions.
- **Pathway:** Ornithine to putrescine by ODC1 (antizyme/AZIN regulated); arginine to agmatine by arginine decarboxylase, then agmatine to putrescine by agmatinase (AGMAT), a real human input the standard scheme omits; methionine to SAM to decarboxylated SAM by AMD1; putrescine + dcSAM to spermidine by spermidine synthase (SRM), then to spermine by spermine synthase. Recycling runs through SSAT acetylation and the polyamine oxidases, and gut bacterial polyamines are absorbed as an additional exogenous input.
- **Amount made:** NOT ESTABLISHED. No human whole-body de novo polyamine synthesis rate has been measured by tracer methods. The usable exposure anchor is cohort intake: dietary spermidine ran at roughly 10 to 12 mg/day across the Bruneck cohort tertiles (PMID 29955838), and food-composition data come from Swedish dairy analyses, where mature cheese contained 1.2 mg/kg spermidine (PMID 21249159).
- **Still matters when:** Inherited polyamine pathway disorders such as spermine synthase loss in Snyder-Robinson syndrome, which oral polyamines do not correct. Otherwise only trial-supported endpoints, and the human data are observational: all-cause mortality fell across thirds of increasing dietary spermidine, fully adjusted HR 0.76 per 1-SD, 95% CI 0.67 to 0.86, P < 0.001, and the paper translated top versus bottom third as equivalent to an age difference of 5.7 years, 3.6 to 8.1 years. Note also that the opposite strategy is under study, since polyamine depletion is a cancer chemoprevention approach, so more is not automatically better.

*Refs. SmartAge PMID 35616942; protocol PMID 31039826*

### Curcumin / turmeric extract

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** About 1,000 mg/d of curcuminoids in the knee osteoarthritis trials. Culinary turmeric is a different exposure entirely.
- **Who it is for:** At most, people with symptomatic knee osteoarthritis, if the meta-analytic signal survives the bias problem.

**Pros**

- A Bayesian network meta-analysis of 23 studies and 2,175 knee osteoarthritis patients found pain on a visual analogue scale -1.63 (-2.91 to -0.45) and total WOMAC -18.85.
- Culinary use in food is entirely reasonable and carries no established risk.

**Cons**

- It was classified in the Journal of Medicinal Chemistry as both a PAINS (pan-assay interference compound) and an invalid metabolic panacea: an unstable, reactive, nonbioavailable compound and therefore a highly improbable lead. That is a formal medicinal-chemistry verdict, not an opinion.
- The dose on the label is disconnected from systemic exposure. Unconjugated plasma curcumin stays below 2 nM even at high dose with piperine, against the 1-100 micromolar concentrations used in the in-vitro work that generated the claims. Adding piperine does not raise unconjugated plasma curcumin.
- The osteoarthritis trials are mostly industry-funded with high heterogeneity.
- Turmeric is the most commonly used of the six botanicals linked to liver injury in US survey data (236 of 9,685 respondents), and 10 DILIN cases published in 2023 included five hospitalisations and one death from acute liver failure. Seven of ten carried HLA-B*35:01.
- Antiplatelet activity raises bleeding risk with warfarin and antiplatelets. Piperine inhibits CYP3A4 broadly, so herb-drug interaction risk with cytotoxics and targeted agents is real.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

The yellow pigment of turmeric. A spice compound, with no role in human physiology.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Curcumin is a diarylheptanoid pigment making up roughly 2 to 5 percent of turmeric rhizome. It binds and weakly inhibits a very large number of unrelated proteins in vitro, which is why it appears in thousands of papers; medicinal chemists classify it as a pan-assay interference compound, since its chemical reactivity, aggregation and fluorescence generate false positives across screening assays. Unformulated curcumin is poorly absorbed, rapidly glucuronidated and sulfated, and essentially undetectable as the free compound in plasma even after gram doses, though piperine, phospholipid and nanoparticle formulations raise exposure. It is a plant secondary metabolite with no physiological role in humans and no requirement.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Turmeric is an ordinary culinary spice, the dried ground rhizome of Curcuma longa, and it carries curcumin at 3.14% by weight, so one level teaspoon delivers 94 mg. Measured against the JECFA/EFSA acceptable daily intake for curcumin of 3 mg/kg body weight (210 mg for a 70 kg adult), a single teaspoon supplies 45% of the ADI, which is a rich dietary source on any normal reading; the earlier TRACE label came from scoring food against a 1,000 mg capsule, which is the dose question and is answered separately below. Turmeric is effectively the only meaningful food source, and commercial curry powder is much weaker. Fresh turmeric rhizome is the same food in unprocessed form and is increasingly sold in UK and US supermarkets, but no verified composition figure for it is available here, so it gets no row.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Turmeric, ground (dried rhizome powder, as sold as a spice) *[unconfirmed]* | 3.14 g curcumin per 100 g (3.14% w/w, the mean for pure turmeric powder and the highest concentration measured across the 28 spice products analysed, which spanned both turmeric and curry powders). Total curcuminoids are conventionally quoted at 1.4 to 1.6 times curcumin alone, roughly 4.5 to 5 g/100 g, since curcumin is 60 to 70% of the curcuminoid fraction | 1 level teaspoon (3.0 g) | 94 mg curcumin. 1 tablespoon (9.4 g) = 295 mg curcumin | 9.4% of a 1,000 mg curcuminoid dose per teaspoon; 30% per tablespoon. Per teaspoon it is also 45% of the 210 mg JECFA/EFSA ADI for a 70 kg adult | Tayyem RF, Heath DD, Al-Delaimy WK, Rock CL. Curcumin content of turmeric and curry powders. Nutr Cancer 2006;55(2):126-31, PMID 17044766, DOI 10.1207/s15327914nc5502_2 (3.14% verified against the abstract). Portion weights 1 tsp = 3.0 g and 1 tbsp = 9.4 g independently confirmed for Spices, turmeric, ground; the FDC ID 172231 previously cited could not be confirmed against a live lookup. The 1.4 to 1.6 times curcuminoid ratio and the derived 4.5 to 5 g/100 g are |
| Curry powder (commercial blend, containing turmeric) *[unconfirmed]* | 0.05 to 0.58 g curcumin per 100 g (0.05 to 0.58% w/w) | 1 tablespoon (about 6 g) used in a dish for two to four people | 3 to 35 mg curcumin in the whole dish | 0.3 to 3.5% of a 1,000 mg dose for the entire dish | Tayyem 2006, PMID 17044766, reports only that curry powders, with one exception, had relatively small amounts of curcumin present with great variability. The 0.05 to 0.58% bounds are |

- **To match the dose:** A 1,000 mg curcuminoid capsule equals about 21 to 32 g of turmeric powder every day: 32 g counting curcumin only at 3.14% w/w, about 21 g counting all three curcuminoids at roughly 4.7% (a conventional ratio the cited source does not establish). That is 7 to 11 level teaspoons, close to a third of a supermarket spice jar per day, and nobody eats that. A generous curry portion carries 1 to 2 g of turmeric, or 30 to 60 mg of curcumin, and a heavy South Asian intake of 2 to 2.5 g turmeric per day yields 63 to 79 mg of curcumin, so the capsule is about 13 to 16 times a heavy dietary intake on a curcumin basis, and about 8.5 to 11 times if the comparison is made on total curcuminoids. There is no eating route to the dose.
- **Food vs supplement:** This is the single most important form gap in the whole list. Culinary turmeric has no liver-injury signal; 95% curcuminoid extracts, especially those boosted with piperine or reformulated as phytosomes, micelles or nanoparticles, are now a leading identified botanical cause of drug-induced liver injury in US and Australian case series, with a reported HLA-B*35:01 association. The EFSA/JECFA acceptable daily intake for curcumin (E 100) is 3 mg/kg body weight per day, 210 mg for a 70 kg adult: one teaspoon of turmeric in food (94 mg curcumin) sits at 45% of it, while a single 1,000 mg curcuminoid capsule is about five times over it. Concentrated extracts also inhibit CYP3A4 and UGT enzymes and interact with anticoagulants, which food-level turmeric does not. Second caveat cutting the other way: scaling up culinary turmeric is not a safe workaround either, because turmeric powder in some supply chains has been adulterated with lead chromate for colour (well documented in Bangladeshi turmeric), and gram-level daily dosing of spice-grade powder magnifies that exposure.
- **Absorption:** Curcumin is close to a worst case for oral absorption: poor solubility, extensive intestinal and hepatic glucuronidation and sulfation, and rapid biliary excretion leave systemic exposure at or below detection limits after dietary amounts. Co-administered piperine 20 mg raised curcumin bioavailability roughly 20-fold in humans (Shoba et al. 1998, PMID 9619120, verified as a 2000% increase), and modern phytosome, micellar and nanoparticle formulations claim 10 to 185-fold increases over unformulated powder. The practical consequence is that milligram equivalence between spice and supplement misleads in both directions: a formulated 500 mg extract can deliver more systemic curcumin than 30 g of raw powder, and the traditional practice of blooming turmeric in hot oil with black pepper is a crude version of the same trick.
- **Does the body make it:** Curcuminoids are plant polyketides: Curcuma longa builds them from feruloyl-CoA and malonyl-CoA using two type III polyketide synthases, diketide-CoA synthase and curcumin synthase. Humans have no phenylpropanoid pathway and no type III polyketide synthase, so curcumin is a xenobiotic the body can only absorb, conjugate and clear. Endogenous synthesis is irrelevant to the buying decision here, and the operative limit is pharmacokinetic: oral curcumin is glucuronidated and sulfated so fast that free plasma concentrations stay very low, which is why any case for it rests on trial evidence for a named indication and on the specific formulation used.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Plant route only: phenylalanine through phenylalanine ammonia-lyase to feruloyl-CoA, then DCS and CURS type III polyketide synthases in Curcuma longa rhizome. Human handling is limited to UGT and SULT conjugation and colonic microbial degradation.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/day. Humans have no biosynthetic route to curcuminoids.
- **Still matters when:** Only on trial evidence for a specific indication, with attention to the formulation used in that trial, since plain extract is poorly bioavailable, and to interactions through CYP and UGT pathways for anyone on prescription medicines. Safety screening also covers herb-induced liver injury with high-dose enhanced-absorption curcumin, associated with HLA-B*35:01, and piperine co-formulation, which raises exposure to other drugs.

*Refs. Nelson J Med Chem 2017 PMID 28074653; iScience 2025 PMC12144411; Zhao PMID 38036015; Halegoua-DeMarzio PMID 36252717; Likhitsup PMID 39102266*

### MSM (methylsulfonylmethane)

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Trials used 2 g/d (the 2023 trial) up to 6 g/d (the 2006 pilot). No effective dose is established.
- **Who it is for:** No population has a defensible claim.

**Pros**

- A single 88-person trial reported a significant between-group difference in a knee quality-of-life composite score at 12 weeks.
- Very low toxicity signal at the doses used.

**Cons**

- The meta-analysis found 3 randomised trials and 326 patients, of which only ONE studied MSM at all. The other two studied DMSO. Reported pain reduction was a non-significant 6.34 mm on a visual analogue scale.
- The 2008 systematic review covering 6 studies and 681 osteoarthritis patients found only 52 had ever received MSM in a controlled trial. That is the entire human evidence base.
- It is present in a large share of joint supplements, which inflates the price of combination products for no measured benefit.
- No characterised drug interactions, largely because almost nobody has looked. No pregnancy or lactation data.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A small sulfur molecule sold for joint pain; no body process is known to need it.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Methylsulfonylmethane is dimethyl sulfone, the oxidised metabolite of dimethyl sulfoxide, present in trace amounts in some foods and in the marine sulfur cycle. It is well absorbed and distributes widely, including into cerebrospinal fluid, and a fraction of its sulfur can enter the methionine and cysteine pools. That contribution is trivial next to dietary protein, which supplies all the sulfur amino acid a person needs. Proposed anti-inflammatory effects via NF-kB inhibition come from cell and animal work, and human osteoarthritis trials are small and short. Sulfur as an element is essential; MSM as a molecule is not, and no human enzyme or structure uses it.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Trace
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Low
- **Body makes it:** Partly

TRACE survives as a label, since nobody claims food delivers grams of MSM, but the evidence behind it is void and the entry's food table has been withdrawn in full. Every number in the widely circulated MSM food table (cow's milk 3.30 mg/kg, coffee 1.60, tomato 0.86, alfalfa 0.77, tea 0.30, beer 0.18, Swiss chard 0.18, corn 0.11) comes from Pearson, Dawson & Lackey 1981, which is titled 'Naturally occurring levels of dimethyl sulfoxide in selected fruits, vegetables, grains, and beverages'. It measured DMSO, a different compound; a 2026 review reproduced those eight values under an MSM heading, and the mislabelling has propagated from there. The accurate statement is that the MSM content of ordinary foods is essentially unquantified in the accessible literature. Bulk supplement MSM is manufactured by oxidising DMSO with hydrogen peroxide or nitrogen dioxide and purifying by distillation or crystallisation, so this is functionally a synthetic bulk chemical with an unmeasured natural presence.

- **To match the dose:** The gap cannot be computed, and the submission's figures (909 kg of milk for a 3 g dose, 1,875 litres of coffee, 450 kg of milk for the 1.5 g low end) are withdrawn because every one of them was built on DMSO measurements.
