Qualitatively the answer is still no: supplement doses are 1.5 to 6 g/day, no food has been shown to contain MSM at anything approaching that, and no MSM deficiency has ever been described. Anyone quoting a per-cup or per-kilogram MSM figure is quoting a mislabelled DMSO table.
- **Food vs supplement:** Yes, and it is mostly about honesty in marketing. Commercial MSM is made by oxidising DMSO, so it is chemically identical to whatever trace natural molecule exists but industrially produced; purity grades differ (distilled versus crystallised) and residual DMSO or solvent is a real quality variable between brands. The common marketing move of pointing at garlic, onions and brassicas as natural MSM foods is wrong: those foods are rich in allicin, thiosulfinates and glucosinolates, which are different sulfur chemistries, and eating them does not deliver MSM. Dietary sulfur adequacy comes from methionine and cysteine in protein, a separate question entirely. No whole food substitutes for an MSM capsule, and equally there is no evidence anyone is MSM-deficient.
- **Absorption:** Absorption is not the limiting factor. MSM is a small (94 Da), highly water-soluble, uncharged molecule, absorbed close to completely after oral dosing and distributed widely including across the blood-brain barrier, which is why it is detectable in cerebrospinal fluid. Circulating MSM in people who take no supplements comes partly from diet and partly from bacterial and endogenous metabolism of other sulfur compounds. Because uptake is near-complete either way, the food-versus-supplement comparison reduces entirely to dose, and the dose cannot currently be stated for food: the ratios the submission gave (about 1:3,700 per cup of milk, which it rounded to 1:4,000) rest on a DMSO measurement and are withdrawn.
- **Does the body make it:** Dimethyl sulfone is a normal trace constituent of human blood and cerebrospinal fluid in people taking no supplement, measured at 0 to 25 micromol/L, and host enzymes do produce it: thiol S-methyltransferase methylates methanethiol to dimethyl sulfide, and flavin-containing monooxygenases oxidise that to DMSO and then to dimethyl sulfone. What is not endogenous is the precursor supply, which comes mostly from bacterial methionine catabolism in the gut and from diet, and no human enzyme has MSM as its designated end product. There is no requirement, no deficiency state and no known physiological function, so the endogenous axis offers no repletion rationale at all and any claim has to stand on symptom trials.
- **Pathway:** No dedicated human biosynthetic pathway, though the terminal oxidation steps are host-catalysed. Methanethiol comes mainly from bacterial methionine catabolism in the gut and from the methionine transamination route; host thiol S-methyltransferase activity yields dimethyl sulfide, and further oxidation by flavin-containing monooxygenases gives DMSO and then dimethyl sulfone. Diet is a further source. A dimethyl sulfone finding was reported in 3 of 4 patients with severe methionine adenosyltransferase I/III deficiency (PMID 15996001), which is the human link between the pool and methionine handling.
- **Amount made:** NOT ESTABLISHED. Only circulating concentrations are documented: 0 to 25 micromol/L in plasma and CSF of healthy unsupplemented controls, against gram-scale supplement doses.
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on a repletion basis, because no requirement or deficiency exists. Use would have to rest on trial evidence for a specific symptom, and the usual claim (osteoarthritis pain) comes from small short trials.

*Refs. Brien PMID 19474240; Brien PMID 18417375; Toguchi PMID 37447322*

### Astaxanthin

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 4-12 mg/d in skin trials. EFSA acceptable daily intake is 0.2 mg/kg body weight, so 14 mg/d for a 70 kg adult.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody with a demonstrated clinical outcome.

**Pros**

- Small improvements in skin moisture (ES 0.53) and elasticity (ES 0.77) across 9 oral randomised trials.
- One oxidative-stress marker, advanced oxidation protein products, fell significantly after exercise.

**Cons**

- The core selling point is a test-tube potency ratio, often quoted as 1,000 times coenzyme Q10 at quenching singlet oxygen. That is an in-vitro number with no demonstrated clinical consequence, and it is exactly the kind of assay-derived claim the PAINS critique warns about.
- Wrinkle depth, the outcome consumers actually buy it for, was NOT significant (-0.26, 95% CI -0.58 to 0.06).
- In exercise trials MDA, TBARS, superoxide dismutase, sulfhydryls, creatine kinase and IL-6 were all non-significant, directly contradicting the antioxidant marketing.
- EFSA noted that children exceed the acceptable daily intake with 8 mg/d supplements, so the common commercial 12 mg dose exceeds the safe intake for a 40 kg adolescent.
- Krill-derived astaxanthin in shellfish allergy. Reported 5-alpha-reductase inhibition is poorly characterised and matters in hormone-sensitive conditions.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A red algal pigment that sits inside cell membranes. Humans have no requirement for it.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Astaxanthin is a xanthophyll carotenoid produced by the microalga Haematococcus pluvialis and concentrated up the food chain into salmon, trout, shrimp and flamingo feathers. Polar keto and hydroxyl groups on both ionone rings let the molecule span the lipid bilayer, which underlies claims that it quenches singlet oxygen and lipid radicals more efficiently than beta-carotene. Unlike beta-carotene it has no provitamin A activity in humans: it is not cleaved to retinal and cannot contribute to vitamin A status. Humans cannot synthesize it and have no need to, since no metabolic process requires it and no intake recommendation exists.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Astaxanthin is genuinely present at milligram levels in ordinary seafood, so this is the strongest food case in the set. Wild sockeye salmon carries 26 to 38 mg per kg of flesh, meaning one 140 g fillet supplies 3.6 to 5.3 mg, the same order of magnitude as a capsule. Several standard sources in the same literature carry no verified figure here and so get no row: salmon roe (ikura and sujiko), wild chinook, pink and chum salmon, Arctic char, red sea bream, and the shell-on preparations (crayfish, small crab, head-on prawns) where the pigment actually concentrates.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Wild sockeye salmon (Oncorhynchus nerka), flesh | 2.6 to 3.8 mg per 100 g (26 to 38 mg/kg flesh). A Raman study of retail fillets measured lower, 1.42 mg/100 g (14.2 mg/kg), showing real variability | 1 fillet (140 g) | 3.6 to 5.3 mg (2.0 mg using the lower retail measurement) | 30 to 44% of a 12 mg clinical dose per fillet | Ambati RR et al., Astaxanthin: sources, extraction, stability, biological activities and its commercial applications, Mar Drugs 2014;12(1):128-52, PMID 24402174, PMC3917265, DOI 10.3390/md12010128, Figure 1, verified, including the note that 165 g of salmon per day supplies 3.6 mg. Retail fillet measurement: Hikima J et al., On-site direct detection of astaxanthin from salmon fillet using Raman spectroscopy, Mar Biotechnol 2017;19(2):157-63, PMID 28378103, verified |
| Rainbow trout, large, flesh | 0.6 mg per 100 g for European-market trout, 2.5 mg per 100 g for Japanese-market trout (6 and 25 mg/kg) | 1 fillet (140 g) | 0.84 to 3.5 mg | 7 to 29% of a 12 mg dose | Ambati RR et al., Mar Drugs 2014;12(1):128-52, PMID 24402174, PMC3917265, Figure 1 (both market figures verified) |
| Farmed Atlantic salmon, flesh | 0.6 to 0.8 mg per 100 g (6 to 8 mg/kg flesh) | 1 fillet (140 g) | 0.84 to 1.12 mg | 7 to 9% of a 12 mg dose | Ambati RR et al., Mar Drugs 2014;12(1):128-52, PMID 24402174, PMC3917265, Figure 1 (verified) |
| Wild coho salmon, flesh | 0.70 mg per 100 g (7.0 mg/kg, measured on retail fillet) | 1 fillet (140 g) | 0.98 mg | 8% of a 12 mg dose | Hikima J et al., On-site direct detection of astaxanthin from salmon fillet using Raman spectroscopy, Mar Biotechnol 2017;19(2):157-63, PMID 28378103 (abstract verified) |
| Antarctic krill (Euphausia superba), whole, wet weight | about 0.8 to 1.0 mg per 100 g of whole wet krill (8 to 10 mg/kg), converted from 37.41 mg/kg in dried krill meal at roughly 78 to 80% water in the live animal | 100 g of whole krill | about 0.8 to 1.0 mg | 7 to 8% of a 12 mg dose | Zheng Y et al., Prevention of the quality degradation of Antarctic krill (Euphausia superba) meal through two-stage drying, Foods 2024;13(11):1706, PMC11171497, DOI 10.3390/foods13111706 (full text verified: 37.41 mg/kg is the best of five drying treatments, which ranged 33.99 to 39.00 mg/kg). That figure is dried krill meal, a feed and aquaculture ingredient rather than a human portion, and the paper gives no value for raw or frozen krill, so the wet-weight conversion here is indicative. The previously claimed 30 to 40 mg/kg for frozen krill has been dropped as unsourced and inconsistent with the fact that removing water concentrates the pigment |
| Shrimp / prawns, peeled cooked meat (Pandalus borealis) *[unconfirmed]* | about 0.86 mg per 100 g of peeled meat (8.6 ug/g, the low end of a reported 8.6 to 70 ug/g span). The high end of that span belongs to shell and head fractions and whole shell-on products, which are a different food from peeled meat | 100 g peeled cooked shrimp, about 12 large prawns | about 0.86 mg | 7% of a 12 mg dose | Jensen HA, Hansen LT, Boknaes N, Mejlholm O, Jacobsen C, Dalgaard P, Northern shrimp (Pandalus borealis): a review on biology, catch, processing, quality changes, shelf-life and product safety, Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 2025, DOI 10.1080/10408398.2025.2505241. Citation genuine but paywalled, so the 8.6 to 70 ug/g span is |

- **To match the dose:** A 12 mg daily dose, the commonest in the clinical literature, equals 315 to 460 g of wild sockeye salmon every day at 26 to 38 mg/kg, or about 550 g using the cited review's own dietary anchor of 3.6 mg per 165 g portion. From farmed Atlantic salmon it is 1.5 to 2.0 kg per day, from peeled shrimp meat about 1.4 kg, and from whole wet krill 1.2 to 1.5 kg. A single 140 g sockeye fillet already covers 30 to 44% of the dose, which no other entry here manages, so a meaningful fraction is reachable by eating while the full dose is not: sustaining 350 to 500 g of oily fish daily would push past standard advice on methylmercury and persistent organic pollutants.
- **Food vs supplement:** Chemical form differs by source. Salmon astaxanthin is largely free or protein-bound; krill and Haematococcus astaxanthin is esterified with fatty acids; and much supplement and feed astaxanthin is synthetic astaxanthin dimethyldisuccinate, a different stereoisomer mixture from the natural 3S,3'S form. This matters for the safety arithmetic: EFSA's acceptable daily intake of 0.2 mg/kg body weight per day (14 mg for a 70 kg adult, established by the FEEDAP Panel in 2019 and replacing the earlier 0.034 mg/kg) was derived from a rat carcinogenicity study on the synthetic material and then applied to the algal form. On that ADI, a 12 mg capsule is 86% of the limit and sits just under it, doses of about 14 mg and above exceed it, and a 24 mg capsule is roughly 1.7 times over, while a salmon fillet stays well below. A safety review of 87 human studies, including 35 at 12 mg/day or more, found no safety concerns with natural astaxanthin and argues the synthetic-derived ADI should not govern it (Brendler & Williamson, Phytother Res 2019;33(12):3090-3111, PMID 31788888, DOI 10.1002/ptr.6514). One honest twist: the astaxanthin in farmed salmon is itself an added feed pigment, so eating farmed salmon for astaxanthin means eating a supplemented animal.
- **Absorption:** Astaxanthin is a lipophilic xanthophyll and absorption depends on fat and micelle formation: uptake from a lipid-based formulation or a fatty meal is severalfold higher than from a dry powder taken fasted, and esterified forms must first be hydrolysed by pancreatic lipase. Overall human bioavailability is low and highly variable (single-digit percentages), which cuts in favour of the food: salmon arrives pre-packaged with its own lipid and a food matrix, so the plasma difference between a 4 mg fillet and a 12 mg dry capsule is smaller than the milligram gap implies.
- **Does the body make it:** Astaxanthin is a ketocarotenoid produced by microalgae such as Haematococcus pluvialis and by some bacteria and yeasts, using a beta-carotene ketolase (BKT/CrtW) plus a hydroxylase (CrtZ). Humans synthesise no carotenoids whatsoever and cannot convert beta-carotene into astaxanthin, and salmon and shrimp are pink from the astaxanthin in their food rather than from their own synthesis. Everything in a human body reflects intake, there is no deficiency state to correct and no provitamin A activity to fall back on, so the supplement stands or falls on trial results.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Algal and microbial route: beta-carotene to echinenone to canthaxanthin by BKT/CrtW ketolase, with CrtZ/CrtR-b hydroxylation to adonirubin and astaxanthin. No human homologue of any step.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/day. Humans have no carotenoid biosynthetic pathway.
- **Still matters when:** Only where trial evidence supports a defined endpoint. With no requirement and no deficiency state, dose selection is entirely empirical.

*Refs. Zhou PMID 34578794; Ma PMID 35091276; You PMID 41710469; EFSA PMID 32874213*

### HMB

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 3 g/d, usually as three 1 g doses. No dose or duration rescues the result in trained people.
- **Who it is for:** On the balance of umbrella reviews, at most untrained beginners or clinically muscle-wasting populations.

**Pros**

- An umbrella review of 11 studies and 41 datasets found small but significant pooled effects on muscle mass (ES 0.21), muscle strength index (0.27) and fat-free mass (0.22).
- No established contraindication, no drug interaction and not a doping risk.

**Cons**

- In trained and competitive athletes it is a flat null across every endpoint. Six randomised trials, n=193: bench press effect size 0.00, leg press 0.09, body mass -0.01, fat-free mass 0.16, fat mass -0.20, all non-significant and described by the authors as trivial. Results were unchanged by dose, duration, training level or dietary co-intervention.
- An umbrella review of 15 systematic reviews found only 5 favoured HMB for lean soft-tissue mass; 10 reported no difference or insufficient evidence.
- Its reputation was built on a handful of early trials from a small number of affiliated laboratories and never replicated independently in trained athletes.
- One of the more expensive ingredients per gram, so it displaces spend from protein and creatine, which work.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A leucine metabolite that slows muscle protein loss during illness, bed rest or ageing.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Beta-hydroxy-beta-methylbutyrate is a downstream metabolite of the amino acid leucine. About 5 percent of leucine catabolism proceeds through ketoisocaproate to HMB, so at ordinary protein intakes the body produces only about 0.2 to 0.4 g a day from dietary leucine, and preformed HMB in food (grapefruit, catfish, alfalfa, corn silage) is present in milligram quantities and contributes essentially nothing. The scale is easier to see from the other direction: obtaining a typical 3 g supplemental dose from food would require the 60 g of leucine contained in over 600 g of high quality protein. Its main described action is suppression of muscle proteolysis through the ubiquitin-proteasome pathway, with a weaker effect on mTOR-mediated protein synthesis than leucine itself. Clinical interest sits in muscle-wasting settings such as bed rest, older adults and cancer cachexia. It is a metabolite of an essential amino acid, not an essential nutrient in its own right.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** None
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Partly

HMB (beta-hydroxy-beta-methylbutyrate) is manufactured, by halogenation of diacetone alcohol with sodium hypobromite or by esterification routes from diacetone alcohol and acetic acid derivatives, at plant scales around 1.5 tonnes per month, with microbial routes emerging (Galactomyces reessii 29.0 g/L, engineered E. coli 17.7 g/L). No food composition database carries an HMB value, and the frequently repeated claim that it occurs in catfish, grapefruit and alfalfa has no quantified composition analysis behind it; an independent literature query returned five papers, none with a food figure. Endogenous production of 0.3-0.4 g/day in healthy adults is metabolism rather than a dietary source, so the earlier TRACE label overstated the dietary presence.

- **To match the dose:** There is no food HMB figure to work from, so the gap has to be argued through the precursor. About 80% of leucine is directed to protein synthesis, and approximately 5-10% of alpha-ketoisocaproate, the leucine transamination product, is converted to HMB; treating that 5-10% as a leucine-to-HMB yield is generous, so the arithmetic below is a ceiling. Even on that generous reading, 3 g of HMB implies about 60 g of leucine per day, which is 2.6 kg of cooked chicken breast at 2.33 g leucine per 100 g, carrying roughly 800 g of protein. At the optimistic 10% figure it is 30 g of leucine, about 1.3 kg of cooked breast and 400 g of protein. Measured endogenous output in healthy adults is only 0.3-0.4 g/day, 8 to 20 times below the 3-6 g/day used in trials, and eating more protein or leucine does not move it, because the bottleneck is the branched-chain pathway rather than the intake.
- **Food vs supplement:** The supplement is a manufactured molecule and not a food extract, so there is no whole-food-versus-extract comparison to make and no reason to describe HMB as something a diet can supply. Regulatory reviews treat 3-6 g/day as generally safe while noting that no international consensus on an optimal dose exists. The one form question that matters commercially is calcium HMB monohydrate versus free-acid HMB, which differ in absorption kinetics rather than in origin. Primary source for the manufacturing, endogenous production and conversion figures: Zhou S, Liu G, Wang Z, Lei Z, Chen W, Wang C, Foods 2025;14(8):1294, PMID 40282696, DOI 10.3390/foods14081294.
- **Absorption:** There is no food form whose absorption can be compared, so the only meaningful statement is about the pathway. Dietary leucine is not a usable precursor at supplement scale: about 80% of leucine goes to protein synthesis, and roughly 5-10% of alpha-ketoisocaproate, the leucine transamination product, is converted to HMB, which is why raising protein or leucine intake lifts endogenous HMB only marginally and total endogenous output stays at 0.3-0.4 g/day (DOI 10.3390/foods14081294). Between the two supplement forms, free-acid HMB is reported to reach peak plasma concentration faster and higher than calcium HMB monohydrate, which is the main practical bioavailability distinction; both bypass the leucine pathway entirely, which is precisely why the supplement exists.
- **Does the body make it:** HMB is a normal human metabolite of leucine, made whenever leucine is metabolised, but only about 5 percent of whole-body leucine flux takes that branch, which comes to roughly 0.2 to 0.4 g/day in a 70 kg adult. The studied dose is 3 g/day, and reaching that from food would take about 60 g of leucine, over 600 g of high quality protein, so endogenous production is real yet roughly a tenth of the dose being tested. That makes supplementation a coherent premise on this axis and hardly a duplication, while whether 3 g/day does anything useful in healthy trained people is a separate and much weaker question, and the form matters, since the free acid and the calcium salt differ in absorption rate and peak plasma concentration.
- **Pathway:** Leucine is reversibly transaminated to alpha-ketoisocaproate (KIC) by mitochondrial branched-chain aminotransferase (BCAT2) in muscle. A minority of KIC is oxidised in the cytosol, largely in liver, to HMB by the enzyme identified as 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate dioxygenase (HPD), so HMB production is a side activity of a tyrosine-catabolism enzyme. HMB is partly converted to HMG-CoA and enters cholesterol synthesis inside the myocyte.
- **Amount made:** About 0.2 to 0.4 g/day in a 70 kg adult, varying with dietary leucine (Nissen S, Abumrad NN, J Nutr Biochem 1997;8:300-11; reviewed PMID 28493406). Roughly 5 percent of whole-body leucine flux is converted to HMB (PMID 28493406), against a studied dose of 3 g/day.
- **Still matters when:** Catabolic and disuse states where the muscle-sparing data are strongest: bed rest, immobilisation, sarcopenia, cachexia, hypocaloric dieting and clinical recovery. In trained, well-fed lifters the effect sizes are small and contested. The axis does not rule HMB out, because supplemental doses are an order of magnitude above endogenous output.

*Refs. Sanchez-Martinez PMID 29249685; Phillips PMID 35818771; Bideshki PMID 39797501*

### Ginkgo biloba

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 240 mg/d of EGb 761, which is the dose that failed in both prevention trials.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody, for preventing dementia. Possibly people with established dementia, on low-certainty evidence.

**Pros**

- The 2026 Cochrane review of 82 studies and 10,613 participants found uncertain benefit for global clinical status at 6 months in people with cognitive complaints, at low certainty.
- Unusually good long-term human safety data for a botanical: 240 mg/d for about 6 years in GEM without a safety signal.

**Cons**

- It is the strongest example in this document of a product that survived its own definitive trials. GEM randomised 3,069 older adults to 240 mg/d for a median 6.1 years: all-cause dementia HR 1.12 (0.94-1.33), Alzheimer disease HR 1.16 (0.97-1.39). The point estimates are above 1.
- GuidAge replicated the null in 2,854 adults over 5 years.
- The bleeding question is genuinely contested rather than settled. A meta-analysis of 18 trials in 1,985 adults found no significant effect on platelet aggregation or coagulation parameters, but case reports of bleeding exist and stopping before elective surgery is prudent.
- Only standardised extracts have any data behind them. Unstandardised leaf products have none.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A leaf extract sold for memory; it is not a nutrient and nothing requires it.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Standardised extracts such as EGb 761 contain about 24% flavone glycosides and 6% terpene lactones, the latter comprising ginkgolides A, B and C plus bilobalide, with ginkgolide B acting as a platelet-activating factor antagonist. The largest prevention trial, the Ginkgo Evaluation of Memory study, randomised 3069 community volunteers aged 75 and over and followed them for a median 6.1 years, finding no reduction in all-cause dementia or Alzheimer disease. Ginkgolides and bilobalide are plant terpenoids absent from human metabolism, so no shortfall is possible from not taking them. The clinically relevant issue is the opposite direction: antiplatelet effect layered onto anticoagulants or aspirin.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Not food
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

The supplement is a concentrated leaf extract, and ginkgo leaf is not a food: it is processed with acetone-water into a standardised extract, of which EGb 761 is the reference, at a drug-to-extract ratio of 35-67:1 containing 22-27% flavone glycosides and 5-7% terpene lactones (2.8-3.4% ginkgolides A, B and C plus 2.6-3.2% bilobalide) with ginkgolic acids held below 5 ppm. The same species does have a real food form, and this is what a flat NOT_FOOD label conceals: ginkgo NUTS are eaten in Japanese chawanmushi, Chinese congee and Korean dishes, and they are chemically a different proposition from the leaf. Seeds carry ginkgotoxin (4'-O-methylpyridoxine and its 5'-glucoside), a vitamin B6 antagonist, at total mature-stage contents of 249-296 ug/g measured across three Chinese samples (249.30, 295.62 and 267.85 ug/g; Gong H et al., J Agric Food Chem 2018;66(30):7916-7922, PMID 29975518). Seeds do contain flavonoids and terpene trilactones occur in several tissues, but nothing in the seed approaches the 24%/6% standardised profile of a 50:1 leaf extract, so the edible organ does not supply the medicinal one.

- **To match the dose:** Clinical dosing is 120-240 mg/day of EGb 761. At a 35-67:1 drug-to-extract ratio that corresponds to 4.2-16 g of dried ginkgo leaf per day, and dried leaf is not something people eat; a leaf tea extracts only part of the terpene lactones and none of the concentration. Via the edible nut the gap cannot be closed at all, because the nut does not carry the leaf profile, and attempting it would be dangerous rather than merely futile: acute poisoning has been reported after 10-50 cooked seeds at one sitting, Hong Kong's Centre for Food Safety advises no more than 10 kernels a day for adults and three for children, and cooking does not destroy ginkgotoxin because it is heat-stable. Adult intoxication with seizures is documented (PMC7007935).
- **Food vs supplement:** Two separate caveats, and both cut against food substitution. First, the safe food (the nut) and the studied medicine (the leaf extract) share no active profile; a bowl of ginkgo-nut congee is not a low dose of EGb 761. Second, the food itself has a hard ceiling: ginkgotoxin blocks pyridoxal-5'-phosphate-dependent enzymes including glutamate decarboxylase, so excess intake causes vomiting, irritability and convulsions, with children at much higher risk. Ginkgotoxin is present in leaf tissue as well, at far lower levels than in seed, and in a typical standardised leaf extract it sits below a 9 ppm limit of detection (Planta Med 2009, PMID 19288402; Toxicon 2021, PMID 34425140; J Nat Prod 2014, PMID 24432981); that 9 ppm figure supports the manufacturing argument better than the false absence claim it replaces. On the extract side, the concentration step is what makes standardisation possible and also what concentrates the hazards manufacturers must strip out: ginkgolic acids are strongly allergenic and are capped at 5 ppm, and unstandardised or adulterated ginkgo products (rutin or quercetin spiking to hit a flavonoid assay) are a recurring market problem. Ginkgo extract also inhibits platelet aggregation and interacts with anticoagulants, and lowers efavirenz and some other drug levels through CYP induction.
- **Absorption:** The two marker classes behave completely differently. Terpene lactones (ginkgolides A and B, bilobalide) are small, reasonably lipophilic and well absorbed after oral dosing of the standardised extract. The flavonol glycosides are not absorbed intact: gut microbiota hydrolyse them to quercetin, kaempferol and isorhamnetin aglycones, which are then heavily glucuronidated and sulfated, so plasma concentrations of the labelled compounds are very low and highly variable between people. This is the practical reason a leaf infusion is not a weak extract: hot water recovers a modest and inconsistent share of the terpene lactones, and the flavonoid fraction ends up as conjugated metabolites regardless. For the nut, the compound with real oral kinetics is ginkgotoxin, which is absorbed efficiently and has been measured in the serum of poisoning patients.
- **Does the body make it:** Standardised extracts contain flavonol glycosides of quercetin and kaempferol plus the terpene trilactones ginkgolide A, B, C, J and bilobalide, a group of molecules found only in Ginkgo biloba. No human tissue produces flavonoids or ginkgolides, so intake is entirely exogenous. That removes any deficiency-correction claim and leaves the outcome trials, which have been largely negative for preventing cognitive decline.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Flavonoids arise from the plant phenylpropanoid pathway and the terpene trilactones from plant terpenoid biosynthesis unique to Ginkgo biloba; neither pathway exists in humans.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway)
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis. Screen on the negative large prevention trials, extract standardisation (EGb 761 type: 24 percent flavone glycosides, 6 percent terpene lactones), and bleeding risk with antiplatelet and anticoagulant drugs.

*Refs. GEM DeKosky PMID 19017911; GuidAge Vellas PMID 22959217; Cochrane Wieland PMID 41641880*

### Valerian

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Roughly 300-600 mg of extract 30-120 minutes before bed. No effective dose is established.
- **Who it is for:** On present evidence, nobody reliably.

**Pros**

- The umbrella review characterises it as having a good safety profile, which is the strongest positive statement the evidence supports.
- The 2006 review found a statistically significant benefit on a dichotomous improved-sleep outcome, relative risk 1.8 (1.2-2.9).

**Cons**

- The 2006 authors reported publication bias, considerable heterogeneity and variable preparations in the same paper that produced the positive number. The positive number does not survive its own caveats.
- The 2024 umbrella review of 8 systematic reviews concluded there is NO evidence of efficacy for the treatment of insomnia, with subjective improvements not corroborated by objective measures.
- The AASM guideline suggests clinicians NOT use valerian for sleep-onset or sleep-maintenance insomnia.
- Additive sedation with benzodiazepines, Z-drugs, barbiturates, opioids, antihistamines and alcohol. Case reports of hepatotoxicity exist, mostly with multi-herb combination products.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A sedative root preparation; nothing in the body is built from it or needs it.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Valeriana officinalis root yields valerenic acid, valerenol and a variable mixture of valepotriates whose content differs widely between commercial products and degrades on storage. Valerenic acid binds a distinct site on GABA-A receptors that depends on the beta2 and beta3 subunits: the N265M point mutation in those subunits abolishes the drug response, and valerenic acid and valerenol are anxiolytic in mouse elevated plus-maze and light/dark tests. Human sleep trials are small, short and inconsistent, in part because extracts are not standardised to any single constituent. No human pathway synthesises or consumes these compounds, so there is no requirement for them.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Not food
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Valerian is the root and rhizome of Valeriana officinalis, a European perennial. It is not a food in any cuisine; it is a bitter, strongly odorous drug taken as a tea, tincture or dried-extract capsule. The constituents credited with the sedative effect, valerenic acid and its relatives plus the flavonoids 6-methylapigenin, hesperidin and linarin, are found in Valeriana species and nowhere in the food supply. Valerian root has been used at low levels as a flavouring in some beverages, which puts trace amounts into a few products, and those are flavour levels, orders of magnitude below anything sedative. Nothing you would call a food supplies valerenic acid.

- **To match the dose:** Typical use is 400-900 mg of dried root extract before bed, or 2-3 g of dried root as an infusion. A quality target of at least 3 mg/g valerenic acid in the dried root has been recommended for commercial material and marketed extracts are sold around 0.8% valerenic acid, so a night's dose is roughly 3-8 mg of valerenic acid. No food at any portion size supplies measurable valerenic acid, so there is no achievable eating quantity to compute. The gap is total rather than large.
- **Food vs supplement:** Valerian is the textbook example of a herb where the preparation determines the chemistry. Valerenic acid concentrations fall significantly during storage, with the greatest loss at 30 C in low humidity (Food Chemistry 2009, changes in valerenic acid content of valerian root during long-term storage), so an old capsule and a fresh one are different products. Valepotriates present in some preparations are unstable and degrade to baldrinals, and aqueous versus ethanolic extraction recovers quite different fractions, which is one reason valerian trials are so inconsistent. Practical cautions: additive sedation with alcohol, benzodiazepines and antihistamines; occasional paradoxical stimulation; rare hepatotoxicity reports, usually in multi-herb products. A valerian tea and a standardised extract capsule are not interchangeable at the same nominal gram amount.
- **Absorption:** Human data are limited. Valerenic acid is detectable in plasma after oral dosing with a short half-life of roughly one to two hours, which fits its use as a bedtime agent rather than a daytime one, and the flavonoid constituents undergo extensive glucuronidation. Because there is no food form there is no food-matrix comparison; the meaningful variable is extraction, since valerenic acid is lipophilic and is recovered far better by ethanol than by a water infusion, so a tea delivers a small and poorly characterised fraction of what a labelled milligram amount of extract implies.
- **Does the body make it:** Valerian's activity is attributed to valerenic acid and the valepotriates, sesquiterpenes whose carbon skeleton is built by a plant terpene synthase (valerenadiene synthase) found in Valeriana officinalis roots. Humans use farnesyl diphosphate for sterols, dolichol, the polyprenyl side chain of ubiquinone (CoQ10), the farnesyl group of heme A and protein farnesylation and geranylgeranylation, and have no sesquiterpene cyclase to close a valerenane ring. So nothing in a valerian capsule is endogenous, a deficiency framing is unavailable, and the modest and inconsistent insomnia evidence has to stand on its own.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Farnesyl diphosphate is cyclised by the plant sesquiterpene synthase valerenadiene synthase in Valeriana officinalis, then oxidised to valerenic acid. Human farnesyl diphosphate goes to sterols, dolichol, ubiquinone, heme A and prenylated proteins; there is no human sesquiterpene cyclase.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway)
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis. Screen on the weak and heterogeneous insomnia trial data, wide variation in valerenic acid content between products, and additive sedation with alcohol and benzodiazepines.

*Refs. Valente umbrella review PMID 38359657; Bent PMID 17145239; Sateia AASM PMID 27998379*

### Rhodiola rosea

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Commercial convention is 200-600 mg/d of extract standardised to about 3% rosavins. There is no evidence-based dose.
- **Who it is for:** Possibly people with subjective mental fatigue, on evidence too weak to justify confidence.

**Pros**

- Three of five randomised trials on mental fatigue were positive, so there is a signal, however unreliable.

**Cons**

- The systematic review screened 206 articles, included 11 trials, and found that ALL of them exhibit either a high risk of bias or reporting flaws that hinder assessment of their true effect. No two studies reported the same outcome, so meta-analysis was impossible.
- Only 2 of 6 physical-fatigue trials were positive.
- The premium adaptogen positioning has essentially no regulatory-grade support. EFSA has authorised no health claim.
- No upper limit can be stated because dose-ranging and toxicity data do not exist. Theoretical additive serotonergic effects with SSRIs, SNRIs and MAOIs, and activation risk in bipolar disorder.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

An Arctic root extract sold for fatigue; the body neither makes nor requires it.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Rhodiola rosea rhizome supplies the rosavins (rosavin, rosin, rosarin) and salidroside, and the best studied preparation, SHR-5, is standardised to roughly 3% rosavins and 1% salidroside. Proposed actions include weak monoamine oxidase inhibition and blunting of stress-induced cortisol responses, neither well characterised in humans. A 2021 systematic review of 39 randomised trials spanning four Rhodiola species (23 of R. rosea, 8 of R. crenulata, 8 of R. wallichiana and 1 of R. sacra, the last excluded from quality assessment) found that no study met all CONSORT criteria, so reporting quality is too poor to support firm efficacy conclusions and trials of different species are routinely conflated. An earlier review restricted to 11 placebo-controlled trials of R. rosea alone judged methodological quality moderate or good (5 of 10 scored above 3 on Jadad) and concluded the species may benefit physical performance, mental performance and certain mental-health conditions, its stated limitation being the absence of independent replication. These are plant secondary metabolites with no endogenous counterpart and no dietary requirement.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Not food
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

The supplement is the rhizome and root of Rhodiola rosea (roseroot), an arctic-alpine succulent in the Crassulaceae, standardised on rosavins (rosavin, rosin, rosarin) and salidroside. The rhizome is a folk-medicine drug of Scandinavia and Russia and is not eaten as food. The plant is not entirely outside the food record: young leaves and shoots have been eaten raw or cooked like spinach and stems cooked like asparagus in Greenland, Alaska and northern North America, and Inuit use mixed leaves and buds with marine fat. That food use involves the aerial parts while rosavins are rhizome constituents, and composition data for the edible shoots are sparse, so the culinary history does not translate into a dietary source of the marker compounds. Rosavins are also close to unique to R. rosea, which is why species substitution is such a persistent problem.

- **To match the dose:** Typical dosing is 200-600 mg/day of extract standardised to 3% rosavins and 1% salidroside (the SHR-5 pattern), roughly 6-18 mg rosavins and 2-6 mg salidroside per day. The dried-rhizome equivalent depends entirely on the content assumed, and the 0.01-3.08% rosavin / 0.07-2.91% salidroside range cited here is COMMERCIAL PRODUCT content rather than measured rhizome, so raw-material content is not established from that source. Stating the assumption explicitly: at 1-3% rosavins, 0.2-1.8 g of dried rhizome carries a daily dose; at 0.1-0.5%, 1.2-18 g; at the 0.01% floor of the cited range, 60-180 g. The submission's flat 0.5-2 g figure silently assumed the top of the range and is optimistic by up to two orders of magnitude. On any assumption this is a herbal dose of a root and no food supplies it, and the edible young shoots cannot be costed at all because the composition work is on rhizome material.
- **Food vs supplement:** Rhodiola has an unusually bad identity record. An analysis of US-market Rhodiola supplements found rosavin and salidroside contents ranging from 0.01% to 3.08% and 0.07% to 2.91% respectively, with substantial departures from label claims, alongside heavy metal and pesticide residue screening (The quality and safety of Rhodiola rosea supplements on the U.S. market, PLoS One 2026, PMC12810810, verified). Those are finished-product figures, not raw-material figures, a distinction the submission blurred. Rhodiola crenulata is a common substitute; it contains salidroside but little or no rosavin, so a product standardised only on salidroside can be a different species. Wild R. rosea is under harvesting pressure and CITES-listed in Appendix II. Field material varies enormously by clone and site, with inter-clonal variation of 92.8% reported for salidroside in Norwegian collections, so growing region and harvest season change the product more than the label does.
- **Absorption:** Salidroside is absorbed rapidly and hydrolysed to tyrosol, and rosavins are cinnamyl glycosides subject to gut microbial deglycosylation, so plasma exposure reflects metabolites rather than the labelled markers; controlled human pharmacokinetic data are limited and no reliable percentage figure can be given. Because there is no food form, the practical absorption question is the extraction solvent: hydroalcoholic extracts recover both rosavins and salidroside, while water infusions of the root recover the glycosides preferentially. Chewing raw shoots, the only genuinely food-like route, has never been characterised.
- **Does the body make it:** The marker compounds are salidroside, a tyrosol glucoside, plus the cinnamyl glycosides rosavin, rosin and rosarin, all made in the rhizome. Tyrosol itself occurs endogenously in humans as a metabolite of tyramine (tyramine to 4-hydroxyphenylacetaldehyde to tyrosol), and hydroxytyrosol arises the same way from dopamine, so the aglycone is present in humans; what is missing is the specific UDP-glucosyltransferase that forms the salidroside O-glucoside, since human phase II conjugation of phenols runs to glucuronides and sulfates, and there is no route to the cinnamyl glycosides. NONE stands for salidroside and the rosavins, so the axis gives neither a reason to take it nor a reason to reject it and the judgement falls entirely to clinical evidence.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for salidroside and the rosavins. Plant phenylpropanoid and shikimate routes in Rhodiola rosea rhizome produce tyrosol, salidroside and the rosavins. Tyrosol is endogenous in humans via tyramine, but no human glucosyltransferase forms the salidroside O-glucoside and no human enzyme assembles the cinnamyl glycosides.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway for salidroside or the rosavins)
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis. Screen on standardisation (typically 3 percent rosavins and 1 percent salidroside), frequent species substitution in commerce, and the modest and inconsistent trial evidence for fatigue and exercise performance.

*Refs. Ishaque BMC Complement Altern Med PMID 22643043*

### Lion's mane (Hericium erinaceus)

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 3,000 mg/d of dried fruiting body for 16 weeks in the pivotal trial. Fruiting body and mycelium contain different compounds and are not interchangeable.
- **Who it is for:** Possibly older adults with mild cognitive impairment, on the basis of a single 30-person trial.

**Pros**

- It is a food mushroom with a long history of dietary use, so the baseline safety expectation is reasonable.
- In the pivotal trial, dementia screening scores improved at weeks 8, 12 and 16.

**Cons**

- The pivotal trial randomised 30 Japanese adults, 15 per arm, to a dementia SCREENING scale. Scores then fell significantly 4 weeks after stopping. There is no meta-analysis and no trial with a hard endpoint.
- The only other cited trial is explicitly self-described as a pilot.
- This is one of the largest gaps between marketing volume and evidence volume in the entire document. The neurogenesis claim rests on in-vitro nerve growth factor stimulation.
- Most retail products do not disclose whether they contain fruiting body or mycelium, so you cannot match the product to the trial.
- Mushroom allergy is a real contraindication. Theoretical antiplatelet effects are uncharacterised in humans.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

An edible mushroom studied for nerve growth effects; no bodily process depends on it.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Hericium erinaceus fruiting bodies contain hericenones and the mycelium contains erinacines, both of which stimulate nerve growth factor synthesis in cultured cells, with erinacine A shown to cross the blood-brain barrier in animals. The main human trial randomised 30 Japanese adults aged 50 to 80 with mild cognitive impairment to 3 g/day of dried powder or placebo for 16 weeks; cognitive scores improved on treatment and drifted back towards baseline during washout. As a culinary mushroom it contributes ordinary food nutrients such as beta-glucans, potassium and B vitamins, in the same way any mushroom does. The hericenones and erinacines themselves are not nutrients, are not made by human tissue, and no physiological process requires them.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** Low
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

This is the one entry whose supplement organism is an ordinary culinary food. Hericium erinaceus is sold fresh in Asian markets and increasingly in Western ones, cooked like scallops or shredded into stir-fries, and it is the same organism used to make the fruiting-body supplement. The best-known human trial used no exotic extract: Mori and colleagues gave 3 g/day of dried Hericium powder, four 250 mg tablets containing 96% dry powder three times daily, to older adults with mild cognitive impairment (Phytother Res 2009;23(3):367-372, PMID 18844328, verified). RICH describes this one species in its several presentations (fresh, dried powder, dried slices), which are one food rather than several independent sources. The important limit is by tissue: the newer and more mechanistically interesting trials use erinacine-A-enriched MYCELIUM, and no amount of the edible fruiting body supplies erinacines. Other edible Hericium species exist, notably H. coralloides and H. americanum, which matters here because the composition paper the submission cited was about H. coralloides rather than H. erinaceus.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lion's mane (Hericium erinaceus), fresh fruiting body, raw before cooking | 8-11 g dry mushroom matter (fresh H. erinaceus is broadly 89-92% water) | 100 g, a generous side portion or about one third of a medium fruiting body | 8-11 g dry matter | roughly 270-370% of the 3 g/day dried-powder dose used in the Mori MCI trial | Moisture given as the published H. erinaceus band of 89-92% rather than a point value. The submission's 91.68% came from J Fungi 2025;11(11):785 (PMID 41295165 / PMC12653868), which is titled Domestication Cultivation and Nutritional Analysis of Hericium CORALLOIDES and measured only a wild Tibetan H. coralloides strain (SH001); that species misattribution has been removed and no H. erinaceus primary moisture measurement was re-traced in this pass, so this range is indicative. Trial dose from Mori et al., Phytother Res 2009;23(3):367-372, PMID 18844328 (verified). The polysaccharide content the submission attached to this row (0.40-0.47 g per 100 g fresh, derived from 4.85-5.68% of dry weight) has been withdrawn because that range is not present in the cited paper. |
| Lion's mane (Hericium erinaceus), dried powder or dried slices, the same food in a dehydrated form | 100 g dried mushroom material (H. erinaceus dry-weight composition: protein 20.08 g, fat 5.1 g, carbohydrate 61.1 g, ash 6.8 g per 100 g) | 1 tablespoon powder (5 g), or 10 g of dried slices rehydrated to roughly 100 g | 5 g dried material per tablespoon; 10 g dried per slice portion | 167% of the 3 g/day trial dose per tablespoon of powder; 333% per 10 g of dried slices | Proximate values are genuine H. erinaceus figures but appear in J Fungi 2025;11(11):785 (PMC12653868) only as a literature comparison quoted from that paper's reference [29]; they were not measured there and the original source was not traced in this pass, so they are indicative for provenance. That paper's own measured values are for H. coralloides (protein 15.4 +/- 1.2, fat 3.5 +/- 0.5, carbohydrate 64.3, ash 6.8 +/- 0.8 g/100 g DW) and are not used here. Trial dose from Mori et al., PMID 18844328 (verified). This row consolidates the submission's separate powder and dried-slice rows, which were one food in two presentations. |

- **To match the dose:** For the fruiting-body dose there is essentially no gap. At 8-11% dry matter, the 3 g/day of dried powder used in the Mori trial is about 27-38 g of fresh lion's mane, a small side portion, or 3 g of dried slices, or a rounded half-tablespoon of powder. A 100 g fresh portion supplies 8-11 g of dry matter, roughly 270-370% of that dose. The gap reappears with a concentrated extract: 3 g/day of a nominal 8:1 hot-water extract corresponds to about 24 g of dried mushroom, roughly 218-300 g of fresh mushroom a day, which is a lot of mushroom and still not absurd. For erinacine-A products the answer is NO at any portion size, because erinacines are mycelial and the fruiting body sold for cooking does not contain them at meaningful levels.
- **Food vs supplement:** The compound classes split cleanly by tissue and by solvent, and this is where most consumer confusion lives. Hericenones C-H occur in the fruiting body; erinacines A-I occur in the mycelium, with erinacine biosynthesis genes consistently downregulated in fruiting-body tissue and no detectable erinacine production there (Fungal Biol Biotechnol 2025, PMC11969743, verified). The Taiwanese trials in early Alzheimer's disease and in depression used erinacine-A-enriched mycelium capsules at around 5 mg/g erinacine A, three 350 mg capsules a day giving 1,050 mg mycelia and about 5.25 mg erinacine A over 49 weeks (PMC7283924, verified), so their results cannot be transferred to eating the mushroom, and equally the Mori fruiting-body result cannot be transferred to a mycelium product. Solvent matters as much as tissue: hericenones are lipophilic and are recovered by ethanol while hot-water extraction recovers polysaccharides, so a dual extract and a hot-water extract deliver different chemistry at the same milligram figure. The specific polysaccharide numbers the submission used to make that point (4.85-5.68% of dry weight for the fruiting body and 3.62% beta-glucan for a hot-water extract) are withdrawn here, because neither is present in the paper cited for them. Many cheap US supplements are mycelium grown on grain and sold with the substrate included, so a large fraction of the capsule weight is starch; a starch assay or a low beta-glucan number relative to labelled polysaccharide is the tell. Safety is good for a food, with occasional contact dermatitis and respiratory allergy reports, and scattered case reports of skin reactions with supplement use.
- **Absorption:** The fungal beta-glucans are not absorbed intact; a water-soluble 1-3/1-6 beta-glucan of about 13.3 kDa has been characterised from the fruiting body, and such polymers act on gut and gut-associated immune receptors rather than entering circulation, so how much reaches the blood is the wrong question for the polysaccharide fraction. The small molecules are the ones with systemic potential: erinacine A is lipophilic, crosses the blood-brain barrier in rodents and is the compound behind the neurotrophic claims, while hericenones are also lipophilic and their human absorption is poorly characterised. Practically, fat in the pan helps the hericenone fraction, a water-based soup extracts the polysaccharides, and the raw mushroom is not the right comparator for either since chitinous cell walls need heat to break down. The relevant food-versus-supplement difference here is which molecules are present at all rather than absorption efficiency.
- **Does the body make it:** The compounds of interest are hericenones from the fruiting body, polyketide-terpenoid hybrids (meroterpenoids) built on a polyketide-derived resorcinol or benzaldehyde core carrying an added prenyl or geranyl chain and a fatty acyl group, and erinacines, cyathane diterpenoids, from the mycelium. These are fungal metabolites; human cells produce neither, and human cells also do not need them for anything. Any benefit has to come from the pharmacology of an ingested fungal compound, and the human evidence is one small trial in mild cognitive impairment (30 subjects, 16 weeks of dosing) with scores falling back 4 weeks after stopping.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Hericenones are fungal polyketide-terpenoid hybrids (meroterpenoids) and erinacines are cyathane diterpenoids, both made in Hericium erinaceus; no human pathway to either.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway)
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis. Screen on the thin human evidence base (30 subjects, 16 weeks, benefit lost 4 weeks after cessation), whether a product is fruiting body or grain-based mycelium, and the fact that nerve growth factor induction is a cell and rodent finding rather than a human one.

*Refs. Mori PMID 18844328; Li PMID 32581767*

### Bacopa monnieri

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 300-320 mg/d of extract standardised to 50-55% bacosides, for at least 12 weeks. It is not an acute agent.
- **Who it is for:** Healthy adults willing to take it daily for 12 weeks for a modest change in processing speed.

**Pros**

- The meta-analysis restricted inclusion to chronic dosing of 12 weeks or more, which is methodologically sound, and found two statistically significant results.

**Cons**

- Read the actual endpoints. The two significant results across 9 trials and 437 analysable subjects were a Trail Making Test B change of -17.9 MILLISECONDS and a choice reaction time change of about 10 ms. Millisecond-scale processing speed is being sold as memory and learning.
- The meta-analysis authors explicitly said larger comparative trials are needed before any recommendation.
- Nothing supports a memory or dementia effect.
- Gastrointestinal adverse effects (nausea, cramping, increased stool frequency) are the commonest problem. Cholinergic activity means caution with donepezil, rivastigmine and galantamine, and with anticholinergics. Animal work suggests thyroid hormone effects, so care with levothyroxine.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A herb used for memory; the body makes no use of its active compounds.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Bacopa monnieri contains triterpenoid saponins known as bacosides, and extracts are standardised to bacoside content, typically 20 to 55%. A systematic review of randomised placebo-controlled trials, all of which ran for 12 weeks, found the most consistent signal in delayed word recall, with attention, working memory and other domains inconsistent across studies. Proposed mechanisms include cholinergic modulation, dendritic branching and antioxidant activity in hippocampus, drawn largely from rodent work. Bacosides are plant secondary metabolites: they are absent from human tissue, no enzyme system requires them, and gastrointestinal upset is the common practical limitation on dosing.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Bacopa monnieri (brahmi, water hyssop) is a real leafy vegetable in parts of India, which makes it the exception among the herbal entries here. Ethnobotanical surveys in West Bengal's Terai-Dooars, Maharashtra, Assam and northeast India record it among wild edible greens gathered and cooked, with tender shoots and leaves eaten fresh, cooked as a green, or made into chutneys and raita, so bacosides genuinely enter the food supply in a way withanolides and valerenic acid do not. RICH here describes concentration, since a culinary serving reaches a fifth to a half of the clinical dose, and it is worth being explicit that this is one regional green rather than several ordinary foods: the availability is narrow and geographically limited, which is a different axis from concentration and is where the submission's TRACE label went wrong. Note the naming trap: in parts of India brahmi refers to Centella asiatica, a different plant with different actives (asiaticosides), and market material is frequently mixed.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bacopa monnieri (brahmi) dried aerial herb / churna powder, elite cultivar DBM-4 | 654 mg of bacoside A3 plus bacopaside II (1.57 mg/g bacoside A3 and 4.97 mg/g bacopaside II in dry herbage) | 1 teaspoon dried powder (2 g) | 13.1 mg bacosides | about 9% of the roughly 150 mg of bacosides in a standard 300 mg dose of a 50-55% bacoside extract | ICAR-Directorate of Medicinal and Aromatic Plants Research, Anand, variety release note for Bacopa monnieri DBM-4 (2020), https://icar.org.in/node/13442. Both figures, the DBM-4 designation and the CIM-Jagriti comparison were verified exactly; the verification pass called this the best-sourced food figure in the submission. Accession-level bacoside A survey in Industrial Crops and Products, PII S0926669021011134, gives the surrounding variation. |

- **To match the dose:** Trials use 300 mg/day of extract standardised to 50-55% bacosides, roughly 150-165 mg of bacosides. At the measured 6.54 mg/g for the two quantified bacoside constituents, that is about 23 g of dried brahmi herb per day, roughly eleven teaspoons of churna, which nobody eats in a day. A 2 g teaspoon of dried powder supplies 13.1 mg, about 9% of the dose. Converting to fresh greens requires a fresh-to-dry ratio the cited sources do not give, so only the dried figure is quoted: a realistic culinary serving of the fresh green, the quantity that goes into a thoran or a chutney, corresponds to single-digit grams of dry matter and therefore to a few tens of milligrams of bacosides, a fifth to a half of the trial dose. Eating brahmi as a vegetable is on the same scale as supplementing it without matching it, which is not true of anything else in this list except lion's mane. Total bacoside A also includes bacopasaponin C and a jujubogenin isomer that the cited analysis does not quantify, so the real herb content is somewhat higher than 6.54 mg/g and the gap correspondingly smaller.
- **Food vs supplement:** Bacoside content in the herb varies several-fold with accession, season and harvest, which is exactly why breeding programmes select elite lines such as DBM-4, so a wild-gathered green is an unpredictable dose. Extraction matters too: bacosides are saponins recovered by hydroalcoholic extraction, and boiling a green in water for a curry leaches an unknown fraction into cooking liquid that may be discarded. The best-studied products (CDRI-08, BacoMind) are specific standardised extracts and their cognitive trial results should not be read across to eating the plant. Bacopa also has real dose-dependent gastrointestinal effects, cramping, nausea and loose stools, more prominent with the concentrated extract than with the vegetable, and it has cholinergic activity that could matter alongside cholinesterase inhibitors. Watch for Centella asiatica sold as brahmi, and for heavy metal accumulation, since Bacopa is a wetland plant with documented capacity to take up metals from sediment.
- **Absorption:** Bacosides are large triterpenoid saponin glycosides with poor membrane permeability and low oral bioavailability; the pharmacologically relevant species are thought to be the deglycosylated sapogenins (jujubogenin and pseudojujubogenin derivatives) generated by gut hydrolysis, which is why supplement makers push phospholipid-complex and nanoparticle formulations. Eating the whole green delivers the same glycosides embedded in a fibrous matrix with fat from the cooking medium, and it has never been compared head to head with an extract in humans, so the fresh-vegetable route is plausible and uncharacterised. The saponins themselves also have surfactant behaviour in the gut, which is part of why higher doses cause GI upset.
- **Does the body make it:** The active fraction is the bacosides, dammarane-type triterpenoid saponins from the plant. Humans cyclise 2,3-oxidosqualene through the protosteryl cation to lanosterol and then to cholesterol and have no dammarenediol synthase, so the dammarane skeleton is never formed at all, and no human glycosyltransferase attaches the bacoside sugar chains. There is no endogenous pool to top up, so the case rests on the cognition trials, which show a small effect on memory acquisition after weeks of dosing.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Plant triterpenoid saponin biosynthesis in Bacopa monnieri routes 2,3-oxidosqualene through the dammarenyl cation via dammarenediol synthase, then glycosyltransferases. Human oxidosqualene cyclase routes the same substrate through the protosteryl cation to lanosterol, so no dammarane intermediate exists in humans.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway)
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis. Screen on the small measured effect (chiefly delayed word recall), the 12 week timescale before any effect appears, bacoside standardisation, and gastrointestinal side effects.

*Refs. Kongkeaw PMID 24252493*

### 5-HTP

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 50-300 mg/d commercially. No authoritative dose exists because no usable trial established one.
- **Who it is for:** No group can be identified with confidence.

**Pros**

- The Cochrane meta-analysis produced a Peto odds ratio of 4.10 (1.28-13.15) with an NNT of 2.78, which sounds spectacular.

**Cons**

- That number comes from 64 patients in 2 trials out of 108 located. Cochrane located 108 trials and could use two. The confidence interval spans a tenfold range and the authors refused to stand behind it, concluding that because effective and safe alternative antidepressants exist, the clinical usefulness of 5-HTP and tryptophan is limited at present.
- The eosinophilia-myalgia syndrome question is unresolved. The 1989 outbreak was linked to contaminated L-tryptophan and Cochrane flagged that the possibility of a similar contaminant in 5-HTP products has not been excluded.
- Serotonin syndrome risk with SSRIs, SNRIs, MAOIs including the irreversible ones, tricyclics, tramadol, triptans, linezolid, lithium and dextromethorphan. This is a direct serotonin precursor, so the risk is mechanistic rather than theoretical.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

The chemical step between tryptophan and serotonin; the body makes its own supply.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** 5-hydroxytryptophan is the intermediate between the essential amino acid tryptophan and serotonin: tryptophan hydroxylase produces it, and aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase converts it onward to serotonin. Supplemental 5-HTP bypasses the rate-limiting hydroxylation step and escapes diversion into the kynurenine pathway, which is why it raises serotonin synthesis more directly than tryptophan does, including in peripheral tissues where that is not wanted. Ordinary food supplies essentially none of it; the dietary requirement is for tryptophan, and that is where any nutritional argument belongs. Impaired central serotonin synthesis in tetrahydrobiopterin disorders and in aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase deficiency is an inborn error of metabolism, entirely separate from intake, and 5-HTP is prescribed or withheld in those conditions on genetic grounds.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** None
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Body makes it

5-hydroxytryptophan is not a dietary constituent. In living tissue it exists only as a transient intracellular intermediate between tryptophan and serotonin, immediately decarboxylated by aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase, so it does not accumulate in any food at measurable levels. The commercial article is a purified single molecule, either extracted from the seeds of Griffonia simplicifolia, a West African climbing shrub whose seeds are an unusual botanical accumulator at 156 mg/g dry weight (about 16%) in a chemically characterised reference sample against literature concentrations of 10-20%, or, increasingly, produced by microbial or enzymatic biosynthesis. Griffonia seed is a pharmaceutical raw material rather than a food, which is why SYNTHETIC fits better than the NOT_FOOD label used for botanical drugs such as valerian or ashwagandha. The foods listed below are tryptophan sources, a different molecule one step upstream, scored against the WHO/FAO/UNU 2007 adult requirement of 4 mg tryptophan per kg per day (280 mg/day for a 70 kg adult) rather than against any 5-HTP dose.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Chicken breast, meat only, roasted | 362 mg tryptophan | 1 breast fillet, cooked (120 g) | 434 mg | 155% of the 280 mg/day adult tryptophan requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 171477. Verified: 0.362 g per 100 g (protein 30.9 g). |
| Salmon, Atlantic, farmed, cooked (dry heat) | 248 mg tryptophan | 1 fillet (150 g) | 372 mg | 133% of the 280 mg/day requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 175168. Verified: 0.248 g per 100 g (protein 22.1 g). |
| Cheddar cheese | 547 mg tryptophan | 1 thick slice / small matchbox (40 g) | 219 mg | 78% of the 280 mg/day requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 173414. Verified: 0.547 g per 100 g (protein 22.87 g). The verification pass flagged that this is 2.4% of protein, well above casein's roughly 1.3% tryptophan, so the USDA figure itself is likely an imputation artefact; the value is reported as the database gives it. |
| Pumpkin and squash seed kernels, dried | 576 mg tryptophan | 2 tablespoons (30 g) | 173 mg | 62% of the 280 mg/day requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 170556. Verified: 0.576 g per 100 g (protein 30.2 g). |
| Egg, whole, raw, fresh | 167 mg tryptophan | 1 large egg (50 g) | 84 mg | 30% of the 280 mg/day requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 171287. Verified: 0.167 g per 100 g, 0.083 g per 50 g egg (the 84 mg is rounding). |
| Milk, whole, 3.25% milkfat | 40 mg tryptophan | 1 glass, 200 mL (206 g) | 82 mg | 29% of the 280 mg/day requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 171265. Verified: 0.040 g per 100 g (protein 3.15 g). |

- **To match the dose:** For 5-HTP itself the gap is absolute: no food contains it in measurable quantity, so no eating quantity reproduces the 50-300 mg/day used in trials. From the manufacturing raw material, 100 mg of 5-HTP sits in about 0.64 g of Griffonia seed at 156 mg/g, a striking number and irrelevant to eating, since Griffonia seed is not a food and carries other constituents. Via the dietary precursor the arithmetic runs deceptively the other way. An ordinary mixed diet supplies roughly 900-1000 mg of tryptophan a day, three to twenty times a 5-HTP dose by mass, and a single chicken breast supplies 434 mg, yet that intake does not act like 5-HTP for three reasons. Over 95% of tryptophan is degraded down the kynurenine pathway by TDO and IDO, with only about 1-2% passing through tryptophan hydroxylase to serotonin (PMC5398323; PMC9292703). Tryptophan competes with the other large neutral amino acids, leucine, isoleucine, valine, phenylalanine and tyrosine, for the LAT1 transporter at the blood-brain barrier, and every protein food raises tryptophan and its competitors together, so a high-protein meal can leave the brain tryptophan ratio flat or lower. And tryptophan hydroxylase is the regulated rate-limiting step, so raising substrate does little. 5-HTP sidesteps all three: it is past the rate-limiting hydroxylation and is decarboxylated by ubiquitous AADC. Adding 300 mg of tryptophan to a meal is therefore not equivalent to 300 mg of 5-HTP, and no realistic quantity of food makes it so.
- **Food vs supplement:** Bypassing the pathway's regulation is what makes 5-HTP a drug rather than a nutrient. AADC is present throughout peripheral tissues, so much of an oral 5-HTP dose is converted to serotonin outside the central nervous system, which produces the characteristic nausea and is why clinical protocols historically paired 5-HTP with a peripheral decarboxylase inhibitor such as carbidopa. Combined with SSRIs, SNRIs, MAOIs, tramadol, triptans or linezolid, 5-HTP carries a genuine serotonin syndrome risk that no amount of dietary tryptophan does. There is also a contamination history worth naming: the 1989 eosinophilia-myalgia syndrome epidemic was traced to a contaminant in bacterially produced L-tryptophan, and a related peak X impurity has been detected in some 5-HTP preparations, so purity of a purified plant extract or fermentation product is not a given. Note also that commercial 98% 5-HTP claims are computed on identified compounds rather than on seed weight, and one commercial extract labelled 95% assayed at about 65%. Griffonia seed should not be eaten as a food substitute; the whole seed carries other constituents and is not characterised for dietary safety. Nothing about the tryptophan-rich foods above raises comparable concerns; they are food, and the warm-milk folklore is folklore. Separately, the food table here is narrower than it should be: it contains no legume, no plant-protein concentrate and no cheese richer than cheddar, and dried spirulina, soybeans and soy protein isolate, Parmesan, sesame seeds, turkey, tuna, oats and peanuts are all absent. No figures for them could be verified in this pass, so they are named rather than tabulated, but their absence makes the requirement look harder to meet than it is.
- **Absorption:** The whole point of 5-HTP is that its absorption route differs from its precursor's. 5-HTP is absorbed from the small intestine and crosses the blood-brain barrier without competing significantly for the LAT1 large-neutral-amino-acid carrier, so plasma and brain 5-HTP rise roughly in proportion to dose, unlike tryptophan whose brain entry depends on the tryptophan-to-LNAA ratio rather than on absolute intake. Dietary tryptophan absorption from protein foods is efficient at the gut level, over 90%, and the losses that matter are metabolic rather than absorptive: hepatic TDO is induced by cortisol and by tryptophan itself, and IDO is induced by inflammation, so illness, stress and high-protein intake all divert precursor toward kynurenine. This is why the classic dietary manoeuvre for raising brain tryptophan is a carbohydrate load with little protein, using an insulin-driven fall in circulating branched-chain amino acids to improve the ratio, an effect measured in single-digit percentage changes and nothing like a 100 mg 5-HTP capsule.
- **Does the body make it:** Every serotonin-producing cell makes its own 5-HTP continuously from dietary tryptophan, and none of it accumulates because aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase converts it straight to serotonin. The recovered row labelled it INTERMEDIATE because 5-HTP is an obligate transient intermediate with no storage pool, no dietary requirement and no measurable body pool; on this enum it rolls up to FULL, since the human pathway is complete and covers basal need in health. Tryptophan hydroxylase is the rate-limiting and regulated step, and swallowing 5-HTP forces the pathway past that control point, producing serotonin wherever aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase sits, mainly the gut, where most body serotonin already is.
- **Pathway:** L-tryptophan to 5-HTP by tryptophan hydroxylase (TPH1 in gut and pancreatic enterochromaffin cells, pinealocytes, mast cells, mononuclear leukocytes, spleen, thymus, pancreatic beta cells and peripheral tissues including heart, lung, kidney, duodenum, adrenal and female reproductive tract; TPH2 in raphe neurons), then 5-HTP to serotonin by aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase. TPH is rate-limiting and subsaturated, because its Km for tryptophan is near or above actual tissue tryptophan concentrations. The liver is not a TPH1 site: hepatic serotonin comes from platelets and gut.
- **Amount made:** NOT ESTABLISHED. 5-HTP is an obligate transient intermediate with no storage pool, and whole-body synthesis flux has not been quantified in humans. Flux anchor for scale: only about 1 to 2 percent of tryptophan flux enters the hydroxylase branch and over 90 percent goes down the kynurenine pathway, so a 100 mg oral dose sits far above the physiological branch it feeds.
- **Still matters when:** Only where bypassing tryptophan hydroxylase regulation is the deliberate aim, and preferably with a peripheral decarboxylase inhibitor such as carbidopa to steer conversion centrally, as used in research settings. Otherwise NEVER ON THIS AXIS: tryptophan intake is the physiological lever, and unopposed 5-HTP alongside an SSRI or MAOI is a serotonin toxicity risk.

*Refs. Cochrane Shaw PMID 11687048*

### Vitamin K2 (MK-7 and MK-4)

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** MK-7 180-720 µg/d in trials. MK-4 45 mg/d in the Japanese fracture trial, roughly 250 times the MK-7 dose.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody with solid evidence of clinical benefit.

**Pros**

- The carboxylation mechanism is real: MK-7 does carboxylate osteocalcin and matrix Gla protein, and the biomarkers move.
- Knapen 2013 (n=244 healthy postmenopausal women, 180 µg/d for 3 years) significantly slowed the age-related decline in bone mineral content and density at lumbar spine and femoral neck. Surrogate endpoint, and not at the total hip.
- No adverse effect level has been identified, so no upper limit exists.

**Cons**

- The core sales pitch, that D3 without K2 calcifies your arteries, is a marketing construct. No randomised trial has ever shown that vitamin D supplementation causes vascular calcification which K2 then prevents.
- The calcification claim has been tested and failed twice. AVADEC: 720 µg MK-7 plus vitamin D for 24 months in 365 men with aortic valve calcification, progression 275 versus 292 AU, no difference. VitaK-CAC: 360 µg MK-7 for 2 years in 167 coronary patients, coronary calcium rose from 135 to 184 AU on MK-7 anyway.
- MK-4 is worse. The impressive vertebral-fracture odds ratios in K2 marketing trace to small Japanese menatetrenone trials, and when the hypothesis was tested properly the OF study (n=4,378, 45 mg/d, 36-48 months) was NULL on new vertebral fractures. One frequently cited supporting paper has been RETRACTED for research misconduct.
- MK-4 has essentially no oral bioavailability at supplement doses. A single 420 µg dose of MK-4 was not detectable in the serum of any subject at any time point, while the same dose of MK-7 peaked at 6 hours and stayed detectable for 48 hours.
- Absolute practical contraindication with warfarin and other vitamin K antagonists. MK-7 has a long half-life, so even modest doses destabilise INR for days to weeks.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Activates proteins that clot blood and that steer calcium into bone rather than artery walls.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** Every form of vitamin K works as the cofactor for gamma-glutamyl carboxylase, the enzyme that converts specific glutamate residues into gamma-carboxyglutamate (Gla), giving those proteins the ability to bind calcium and function. In the liver this activates clotting factors II, VII, IX and X together with the anticoagulant proteins C, S and Z, which is the function the dietary requirement is set on. Outside the liver the same reaction activates osteocalcin in bone and matrix Gla protein in vascular smooth muscle, matrix Gla protein being a local inhibitor of arterial calcification. MK-7 differs from phylloquinone (K1) mainly in pharmacokinetics: a long isoprenoid side chain gives it a circulating half-life of days rather than hours, so it reaches extrahepatic tissue more consistently. MK-4 is the form tissues synthesise for themselves from K1 or menadione via UBIAD1, and as a supplement it clears within hours. Whether raising menaquinone intake above the amount that maintains normal carboxylation changes bone or vascular outcomes is a question about supplementation, separate from the vitamin's essential role.
- **Deficiency disease:** Vitamin K deficiency bleeding (VKDB), historically called haemorrhagic disease of the newborn. No deficiency disease is attributed to menaquinones specifically: intake recommendations are set for total vitamin K and are based on phylloquinone, and no separate K2 requirement has been established.
- **First signs:** A rising PIVKA-II (undercarboxylated prothrombin) is the earliest coagulation marker, followed by a prolonged prothrombin time and raised INR. Clinically, easy bruising, oozing from puncture or venepuncture sites, nosebleeds, bleeding gums or heavy menstrual loss. Undercarboxylated osteocalcin rises before any of the clotting markers, but on its own it is a biochemical finding whose clinical significance is unsettled.
- **Untreated:** In newborns, classic VKDB appears in the first week as bleeding from the umbilical stump, gastrointestinal tract, circumcision site or puncture wounds. Late VKDB appears at 2 to 12 weeks and is the dangerous form: roughly half of cases present with intracranial haemorrhage, causing death or lasting neurological injury. In adults with severe malabsorption, cholestasis or prolonged broad-spectrum antibiotics plus poor intake, deficiency produces spontaneous gastrointestinal, retroperitoneal or intracranial bleeding.
- **Does it reverse:** The coagulopathy itself corrects completely and quickly: parenteral vitamin K restores clotting factor activity within hours, and prothrombin complex concentrate acts immediately. What does not reverse is the injury from a bleed that has already happened, and intracranial haemorrhage in late VKDB leaves permanent neurological deficit or death in a substantial proportion of affected infants. This is why prophylaxis at birth matters more than treatment.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Newborns above all, who are born with low stores, receive little vitamin K in breast milk and have an immature gut flora. Intramuscular vitamin K at birth prevents this, and declining or omitting that injection is the main cause of late VKDB in high-income countries today.
