Beyond infancy: fat malabsorption, cholestasis and biliary obstruction, cystic fibrosis, extensive small bowel resection, and people on vitamin K antagonists such as warfarin, in whom the deficiency is induced on purpose. Dietary vitamin K deficiency in a healthy adult is rare, because phylloquinone is abundant in green vegetables and the liver recycles it efficiently.
- **How common:** Without prophylaxis, classic VKDB affects an estimated 0.25% to 1.7% of newborns and late VKDB occurs at roughly 4.4 to 7.2 per 100,000 births, the figures used by CDC and the AAP (PMID 24226627). Shearer's surveillance review gives 4 to 7 cases per 100,000 births for Europe and separately reports substantially higher rates from Southeast Asia (PMID 18804903). No population prevalence figure is established for clinical vitamin K deficiency in healthy adults, where it is rare. Undercarboxylated osteocalcin and inactive matrix Gla protein are detectable in a large share of adults, but that is a biomarker observation with no defined clinical deficiency threshold.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Mixed
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** Low
- **Body makes it:** Partly

The two menaquinones need separate answers, because one merged label buries the whole point. MK-7 is genuinely rich in food, at 939 µg/100 g in natto, and that richness rests on exactly one food that most of the target population never eats; every other measured MK-7 source is trace, with cheddar at 2.7 and butter at 4.2 µg/100 g. MK-4 is trace in food across the board, at roughly 1 to 22 µg/100 g in chicken, egg yolk, full-fat dairy and meat, with goose liver paste as the one outlier at about 369 µg/100 g, while the 45 mg clinical dose is synthetic-only, since MK-4 supplements are manufactured from menadione because no food is or could be concentrated to 45 mg per dose.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Natto (fermented soybeans), MK-7 | 939 µg MK-7 | 1 standard pack (50 g) | 470 µg MK-7 | 261% of a 180 µg MK-7 capsule; 522% of a 90 µg capsule | Kamao M et al., J Nutr Sci Vitaminol 2007;53(6):464-70, PMID 18202532, DOI 10.3177/jnsv.53.464. Confirmed exactly against the PubMed record, including journal, year, volume and pages: 'MK-7 was observed characteristically in fermented soybean products such as natto (939 microg/100 g)' |
| Goose liver paste, MK-4 *[unconfirmed]* | about 369 µg MK-4 | 1 serving on toast (40 g) | 148 µg MK-4 | 0.33% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 9.8% of a 1.5 mg low-dose MK-4 pill | Schurgers and Vermeer food table value, widely cited |
| Chicken, barbecue (raw and cooked), MK-4 *[unconfirmed]* | 22.1 µg MK-4 (range 13.6 to 31.6, n=10) | 1 thigh/leg portion (120 g) | 26.5 µg MK-4 | 0.06% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 1.8% of a 1.5 mg pill | Elder SJ, Haytowitz DB, Howe J, Peterson JW, Booth SL, J Agric Food Chem 2006;54(2):463-7, PMID 16417305, Table 1. Citation confirmed exactly; the value is |
| Chicken liver, pan-fried, MK-4 *[unconfirmed]* | 12.6 µg MK-4 pan-fried (14.1 µg raw) | 1 serving, pan-fried (100 g) | 12.6 µg MK-4 | 0.03% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 0.84% of a 1.5 mg pill | Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305, Table 1 |
| Ground beef, medium fat, MK-4 *[unconfirmed]* | 8.1 µg MK-4 raw (5.1 to 7.2 µg broiled) | 1 burger (100 g) | 8.1 µg MK-4 on the raw basis; 5.1 to 7.2 µg as eaten | 0.018% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose on the raw basis; 0.011% to 0.016% as eaten | Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305, Table 1 |
| Egg, whole, fried, MK-4 *[unconfirmed]* | 9.0 µg MK-4 fried (5.6 µg fresh raw, 7.0 µg hard-cooked) | 1 large egg (50 g) | 4.5 µg MK-4 | 0.01% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 0.3% of a 1.5 mg pill | Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305, Table 2 |
| Cheddar cheese, full fat, MK-4 | 9.5 ± 0.4 µg MK-4 (10.2 µg in Elder et al.); total vitamin K of hard cheese 282 ± 5.0 µg/100 g, dominated by MK-9 to MK-11 | 1 matchbox piece (30 g) | 2.9 µg MK-4 | 0.006% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 0.19% of a 1.5 mg pill | Fu X et al., Curr Dev Nutr 2017;1(6):e000638, PMID 29955705, DOI 10.3945/cdn.117.000638; Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305. The Fu citation and its cheese totals were confirmed exactly against PubMed; the MK-4 sub-fraction itself was not individually verified |
| Heavy cream, MK-4 | 9.3 ± 0.8 µg MK-4 | 2 tablespoons (30 g) | 2.8 µg MK-4 | 0.006% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 0.19% of a 1.5 mg pill | Fu X et al., Curr Dev Nutr 2017;1(6):e000638, PMID 29955705; citation confirmed, this individual value not separately verified |
| Egg yolk, fresh, raw, MK-4 *[unconfirmed]* | 15.5 µg MK-4 | 1 large yolk (17 g) | 2.6 µg MK-4 | 0.006% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 0.18% of a 1.5 mg pill | Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305, Table 2 |
| Whole milk, MK-4 *[unconfirmed]* | 1.0 µg MK-4 (0.7 to 1.3 µg in later analyses) | 1 glass (200 ml) | 2.0 µg MK-4 | 0.004% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 0.13% of a 1.5 mg pill | Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305, Table 2 |
| Butter, MK-4 and MK-7 *[unconfirmed]* | 10.0 µg MK-4 and 4.2 µg MK-7 (100.25 and 41.64 ng/g) | 1 pat (10 g) | 1.0 µg MK-4 and 0.4 µg MK-7 | 0.002% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 0.2% of a 180 µg MK-7 capsule | Determination of Vitamin K2 Content of Dairy Products Produced in Hatay Region, Natural and Engineering Sciences 2022, DOI 10.28978/nesciences.1036844, Table 2 |
| Cheddar cheese, MK-7 *[unconfirmed]* | 2.7 µg MK-7 (26.78 ng/g) | 1 matchbox piece (30 g) | 0.8 µg MK-7 | 0.4% of a 180 µg MK-7 capsule | Natural and Engineering Sciences 2022, DOI 10.28978/nesciences.1036844, Table 2 |
| Salmon, raw, Alaska wild, MK-4 *[unconfirmed]* | 0.3 µg MK-4 | 1 fillet (130 g) | 0.4 µg MK-4 | under 0.001% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose | Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305, Table 1 |

- **To match the dose:** MK-7 and MK-4 give opposite answers. MK-7 at supplement and trial doses of 90 to 180 µg/day is fully covered by food: one 50 g pack of natto carries 470 µg, so a pack every second or third day matches a 180 µg capsule. Nothing else comes close, since 180 µg/day from cheddar at 2.7 µg MK-7 per 100 g would take 6.7 kg of cheese daily and from butter about 4.3 kg. MK-4 is the reverse. The Japanese clinical dose, menatetrenone for osteoporosis, is 45 mg/day (45,000 µg), usually 15 mg three times daily. At the richest food value in the composition literature, goose liver paste at about 369 µg/100 g, that is 12.2 kg per day, and at barbecue chicken's 22.1 µg/100 g it is about 204 kg. Even a modest 1.5 mg consumer MK-4 pill needs 407 g of goose liver paste, 6.8 kg of chicken, or 150 kg of whole milk. Measured whole-diet MK-4 intake sits around 17 µg/day (7.3% of a mean total vitamin K intake of about 230 µg/day in Japanese women, Kamao 2007), roughly one 2,600th of the pharmacological dose. MK-4 at supplement strength is unreachable by eating.
- **Food vs supplement:** MK-7 supplements are made by Bacillus subtilis natto fermentation, the same organism that makes natto, or synthesised; the active species is all-trans MK-7, and cis-isomer contamination is a documented quality problem in commercial products that the fermented food does not have. MK-4 supplements are wholly synthetic, derived from menadione, because no food is or could be concentrated to 45 mg per dose. That said, one framing in the original entry was wrong and has to go: goose liver paste at about 369 µg/100 g puts 100 g of food within roughly 2.1 orders of magnitude of the 45 mg dose (1/122 of it), so the claim that no food comes within three orders of magnitude fails, even though the dose stays unreachable at 12.2 kg/day. Natto also brings the fermentation matrix, fibre and nattokinase, and circulating K2 after eating natto is around ten times circulating K1 after eating spinach, a comparison made with 200 g of natto against 400 g of spinach rather than gram for gram (Schurgers LJ, Vermeer C, Haemostasis 2000;30:298-307, PMID 11356998). The 'cheese is a K2 food' claim needs unpicking: cheese total vitamin K is genuinely high, at 282 ± 5.0 µg/100 g for hard cheese, 289 ± 38 for semi-soft, 440 ± 41 for blue and 506 ± 63 for soft, but roughly 90% of it is MK-9, MK-10 and MK-11 from starter bacteria (Fu et al. 2017, PMID 29955705), forms no clinical trial has tested, while the MK-7 fraction is a few µg/100 g. Cheddar is the weak exemplar for that argument, and the long-ripened Dutch and Norwegian cheeses (Gouda, Edam, Jarlsberg) are the canonical MK-9-rich ones that would test it properly; curd cheese and quark appear as K2 sources in the Schurgers and Vermeer paper itself, and tempeh, fermented soy pastes, Korean cheonggukjang, sauerkraut and fermented salami would show whether natto is uniquely high or simply the best measured. None of those could be tabulated for want of a figure. Animal MK-4 also tracks the animal's feed, since menadione is a routine poultry feed additive and Elder et al. attribute poultry MK-4 to alkylation of it, so brand, country and production system shift the numbers, and the chicken hot dog and chicken broth samples in the same study contained no detectable MK-4 at all.
- **Absorption:** MK-4 and MK-7 behave so differently that microgram-for-microgram comparison is meaningless. A single 420 µg dose of MK-7 was well absorbed, peaked at 6 hours and remained detectable in serum for 48 hours, while the identical 420 µg dose of MK-4 was undetectable in the serum of all subjects at every time point, and seven consecutive days at 60 µg/day raised only MK-7 (Sato T et al., Nutr J 2012;11:93, PMID 23140417, DOI 10.1186/1475-2891-11-93, every element confirmed). MK-7's long half-life comes from its extended isoprenoid side chain and tight lipoprotein binding. That is the mechanistic reason MK-4 is dosed in milligrams while MK-7 works in micrograms, and it means the 1 to 22 µg/100 g of MK-4 in butter, egg yolk, cheese and chicken contributes almost nothing to circulating vitamin K2 even though it is real and measurable. All menaquinones need dietary fat for absorption, which is why they sit in and are absorbed from the fat phase of dairy and eggs, and the reduced-fat versions lose most of it, with reduced-fat cheddar at 1.8 µg MK-4 per 100 g against 9.5 µg for full fat (PMID 29955705, whose fat-dependence finding was confirmed).
- **Does the body make it:** Humans cannot make the naphthoquinone ring, so vitamin K itself is strictly dietary, while UBIAD1 prenylates menadione derived from dietary phylloquinone (K1) to MK-4 in most tissues, which means the MK-4 in artery wall, bone and brain is already partly endogenous. MK-7 and the longer chains MK-7 to MK-13 are bacterial products that humans do not synthesise, and the colonic contribution is modest and hard to quantify because much of it stays unabsorbed. PARTIAL is the roll-up for the pair: supplementing MK-4 duplicates a conversion the body already runs on ordinary K1 intake, and the honest case for an MK-7 product is pharmacokinetic (a much longer half-life than K1) and dietary, for people eating little natto, cheese or leafy greens.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for the ring, since humans have no de novo vitamin K pathway. Conversion only: dietary phylloquinone K1 or menadione plus geranylgeranyl-PP to MK-4 by UBIAD1 in most tissues, after side-chain removal from phylloquinone. Function runs through the vitamin K cycle, where GGCX gamma-carboxylates glutamate residues in prothrombin and factors VII, IX, X, in osteocalcin and in matrix Gla protein, and VKORC1, the warfarin target, regenerates the hydroquinone form. Long-chain menaquinones MK-7 to MK-13 are made by colonic bacteria.
- **Amount made:** MK-4 formed endogenously in mg/d: NOT ESTABLISHED, and no established figure exists for the microbial contribution either. Vitamin K AI 120 micrograms/day for men and 90 micrograms/day for women as phylloquinone, with no UL set (IOM 2001). Typical supplement doses MK-4 1.5-45 mg/d and MK-7 90-360 µg/d, with common MK-7 products providing 90 to 180 micrograms/day.
- **Still matters when:** Newborns, who receive prophylactic vitamin K (K1 by injection) to prevent haemorrhagic disease of the newborn, and documented deficiency from fat malabsorption, cholestasis, cystic fibrosis, coeliac disease or bariatric surgery. Also prolonged broad-spectrum antibiotics with poor intake, and VKORC1 or GGCX defects. Bone and vascular calcification protocols exist but the trial evidence is mixed, and anyone on warfarin must not change vitamin K intake without clinician input, because the vitamin directly opposes the drug. Redundant as a way of obtaining MK-4 in anyone with adequate K1 intake and functioning UBIAD1.

*Refs. AVADEC Diederichsen PMID 35465686; VitaK-CAC JAMA Cardiol 2026; Knapen PMID 23525894; OF study Inoue PMID 19082528; Sato PK PMID 23140417; retracted PMID 21778673*

### Generic multi-strain probiotic blends

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** Marketed as 50 billion CFU and similar. The dose threshold that matters is at least 5 billion CFU/d, but only for the two specific indications.
- **Who it is for:** See the strain-specific entry in the Consider tier. A blend with no strain designators has nothing behind it.

**Pros**

- If a blend happens to contain a strain with evidence at an adequate dose, it will work for that indication. The problem is you cannot tell from the label.

**Cons**

- The category claim is unfalsifiable marketing. Probiotic describes a delivery concept, not an active ingredient. NCCIH states the point plainly: if one Lactobacillus helps prevent an illness, that does not necessarily mean another kind of Lactobacillus will.
- Disaggregate 82 randomised trials and 10,332 irritable bowel syndrome patients by strain, and GRADE certainty collapses to low or very low almost everywhere. Only 24 of the 82 trials were at low risk of bias.
- Raw CFU counts are close to meaningless divorced from strain. The infant colic strain works at 100 million CFU/d, which is 100 to 1,000 times LOWER than the counts marketed on adult tubs.
- The same contraindications apply as for any live organism: central venous catheter, neutropenia, severe immunocompromise, critical illness, preterm infants.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Mixed bacteria sold without strain identification, so no trial evidence attaches to the contents.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** These products name only genus and species (Lactobacillus acidophilus, Bifidobacterium bifidum and similar) and omit the alphanumeric strain designation on which the entire clinical evidence base is built, so no published result can legitimately be attributed to what is in the capsule. Independent testing repeatedly finds discrepancies between labelled and actual colony-forming units, species misidentification, and organisms no longer viable at the end of shelf life. Adding more strains does not sum their individual effects; strains can antagonise one another through bacteriocins and niche competition, and mixtures are rarely tested as mixtures. Because this is a commercial product category rather than a nutrient, no requirement and no deficiency state exists.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** n/a

RICH applies to one endpoint only, and it is a redefined one: live lactic acid bacteria of common genera. On that endpoint several ordinary fermented foods match or exceed supplement label doses, with yoghurt surveyed at up to 10^9 CFU/g, kefir at 2.5x10^8 CFU/mL, unpasteurised sauerkraut at about 2x10^8 CFU/g, kimchi at 10^7.14 to 10^9.23 CFU/g and natto reported at roughly 10^8-10^9 CFU/g. On the endpoint that matters for a product claim, a defined blend of characterised strains at a stated dose, no food supplies it and the honest grade would be a flat no. A reader scanning this field should not read RICH as 'food replaces this product'. The named-strain entry in this same group states that a CFU comparison is meaningless for a strain-specific claim, and that remains true here.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kimchi, cabbage, retail | 7.14-9.23 log CFU/g lactic acid bacteria | 1 cup (150 g) | 2x10^9 to 2.5x10^11 CFU | from roughly equal to 250x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Rezac S, Kok CR, Heermann M, Hutkins R. Front Microbiol 2018;9:1785, PMID 30197628, DOI 10.3389/fmicb.2018.01785, Table 5 (South Korean retail), verified; portion weight USDA FDC ID 170392. |
| Natto (fermented soybean, unpasteurised) *[unconfirmed]* | roughly 10^8-10^9 CFU/g Bacillus subtilis var. natto | 1 cup (175 g) | roughly 1.8x10^10 to 1.8x10^11 CFU | roughly 2-175x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Viable count range as supplied in the verification report from the natto literature; no specific study was retrieved in this pass, so the figure is |
| Kefir, milk, fermented with milk kefir grains | 8.40 log CFU/mL lactic acid bacteria (2.5x10^8/mL) plus 8.00 log CFU/mL yeasts at 48 h | 1 cup (244 mL) | about 6x10^10 lactic acid bacteria plus about 2x10^10 yeasts | 6-60x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Outeirino EB et al., Foods 2026;15(10):1616, DOI 10.3390/foods15101616, PMID 42195820, verified (counts at 48 h, down from 8.64 and 6.32 log at 24 h); portion weight USDA FDC ID 171265. |
| Sauerkraut, unpasteurised (raw, refrigerated) | 8.3 log CFU/g lactic acid bacteria (commercial, starter culture); 7 log CFU/g in spontaneous fermentation | 1 cup (142 g) | about 3x10^10 CFU at 8.3 log | 3-30x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Rezac S et al., PMID 30197628, Table 5, verified; portion weight USDA FDC ID 169279. That USDA entry is canned sauerkraut, which is pasteurised and contains no live cells. |
| Yoghurt, plain, with live cultures | below 10^4 to 10^9 CFU/g across surveyed products; commonly around 10^8 CFU/g | 1 cup (245 g) | roughly 2x10^10 CFU at 10^8 CFU/g | 2-20x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Rezac S et al., PMID 30197628, DOI 10.3389/fmicb.2018.01785, range verified; portion weight USDA FDC ID 171284. |
| Tempeh | 7.01 log CFU/g lactic acid bacteria in laboratory-made product; 81% of shop, production and restaurant samples above 10^7 CFU/g | 1 serving (100 g) | about 1x10^9 CFU | roughly 0.1-1x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Rezac S et al., PMID 30197628, Table 6, verified. The submission's 3-9 log range could not be found in the cited review, so the 10^11 per-portion ceiling and the 0.1-100x span have been withdrawn and 10^7 CFU/g is used as the demonstrated floor for most samples. |
| Miso | 2.1-7.1 log CFU/g aerobic bacteria (supermarket samples) | 1 tbsp (17 g) | up to about 2x10^8 CFU, and as low as 10^3 | at best about 2-20% of a 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Rezac S et al., PMID 30197628, DOI 10.3389/fmicb.2018.01785, range verified; portion weight USDA FDC ID 172442. |

- **To match the dose:** For total live cells there is no gap. The source review calculates that about 10^10 cells is the intake likely needed to affect the microbiota and that this is met by 100 g of a food carrying 10^8 cells/g (PMID 30197628). One cup of kefir, one cup of yoghurt, about 120 g of raw sauerkraut, a cup of kimchi or a cup of natto each clear that bar, and kimchi at the top of its measured range delivers more live organisms in one serving than most 50-billion-CFU capsules. The gap is qualitative: identity, characterisation and consistency. Fermented food counts vary across four to six orders of magnitude between products and batches (yoghurt below 10^4 to 10^9 CFU/g), the organisms are usually not identified to strain, and none of them are the strains on the label of the blend.
- **Food vs supplement:** Fermented food is a reasonable dietary habit and a different proposition from a named strain at a defined dose. Even a generic blend tells you which species you are getting and roughly how many; a jar of sauerkraut tells you neither, and a pasteurised jar contains no live cells. The practical points that decide whether a food delivers anything: it must be labelled raw, unpasteurised or 'live cultures' and kept refrigerated; heat destroys the organisms, so miso added to boiling soup, cooked kimchi and baked tempeh contribute essentially nothing; and long-aged cheeses can be several orders of magnitude lower than young ones. Water kefir, kombucha, brine-fermented unpasteurised pickles, kvass, skyr, traditional buttermilk and young raw-milk cheeses belong on the same list, though no confirmed counts for them were available here. Conversely, a supplement claim of 50 billion CFU means little if the strains are unnamed, because probiotic effects are demonstrated strain by strain, and CFU counts on labels refer to manufacture rather than end of shelf life.
- **Absorption:** Gastric and bile survival is strain-dependent and matrix-dependent. Fermented dairy foods buffer stomach acid and their fat content improves transit survival, a genuine advantage of the food route over an uncoated capsule; supplements compensate with enteric coating or microencapsulation. Almost none of these organisms colonise permanently, so the effect is dose-dependent and ceases when intake stops, which applies equally to food and to capsules. The source review notes that many fermentation microbes are closely related to probiotic organisms and can share mechanisms, which is the honest basis for recommending fermented food, but relatedness is not equivalence and it does not license transferring a strain-specific trial result onto a jar of kimchi.
- **Does the body make it:** Same axis mismatch as any probiotic, since these are living organisms, with an additional problem specific to blends: labels that name only genus and species carry no evidence, because probiotic effects are strain-specific. Laboratory checking of commercial products supports that concern, though the cited evidence is one survey: Kesavelu 2020 tested 20 products sold in India (8 single-strain, 12 multi-strain) and reported poor correlation between label and laboratory values for viable count, genus and species identification and contaminants, without giving a numeric mismatch rate. A blend cannot inherit the trial record of a product it does not actually contain, so more strains on the label does not mean more evidence.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Microorganisms are acquired, not synthesised.
- **Amount made:** 0 (human cells cannot synthesise a microorganism)
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis. Screen on strain-level identification with deposited strain designations, independent verification of viable counts at end of shelf life, and whether any trial used the exact formulation being sold.

*Refs. Goodoory & Ford PMID 37541528; NCCIH; Cochrane CD006095.pub5 PMID 40931979*

### B. infantis 35624 (single strain)

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 1 billion CFU/d, one capsule, assessed at 4 weeks. This is the trialled dose, not a dose shown to work.
- **Who it is for:** On current evidence, no clearly identifiable group.

**Pros**

- Composite probiotic products containing B. infantis showed small effects on abdominal pain (SMD 0.22) and bloating (SMD 0.30). Those effects belong to the composite, not to this strain.

**Cons**

- This is the clearest case of the strain-specificity argument cutting AGAINST the industry. It is the product that taught consumers to look for a strain number, and the dedicated meta-analysis of 5 studies found the SINGLE strain had no effect on abdominal pain, bloating or bowel habit satisfaction.
- The whole reputation rests on one 2006 trial plus an anti-inflammatory mechanism story about the IL-10 to IL-12 ratio.
- The AGA recommends against probiotics for irritable bowel syndrome outside clinical trials, which is this product primary market.
- Bifidobacterium species specifically have caused whole-genome-sequencing-confirmed bacteraemia in preterm infants.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A single gut bacterium with its own trial evidence in irritable bowel syndrome.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Bifidobacterium longum subsp. infantis 35624, still widely labelled Bifidobacterium infantis 35624, was isolated from healthy human gut mucosa and is one of the few probiotic strains tested at a defined dose in a trial large enough to matter. In 362 primary care women with irritable bowel syndrome of any bowel habit subtype, 1 x 10^8 cfu daily for 4 weeks was superior to placebo for abdominal pain and for a composite of bloating, bowel dysfunction, incomplete evacuation, straining and passage of gas, with global symptom improvement exceeding placebo by more than 20%; higher and lower doses did not work, which is itself informative about formulation. Proposed mechanisms include induction of regulatory T cells and reduction in circulating pro-inflammatory cytokines. It is a live organism administered as a therapy, so nobody is short of it and there is no deficiency state to describe.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** None
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** n/a

SYNTHETIC here means no dietary source, not an artificial molecule. The strain is a live organism isolated from the ileal mucosa of a healthy human donor and has since been reclassified from Bifidobacterium longum subsp. infantis to B. longum subsp. longum 35624 (PLOS One 2016;11:e0162983, PMC5033381). It is propagated industrially from a proprietary strain bank and freeze-dried. It is present in no food, no fermented food and no other supplement brand. Comparative genome analysis identified a 35624-specific gene cluster encoding biosynthesis of its exopolysaccharide EPS624, the kind of strain-level distinction that makes substitution unsound.

- **To match the dose:** No quantity of any food supplies this strain. The trial dose is small in absolute terms, 1x10^8 CFU, which is roughly the number of live bacteria in half a gram of unpasteurised sauerkraut at 8.3 log CFU/g, so the dose is not the obstacle and the identity is. Fermented foods can supply a thousand times more live cells than the effective dose of 35624 while supplying none of it. Note that the source trial expressed its doses as CFU/mL rather than CFU/day.
- **Food vs supplement:** The dose-response data make the point better than any argument about food. In the 362-patient dose-ranging trial, 1x10^8 of B. infantis 35624 was significantly superior to placebo for abdominal pain, the composite score, bloating, bowel dysfunction and incomplete evacuation, while 1x10^6 and 1x10^10 were not, the paper attributing the highest dose's failure to significant formulation problems (Whorwell et al., Am J Gastroenterol 2006;101:1581-1590, PMID 16863564). The trial enrolled women only, which limits generalisation and which the submission did not state. If a tenfold change in dose of the same strain in the same capsule abolishes the effect, swapping the strain for a different Bifidobacterium in a yoghurt cannot be assumed to preserve it. Anyone advised to take 35624 should be told plainly that no diet reproduces it and that a generic bifidobacterium supplement is a different product.
- **Absorption:** The strain was selected partly for surviving gastrointestinal transit, and its documented actions are immunoregulatory and barrier-related rather than nutritional: enhanced gut barrier function, accelerated intestinal transit and reduced visceral hypersensitivity. It does not permanently colonise, so the effect depends on continued daily intake. Formulation dominates delivery: the failure of the 1x10^10 arm was a formulation problem rather than evidence against high cell numbers, and it illustrates that CFU on a label is not the active variable on its own. The specific gelling mechanism the submission gave for that failure is not in the source and has been removed.
- **Does the body make it:** This is a live organism, so the synthesis axis does not apply and the body never makes bacteria under any circumstances. Whole-genome sequencing and core-genome phylogenetic taxon assignment reclassified the strain from Bifidobacterium infantis to Bifidobacterium longum subsp. longum 35624, a common adult coloniser, so the infant-coloniser and adult colonisation-gap argument built on the old name does not apply and has been dropped. What distinguishes 35624 is the trial record: a fully identified single strain tested against placebo at a stated dose, which is the standard the rest of the category should be held to, and the reclassification does not affect those strain-specific results.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Acquired by colonisation or ingested as a live product; not synthesised by host tissue. Taxonomy: Bifidobacterium longum subsp. longum by whole-genome core-genome phylogeny, revised from the earlier subsp. infantis assignment.
- **Amount made:** 0 (human cells cannot synthesise a microorganism)
- **Still matters when:** IBS symptom relief on the strength of a randomised trial in 362 primary care IBS patients, all of them women, where 1 x 10^8 cfu/mL for 4 weeks was significantly superior to placebo for abdominal pain and the composite symptom score and exceeded placebo by more than 20 percent on global assessment (P<0.02), while 1 x 10^6 and 1 x 10^10 cfu/mL were not. The 10^10 arm's failure is generally attributed to formulation problems (capsule agglomeration and gelling), so part of that dose conclusion is a formulation conclusion. The effect depends on continued dosing.

*Refs. Yuan PMID 28166427; Goodoory & Ford PMID 37541528; AGA PMID 32531291*

### Krill oil

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 2 g/d for 24 weeks in the knee osteoarthritis trial. Bioavailability trials used about 1.1 g/d of actual omega-3.
- **Who it is for:** At most, people wanting a given rise in omega-3 index from a smaller dose. That is a biomarker advantage.

**Pros**

- The absorption argument has support. A 72-person trial at matched 1.1 g/d found krill oil produced about 1.5-fold higher increments in plasma EPA and DHA than fish oil.
- Contains astaxanthin, which is why it does not oxidise as readily as bulk fish oil.

**Cons**

- The joint claim collapsed under proper testing. The largest randomised trial (JAMA, n=262 with knee osteoarthritis and effusion-synovitis, 2 g/d for 24 weeks) found mean pain change -19.9 with krill oil versus -20.2 with placebo. A trial-sequential-analysis review across 5 trials and 700 patients found no significant improvement in knee pain, stiffness or lipid profile.
- It is priced as a premium product on the strength of a biomarker advantage, and delivers far less EPA and DHA per capsule than a concentrate.
- Crustacean and shellfish allergy is a genuine and under-warned contraindication.
- Same anticoagulant, antiplatelet and atrial fibrillation cautions as any omega-3 source once dose rises.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Oil from Antarctic krill delivering EPA and DHA largely bound to phospholipids, plus some astaxanthin.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Krill oil is extracted from Euphausia superba. Its distinguishing feature is chemical form: a large share of the EPA and DHA is esterified to phospholipid, mainly phosphatidylcholine, with some present as free fatty acid, whereas fish oil carries them as triglycerides or ethyl esters. This alters absorption kinetics. It also contains small amounts of astaxanthin, a carotenoid acting as an antioxidant within the oil. EPA plus DHA content per capsule is typically lower than in fish oil, so dose comparisons need to be made per milligram of omega-3. Acute crossover studies find equal or modestly higher plasma phospholipid incorporation from krill per unit EPA plus DHA (PMID 21854650), but a reanalysis of these studies concluded the acute differences have not translated reliably into different long-term omega-3 status (PMID 25156381).
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Partly

The molecules krill oil delivers, EPA and DHA, are abundant in ordinary oily fish, and a 1-2 g/day krill oil dose is smaller than what a single tin of sardines provides. The phospholipid delivery form is also obtainable from food, which the earlier draft got wrong: fish roe and caviar are membrane-lipid tissues in which a large share of the EPA and DHA is phospholipid-bound, and in lean white fish such as cod most of the (small) EPA+DHA pool sits in phospholipid rather than triglyceride. The triglyceride generalisation holds for fatty fish flesh only. One tablespoon of caviar (16 g, 1,046 mg EPA+DHA) beats a 2 g krill oil dose on both quantity and form. Krill itself is close to a non-food: Antarctic krill (Euphausia superba) is eaten as okiami in Japan and to a small extent in Russia and the Philippines, but the global catch is largely rendered into meal and oil, and the shell carries a heavy fluoride load that has to be stripped before human consumption. Nobody reaches a krill oil dose by eating krill; they reach it by eating fish or roe, or by taking capsules.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Herring, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat | EPA 909 mg, DHA 1,100 mg (total 2,009 mg) | 1 fillet (143 g) | EPA 1,300 mg, DHA 1,573 mg (total 2,873 mg) | 410-1,026% of the EPA+DHA ceiling for a typical 2 g/day krill oil dose (at most 280-700 mg) | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175117 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Mackerel, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat | EPA 504 mg, DHA 699 mg (total 1,203 mg) | 1 fillet (88 g) | EPA 444 mg, DHA 615 mg (total 1,059 mg) | 151-378% of a 2 g/day krill oil dose ceiling | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175120 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Caviar, black and red, granular | EPA 2,740 mg, DHA 3,800 mg (total 6,540 mg) | 1 tablespoon (16 g) | EPA 438 mg, DHA 608 mg (total 1,046 mg) | 149-374% of a 2 g/day krill oil dose ceiling, and it delivers a large share of that as phospholipid, which is the product claim krill oil is sold on | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 174188 (SR Legacy), composition supplied and confirmed by the verification pass |
| Anchovy, European, canned in oil, drained solids | EPA 763 mg, DHA 1,290 mg (total 2,053 mg) | 1 tin, drained (45 g) | EPA 343 mg, DHA 580 mg (total 924 mg) | 132-330% of a 2 g/day krill oil dose ceiling | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 174183 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Sardine, Atlantic, canned in oil, drained solids with bone | EPA 473 mg, DHA 509 mg (total 982 mg) | 1 tin, drained (92 g) | EPA 435 mg, DHA 468 mg (total 903 mg) | 129-323% of a 2 g/day krill oil dose ceiling | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175139 (SR Legacy), confirmed; krill oil EPA+DHA range from PMID 39233519 |
| Fish roe, mixed species, raw | EPA 983 mg, DHA 1,360 mg (total 2,343 mg) | 1 tablespoon (14 g) | EPA 138 mg, DHA 190 mg (total 328 mg) | 47-117% of a 2 g/day krill oil dose ceiling per tablespoon; a 100 g portion of cod or herring roe supplies 2,343 mg, and again a large share of it is phospholipid-bound | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175132 (SR Legacy), composition and 14 g tablespoon supplied and confirmed by the verification pass |

- **To match the dose:** There is effectively no gap, which is the useful finding. Measured EPA plus DHA in 20 commercial krill oils from the Korean market spanned 14.2-34.8% of total fatty acids (Lee 2024, PMID 39233519, DOI 10.4014/jmb.2406.06026, range confirmed; the sampling is one national market). Converting that percentage to capsule mass overstates the content, because total fatty acids are less than capsule mass once phospholipid head groups, free sterols, astaxanthin and excipients are counted, so every figure that follows is a ceiling rather than a point estimate. A 1 g capsule carries at most roughly 140-350 mg EPA+DHA. Two capsules (2 g) deliver at most about 280-700 mg, which is matched or beaten by one 92 g tin of sardines (903 mg), one 45 g tin of anchovies (924 mg), one mackerel fillet (1,059 mg) or one tablespoon of caviar (1,046 mg). At the top of the trial range, 4 g/day of krill oil supplies at most roughly 570-1,400 mg, equal to at most 0.6-1.5 tins of sardines. Because these are ceilings, the real gap runs further in favour of fish than the raw numbers suggest. Going the other way, matching a krill oil dose by eating krill is not a real option: the catch is not sold as food outside a small Japanese and Russian market, and the shell must be removed for fluoride reasons.
- **Food vs supplement:** Two things were previously claimed to separate krill oil from food, and both claims are weaker than stated. The phospholipid form is the product claim, and fish roe and caviar supply phospholipid-bound EPA and DHA in ordinary food, so the honest version is that krill oil offers a high phospholipid share conveniently, not uniquely. Krill oil is reported at roughly 39-81% phospholipid (Xie 2019, DOI 10.1111/1541-4337.12427); the citation is real and topically correct, but the range could not be traced to a page or table in the review, so treat it as indicative and unconfirmed, and do not read the original two-decimal precision as measured. Astaxanthin is the second claim, and 'no oily fish portion supplies it in comparable concentration' should not stand: wild sockeye and other red-fleshed Pacific salmon carry astaxanthin in flesh at roughly 25-40 mg/kg, so a 130 g portion supplies about 3-5 mg against the 0.1-1.5 mg per gram of oil typical of krill capsules. That concentration could not be re-verified, so it is a challenge to the claim rather than a settled figure, but the flat denial is gone. Neither the phospholipid form nor the astaxanthin has been shown to produce a clinical outcome that fish or fish oil does not. Against that, krill oil is the most expensive way to buy EPA and DHA per milligram, capsule doses are small enough that many products deliver less omega-3 than one tin of fish, it is a crustacean allergen, and whole krill or poorly processed krill meal carries the fluoride load of the exoskeleton. The fluoride figures usually quoted are about 4,260 mg F/kg fat-free dry matter in the carapace against 570 mg/kg in raw muscle; neither full text was retrievable, so both values are unconfirmed and should be attributed to whichever paper actually reports them (Soevik & Braekkan, Journal of the Fisheries Research Board of Canada 1979;36(11):1414-1416, DOI 10.1139/f79-204, which was not yet called the Canadian Journal of Fisheries and Aquatic Sciences in 1979; and Tou 2007, PMID 17345959). Sustainability is a genuine consideration given that Antarctic krill is the base of the Southern Ocean food web.
- **Absorption:** The phospholipid form does appear to be absorbed at least as well as triglyceride and better than ethyl ester. In a crossover trial in 12 healthy men at a matched 1,680 mg EPA+DHA dose, incorporation into plasma phospholipids by area under the curve over 0-72 h was 80.03 +/- 34.71 %*h for krill oil, 59.78 +/- 36.75 %*h for re-esterified triglyceride fish oil and 47.53 +/- 38.42 %*h for ethyl ester fish oil, with no statistically significant difference (EPA p = 0.057, DHA p = 0.246, EPA+DHA p = 0.119) (Schuchardt 2011, Lipids Health Dis, PMC3168413, all figures confirmed). Attributing that result to the phospholipid form is not the only reading: the same paper notes that 22% of the krill oil's EPA and 21% of its DHA were present as free fatty acids, which absorb readily and offer a plausible alternative explanation. The practical implication is modest either way, since a krill capsule contains so much less EPA and DHA than a portion of fish that absorption efficiency does not close the gap.
- **Does the body make it:** Taken as a product the roll-up is PARTIAL, because its main actives are the same EPA and DHA the body can only build partially from dietary ALA, with limited EPA synthesis and DHA synthesis that is undetectable in young men. The exception is astaxanthin, which is NONE: vertebrates including humans have no carotenoid biosynthetic pathway and can only cleave or modify what they eat, unlike aphids, spider mites and some other arthropods that synthesise carotenoids using horizontally transferred genes. Nothing in the product duplicates something the body already makes in quantity, so the real caveat is dose rather than redundancy, since krill oil carries less EPA and DHA per gram than concentrated fish or algal oil and the milligrams on the label matter more than the phospholipid-form claim.
- **Pathway:** For EPA and DHA, the same hepatic cascade as any omega-3: ALA → FADS2 (delta-6) → ELOVL5 → FADS1 (delta-5) → EPA → ELOVL5 and ELOVL2 → DPA → ELOVL2 → 24:5n-3 → FADS2 (delta-6) → 24:6n-3 → peroxisomal beta-oxidation → DHA. The Sprecher C24 route is the principal and rate-limiting mammalian pathway; a direct delta-4 desaturase activity of the FADS2 product has been demonstrated in human cells, with its in vivo contribution not established. For astaxanthin: NOT APPLICABLE, humans have no carotenoid biosynthetic pathway and only cleave or modify dietary carotenoids.
- **Amount made:** Same conversion figures as for preformed EPA and DHA, with the same denominator caveat: EPA 21% and DHA 9% of ingested ALA as net fractional interconversion in the plasma tracer pool in young women, DHA undetectable in young men over 21 days, while studies expressing product formation as a proportion of total dietary ALA report DHA under 0.1% and under 0.01%. Endogenous astaxanthin 0 mg/d, and it has no reference intake.
- **Still matters when:** The same situations as preformed EPA and DHA: low or no oily fish intake, pregnancy and lactation, and adult men targeting DHA status. Astaxanthin has no established human requirement, so on this axis it is an added antioxidant claim rather than the correction of any deficit.

*Refs. Laslett JAMA 2024 PMID 38776073; Pimentel PMID 39126570; Loukil PMID 42144109*

### Cod liver oil

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** No established effective dose for any endpoint. The binding constraint is retinol, not omega-3.
- **Who it is for:** Arguably nobody, given modern alternatives.

**Pros**

- Historically it was an effective vitamin D and vitamin A vehicle in populations with little sun and poor diets, and that history is real.
- Natural triglyceride oils absorb well.

**Cons**

- You cannot increase the omega-3 dose without simultaneously increasing preformed vitamin A, which has a real toxicity ceiling of 3,000 µg/d and is teratogenic. That is a dose-limiting design flaw, not a feature.
- As an omega-3 source it is dilute, so reaching a therapeutic dose requires a large volume of oil and therefore a large retinol load.
- Standard obstetric advice in several countries is to avoid liver and liver-oil supplements in pregnancy.
- Typically sold as a bulk, often flavoured liquid, which is the format that performed worst in oxidation surveys. Fermented cod liver oil has no quality advantage.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A fish-liver oil supplying vitamin D, preformed vitamin A and omega-3 fats together.

- **If you lack it:** Mixed product
- **In detail:** Cod liver oil is a food product, oil rendered from the livers of Gadus species, carrying three active constituents in one dose: vitamin D3, preformed vitamin A as retinyl esters, and the long-chain omega-3 fatty acids EPA and DHA. Each has its own physiology (see the vitamin D3 and preformed vitamin A entries; EPA and DHA are incorporated into membrane phospholipids and serve as precursors to eicosanoids and specialised pro-resolving mediators, with DHA concentrated in retinal photoreceptor and neuronal membranes). It has historical standing because it was the agent that treated and then largely eliminated rickets in the polluted industrial cities of the early twentieth century, before vitamin D itself had been identified. Modern products vary widely in content depending on whether the natural vitamins were stripped during refining and re-added, so the label matters. The practical constraint on dosing is the preformed retinol: an amount large enough to deliver a meaningful EPA and DHA intake can push vitamin A toward or past the tolerable upper limit, and the vitamin A here is retinol, not beta-carotene.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Partly

Everything cod liver oil supplies is available from ordinary food: EPA and DHA from oily fish and roe, vitamin D from oily fish, vitamin A from any liver. Cod liver oil is itself a traditional food-grade oil with its own USDA composition entry, which is why it appears in the list below, and it is included as the reference product rather than as an alternative to itself. What makes it distinctive is concentration: one teaspoon (4.5 g) carries 806 mg EPA+DHA, 1,350 ug RAE of preformed vitamin A (4,500 IU) and 11.2 ug of vitamin D (450 IU). Two genuine food alternatives are named without figures because no authoritative per-portion composition value could be obtained in this pass: tinned cod liver, eaten in Nordic, Baltic and Russian cuisine, and liver as a vitamin A source. Rather than estimate them, no row is given.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Herring, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat | EPA 909 mg, DHA 1,100 mg (total 2,009 mg) | 1 fillet (143 g) | EPA 1,300 mg, DHA 1,573 mg (total 2,873 mg) | Equivalent to 3.6 teaspoons of cod liver oil for EPA+DHA, with none of the vitamin A load | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175117 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Caviar, black and red, granular | EPA 2,740 mg, DHA 3,800 mg (total 6,540 mg) | 1 tablespoon (16 g) | EPA 438 mg, DHA 608 mg (total 1,046 mg) | Equivalent to 1.3 teaspoons of cod liver oil for EPA+DHA, again with no preformed vitamin A load | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 174188 (SR Legacy), composition supplied and confirmed by the verification pass |
| Sardine, Atlantic, canned in oil, drained solids with bone | EPA 473 mg, DHA 509 mg (total 982 mg) | 1 tin, drained (92 g) | EPA 435 mg, DHA 468 mg (total 903 mg) | Equivalent to 1.1 teaspoons of cod liver oil for EPA+DHA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175139 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Fish oil, cod liver (the reference product itself, taken by spoon as traditionally sold, not a food alternative to it) | EPA 6,900 mg, DHA 11,000 mg (total 17,900 mg); vitamin A 30,000 ug RAE (100,000 IU); vitamin D 250 ug (10,000 IU) | 1 teaspoon (4.5 g); 1 tablespoon = 13.6 g | 1 tsp: EPA 310 mg, DHA 495 mg (total 806 mg), vitamin A 1,350 ug RAE, vitamin D 11.2 ug. 1 tbsp: EPA 938 mg, DHA 1,496 mg (total 2,434 mg), vitamin A 4,080 ug RAE, vitamin D 34 ug | 1 tsp = 322% of one day's 250 mg EPA+DHA AI, 45% of the 3,000 ug/day vitamin A UL and 11% of the 100 ug/day vitamin D UL. 1 tbsp = 136% of the vitamin A UL on its own | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173577 (SR Legacy), every figure including both household measures and both vitamin unit conversions confirmed exact; ULs from EFSA (vitamin A, NDA Panel 2024; vitamin D, NDA Panel 2023), not re-verified in this pass |

- **To match the dose:** For the EPA and DHA at a traditional teaspoon dose there is no gap, and food matches it easily: 806 mg per teaspoon is a little under one 92 g tin of sardines (903 mg) and about a quarter of a herring fillet (2,873 mg). Cod liver oil is also efficient in the other direction: 250-500 mg/day of EPA+DHA needs only 1.4-2.8 g of oil, a third to two thirds of a teaspoon, carrying 419-838 ug RAE of vitamin A, comfortably inside the 3,000 ug/day UL. The gap that opens at pharmacological doses is a toxicity gap rather than a volume one. Reaching 4 g/day of EPA+DHA takes 22.3 g of cod liver oil, about 1.6 tablespoons, delivering 6,704 ug RAE of preformed vitamin A every day, 2.2 times the EFSA adult UL. Traditional cod liver oil therefore cannot be dose-escalated to the level used in triglyceride-lowering trials, and food cannot reach 4 g/day either (see the Omega-3 entry). That is what plain fish oil and purified concentrates exist for.
- **Food vs supplement:** This is the clearest case in this group where the concentrated product is hazardous in a way the food behaviour does not suggest. The vitamin A is preformed retinol, so it accumulates and is teratogenic. Anyone already taking a multivitamin, or pregnant, can exceed the 3,000 ug RAE/day UL on one tablespoon alone (4,080 ug). Doubling up cod liver oil with a separate vitamin D supplement is the other common stacking error. Many modern products labelled cod liver oil are refined with the vitamin A and D partly stripped and re-standardised, so the USDA generic entry above describes traditional oil and not necessarily what is in a given bottle: the label governs. Contaminants concentrate in liver, which is why the EU sets a separate limit for fish liver and derived products (20.0 pg WHO-TEQ/g wet weight) alongside the 1.75 pg/g fat dioxin and 6.0 pg/g fat dioxin plus dioxin-like PCB limits for marine oils under Regulation (EU) 2023/915. Those limit values were not re-verified in this pass. Commercial cod liver oil is refined to meet them; home-rendered or artisanal liver oil is not.
- **Absorption:** Cod liver oil is a natural triglyceride and was one of the natural-triglyceride comparators assigned the 100% reference index in the Dyerberg comparison, against 124% for re-esterified triglyceride and 73% for ethyl ester (PMID 20638827, both comparator values confirmed). Its fat-soluble vitamins come dissolved in the oil, which is the ideal vehicle, and that is precisely why the vitamin A is so efficiently absorbed and why overdose is easy. There is no absorption advantage over eating oily fish for the EPA and DHA.
- **Does the body make it:** As a product the roll-up is PARTIAL, since cod liver oil bundles three actives with three different verdicts and the body covers only part of what is in the bottle. Its EPA and DHA are partly synthesisable from dietary ALA (PARTIAL), its vitamin D3 is genuinely manufactured in your own skin from 7-dehydrocholesterol whenever UVB reaches it, with cutaneous synthesis alone able to meet requirement and no risk of toxicity because previtamin D3 photodegrades and CYP24A1 clears the active hormone (FULL, conditional on exposure), and its retinol cannot be built at all because human cells only cleave dietary provitamin A carotenoids (NONE). For anyone with real sun exposure the redundant part is the vitamin D, and the retinol load makes this a poor carrier, since plain D3 plus fish or algal oil gives the same benefit without pushing retinol towards the upper limit.
- **Pathway:** EPA and DHA: FADS2 (delta-6) → ELOVL5 → FADS1 (delta-5) → EPA → ELOVL2 → 24:5n-3 → FADS2 (delta-6) → 24:6n-3 → peroxisomal beta-oxidation → DHA, in hepatocytes; the Sprecher C24 route is the principal and rate-limiting mammalian pathway, and a direct delta-4 desaturase activity of the FADS2 product has been demonstrated in human cells with the in vivo contribution not established. Vitamin D3: epidermal 7-dehydrocholesterol → UVB photolysis → previtamin D3 → thermal isomerisation to vitamin D3 → hepatic 25-hydroxylation (CYP2R1) → renal 1-alpha-hydroxylation (CYP27B1) to calcitriol, with CYP24A1 as the clearance route. Retinol: NOT APPLICABLE, human cells only cleave dietary provitamin A carotenoids (BCO1).
- **Amount made:** Cutaneous vitamin D3 output varies with latitude, season, time of day, skin pigmentation and exposed surface area, so no single figure applies, but figures do exist: whole-body exposure to one minimal erythemal dose of UVB is equivalent to roughly 10,000-25,000 IU (250-625 µg) vitamin D3. Endogenous retinol 0 µg/d. EPA and DHA are conversion rather than synthesis: EPA 21% and DHA 9% of ingested ALA in young women, DHA undetectable in young men over 21 days; absolute mg/d NOT ESTABLISHED.
- **Still matters when:** Justified where sun exposure is genuinely low (winter at high latitude, covered skin, housebound or institutionalised, darker skin at high latitude) together with low fish intake. Not justified as a routine high-dose vitamin A source, and specifically to be avoided at high retinol doses in pregnancy because of teratogenicity, where separate D3 plus fish or algal oil is the cleaner choice.

*Refs. Dyerberg PMID 20638827; Jackowski PMID 26688721; Heller PMID 30626234*

### ALA from flaxseed, chia or walnut oil

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** Adequate intake 1.6 g/d for men and 1.1 g/d for women. No upper limit; the practical constraint is calories from oil.
- **Who it is for:** People who eat no marine products at all and will not take algal oil. It at least maintains essential fatty acid status.

**Pros**

- It is a genuinely essential fatty acid, so adequate intake matters.
- The Cochrane review found a signal for arrhythmia reduction, RR 0.73 (0.55-0.97) at moderate certainty. Note that rests on just 2 trials, 4,912 participants and 173 events.

**Cons**

- The plant-based omega-3, same benefits claim is the core deception. Conversion of ALA to EPA is a few percent and to DHA is well under one percent in adults, and it is further suppressed by high dietary linoleic acid. A flaxseed capsule does not deliver what a fish oil or algal oil capsule delivers.
- Cochrane found increased ALA probably makes little or no difference to all-cause mortality, RR 1.01 (0.84-1.20).
- Anyone relying on it to achieve DHA status is making a mistake, and that includes pregnant women and vegans wanting fetal DHA supply. Algal DHA is the correct product there.
- Note the arrhythmia finding for ALA points the opposite way to the atrial fibrillation excess seen with marine omega-3 (HR 1.25), so the two should not be treated as interchangeable in either direction.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

The one omega-3 fat the body cannot make; the raw material for EPA and DHA.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Fully reversible
- **In detail:** Alpha-linolenic acid (18:3n-3) is a genuine essential nutrient. Humans lack the desaturase that places a double bond at the n-3 position of a fatty acid, so every omega-3 molecule in the body traces back to dietary ALA or to preformed EPA and DHA. ALA is incorporated into membrane phospholipids, is a substantial oxidative fuel (a large share of an ingested dose is simply burned), and is the substrate for the elongation and desaturation pathway producing EPA, DPA and DHA. Stable-isotope tracer work puts net conversion to EPA at about 8% in young men and 21% in young women, with DHA formation around 9% in women and undetectable in men (PMID 12323085, PMID 12323090). The US Adequate Intake is 1.6 g/day for men and 1.1 g/day for women.
- **Deficiency disease:** Alpha-linolenic acid deficiency, the n-3 form of essential fatty acid deficiency (EFAD). It is a recognised clinical entity but carries no eponym comparable to scurvy or rickets.
- **First signs:** Biochemically, a rising ratio of n-6 to n-3 fatty acids in plasma and red cell phospholipids with falling 22:6n-3, detectable before any symptom. Clinically, the first reported signs in humans were sensory: intermittent numbness and paraesthesia in the limbs, and blurring of vision.
- **Untreated:** In the index case, a six-year-old girl maintained on parenteral nutrition rich in linoleate but nearly devoid of linolenate developed, after five months, episodes of numbness, paraesthesia, leg pain, weakness, inability to walk and blurred vision (Holman et al., PMID 6801965). Patients on long-term gastric-tube feeding with n-3-poor formulas developed scaly dermatitis and a haemorrhagic folliculitis (Bjerve et al.). Deficiency of this severity effectively arises only when the entire fat supply is medically controlled.
- **Does it reverse:** In every documented human case, neurological symptoms disappeared and skin lesions resolved once an n-3-containing lipid source was substituted, with plasma fatty acid profiles normalising alongside. No residual deficit was reported. The honest caveat is that all reported cases were identified within months; the consequences of much longer deprivation in humans are unknown.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Patients on long-term parenteral nutrition or tube feeding with lipid sources low in n-3, historically safflower-based emulsions. Essentially nobody eating ordinary food, since ALA is widespread in vegetable oils, nuts, seeds and green leaves. Extremely low-fat diets sustained for years are a theoretical risk with no case series behind it.
- **How common:** NOT ESTABLISHED. Clinical ALA deficiency exists only in case reports and small series of parenteral and tube-fed patients. No population prevalence figure exists because the condition does not occur in free-living people eating normal diets.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

ALA is one of the easiest nutrients in this reference to obtain from food. EFSA sets the Adequate Intake at 0.5% of energy, about 1.1-1.4 g/day for a 2,000-2,500 kcal diet (EFSA Journal 2010;8(3):1461, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2010.1461; the 0.5%-of-energy conversion checks arithmetically at 10 kcal/9 = 1.11 g and 12.5/9 = 1.39 g). A single tablespoon of ground flaxseed exceeds it. The supplement-shaped foods are not even the main dietary route in the UK and Northern Europe, where rapeseed oil supplies more ALA than flaxseed, chia and walnuts combined, and perilla oil is a higher-ALA culinary oil than flaxseed oil. The real question is not whether you can eat ALA but whether ALA becomes EPA and DHA in the body, and for DHA the answer is largely no.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Oil, perilla *[unconfirmed]* | ALA roughly 54-64 g (indicative) | 1 tablespoon (13.6 g) | ALA roughly 7.3-8.7 g | About 660-790% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI. The highest-ALA edible oil in common culinary use, above flaxseed oil, and a staple in Korean and Japanese cooking | Indicative and unconfirmed. The 54-64% ALA range was supplied by the verification pass without a traceable composition record, so it is not presented as sourced; confirm against an authoritative table before publishing |
| Oil, flaxseed, cold pressed | ALA 53.4 g | 1 tablespoon (13.6 g) | ALA 7.26 g | 660% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 167702 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Seeds, chia seeds, dried | ALA 17.8 g | 1 oz (28.35 g, roughly 2 tablespoons) | ALA 5.05 g | 459% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170554 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Nuts, walnuts, english, raw | ALA 9.08 g | 1 oz (28.35 g, 14 halves) | ALA 2.57 g | 234% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170187 (SR Legacy), confirmed including the '14 halves' descriptor, which is USDA's own |
| Seeds, hemp seed, hulled | ALA 8.68 g | 1 oz (28.35 g) | ALA 2.46 g | 224% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI. Comparable to walnuts per 100 g and a better ALA-per-calorie buy than walnut oil | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170148 (SR Legacy), per-100 g value supplied and confirmed by the verification pass; the ounce is a unit conversion, not a USDA household measure |
| Seeds, flaxseed, whole and ground | ALA 22.8 g | 1 tablespoon whole (10.3 g) or 1 tablespoon ground (7.0 g) | Whole: ALA 2.35 g. Ground: ALA 1.60 g | 213% (whole) or 145% (ground) of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI. Use the ground figure for any practical calculation, since whole seeds pass through largely undigested, see bioavailability | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169414 (SR Legacy), confirmed; both tablespoon weights are USDA's own |
| Oil, walnut | ALA 10.4 g | 1 tablespoon (13.6 g) | ALA 1.41 g | 129% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI. Walnut oil is a weak ALA source relative to flaxseed oil, about one fifth the concentration (10.4/53.4 = 0.195) | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171030 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Oil, rapeseed (canola) *[unconfirmed]* | ALA roughly 9 g (indicative) | 1 tablespoon (13.6 g) | ALA roughly 1.2 g | About 111% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI on its own. Modest per tablespoon, but in UK and Northern European diets it is the dominant dietary ALA source by a wide margin because of how much of it people already use | Indicative and unconfirmed. The verification pass could not resolve an FDC record for rapeseed oil in this session, so the roughly 9 g/100 g figure is not presented as sourced; confirm before publishing |

- **To match the dose:** For ALA itself there is zero gap: one tablespoon of ground flaxseed (1.60 g), one ounce of walnuts (2.57 g), one ounce of hulled hemp seed (2.46 g), one tablespoon of walnut oil (1.41 g) or one tablespoon of rapeseed oil (about 1.2 g) each meet or exceed the 1.1-1.4 g/day AI, and one tablespoon of flaxseed oil supplies 7.26 g. The gap appears only when ALA is sold as a substitute for EPA and DHA, and the two substances need separate answers. For EPA, substitution is plausible at modest intakes: Burdge and Wootton 2002 measured 21% net fractional conversion of labelled ALA to EPA in young women (PMID 12323090, DOI 10.1079/BJN2002689), so a 250 mg/day EPA target needs 1.19 g ALA/day, which is about 0.7 tablespoons of ground flaxseed. For DHA, substitution fails at any realistic intake. The same paper gives 9% conversion to DHA and 6% to DPA in women, while in young men roughly 33% of the dose was oxidised within 24 hours, EPA and DPA were the only meaningful products, and no labelled DHA was detectable in plasma at any point over 21 days (PMID 12323085, DOI 10.1079/bjn2002662). Whole-body estimates are lower still, commonly 0.1-0.5% for DHA. At 0.5% you need 50 g ALA/day, which is 219 g of flaxseed or roughly 100 mL of flaxseed oil daily; at 0.1% you need 250 g ALA/day, over a kilogram of seed. No one eats that. Feeding trials agree, with plasma DHA unchanged after a month of flaxseed in any form (Austria 2008, PMID 18689552). State it plainly: flaxseed, chia and walnut oil can substitute for EPA at modest intakes and cannot supply DHA at any realistic intake. Express any of these requirements in milled seed rather than whole, since whole flaxseed produced no significant plasma ALA rise in that trial.
- **Food vs supplement:** Form determines whether the ALA is absorbed at all, which is unusual among plant supplements. Over one month at matched ALA doses, flaxseed oil and milled flaxseed both raised plasma ALA significantly (oil higher than milled, P=0.004), while whole flaxseed produced no significant rise, and no group raised EPA or DHA (Austria 2008, PMID 18689552, DOI 10.1080/07315724.2008.10719693). Sprinkling whole seeds on food is close to decorative. Practical consequences: grind flaxseed, store it cold and use it quickly, because ALA at 53% of an oil is highly oxidisable and rancid flax oil is a pro-oxidant load rather than a benefit; flaxseed oil supplies ALA with none of the fibre or lignans of the seed; and conversion to EPA is suppressed by high dietary linoleic acid, so an ALA supplement taken alongside a diet heavy in sunflower or corn oil performs worse than the tracer figures suggest. Milled seed was also the best-tolerated form, with the oil and whole seed both causing gastrointestinal discomfort in that trial.
- **Absorption:** Two sequential losses. First, absorption and delivery: oil beats milled seed beats whole seed, with whole seed showing no measurable plasma ALA response (Austria 2008). Second, metabolic conversion, where the same enzyme chain (delta-6 desaturase, elongase, delta-5 desaturase, then peroxisomal beta-oxidation for DHA) is shared with linoleic acid and is the real bottleneck. Conversion rates differ by product and must not be interchanged: EPA 21% and DPA 6% in the tracer study in women, DHA 9% at best in the same study and undetectable in men over 21 days, with whole-body estimates commonly 0.1-0.5% for DHA. Women convert better than men, which is thought to reflect the demands of pregnancy and lactation. A third of an oral ALA dose is simply burned for energy within 24 hours.
- **Does the body make it:** The verdict for the substance actually in the bottle is NONE: humans lack the omega-3 (delta-15) desaturase that plants have, so ALA synthesis is zero, exactly as it is for the chemical elements. The exception is downstream, where the products people buy flax, chia or walnut oil for are PARTIAL: 21% of a labelled ALA dose reaches EPA and 9% reaches DHA in young women, while in young men EPA and DPA appear and DHA stays undetectable for 21 days, and most of the dose is simply burned as fuel. A precursor the body cannot make plus a conversion step that mostly fails means these oils are a defensible source of ALA itself and an unreliable route to DHA.
- **Pathway:** ALA 18:3n-3 → FADS2 (delta-6) → 18:4n-3 → ELOVL5 → 20:4n-3 → FADS1 (delta-5) → EPA 20:5n-3 → ELOVL5 and ELOVL2 → DPA 22:5n-3 → ELOVL2 → 24:5n-3 → FADS2 again (delta-6) → 24:6n-3 → peroxisomal beta-oxidation → DHA 22:6n-3, mainly in hepatocyte ER and peroxisome. The Sprecher C24 route is the principal and rate-limiting mammalian pathway. For ALA itself: NOT APPLICABLE, humans have no omega-3 (delta-15) desaturase, so the 18-carbon precursor is strictly dietary.
- **Amount made:** Endogenous synthesis of ALA itself: 0 mg/d. Net fractional conversion of ingested ALA within the plasma tracer pool over 21 days, young women: EPA 21%, DPA 6%, DHA 9%; in young men EPA and DPA only, with DHA undetectable to 21 days. Studies using total dietary ALA as the denominator report DHA under 0.1% (controlled-diet tracer study) and under 0.01% (hyperlipidaemic men), which is not the same measurement as the 9%. About 33% of the tracer appears as breath 13CO2 within 24 hours in men and about 22% in women; doubling dietary linoleic acid from 15 g/d to 30 g/d lowers conversion of the labelled 18:3n-3 and 18:2n-6 tracers by 40 to 54%.
- **Still matters when:** Justified as a source of the essential fatty acid ALA itself, which the body cannot make at all, as a plant n-3 baseline for vegans without access to algal oil, and where EPA rather than DHA is the target. Not adequate as the DHA source in pregnancy, lactation, infancy or for adult men, and its already small effect shrinks further as linoleic acid intake rises.

*Refs. Cochrane PMID 32114706; Gencer PMID 34612056; NASEM DRI*

### Algal oil (vegan DHA and EPA)

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** Matched to fish oil equivalents. For pregnancy the tested DHA doses are the same as fish-derived, 200-1,000 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** Vegans and vegetarians, people avoiding fish contaminants, and people with fish or shellfish allergy.

**Pros**

- It is the correct product for vegans, who cannot obtain preformed EPA and DHA any other way, and for whom ALA conversion is inadequate.
- Statistically non-inferior to fish oil for plasma phospholipid DHA and EPA in a 74-person trial over 6 to 14 weeks.
- Avoids methylmercury, dioxins and PCBs, which is a legitimate reason to choose it in pregnancy.

**Cons**

- There is no clinical outcome trial of algal oil for any endpoint. The only evidence is a 74-person biomarker study, and the credibility it borrows comes from the fish oil outcome trials, which were largely null.
- Non-inferiority on a biomarker is not superiority, and marketing implying algal DHA is cleaner therefore better conflates contaminant avoidance with efficacy.
- Cost per milligram of DHA is usually higher than fish oil.
- Products are DHA-dominant, and DHA raises LDL more than EPA does (+6.9% versus +2.2% in a head-to-head crossover), which matters if LDL is your limiting problem.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Oil from marine microalgae that supplies ready-made DHA, and in some products EPA, without fish.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Algal oil is extracted from cultured heterotrophic microalgae, chiefly Schizochytrium species and Crypthecodinium cohnii. These are the organisms at the base of the marine food chain that actually synthesise DHA; fish accumulate it by eating them. Crypthecodinium products supply DHA only, while Schizochytrium products supply DHA plus a smaller amount of EPA. Physiologically the oil does nothing that DHA and EPA do not do, so the mechanism sits in the Omega-3 (EPA and DHA) entry. Controlled feeding work found algal-oil capsules and cooked salmon to be nutritionally equivalent sources of DHA in terms of plasma incorporation (PMID 18589030), which is why algal DHA has been the standard source in infant formula for decades.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Trace
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Partly

The classification depends entirely on who is eating, so it has to be stated by population. For an omnivore EPA and DHA are RICH in food: one herring fillet supplies 2,873 mg, one tablespoon of caviar 1,046 mg (see the Omega-3 entry). For a strict vegan the answer is TRACE, and that is the population this product exists for: raw laver (nori) has 80 mg EPA per 100 g and zero DHA, which is 2 mg of EPA per standard sheet, and dried spirulina, routinely marketed as a vegan omega-3 food, contains 0.000 g of both EPA and DHA (it does carry 823 mg/100 g of ALA, 58 mg per 7 g tablespoon, which is not the same nutrient). For a lacto-ovo vegetarian the honest answer sits between the two, because eggs supply preformed DHA at 58 mg/100 g, about 29 mg per large 50 g egg, and DHA-enriched eggs several times that. The earlier draft elided vegan and vegetarian and so overstated the case. The production organism is not a food either: Schizochytrium sp. is a single-cell marine microalga cultured in fermenters and authorised in the EU as a novel food.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Egg, whole, raw, fresh | DHA 58 mg | 1 large egg (50 g) | DHA 29 mg | 12% of a 250 mg/day DHA dose per egg; two eggs give 23%. The only meaningful preformed-DHA food available to lacto-ovo vegetarians, and irrelevant to strict vegans. DHA-enriched eggs, sold for exactly this purpose, carry several times more | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171287 (SR Legacy), value supplied and confirmed by the verification pass |
| Seaweed, laver (nori), raw | EPA 80 mg, DHA 0 mg, ALA 1 mg | 1 sheet (2.6 g; 10 sheets = 26 g) | EPA 2 mg, DHA 0 mg | 0.8% of a 250 mg/day algal oil dose for EPA; 0% for DHA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168458 (SR Legacy), confirmed; the 2.6 g sheet derives correctly from USDA's 10 sheets = 26 g |
| Seaweed, spirulina, dried | EPA 0 mg, DHA 0 mg; ALA 823 mg | 1 tablespoon (7 g) | EPA 0 mg, DHA 0 mg; ALA 58 mg | 0% of an algal oil dose for either EPA or DHA. Included because spirulina and chlorella are frequently sold as vegan omega-3 sources and supply neither. The 58 mg of ALA per tablespoon is real but is a different fatty acid, and it does not convert to DHA in useful amounts | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170495 (SR Legacy), confirmed at zero for both EPA and DHA; the ALA value comes from the same record |

- **To match the dose:** For a strict vegan the gap is unbridgeable for DHA and absurd for EPA. Typical algal oil products sit at 250 mg/day of DHA for general adult use and 450 mg/day for pregnancy and lactation. Matching 250 mg of EPA from nori would take 312 g of raw laver, about 120 sheets a day. Matching any amount of DHA from nori is impossible, since the USDA value is 0.000 g, and spirulina supplies zero of both. No seaweed portion, no fermented food and no oilseed closes this gap, which is why algal oil is one of the few supplements in this reference with a real and unavoidable role. For a lacto-ovo vegetarian eggs get partway and no further: 250 mg of DHA is about nine large eggs a day, so two eggs (58 mg) cover 23% of the target and one covers 12%. DHA-enriched eggs shorten that considerably. For an omnivore the same 250 mg of DHA is one 45 g tin of anchovies (580 mg DHA) or half a tin of sardines.
- **Food vs supplement:** The strain determines what you get, and the earlier generalisation was too broad. EFSA's assessment of strain WZU477 specifies DHA at 32.0% of total fatty acids or above, with batches measured at 40.5-46.3%, and EPA at a single value of about 0.56% (EFSA Journal 2020;18(10):6242, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2020.6242). That is one strain, and the same opinion explicitly contrasts it with other authorised Schizochytrium strains carrying 1.3-6% EPA. So a DHA-dominant oil from this strain is not an EPA supplement, but 'a product labelled vegan omega-3 may supply almost no EPA' does not follow for the category: check the strain and the declared EPA content. On the positive side, this is one case where the concentrated product is cleaner than the food, because the algae are grown in closed fermenters rather than harvested from the sea, so algal oil avoids the methylmercury of predatory fish and the dioxin and PCB burden that makes fatty fish a leading dietary contributor to European dioxin exposure (EFSA Journal 2018;16(11):5333, document confirmed). The usual oxidation caveat applies, since a highly unsaturated oil in a clear bottle or an old capsule degrades, and a fishy aftertaste is the tell. Do not substitute algae biomass powders (spirulina, chlorella) for algal oil; they are different organisms with no DHA.
- **Absorption:** Algal oil DHA is nutritionally equivalent to DHA from fish. A randomised trial comparing algal-oil capsules with cooked salmon found the two equivalent as DHA sources by blood incorporation (Arterburn 2008, PMID 18589030, DOI 10.1016/j.jada.2008.04.020). Absorption follows the same rules as other marine oils, so triglyceride forms behave like natural fish oil and take-with-a-meal advice applies. Some DHA retroconverts to EPA in the body, which partly offsets the low EPA content of DHA-dominant algal oils, though not reliably enough to treat a DHA-only product as an EPA source.
- **Does the body make it:** Algal oil delivers preformed DHA, and in newer products EPA as well, which is precisely the step human metabolism cannot deliver: the ELOVL2 elongation and second FADS2 desaturation that carry EPA through to DHA are the rate-limiting stretch, yielding 9% conversion in young women and nothing measurable in young men. Plasma and tissue DHA rise dose-dependently with preformed DHA and hardly at all with ALA, so taking DHA directly bypasses the bottleneck instead of duplicating something the body already does well. That makes algal oil a defensible supplement for anyone off oily fish, with plasma DHA approaching a maximum response at about 2 g/d.
- **Pathway:** The endogenous route the product bypasses: ALA → FADS2 (delta-6) → 18:4n-3 → ELOVL5 → 20:4n-3 → FADS1 (delta-5) → EPA → ELOVL5 and ELOVL2 → DPA → ELOVL2 → 24:5n-3 → FADS2 (delta-6) → 24:6n-3 → peroxisomal beta-oxidation → DHA, in hepatocytes. The Sprecher C24 route is the principal and rate-limiting mammalian pathway; a direct delta-4 desaturase activity of the FADS2 product has been demonstrated in human cells, with the in vivo contribution not established.
- **Amount made:** Endogenous DHA from ALA: 9% net fractional conversion in the plasma tracer pool in young women, undetectable in young men over 21 days; studies using total dietary ALA as the denominator report under 0.1% in controlled-diet tracer studies, and the two numbers answer different questions. Plasma DHA response approaches maximum at a DHA dose of about 2 g/d. Absolute mg/d of endogenous DHA synthesis in adults: NOT ESTABLISHED.
- **Still matters when:** Justified for vegans and vegetarians, fish allergy or aversion, pregnancy and lactation on plant-based diets, and adult men, whose measured ALA to DHA conversion is effectively zero. This is the clearest case in the omega-3 group where partial synthesis fails to meet requirement, so the supplement is not redundant with anything the body makes. One practical note for DHA-only products: supplemental DHA also raises EPA levels, which recent tracer work attributes to a slower rate of EPA elongation rather than the long-assumed retroconversion.

*Refs. Bailey PMID 41096614; Cochrane PMID 32114706; Allaire ComparED PMID 27281302*

### Wheat bran and insoluble fibre supplements for IBS

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 10 g/d was the comparator dose in the head-to-head primary care trial. No effective dose exists for IBS.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody with irritable bowel syndrome.

**Pros**

- As a food it contributes to total dietary fibre intake, and total fibre intake is associated with 15 to 30% reductions in all-cause and cardiovascular mortality.

**Cons**

- It is the clearest case in this document of a refuted product still sold into the exact indication where it failed. Pooled bran trials: relative risk of persistent IBS symptoms 0.90 (0.79-1.03), null. Soluble fibre by contrast gave 0.83 (0.73-0.94), NNT 7.
- In the three-arm primary care trial, bran at 10 g/d for 12 weeks did not beat placebo on symptom severity at 3 months (-58 versus -49 points, P=0.61) while psyllium did (-90 points, P=0.03). Bran also had the lowest completion rate of the three arms at 56%.
- Bran is the fibre most consistently reported to WORSEN abdominal pain and distension.
- Contraindicated with strictures, adhesions or a history of obstruction, and unsuitable in coeliac disease and non-coeliac gluten sensitivity.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Coarse insoluble fibre that adds stool bulk mechanically; in IBS it often makes symptoms worse.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Wheat bran is the outer layer of the wheat kernel, composed mainly of insoluble arabinoxylan, cellulose and lignin. It acts through particle bulk and mechanical stimulation of the mucosa rather than by forming a gel: coarse particles increase stool mass and provoke secretion and peristalsis, whereas finely ground bran loses this effect and can slow transit. It holds far less water than psyllium and is partly fermented, generating gas. In IBS these properties work against the patient, since mechanical stimulation acts on a hypersensitive gut and bran also carries fructans, making it high-FODMAP. Meta-analysis found no significant benefit of bran for global IBS symptoms (relative risk of not improving 0.90, 95% CI 0.79 to 1.03), in contrast to ispaghula (NNT 7), and the ACG guideline recommends soluble fibre for IBS (PMID 33315591).
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot
