Wheat bran is a food, sold as a plain milled cereal fraction, and at 42.8 g total dietary fibre per 100 g (USDA FDC 169722) it is one of the most fibre-dense ordinary cereal fractions. The submission's claim that it is among the most fibre-dense foods in any composition table is false and has been removed: corn bran at 79 g/100 g ranks first among roughly 4,000 USDA foods, nearly double wheat bran, and psyllium husk is about 78 g/100 g. RICH is demonstrated for the cereal-bran supplements specifically, which is where food comparators exist: wheat bran, corn bran, rice bran. The other common insoluble-fibre supplement base, powdered or microcrystalline cellulose and methylcellulose, is a manufactured material with no dietary equivalent, so the RICH grade does not extend to it. Availability is not the problem with this product category; the indication is.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Wheat bran, crude (plain, unprocessed) | 42.8 g total dietary fibre; 35.0-48.4 g insoluble and 1.5-4.0 g soluble | 1 cup (58 g) | 24.8 g total dietary fibre (roughly 20-28 g insoluble) | 83% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake; 5.8x the 10 g/day bran dose used in the Bijkerk IBS trial | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 169722 (42.8 g/100 g confirmed). Fibre fractions from Stevenson L et al., DOI 10.3109/09637486.2012.687366. Note an unresolved discrepancy in that source: it also states wheat soluble fibre is under 1% of dry weight, which sits below the 1.5-4.0 g/100 g quoted for the bran fraction, so the two figures are probably on different bases (whole grain versus bran). |
| Corn bran, crude | 79.0 g total dietary fibre, the highest fibre value among roughly 4,000 USDA foods | 30 g (conventional bran serving) | 23.7 g total dietary fibre | 79% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | USDA composition data for corn bran, crude, as supplied and ranked in the verification report. The portion weight is a conventional bran serving, not from a source record. |
| Whole-grain wheat flour | 10.7 g total dietary fibre | 1 cup (120 g) | 12.8 g total dietary fibre | 43% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 168893; value is the standard USDA figure and the arithmetic was re-verified. |
| Extruded all-bran type breakfast cereal | about 29-30 g total dietary fibre | 30 g (conventional serving) | 8.7-9.0 g total dietary fibre | 29-30% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | Fibre range as supplied in the verification report; brand-specific record not traced in this pass, so treat as indicative. Portion weight is a conventional serving. |
| Rice bran, crude | about 21 g total dietary fibre | 30 g (conventional bran serving) | 6.3 g total dietary fibre | 21% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | Value as supplied in the verification report (about 21 g/100 g); specific USDA record not traced in this pass, so treat as indicative. Portion weight is a conventional bran serving. |
| Wheat bran flakes, ready-to-eat breakfast cereal *[unconfirmed]* | 16.9 g total dietary fibre | 1 NLEA serving, 0.75 cup (29 g) | 4.9 g total dietary fibre | 16% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | Arithmetic verified (16.9 x 0.29 = 4.90), but FDC ID 173002 could not be confirmed as this food, so the per-100 g value is |
| Bread, whole-wheat, commercially prepared | 6.0 g total dietary fibre | 2 slices (56 g, at the 28 g slice USDA legacy commonly lists for this record) | 3.4 g total dietary fibre | 11% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 172688; the 6.0 g/100 g is confirmed, the slice weight is not. The submission's 32 g slice could not be verified, so the figures here use 28 g and should be restated if the record's slice weight is confirmed otherwise. |

- **To match the dose:** The 10 g/day of bran tested against psyllium and placebo in IBS (PMID 19713235) is about 3 heaped tablespoons of plain wheat bran, which carries 4.28 g of dietary fibre. Matched on fibre, that is about 1 serving of bran flakes, not the 2 the submission claimed: one 29 g serving supplies 4.90 g, so two servings deliver more than double the fibre in the trial dose. A single cup of crude bran (58 g) is 5.8x the trial dose by mass. No supplement is needed to reach it. What the trial found is that reaching it does not help: bran was no better than placebo in months one and two, its month-three advantage did not survive worst-case analysis, and early dropout was highest in the bran arm because IBS symptoms worsened. A review of the wheat bran literature reaches the same conclusion and quotes official guidance that wheat bran should not be recommended to people with IBS (DOI 10.3109/09637486.2012.687366).
- **Food vs supplement:** The food is safe and useful; the supplement indication is the problem. Wheat bran is a genuinely effective faecal bulking agent, increasing stool by roughly 5 g per 1 g of bran consumed, at the top of the accepted 3-5.4 g range (DOI 10.3109/09637486.2012.687366), and it is a reasonable food for ordinary constipation and general fibre intake. In IBS it commonly worsens bloating, distension and pain, and it was the arm patients dropped out of. A less obvious caveat: wheat bran carries fructan (white wheat flour measures 0.75 g fructan/100 g, DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771), so bran-loading also raises FODMAP intake in exactly the patients least able to tolerate it. Insoluble-fibre supplements sold as 'IBS fibre' are marketing the wrong fibre class; the evidence in IBS favours the viscous, poorly fermented kind.
- **Absorption:** Not absorbed. The mechanism is mechanical bulking plus water-holding by arabinoxylan and cellulose, and the non-starch polysaccharide of the bran is 70% arabinoxylan, 24% cellulose and 6% beta-glucan (Stevenson et al., PMID 22716911, quotation verified). Bran accelerates small bowel transit and increases faecal weight dose-dependently. Particle size matters more than gram total, because coarse bran bulks and irritates while finely milled bran loses much of the effect, so two products with the same fibre label are not interchangeable. Only 1.5-4.0 g per 100 g is soluble, so bran contributes almost nothing to the viscous-gel effects on cholesterol, glycaemia and stool consistency that people often assume fibre delivers.
- **Does the body make it:** Insoluble bran is non-digestible by definition, so nothing about it is endogenous and every gram is eaten. Here the axis being silent is no licence, and the trial record has to be reported in full: in the same primary care trial that supported psyllium, 10 g/day bran beat placebo at month 3 in the primary analysis (adequate relief 57 percent versus 32 percent, RR 1.70, 95 percent CI 1.12 to 2.57) but not in the worst-case sensitivity analysis of that same result (RR 1.45, 95 percent CI 0.97 to 2.16), and it reduced symptom severity by only 58 points versus 49 for placebo (P=0.61), with symptoms often worse in the first weeks and the highest dropout of the three arms (completion: psyllium 64 percent, bran 56 percent, placebo 60 percent). Coarse natural fibre is not a reason in itself, and the bran signal is weaker and less stable than psyllium's.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Insoluble cellulose, hemicellulose and lignin resist human digestive enzymes; no human synthesis and no human requirement for this specific material.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway)
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis, and the trial evidence for bran in IBS is inconsistent: significant at month 3 in the primary analysis, null in the worst-case analysis, no effect on symptom severity, early worsening and the highest dropout. Insoluble fibre still has a role in ordinary constipation and general diet quality; in IBS, prefer a soluble gel-forming fibre.

*Refs. Moayyedi PMID 25070054; Bijkerk BMJ 2009 PMID 19713235; Nagarajan PMID 26148247*

### Over-the-counter digestive enzyme blends

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** No evidence-derived dose exists. Products are dosed in non-comparable proprietary activity units by design.
- **Who it is for:** No identified group. Use a matched single enzyme for a diagnosed deficiency instead.

**Pros**

- One 120-participant double-blind placebo-controlled trial of a fungal multi-enzyme blend reported improvement in functional dyspepsia symptom and quality-of-life scores. Single centre, proprietary product, symptom-scale endpoints.

**Cons**

- The category is built on a fabricated disease. There is no recognised syndrome of generalised digestive enzyme insufficiency in healthy adults, and clinically meaningful malabsorption does not appear until roughly 90% of exocrine pancreatic function is lost.
- It borrows credibility from prescription pancreatic enzyme replacement, which treats a real, objectively diagnosable condition.
- The main harm is diagnostic delay. Self-treating undiagnosed persistent gut symptoms delays diagnosis of coeliac disease, inflammatory bowel disease, pancreatic disease and colorectal cancer.
- A properly controlled trial in autism (43 children, double-blind crossover) found no clinically significant improvement, in a population where these products were heavily promoted.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Mixed plant and fungal enzymes sold for bloating; prescription pancreatic enzymes are a different product.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** These blends typically combine fungal amylase and protease, cellulase, bromelain, papain and sometimes lipase, at doses and in formulations that are not designed to survive gastric acid or to be matched to a meal's fat content. Prescription pancrelipase is a different class of product: enteric-coated, dosed in thousands of lipase units per gram of dietary fat, and regulated in the US as a drug requiring an approved application since 2010. Two components have a defined use in people who need them, lactase for lactose intolerance and alpha-galactosidase for gas from raffinose-family oligosaccharides in legumes; the broad multi-enzyme blends have no established indication in anyone with a normal pancreas, since a healthy gland already secretes roughly ten times the lipase needed. This is a commercial product category rather than a nutrient, so no requirement and no deficiency state applies.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Trace
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Low
- **Body makes it:** n/a

Reclassified from NOT_FOOD, which was too strong and was contradicted by this entry's own form caveat. The plant proteases do occur in ordinary food: fruit bromelain is present in edible pineapple flesh and juice with real proteolytic activity, which is exactly why fresh pineapple stops gelatin setting and canned does not; papain occurs in unripe papaya flesh as well as in the latex that is the commercial source; and actinidin, a cysteine protease, is present in ordinary edible ripe green kiwifruit, with human data comparing green (actinidin-containing) against gold (actinidin-free) kiwifruit for gastric protein digestion. Ginger (zingibain), fig latex (ficin), malted barley (diastatic amylase) and raw honey (diastase) are further real dietary enzyme sources. What remains genuinely NOT_FOOD is the other half of these blends: the amylase, protease, lipase and lactase components are Aspergillus or yeast fermentation products with no dietary presence. TRACE rather than PARTIAL because the activity in edible flesh is present without any confirmed quantification, and the parts carrying the measured activity are not eaten.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kiwifruit, green, ripe, raw | contains actinidin, a cysteine protease; no per-100 g activity figure was retrieved in this pass | 1 fruit (about 75 g), conventional portion | proteolytic activity present but unquantified here | not expressible against a GDU-standardised bromelain dose; the units and the enzyme differ and no activity value for the flesh was confirmed | Human crossover data in healthy older adults compared green (actinidin-containing) against gold (actinidin-free) kiwifruit with beef meals for gastric protein digestion, with supporting in vitro work at gastric pH, as summarised in the verification report; no specific citation or activity value retrieved here, so the row is qualitative. This is the clearest food route for a swallowed protease and it is why the NOT_FOOD grade was withdrawn. |
| Pineapple, raw flesh | contains fruit bromelain (pI 4.6, distinct from stem bromelain at pI 9.5); no proteolytic activity value for the eaten flesh was retrieved in this pass | 1 cup chunks (about 165 g), conventional portion | proteolytic activity present but unquantified here | not expressible; the measured GDU/g values in the literature belong to basal stem, peel and core rather than to flesh | USDA FDC 169124 records the edible fruit but carries no enzyme activity value. Isoform distinction from the bromelain literature (DOI 10.3390/foods13040589). The absence of a flesh activity figure is a sourcing gap here rather than an absence in the literature. |

- **To match the dose:** Bromelain supplements are dosed by activity, typically 500-2,000 mg of a preparation standardised to around 2,400 GDU/g, giving roughly 1,200-4,800 GDU per dose. On the activities reported for pineapple by-products, that is equivalent to about 0.41-1.65 g of basal stem or about 1.0-4.1 g of peel, and neither the basal stem nor the peel is eaten (those per-gram activity values are from a genuine, on-topic paper whose individual figures could not be retrieved, so treat them as unconfirmed). Expressed against food you would actually put in your mouth, the dose cannot be matched: the crown has no detectable activity, the core is usually discarded, and no sourced activity figure exists for the ripe flesh. Fresh pineapple and green kiwifruit do deliver active protease into the stomach, so the effect is not zero, but with no confirmed activity value for edible flesh there is no way to express a food equivalent of a GDU dose. For papain the source material is the latex of the unripe fruit, which is irritant and is not food. For the fungal amylase, protease and lipase components there is no food source at all.
- **Food vs supplement:** The whole food is safe and the concentrate is not always. Fresh pineapple and papaya are ordinary fruit; concentrated bromelain has anticoagulant and antiplatelet activity and interacts with warfarin and antiplatelet drugs, and papain and chymopapain are recognised respiratory and contact allergens, which EFSA flags in its allergenicity assessments of the food enzyme (the specific identifier cited in the submission, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2026.9950, could not be confirmed; EFSA does publish opinions on papain from papaya latex, so the substance of the point stands while that reference does not). The matrix also destroys the premise: these are proteins, so heat and gastric acid inactivate them. Canned and cooked pineapple has no meaningful proteolytic activity left, which is why canned pineapple does not stop gelatin setting while fresh pineapple does, and any orally swallowed enzyme without enteric protection meets pepsin and pH 2 before reaching the small intestine. The most important caveat is scope: these blends are not equivalent to pancreatic enzyme replacement and must not be substituted for it in diagnosed pancreatic exocrine insufficiency, where the requirement is 40,000-50,000 lipase units per meal in an enteric-coated porcine preparation (PMID 42011215). Nor is the plant-derived lactase in a blend interchangeable with a dose-standardised lactase product.
- **Absorption:** Enzymes are not absorbed and do not need to be; they act in the lumen, so what matters is surviving to the right pH and place. Stem bromelain and fruit bromelain differ even in isoelectric point (pI 9.5 against 4.6), which is why extraction pH determines which enzyme you actually get and why 'bromelain' on a label does not identify a single molecule. Activity rather than milligrams is the meaningful unit, and GDU, CDU, FIP and USP units are not interconvertible, so a milligram figure on a bottle says almost nothing about what it will do. Both bromelain and papain are irreversibly denatured by cooking and largely by gastric acid unless enterically protected, so an uncoated capsule and a bowl of fresh pineapple share the same delivery problem.
- **Does the body make it:** This is a product category rather than a defined substance, so the axis label is NA and the split has to be read ingredient by ingredient. The amylase, protease and lipase in these blends duplicate what a working pancreas already secretes in large excess, so for those three the axis reads FULL and the capsule is redundant; the same capsule usually also carries enzyme functions the human gut lumen does not perform, namely fungal alpha-galactosidase for raffinose-family oligosaccharides, fungal cellulase, and the plant proteases bromelain and papain, which read NONE. Humans do make an alpha-galactosidase, the lysosomal alpha-galactosidase A encoded by GLA whose deficiency causes Fabry disease, plus alpha-N-acetylgalactosaminidase (NAGA), and what is missing is a luminal or brush-border alpha-galactosidase, so a blend has no coherent target in a person with a normal pancreas.
- **Pathway:** Pancreatic amylase, proteases and lipase are synthesised in pancreatic acinar cells and secreted into the duodenum, as in the lactase and pancreatic enzyme row. Alpha-galactosidase: humans have the lysosomal GLA enzyme and NAGA but no luminal digestive alpha-galactosidase, and the supplement enzyme is Aspergillus niger derived. Cellulase: fungal, with no human enzyme at all. Bromelain and papain: plant cysteine proteases, absent as such from humans, who nonetheless express papain-family (clan CA) cathepsins.
- **Amount made:** Pancreatic lipase output 253.5 +/- 95.5 mg per liquid test meal and 202.9 +/- 96.1 mg per solid test meal in healthy volunteers (PMID 11040182). Do not compare that mass against a capsule's labelled activity units, because they are different quantities; use the specific activities given in the same paper for the conversion (43 to 47 micromol/min/mg for the liquid meal, 12 to 15 micromol/min/mg for the solid meal). For the fungal and plant enzymes in the blend, 0 (no human synthesis).
- **Still matters when:** Documented exocrine pancreatic insufficiency, where a prescription enteric-coated product dosed from at least 40,000 USP units of lipase per meal (half with snacks, above 120,000 units per meal seldom needed) is the right answer and an unregulated OTC blend is not. Lactase for lactase non-persistence, and fungal alpha-galactosidase for legume-related gas, are separate non-endogenous cases with a real target. For bloating in someone with a normal pancreas, NEVER ON THIS AXIS.

*Refs. Ullah PMID 37976892; Munasinghe PMID 20204691; de la Iglesia-Garcia PMID 27941156*

### L-glutamine for gut repair

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 15 g/d as 5 g three times daily for 8 weeks in the only supportive gut trial.
- **Who it is for:** Narrowly, post-infectious diarrhoea-predominant IBS with objectively documented increased intestinal permeability.

**Pros**

- The one supportive trial is genuinely striking: 106 patients, 79.6% versus 5.8% achieved a 50-point reduction in IBS severity score over 8 weeks, with normalisation of intestinal permeability.
- It has a licensed pharmaceutical use in sickle cell disease, where a phase 3 trial reduced median pain crises from 4.0 to 3.0.

**Cons**

- A 14-fold difference in response rate is an effect size almost never seen in functional gastrointestinal disease, and the 5.8% placebo response is far below the 30 to 40% typical of IBS trials. That combination demands scepticism, not enthusiasm. It is a single-centre, unreplicated result.
- There is a mortality signal in the critically ill. In the REDOXS trial, high-dose glutamine given to 1,223 critically ill adults with multi-organ failure raised 28-day mortality to 32.4% versus 27.2%, adjusted OR 1.28.
- Contraindicated in hepatic impairment, cirrhosis and any history of hepatic encephalopathy, because glutamine is metabolised to glutamate and ammonia.
- It is the chemical anchor of the entire leaky gut supplement economy, and the gap between that market and the evidence is enormous.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

An amino acid the body makes itself; a preferred fuel for gut lining and immune cells.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Glutamine is the most abundant free amino acid in plasma and skeletal muscle. Muscle synthesises and exports it continuously via glutamine synthetase, so supply is normally set by endogenous production rather than by diet. Enterocytes and rapidly dividing immune cells use it as a preferred respiratory fuel and as the nitrogen donor for purine, pyrimidine and glutathione synthesis, which is the biochemistry behind the gut-repair claim. It is classed as non-essential in health and conditionally essential in severe catabolic states, where muscle export cannot keep pace with consumption. Human trials of oral glutamine for intestinal permeability, IBS and inflammatory bowel disease have been small and inconsistent, and the strongest evidence is in specific clinical contexts rather than in healthy people.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Usually

Glutamine is one of the most abundant amino acids in ordinary dietary protein, so any protein-containing meal supplies it. One caveat governs every number below: USDA composition tables report 'glutamic acid', which after the acid hydrolysis used in amino acid analysis is the sum of glutamate and glutamine, because glutamine is converted to glutamate during hydrolysis. The figures are therefore the combined Glu plus Gln pool, and the glutamine share of that pool is roughly half in most animal proteins. Treat the values as an upper bound on glutamine content.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Vital wheat gluten (seitan base), dry | roughly 25-30 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool), the densest ordinary food source by a wide margin | about 30 g dry (conventional serving, roughly 1/4 cup) | 7.5-9.0 g Glu + Gln | 50-60% of the 15 g/day glutamine dose used in the post-infectious IBS trial (PMID 30108163) | Derived figure: vital wheat gluten protein content about 75 g/100 g (USDA-derived nutrition databases) with glutamate at roughly 35% of gluten protein (cereal protein composition literature). This is a calculation rather than a direct composition record, and the portion weight is conventional, so treat the range as indicative. |
| Cod, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat | 3.408 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 1 fillet (180 g) | 6.13 g Glu + Gln | 41% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 171956 (3.4 g/100 g confirmed, protein 22.8 g). The 180 g fillet weight was not independently confirmed but is the standard USDA portion for this record. |
| Natto (fermented soybean) | 3.337 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 1 cup (175 g) | 5.84 g Glu + Gln | 39% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 172443 (3.3 g/100 g confirmed, protein 19.4 g). |
| Soy protein isolate, powder | roughly 17-19 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | about 30 g (conventional scoop) | 5.1-5.7 g Glu + Gln | 34-38% of the 15 g/day dose | Derived figure from soy protein isolate protein content of about 88 g/100 g and the glutamate share of soy protein, as supplied in the verification report. A calculation rather than a direct composition record, with a conventional portion weight; indicative. |
| Chicken breast, meat only, roasted | 4.645 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | half a breast, bone and skin removed (86 g) | 4.00 g Glu + Gln | 27% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 171477; per-100 g value is the standard USDA figure and the arithmetic was re-verified. |
| Beef, top sirloin cap steak, grilled | 4.251 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 3 oz (85 g) | 3.61 g Glu + Gln | 24% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 173061; arithmetic re-verified. |
| Lentils, mature seeds, cooked, boiled | 1.399 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 1 cup cooked (198 g) | 2.77 g Glu + Gln | 18% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 172421; arithmetic re-verified. |
| Parmesan cheese, hard | 8.209 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool), the densest ordinary animal-food source but well below vital wheat gluten | 1 oz (28.35 g) | 2.33 g Glu + Gln | 16% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 170848; the 8.209 g/100 g is the standard USDA value. The submission's claim that this is 'the densest ordinary food source' is false and has been removed: vital wheat gluten runs roughly three to four times higher. |
| Tofu, firm (calcium sulfate and nigari) | 1.721 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | half a cup (126 g) | 2.17 g Glu + Gln | 14% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 172448; arithmetic re-verified. |
| Milk, whole, 3.25% fat | 0.708 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 1 cup (244 g) | 1.73 g Glu + Gln | 12% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 171265; the per-100 g value was not independently retrieved but is consistent with whole milk protein of about 3.2 g/100 g. |
| Egg, whole, hard-boiled | 1.644 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 1 large egg (50 g) | 0.82 g Glu + Gln | 5% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 173424; arithmetic re-verified. |

- **To match the dose:** The trial dose is 5 g three times daily, 15 g/day for 8 weeks, in post-infectious diarrhoea-predominant IBS with increased intestinal permeability (Zhou et al., Gut 2019;68:996-1002, PMID 30108163). On the combined Glu plus Gln figures, 15 g comes from about 50-60 g of vital wheat gluten, about 79-88 g of soy protein isolate, about 183 g of parmesan, about 323 g of cooked chicken breast, about 440 g of cod (about 2.5 fillets), about 450 g of natto (corrected from the submission's 865 g, which was wrong by 1.9x) or roughly 1.1 kg of cooked lentils. Correcting for the fact that only about half that pool is glutamine, matching 15 g of glutamine specifically needs roughly double: about 100-120 g of vital wheat gluten a day, or 600-700 g of cooked chicken breast. The baseline matters more than the ceiling. Glutamine is roughly 4-8% of food protein, so a diet supplying 80-100 g of protein delivers about 4-7 g/day, consistent with the conventional 3-6 g/day estimate. A 15 g/day supplement is therefore a three- to four-fold increase over habitual intake, not the doubling the submission claimed, which makes this a moderate gap rather than a small one. It is still nothing like creatine or NMN, where food cannot get close.
- **Food vs supplement:** The difference is in the delivery rather than the amount. The trial gave 5 g of free crystalline L-glutamine three times a day on an empty-stomach schedule, producing a sharp luminal and plasma bolus; dietary glutamine arrives protein-bound, released slowly by digestion, and a large fraction is consumed by the enterocytes themselves on first pass. Whether the clinical effect depends on that bolus pattern has not been tested against a food-protein comparator, so 'just eat more protein' remains plausible and unproven. Two practical points: free glutamine is heat-labile and degrades in aqueous solution, so cooking and storage reduce free glutamine in food while leaving protein-bound glutamine intact; and glutamine supplementation is not risk-free in everyone, since it is a nitrogen donor and high doses warrant caution in hepatic or renal impairment, whereas the same amount arriving as ordinary food protein raises no comparable concern.
- **Absorption:** Absorbed as free amino acid and as di- and tripeptides, then extensively extracted by the small intestinal mucosa on first pass, which is the mechanistic argument for a gut-directed effect and also the reason plasma appearance underestimates delivery to the target tissue. Because USDA reports Glu and Gln as one pooled value, no composition table can give the true glutamine content of a food, and any per-food glutamine figure quoted to two decimal places should be treated with suspicion. In the trial, the measured mechanism was normalisation of an elevated urinary lactulose/mannitol ratio, so the endpoint was permeability rather than amino acid status; a food equivalent would have to reproduce the permeability effect, which has not been shown.
- **Does the body make it:** Glutamine is the most abundant free amino acid in the body, about 20 percent of the free amino acid pool in blood and 40 to 60 percent in muscle and liver, and whole-body endogenous production is 40 to 80 g/day, with skeletal muscle the dominant synthetic site and net exporter to enterocytes and immune cells while lung, liver, brain and adipose also contribute. A healthy adult eating normally is nowhere near short, so a 5 g scoop for leaky gut is a rounding error against the flux already arriving at the gut wall, and leaky gut is not a validated clinical entity in the first place, so the scoop has no defined target. Severe catabolic illness is where this changes, with demand exceeding synthesis and plasma glutamine falling, which is what CONDITIONAL means: self-sufficient in health and insufficient in named states.
- **Pathway:** Glutamate plus ammonia gives glutamine via ATP-dependent glutamine synthetase (GLUL), predominantly in skeletal muscle and also in liver, lung, brain and adipose tissue. Muscle holds about 80 percent of body glutamine and releases it to plasma, while enterocytes and lymphocytes consume it via glutaminase (GLS) as fuel and nitrogen donor. Two roles the gut-repair pitch ignores: glutamine is the body's ammonia shuttle, carrying nitrogen from peripheral tissue to the liver for urea synthesis, and to the kidney, where renal glutaminase releases ammonia for acid-base handling.
- **Amount made:** Whole-body endogenous production is 40 to 80 g/day by isotopic and pharmacokinetic estimates, with muscle the dominant site rather than the sole one (PMC6266414, Cruzat V et al., Nutrients 2018;10:1564).
- **Still matters when:** Burns, major trauma, major surgery, sepsis, short bowel syndrome and long-term parenteral nutrition; sickle cell disease, where oral glutamine has a licensed indication. Dose and route are not neutral: high-dose glutamine given to unselected critically ill patients with multiorgan failure increased mortality in the REDOXS trial (PMID 23594003). For a healthy adult with digestive complaints, the 40 to 80 g/day figure is the reason not to buy it.

*Refs. Zhou Gut 2019 PMID 30108163; REDOXS PMID 23594003; Niihara NEJM PMID 30021096*

### Prebiotics: inulin, FOS, GOS

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 5-15 g/d of inulin-type fructans, clustering around 10-12 g/d in constipation trials. Tolerance is the binding constraint above 10-15 g/d.
- **Who it is for:** Adults with chronic constipation and habitually low fibre intake who tolerate fermentation.

**Pros**

- Inulin does something measurable for constipation: stool frequency SMD 0.69 and Bristol stool consistency SMD 1.07 across 5 trials and 252 subjects.
- It reliably increases faecal bifidobacteria, and it is cheap.
- EFSA has an authorised claim for native chicory inulin at 12 g/d for maintenance of normal defecation, which is more regulatory support than most items here have.

**Cons**

- Feed your good bacteria is the current premium reframing of what used to be sold as roughage, at roughly ten times the price per gram of fibre.
- The endpoint switch is the specific dishonesty: marketing leans on the reliable rise in faecal bifidobacteria, which is a microbiological measurement, and lets the consumer infer a clinical benefit.
- The inulin meta-analysis states explicitly that pain and bloating do NOT improve. The largest systematic review of prebiotics in IBS found the data sparse and insufficient to establish efficacy.
- Self-defeating for IBS patients on or considering a low-FODMAP diet, because inulin, FOS and GOS ARE the fermentable oligosaccharides that diet removes. Anyone whose dominant symptom is bloating or flatulence should not take these.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Fibres your gut bacteria feed on, boosting bifidobacteria and producing short-chain fatty acids in the colon.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Inulin and fructo-oligosaccharides are fructose polymers from chicory root, Jerusalem artichoke, onion, garlic and wheat, differing mainly in chain length: FOS has a degree of polymerisation below about 9, inulin extends to 60. GOS are galactose oligomers produced enzymatically from lactose and are structural analogues of human milk oligosaccharides. All three resist human digestive enzymes, reach the colon intact and are selectively fermented, reproducibly raising Bifidobacterium abundance, which is the basis of the ISAPP definition of a prebiotic as a substrate selectively used by host microorganisms to confer a health benefit (PMID 28611480). Fermentation yields acetate, propionate and butyrate; butyrate is the primary respiratory fuel of the colonocyte, and the short-chain fatty acid pool lowers luminal pH and supports barrier function. That same fermentability produces the gas and bloating these compounds are known for, and is why they are classed as high-FODMAP.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

This item bundles three substances with two different answers. Inulin and fructo-oligosaccharide (FOS) are RICH: chicory root at 15-20 g per 100 g fresh weight, Jerusalem artichoke at roughly 8-22 g, garlic at about 9-16 g, onion at about 2 g, with smaller amounts in globe artichoke, shallot, salsify, leek, asparagus, banana, wheat and rye. Beta-galacto-oligosaccharide (GOS) is SYNTHETIC and should not be carried under the same heading: the beta-GOS used in trials is manufactured enzymatically by transgalactosylation of lactose and has no meaningful presence in ordinary food. The galacto-oligosaccharides that do occur in legumes are alpha-galactosides (raffinose, stachyose, verbascose), a chemically different family with different bacterial targets. Habitual dietary intake of the fructan class is low: mean 1.6-1.7 g/day in a Slovenian survey (DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771) and median inulin 1.3 g/day in UK schoolchildren (PMID 40808333).

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Jerusalem artichoke, raw tuber | roughly 8-22 g inulin per 100 g fresh tuber, depending on cultivar, season and storage (HPLC studies 8.16-13.46%; a British Journal of Nutrition source 14-19%; Van Loo 16-20%) | 1 cup slices (150 g) | roughly 12-33 g inulin | 240-660% of a 5 g/day supplement dose; 1.2-3.3x the 10 g/day tolerated ceiling for native inulin | Composition literature on Jerusalem artichoke inulin (8-22 g/100 g fresh weight) and Van Loo et al., Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 1995;35:525-52, PMID 8777017; portion weight USDA FDC ID 169236. The submission's narrower 12-13.6 g/100 g used the low end of a wide range. |
| Salsify, raw root *[unconfirmed]* | named as inulin-rich in the 15.6-16.7 g/100 g group (Nutr Bull 2025, PMID 40808333, citing Van Loo et al. 1995); no salsify-specific value was confirmed | 1 cup sliced (133 g), conventional portion | roughly 21 g inulin if the group figure applies to salsify; indicative only | roughly 4x a 5 g/day supplement dose on that unconfirmed figure; about 30 g of root would supply 5 g | Nutrition Bulletin 2025 (PMID 40808333) names salsify in the same sentence as the 15.6-16.7 g/100 g range, which spans four foods and is not salsify's own measured value |
| Chicory root, raw (industrial inulin source; not eaten as a vegetable) | 15-20 g inulin per 100 g fresh root | 1 root (60 g) | roughly 9-12 g inulin | 180-240% of a typical 5 g/day supplement dose, and at or above the 10 g/day tolerance ceiling for native inulin | Inulin range from extraction and composition literature (chicory 15-20 g/100 g fresh weight); portion weight USDA FDC ID 169993, whose own total dietary fibre field reads 1.5 g/100 g because the method used does not capture inulin. The submission's Molecules 2025 citation is a fruit-snack formulation paper and is a secondary source for the value. |
| Globe artichoke (Cynara scolymus), raw *[unconfirmed]* | placed in the top fructan group by Muir et al. 2007 (PMID 17625872), whose high-fructan range is 1.2-17.4 g/100 g; no artichoke-specific value confirmed here | 1 medium head, edible portion (about 128 g), conventional portion | grams rather than milligrams, but not resolvable from the confirmed group range | likely a meaningful fraction of a 5 g dose, and possibly above it; cannot be stated precisely from the confirmed sources | Muir HJ et al. 2007, PMID 17625872, top fructan group |
| Onion, mature, raw | 1.97 +/- 0.05 g fructan (AOAC 999.03) | 1 medium onion (110 g) | 2.2 g fructan | 43% of a 5 g/day supplement dose | Ferjancic B et al., Front Nutr 2024;11:1446771, DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771 (value verified verbatim); portion weight USDA FDC ID 170000. In that survey onion was the largest single contributor to habitual fructan intake at 0.72 g/day. |
| Shallot, raw *[unconfirmed]* | placed in the top fructan group by Muir et al. 2007 (PMID 17625872), high-fructan range 1.2-17.4 g/100 g; no shallot-specific value confirmed here | 1 shallot (about 25 g), conventional portion | roughly 0.3-4.4 g fructan on the group range; too wide to be treated as a figure | 6-87% of a 5 g/day dose on that range; indicative only | Muir HJ et al. 2007, PMID 17625872, top fructan group |
| Garlic, raw | about 9-16 g inulin | 1 clove (3 g) | 0.27-0.48 g inulin, about 0.38 g at the midpoint | about 7.5% of a 5 g/day supplement dose at the midpoint | Van Loo et al., Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 1995;35:525-52, PMID 8777017 (garlic roughly 9-16 g/100 g); portion weight USDA FDC ID 169230. The submission's 15.6-16.7 g/100 g was a misreading: that range is given in Nutr Bull 2025 (PMID 40808333) across four foods (Jerusalem artichoke, chicory root, salsify, garlic), not as garlic's own value. |
| Asparagus, raw spear | 1.48 +/- 0.17 g fructan per 100 g dry matter, which converts to about 0.1 g per 100 g fresh at 7% dry matter; other datasets give up to about 0.5 g per 100 g fresh | 1 cup raw (134 g) | roughly 0.13-0.67 g fructan | roughly 3-13% of a 5 g/day supplement dose | Goni I et al., Foods 2024;13(8):1154, DOI 10.3390/foods13081154, PMID 38672827 (dry-matter basis, abstract verified); comparative fructan datasets for the higher fresh-weight figures; portion weight USDA FDC ID 168389. The fresh-weight value is uncertain by roughly fivefold, so it is given as a range rather than the submission's single 0.1 g figure. |
| Leek, bulb and lower leaf, raw | 0.24 +/- 0.00 g fructan (AOAC 999.03) | 1 leek (89 g) | 0.21 g fructan | 4% of a 5 g/day supplement dose | Ferjancic B et al., DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771 (value verified verbatim); portion weight USDA FDC ID 169246. Muir et al. (PMID 17625872) found 1.2-17.4 g/100 g across high-fructan alliums including leek bulb, so leek is highly variable by part and cultivar. |
| Banana, raw | 0.11 +/- 0.01 g fructan (AOAC 999.03) | 1 medium banana (118 g) | 0.13 g fructan | under 3% of a 5 g/day supplement dose | Ferjancic B et al., DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771 (value verified verbatim); portion weight USDA FDC ID 173944. |
| White wheat bread and white wheat flour | 0.14 +/- 0.03 g fructan in white bread; 0.75 +/- 0.04 g in white wheat flour T500 | 2 slices bread (56 g, at a 28 g slice) | 0.08 g fructan | under 2% of a 5 g/day supplement dose | Ferjancic B et al., DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771 (values verified verbatim). In that Slovenian survey white flour T500 was the second largest contributor at 0.41 g/day, behind onion at 0.72 g/day; the claim that bread and flour supplied the largest share belongs to the separate UK children's study (PMID 40808333), where cereals supplied 58.1% of inulin intake and wheat 69% in the comparative data. Slice weight is 28 g; the submission's 32 g slice was unconfirmed. |

- **To match the dose:** For inulin and FOS the answer depends on which food. A 5 g/day dose is reachable from about 250 g of raw onion (two to three medium onions), about 10-19 cloves of garlic on the corrected 9-16 g/100 g range (the submission's 30 cloves was wrong by roughly 3x and contradicted its own food row), about 23-63 g of Jerusalem artichoke, or about 30 g of salsify on an unconfirmed figure. It is out of reach from banana (about 4.5 kg), leek (about 2.1 kg) and asparagus (roughly 1-5 kg, the range reflecting a fivefold uncertainty in the fresh-weight figure; the submission's flat 5 kg used the low end only). The tolerance ceiling is the more useful number than the dose: up to 10 g/day of native inulin and up to 5 g/day of oligofructose were well tolerated in a randomised crossover trial, while 10 g of oligofructose substantially increased symptoms, chiefly flatulence then bloating (Bonnema et al., PMID 20497775). One 150 g cup of Jerusalem artichoke delivers roughly 12-33 g of inulin, so between one and three times the tolerated daily ceiling in a single sitting. For GOS the answer is a flat no: the beta-GOS doses used in trials are made industrially and no ordinary food supplies them.
- **Food vs supplement:** Chicory root is the classic textbook answer and a poor recommendation: it is the industrial source of inulin and is roasted as a coffee substitute rather than eaten as a vegetable, so its 15-20 g/100 g headline is not a dietary route. Dandelion greens, which are eaten as a vegetable, and burdock root and yacon are recognised high-inulin foods that answer that objection, though no confirmed figures for them were available here. Chicory also illustrates why fibre tables and fructan assays disagree, since USDA lists chicory root at only 1.5 g total dietary fibre per 100 g (FDC 169993). Purified inulin and oligofructose are safe as food ingredients, and concentration is what causes symptoms: the same molecule that is unremarkable spread across a day's vegetables provokes flatulence and bloating as a single 10 g bolus (PMID 20497775). Briet et al. (PMID 7588500) reported a symptom ladder for FOS (excess flatus above 30 g/day, borborygmi and bloating above 40 g, cramps and diarrhoea at 50 g, with no tolerance developing on chronic dosing); the citation is correct and on topic, but those specific thresholds could not be retrieved from the full text and are unconfirmed. The reverse caveat matters clinically: the 'eat more prebiotic vegetables' list (onion, garlic, leek, globe artichoke, Jerusalem artichoke, wheat, rye) is precisely the list a low-FODMAP diet removes, so in IBS the food route and the supplement route can both make things worse. Finally, do not treat legume alpha-GOS as a food form of supplemental beta-GOS; the reported contents in Phaseolus vulgaris (raffinose 0.8-7.4 mg/g, stachyose 20.0-30.9 mg/g, verbascose 0.3-1.3 mg/g, DOI 10.3390/foods15020391) come from a genuine and on-topic paper whose individual values could not be confirmed, and they are different molecules with a flatulence profile of their own.
- **Absorption:** Fructans are not absorbed at all, so the variable is where and how fast they ferment, and chain length decides that. Oligofructose (DP 2-8) ferments proximally and quickly, which is why its tolerated dose was half that of long-chain native inulin in the same trial, 5 g against 10 g (PMID 20497775). Assay and plant part also drive the numbers: onion measured 1.97 g/100 g and leek 0.24 g/100 g by AOAC 999.03 in one national survey (DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771), while an Australian survey using the Megazyme assay reported 1.2-17.4 g/100 g across high-fructan alliums including leek bulb (PMID 17625872). Storage matters too, since fructan is depolymerised during onion storage. Single figures should be read as indicative rather than exact. GOS is fermented similarly but is a distinct substrate: beta-GOS and legume alpha-galactosides are not interchangeable.
- **Does the body make it:** Inulin and fructo-oligosaccharides are beta(2->1) fructans, and commercial galacto-oligosaccharides are galactose oligomers of DP2 to DP6 usually carrying a terminal glucose, produced by beta-galactosidase transgalactosylation of lactose. The 2017 ISAPP consensus defines a prebiotic as a substrate selectively utilized by host microorganisms conferring a health benefit, and imposes no non-digestibility requirement, so the operative criteria are selective utilisation plus a demonstrated benefit; non-digestibility remains a practical property of the common fructans and of GOS at DP3 and above, which is why they reach the colon to be fermented. Nothing here is endogenous, so the real question is dose tolerance, because the fermentation that produces the benefit also produces gas.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE, with a degree-of-polymerisation caveat. Humans lack inulinase and beta-fructofuranosidase, and 96 +/- 25 percent of FOS with DP>=2 is recovered intact at the distal ileum. GOS at DP3 to DP6 also passes unchanged, but most GOS DP2 is hydrolysed by host brush-border enzymes in a linkage-dependent way, roughly 77 to 81 percent for beta(11), beta(1->2) and beta(1->3) and roughly 32 to 56 percent for beta(1->4) and beta(1->6).
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway; intake is the only variable). Note that the ISAPP 2017 definition imposes no non-digestibility requirement, so the axis argument here rests on the measured ileal recovery figures rather than on the definition itself.
- **Still matters when:** All intake is dietary, so dose is the lever. Reasonable where the aim is to shift colonic fermentation, discounting the GOS DP2 fraction as host-digested, with the caveat that fermentable oligosaccharides are the F in FODMAP and often provoke bloating and pain in IBS at the doses used in studies.

*Refs. Ford PMID 30294792; Collado Yurrita PMID 25208775; Reynolds Lancet 2019 PMID 30638909*

### Saffron, SAMe and other mood botanicals

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** Saffron 30 mg/d of standardised extract. SAMe 1,600-3,200 mg/d as an adjunct.
- **Who it is for:** At most, adults with mild to moderate depression taking no serotonergic medication, and only with a clinician involved.

**Pros**

- Saffron: 21 randomised trials found Beck Depression Inventory -4.86 (-6.58 to -3.14) and Beck Anxiety Inventory -5.29.
- SAMe: one 2024 meta-analysis of 23 trials and 2,183 participants found monotherapy better than placebo, SMD -0.58 (-0.93 to -0.23), at moderate certainty.

**Cons**

- Saffron effect sizes are implausibly large for the trial size. One meta-analysis reported SMD -1.22 versus placebo, which is better than licensed antidepressants achieve. Effect sizes that large in small short trials usually indicate small-study effects and a narrow research base, so treat it as a red flag rather than a selling point.
- Self-report instruments move while clinician-rated instruments do not, which is the classic pattern of an unblinded expectancy effect.
- SAMe has directly conflicting meta-analyses published in the same year. A second 2024 analysis of 14 trials and 1,522 participants found NO significant difference versus placebo, versus imipramine or versus escitalopram. Report both.
- SAMe is associated with switching into mania or hypomania in bipolar disorder, and homocysteine can rise on it. Both saffron and SAMe carry serotonin syndrome risk with serotonergic medication. Saffron should be avoided in pregnancy on the basis of traditional emmenagogue use.
- Depression is a condition where delaying effective treatment carries real risk. Neither of these should displace a clinical assessment.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Mood supplements of different chemistry; none is a nutrient the body must obtain.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Saffron supplies crocin, crocetin and safranal from Crocus sativus stigmas, and trials of about 30 mg/day over 6 to 8 weeks report antidepressant effects, though most are small, brief and concentrated in a narrow set of centres, which limits confidence. SAMe is chemically different in kind: the body synthesises it from methionine and ATP through methionine adenosyltransferase, and it serves as the universal methyl donor for DNA, phospholipid, catecholamine and creatine methylation. A Cochrane review of 8 trials and 934 participants found no clear difference between SAMe and imipramine or escitalopram used alone, and only low-quality evidence favouring SAMe added to an SSRI. Neither category is an essential nutrient: saffron constituents are plant secondary metabolites, and SAMe is made endogenously, with low tissue SAMe appearing only as a downstream consequence of folate or vitamin B12 deficiency or of the inborn error MAT1A deficiency.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Partly

This item bundles three different answers, so each gets its own sentence. Saffron is RICH: it is a supermarket spice with USDA composition and portion entries, its clinical dose is a cooking quantity, and it is listed under foods below, which is why the previous NOT_FOOD label on the group was the clearest error in the submission. SAMe has no dietary source: there is no meaningful preformed SAMe in food and no composition-table entry, the body synthesises it continuously from methionine and ATP by methionine adenosyltransferase, and the commercial material is made by Saccharomyces cerevisiae fermentation and stabilised as the tosylate disulfate or 1,4-butanedisulfonate salt, so it is biosynthetic in origin and the SYNTHETIC label holds in the sense of 'no dietary source' rather than 'chemically synthesised'. St John's wort aerial parts, rhodiola root, ashwagandha root and Griffonia simplicifolia seed (the source of 5-HTP) are NOT_FOOD: nobody eats them in any normal diet, they have no composition-table entry, and they have no portion size.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Saffron, dried stigmas (Crocus sativus) | 100 g of saffron is 3,333 clinical doses of 30 mg. No apocarotenoid percentage is stated here: the previously cited '8% of dry weight' for crocins, picrocrocin, safranal and crocetin combined and the 'roughly 3.4 mg of crocins per 30 mg dose' could not be confirmed against any source reached, and they contradict each other, since 3.4 mg in 30 mg is 11.3% crocins alone, above the claimed 8% ceiling for all apocarotenoids | 1 teaspoon (0.7 g) | 700 mg of saffron, 23 times the 30 mg dose used in depression RCTs | 2,300% of a 30 mg trial dose | USDA portion weights for Spices, saffron (1 tbsp = 2.1 g, so 1 tsp = 0.7 g) independently confirmed; the FDC ID 170934 previously cited could not be confirmed against a live lookup. Trial dose: Akhondzadeh S, Tahmacebi-Pour N, Noorbala AA et al., Crocus sativus L. in the treatment of mild to moderate depression: a double-blind, randomized and placebo-controlled trial, Phytother Res 2005;19(2):148-51, PMID 15852492 (30 mg/day given as 15 mg twice daily for 6 weeks, 40 outpatients with DSM-IV major depression and baseline HAM-D of 18 or more, against PLACEBO). The fluoxetine comparison belongs to a separate trial (Noorbala AA et al., J Ethnopharmacol 2005) |
| Saffron, culinary pinch as used to season a dish | as above | a generous pinch, about 0.1 g, seasoning a dish for four (roughly 50 threads at published thread weights near 2 mg; the commonly quoted 20 to 30 threads per 0.1 g would imply a heavier 3 to 5 mg thread). For reference the FDA reference amount customarily consumed for saffron is 0.125 g | about 25 mg of saffron per person from a dish for four; 125 mg for a single 0.125 g reference serving | 83% of the 30 mg trial dose per serving of the dish; over 400% for one 0.125 g reference serving | USDA portion and reference-serving figures for Spices, saffron (portion weights confirmed, FDC ID unconfirmed); trial dose PMID 15852492 (placebo-controlled) |

- **To match the dose:** For saffron the gap is zero or negative, which is the most useful finding in the set: the 30 mg/day used in the depression RCTs is about 15 threads at published thread weights near 2 mg, or about 6 to 9 threads if a thread weighs the 3 to 5 mg implied by the commonly quoted 20 to 30 threads per 0.1 g, which is under a generous culinary pinch, and one teaspoon of saffron in a paella is over twenty doses. The barrier is cost rather than physiology. For SAMe there is nothing to compute, because there is no food source: trials use 400 to 1,600 mg/day of a manufactured molecule, while the body's own SAMe turnover (on the order of grams per day) is fed by roughly 1 g/day of dietary methionine from protein foods, which is a different intervention from swallowing SAMe. For the remaining botanicals (St John's wort typically 300 mg three times daily of a 0.3% hypericin extract, rhodiola 200 to 600 mg, ashwagandha 300 to 600 mg root extract, 5-HTP 100 to 300 mg) the food gap is undefined because the source plants are not foods at any quantity.
- **Food vs supplement:** Saffron is the mildest food-versus-extract case in this whole set, since trials use either powdered stigma or a hydroalcoholic extract standardised to crocins and safranal, so kitchen saffron is close to the trial material. The caveat is at the top end: saffron is toxic at gram doses, with roughly 5 g reported as a toxic dose and 12 to 20 g as potentially lethal, and it is a traditional abortifacient, so scaling from a pinch to a spoonful is genuinely hazardous. Standardised extracts also concentrate crocins, so milligram for milligram an extract is not the spice. For the rest of the group the form gap is total: St John's wort is a potent CYP3A4 and P-glycoprotein inducer that has caused transplant rejection, contraceptive failure and serotonin syndrome with SSRIs; ashwagandha root extracts have accumulated a documented cluster of hepatotoxicity case reports, including in regulatory reviews, while traditional use was of small amounts of root powder in milk as medicine and never as a food; purified 5-HTP bypasses the rate-limiting tryptophan hydroxylase step that normally regulates serotonin synthesis, which is exactly why it carries serotonin syndrome risk with serotonergic drugs and why no food behaves like it.
- **Absorption:** Saffron's crocins are poorly absorbed intact and are hydrolysed in the gut to crocetin, which is what appears in plasma; safranal is volatile and is partly lost in long simmering, so a slow-cooked dish delivers a different apocarotenoid profile than a capsule taken cold, and blooming saffron in warm liquid before adding it is the culinary analogue of extraction. SAMe is chemically unstable and is degraded in gastric acid unless enterically coated, and oral bioavailability is only about 1% because of extensive first-pass metabolism, which is why oral doses are 400 to 1,600 mg while parenteral doses are far smaller. The classic bad recommendation in this space is 'eat turkey or eggs for 5-HTP': food supplies tryptophan, and dietary tryptophan competes with the other large neutral amino acids in the same protein meal for LAT1 transport across the blood-brain barrier, so a high-protein meal can lower rather than raise brain tryptophan availability. Food is a poor delivery route for this entire group.
- **Does the body make it:** Saffron's constituents are apocarotenoids, the crocins plus crocetin, picrocrocin and safranal, cleaved from zeaxanthin in Crocus sativus stigmas, and human tissue makes no part of them, which holds for the other plant members of this group too, so the roll-up for the bundle is NONE. The one member at the opposite end of the axis is SAMe, which is FULL: the body's universal methyl donor, made in every cell from methionine and ATP and most heavily in the liver, with a CONDITIONAL carve-out in chronic liver disease and cholestasis where MAT1A activity and hepatic SAMe fall. For the botanicals the axis is silent and the case rests on trial evidence; for SAMe an oral dose is pharmacology, since a healthy person eating normal protein cannot be deficient.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for saffron and the other botanicals: apocarotenoids are cleaved from zeaxanthin by carotenoid cleavage dioxygenase (CCD2) in Crocus sativus stigmas and then glycosylated to crocins, with no human counterpart. SAMe: L-methionine plus ATP, catalysed by methionine adenosyltransferase (MAT1A in liver, MAT2A in other tissues); the liver metabolises about 50 percent of dietary methionine and hosts up to 85 percent of all transmethylation reactions. SAMe donates its methyl group to give S-adenosylhomocysteine, hydrolysed by AHCY to homocysteine, which is either remethylated by MTR with 5-methyl-THF and vitamin B12 or by BHMT with betaine, or committed to transsulfuration by CBS with vitamin B6.
- **Amount made:** 0 for saffron and the other botanicals (no human biosynthetic pathway). SAMe is not directly measured as a synthesis rate; it is bounded by measured whole-body methionine transmethylation flux, which stable-isotope tracer work puts on the order of several grams of SAM turnover per day in adults with hepatic synthesis dominant, and no single consensus figure is established. Supplement doses are 400 to 1,600 mg/day.
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis for the botanicals: screen saffron on trial size and independence (small trials, several from the same groups), on adulteration of one of the world's most expensive spices with dyed safflower, turmeric or other Crocus material, on the 28 to 30 mg/day extract dose used in trials, and on serotonergic additivity. For SAMe, chronic liver disease and cholestasis, where MAT activity and hepatic SAMe are genuinely reduced, is the one setting with a mechanistic replacement case; the mood indication is pharmacological, with caution in bipolar disorder because of switch to mania and with serotonergic drugs.

*Refs. Yang PMID 29849461; Ghaderi PMID 31987241; Limveeraprajak PMID 38423354; Peng PMID 38199136*

### Quercetin and taurine

**Verdict:** Skip it &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** Quercetin at least 500 mg/d for the blood pressure effect. Taurine 0.5-6 g/d.
- **Who it is for:** At most, people with elevated blood pressure or components of metabolic syndrome, for a small surrogate effect.

**Pros**

- Taurine has the strongest human surrogate data of any longevity compound here: 25 trials, 1,024 participants, systolic -4.0 mmHg (-7.29 to -0.71), diastolic -1.51 mmHg, fasting glucose -5.9 mg/dL, all dose-dependent.
- Quercetin at 500 mg/d or more gave systolic -4.45 mmHg and diastolic -2.98 mmHg in the dose-stratified analysis.

**Cons**

- The taurine longevity story was directly refuted in humans in 2025. The 2023 Science paper showed taurine declines with age in mice, monkeys and humans and extends lifespan in mice. A 2025 Aging Cell paper found circulating taurine is NOT associated with age, muscle mass, strength, physical performance or mitochondrial function in humans.
- Quercetin effect sizes are shrinking as the evidence matures. The 2016 meta-analysis gave systolic -3.04 mmHg; the 2023 umbrella review of meta-analyses found only -1.9 mmHg with heterogeneity of 88%, and nothing else significant, at very low certainty.
- A 2 to 4 mmHg systolic reduction is small next to any antihypertensive drug or to sodium reduction.
- Quercetin inhibits CYP3A4 and P-glycoprotein, so treat it as an interaction risk with ciclosporin, tacrolimus, CYP3A4-metabolised statins, warfarin and many chemotherapeutics. It is also a topoisomerase inhibitor in vitro, which is a specific reason not to combine it with chemotherapy.
- Taurine effects are additive with antihypertensives, insulin and sulfonylureas.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Quercetin is a plant pigment with no bodily role; taurine supports bile, heart and retina.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** These are two unrelated molecules sold together for convenience. Quercetin is a plant flavonol from onions, capers, apples and tea; it is heavily glucuronidated and sulfated on first pass, so free aglycone concentrations in plasma stay in the nanomolar range and most antioxidant actions demonstrated in cell culture cannot occur at achievable oral doses. It has no physiological role in humans and no deficiency state of any kind. Taurine is a sulfur-containing beta-amino acid, the most abundant free amino acid in heart, retina and leukocytes. It conjugates bile acids, stabilises membranes, modulates calcium handling and cell volume, and is required for the mitochondrial tRNA modification that supports respiratory chain protein synthesis. Adults synthesize taurine from cysteine via cysteine sulfinic acid decarboxylase and obtain roughly 40 to 400 mg a day from meat and seafood, so nobody eating ordinary food becomes taurine depleted. Taurine is classified as conditionally essential, and the one documented human depletion is iatrogenic rather than dietary: in children fed long-term on taurine-free parenteral nutrition, plasma taurine ran about 26 plus or minus 13 versus 57 plus or minus 16 micromol/L in controls, and all 8 children tested had abnormal electroretinograms with delayed rod and cone implicit times, detectable before any visual problem was noticed. Taurine depletion also contributes to parenteral-nutrition-associated cholestasis in infants. Adding taurine to the intravenous solution restored plasma taurine in 4 supplemented children and normalised the electroretinogram in 3 of those 4, though plasma taurine relapsed in 2 of those 3 within a year of intravenous taurine being stopped, and the whole evidence base is a small 1985 case series. Paediatric parenteral amino acid solutions now routinely contain taurine, which prevents the problem at source.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Partly

Quercetin is present across ordinary plant food: raw capers 233.84 mg/100 g, lovage leaves 170.00, dock (Rumex) 86.20, radish leaves 70.37, wild rocket 66.19, fresh dill 55.15, coriander leaves 52.90, yellow wax hot peppers 50.63, fennel leaves 48.80 and red onion 39.21, against habitual intakes of tens of milligrams a day, so naming capers as the only rich source overstated how narrow the food route is. Taurine is present in animal food only, richest in shellfish, with beef muscle at 61.1 mg/100 g wet weight and chicken thigh at 47.34 mg/100 g; it was undetectable in all 48 plant foods Laidlaw analysed, so a vegan diet supplies none, while quercetin intake is unaffected by avoiding animal food. The two compounds are RICH for opposite populations, which is why they are described separately throughout this entry.

- **To match the dose:** QUERCETIN: trials use 500-1,000 mg/day. From raw capers at 233.84 mg/100 g that is 214-428 g per day, 14 to 29 tablespoons of a salt-cured condiment and a salt load nobody should take; from dock leaves at 86.20 mg/100 g it is 580 g to 1.16 kg; from wild rocket at 66.19 mg/100 g it is 756 g to 1.51 kg; from raw red onion at 39.21 mg/100 g it is 1.28-2.55 kg; from apples with skin at 4.01 mg/100 g it is 12.5-25 kg, about 70 to 140 apples. A 500 mg capsule is roughly 25 to 50 times what a diet delivers. TAURINE: trials use 1-6 g/day, commonly 3 g. From beef at 61.1 mg/100 g that is 4.9 kg of raw steak per day, from chicken thigh at 47.34 mg/100 g it is 6.3 kg, and from clam meat at roughly 450 mg/100 g as eaten it is about 670 g of clam meat daily, the only route within an order of magnitude of feasible and still nothing a person eats. Neither dose is reachable by eating normally.
- **Food vs supplement:** QUERCETIN, MAJOR. Supplements are almost always the aglycone, extracted from Sophora japonica flower buds or Dimorphandra mollis pods, which are not foods anyone eats; food quercetin is glycosides (glucosides in onions, rutin in tea and apples). The forms are not interchangeable in the gut, and a 500-1,000 mg aglycone dose is a pharmacological exposure that inhibits drug-metabolising enzymes and transporters, whereas 10-30 mg/day from onions and apples never approaches it. Capers are the richest source per 100 g and the worst practical recommendation, since the value is for a salt-cured condiment eaten a tablespoon at a time and the canned figure ranges 45.05-519.85 mg/100 g across 20 samples, an elevenfold spread; fresh herbs, dock, radish leaves and wild rocket at 48-86 mg/100 g are the more usable everyday sources. Apples with skin are the classic textbook answer and are roughly 60 times too dilute; peeling drops them from 4.01 to 1.05 mg/100 g. TAURINE, MODERATE. The supplement is synthetic and chemically identical to food taurine, so there is no extract-versus-food problem here. The two real caveats are analytical and culinary. Shellfish taurine numbers in circulation are often dry-weight figures presented as if they were per 100 g of food, which inflates them four- to sixfold; the three bivalve rows above are dry-weight values from an umami study, converted with an assumed 80-83% moisture that is labelled as an assumption in each row. And taurine is a free amino acid rather than protein-bound, so it leaches: taurine and creatine were the compounds most reduced by cooking beef, partly into the cooking juices (Purchas RW, Rutherfurd SM, Pearce PD, Vather R, Wilkinson BHP, Meat Sci 2004;68(2):201-7, PMID 22062229; the companion 90 min at 70 C result is from lamb muscle, Purchas RW et al., Meat Sci 2004;66(3):629-37, PMID 22060873). A stew keeps its taurine in the broth; a grilled steak with the juices discarded does not.
- **Absorption:** QUERCETIN, quantified and decisive. In healthy ileostomy volunteers absorption was 52 plus or minus 15% for quercetin glucosides from fried onions, 17 plus or minus 15% for quercetin rutinoside (the main form in tea and apples) and 24 plus or minus 9% for pure quercetin aglycone, the form used in supplements; conjugation with glucose enhances absorption (Hollman PC, de Vries JH, van Leeuwen SD, Mengelers MJ, Katan MB, Am J Clin Nutr 1995;62(6):1276-82, PMID 7491892, citation and values confirmed). So per milligram, onion quercetin is roughly twice as absorbable as the capsule and three times as absorbable as tea or apple rutin, which narrows the effective gap a little and nowhere near enough to close it, since urinary recovery was only 0.5% of the amount absorbed. TAURINE. It is present in food as the free amino acid rather than bound in protein, absorbed by the TauT transporter with high oral bioavailability, and the supplement form is identical, so absorption is not where food loses. Food loses on concentration and on cooking: the content of a portion depends heavily on whether the cooking liquid is consumed, and reported values vary severalfold with muscle type (beef taurine ranged 0.552 to 23.861 umol/g across 21 muscles, PMID 30811817) and with breed in poultry (39.29-47.34 mg/100 g, PMID 36230082). Reading dry-weight shellfish values as wet-weight is the single commonest error in this literature, and it is the weak point of the shellfish rows here.
- **Does the body make it:** Two different verdicts in one row, so the roll-up is PARTIAL and the split has to be named. Quercetin scores NONE: it is a plant flavonol, no animal synthesises flavonoids at all because the pathway requires chalcone synthase, which humans lack, so every microgram is dietary and there is no requirement and no deficiency state. Taurine scores PARTIAL: you make it from cysteine by three routes, CSAD, which has low activity in humans, GADL1, which decarboxylates cysteine sulfinate to hypotaurine, and the cysteamine route where cysteamine from coenzyme A degradation is oxidised to hypotaurine by cysteamine dioxygenase (ADO), and that limited overall capacity is why tissue and plasma taurine track intake of meat, fish and shellfish. So any case for quercetin has to be pharmacological and runs into poor bioavailability and heavy conjugation, while taurine has a genuine dietary contribution and a short list of states where it matters.
- **Pathway:** Quercetin: NOT APPLICABLE in humans. Plants build it from phenylalanine via PAL, 4-coumaroyl-CoA, chalcone synthase, flavanone 3-hydroxylase and flavonol synthase; human enzymology is limited to conjugation and degradation (intestinal and hepatic UGT, SULT and COMT, plus gut microbial ring fission). Taurine: cysteine is oxidised by cysteine dioxygenase (CDO1) to cysteine sulfinate, decarboxylated by CSAD or GADL1 to hypotaurine and oxidised to taurine, with a parallel cysteamine route through ADO; liver is the main site, taurine is conjugated to bile acids by BAAT and held in tissue by the transporter SLC6A6 (TauT), which the kidney upregulates on low intake.
- **Amount made:** Quercetin: 0 mg/day, no human synthesis. Taurine: NOT ESTABLISHED, there is no validated human de novo synthesis rate, and the evidence for the limitation is enzymatic, with CSAD the rate-limiting step and very low in activity in humans (PMC8153620). Intake-side reference points: roughly 40 to 400 mg/day in omnivores, close to zero on a strict vegan diet, supplement doses of 500 to 3,000 mg/day, and about 1,000 mg in a can of energy drink.
- **Still matters when:** Taurine: vegans and vegetarians with near-zero intake, preterm infants and long-term parenteral nutrition (which is why formula and PN solutions are taurine-fortified), SLC6A6 mutations causing retinal degeneration and cardiomyopathy, where it is treatment, and supervised clinical use in heart failure or hepatic disease; the CSAD-independent routes plus efficient renal reabsorption through TauT are why no deficiency syndrome appears in healthy omnivores. Quercetin: NEVER ON THIS AXIS, since there is no requirement and no deficiency, and at ordinary oral doses free aglycone barely reaches plasma.

*Refs. Tzang PMID 38755142; Singh Science 2023; Marcangeli Aging Cell 2025 PMC12507425; Serban PMID 27405810; Arabi PMID 37654199*

## 6. Avoid it

Published evidence of harm from randomised trials, registries or national surveillance. Where a legitimate narrow medical indication exists it is stated, because the harm attaches to consumer self-dosing rather than supervised use.

### Beta-carotene supplements

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** No effective dose exists. The doses tested and found harmful were 20 mg/d and 30 mg/d combined with retinyl palmitate.
- **Who it is for:** Essentially nobody at supplemental doses. Narrow exceptions: clinical vitamin A deficiency and erythropoietic protoporphyria.

**Pros**

- Dietary carotenoids from vegetables are not implicated in any of the harm below. Eating carrots is fine.

**Cons**

- ATBC (n=29,133 male smokers, Finland, 5-8 years): 20 mg/d increased lung cancer incidence by 18% (95% CI 3 to 36%) and total mortality by 8% (1 to 16%).
- CARET (n=18,314 smokers, former smokers and asbestos-exposed workers): lung cancer RR 1.28 (1.04-1.57), all-cause death RR 1.17 (1.03-1.33), lung cancer death RR 1.46 (1.07-2.00). Stopped early.
- Cochrane, 78 randomised trials and 296,707 participants: beta-carotene increased mortality, RR 1.05 (1.01-1.09).
- This is the canonical case where the industry own logic failed in public. The observational data were strong, the mechanism was plausible, the trials were large and well conducted, and the answer was that the pill killed people.
- Absolute avoidance in current smokers, former smokers and anyone with asbestos exposure. USPSTF grade D recommendation.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A plant pigment the body can turn into vitamin A; nothing in the body needs it directly.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Beta-carotene is a provitamin A carotenoid, the orange pigment of carrots, sweet potato, pumpkin and dark green leaves. In the intestinal mucosa, beta-carotene 15,15'-oxygenase (BCO1) cleaves it to retinal, which is reduced to retinol and esterified for storage. The conversion is feedback-regulated and inefficient: the Institute of Medicine assigns 12 micrograms of dietary beta-carotene the vitamin A activity of 1 microgram of retinol. Purified beta-carotene in an oil matrix, the form in a capsule, bypasses the food-matrix release step and carries a separate and much more favourable ratio of 2 micrograms per 1 microgram retinol activity equivalent, so a supplement delivers roughly six times the retinol activity of the same weight eaten in food. Common BCO1 variants reduce conversion markedly in a substantial minority of people. Uncleaved beta-carotene acts as a lipid-phase antioxidant and singlet oxygen quencher in laboratory systems, but no human physiological process depends on the intact molecule, and every requirement it can meet is a vitamin A requirement that preformed retinol meets directly.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Carrots, carrot juice, sweet potato, squash and dark leafy greens are dense, ordinary sources: baked sweet potato flesh carries 11,509 µg beta-carotene per 100 g, carrot juice about 9,300 and raw carrot 8,285. Red palm oil is denser again at roughly 20,000 to 40,000 µg total carotenes per 100 g. By mass the 20 to 30 mg doses used in the intervention trials sit within reach of a few large portions, which makes this the one case where food and supplement look superficially comparable and behave completely differently.

| Sweet potato, baked in skin, flesh only | 11,509 µg beta-carotene | 1 medium sweet potato, flesh (130 g) | 14,962 µg | 75% of a 20 mg dose; 50% of a 30 mg dose | USDA sweet potato baked in skin, flesh, without salt, 23,018 µg per 200 g = 11,509 µg/100 g, via FDC mirror. | Carrots, old, boiled in unsalted water *[unconfirmed]* | 9,616 µg beta-carotene (plus 2,962 µg alpha-carotene) | 1 serving (80 g) | 7,693 µg | 38% of a 20 mg dose; 26% of a 30 mg dose | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 13-497 (vitamin fractions sheet) |

| Spinach, baby, boiled in unsalted water | about 6,288 µg beta-carotene | 1 serving, cooked (90 g) | 5,659 µg | 28% of a 20 mg dose | USDA cooked spinach, about 6,288 µg/100 g, via FDC mirror. Replaces the submission's 2,172 µg/100 g, which was understated on the same scale as the raw spinach value |
| Carrots, old, raw | 8,285 µg beta-carotene (plus 3,477 µg alpha-carotene) | 1 medium carrot (60 g) | 4,971 µg | 25% of a 20 mg dose; 17% of a 30 mg dose | USDA raw carrots, 10,604.8 µg beta-carotene and 4,450.6 µg alpha-carotene per 128 g, via FDC mirror. Replaces the submission's 9,149 µg beta (10% high) and 5,230 µg alpha (50% high) |
| Spinach, baby, raw | 5,626 µg beta-carotene | large salad bowl (80 g) | 4,501 µg | 23% of a 20 mg dose | USDA raw spinach, 1,687.8 µg per 30 g = 5,626 µg/100 g, via FDC mirror. Replaces the submission's 1,559 µg/100 g, which ran 3.6x low and produced a false comparative claim |

| Cantaloupe melon *[unconfirmed]* | about 2,000 µg beta-carotene | 1 large wedge (150 g) | 3,000 µg | 15% of a 20 mg dose | Figure as given in the verification report's missing-foods list |

Worth noting the published range for kale is unusually wide, since the older USDA SR Legacy figure was 9,226 µg/100 g, and a single value overstates the precision available |
| Curly kale, boiled in unsalted water *[unconfirmed]* | 2,097 µg beta-carotene | 1 serving, cooked (95 g) | 1,992 µg | 10% of a 20 mg dose | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 13-649 (11 UK samples) |
