Grass-fed summer butter genuinely carries more carotene than US commodity butter, and that has to be stated rather than folded silently into a single value |

- **To match the dose:** By label mass the trial doses are reachable: 20 mg/day is about 241 g of raw carrot (four medium carrots), 174 g of baked sweet potato, or 215 g of carrot juice, and 30 mg/day is about 362 g of raw carrot. Carrot juice is the case that really tests this, because it is the one form anyone actually consumes in the volume required. By absorbed dose the answer flips. The Dietary Reference Intake conversion factors treat 12 µg of food beta-carotene as equivalent to 1 µg retinol activity against 2 µg for supplemental beta-carotene in oil, implying the supplement form is about six times more available (Institute of Medicine, DRIs for Vitamin A and related micronutrients, 2001, DOI 10.17226/10026). Matching the absorbed exposure of a 20 mg capsule therefore takes on the order of 120 mg of food beta-carotene, roughly 1.45 kg of raw carrots daily, which nobody eats and the gut would not absorb anyway. Food matches the label number and does not match the plasma exposure that produced the trial results.
- **Food vs supplement:** This is the clearest food-versus-extract case in the set. Supplement beta-carotene is synthetic all-trans-beta-carotene or algal/fungal beta-carotene, formulated in oil or water-dispersible beadlets specifically to defeat the poor bioavailability of the food form, so it is engineered to do what a carrot cannot. Food never delivers beta-carotene alone: the same carrot supplies 3,477 µg/100 g of alpha-carotene, red palm oil is roughly a third alpha-carotene, and dark greens supply lutein with less beta-carotene. Dietary carotenoids carry none of the supplement harm for three reasons. First, uptake and cleavage are feedback-regulated, since absorbed beta-carotene raises intracellular retinoic acid, which induces ISX, which represses SR-B1 and BCO1 in the enterocyte, so absorption falls as vitamin A status rises (Reboul E, Nutrients 2019;11(4):838, PMID 31013870; the ISX loop itself traces to Lobo et al. and Widjaja-Adhi et al.). A capsule of purified beta-carotene in oil pushes plasma concentrations well past anything food produces, and the IOM 2:1 factor strictly applies to purified beta-carotene in oil, so applying it to beadlet formulations is an approximation. Second, the harm signals came from isolated high doses in high-risk groups: 20 mg/day raised lung cancer incidence by 18% in male smokers (ATBC, N Engl J Med 1994;330:1029-35, PMID 8127329) and 30 mg/day plus 25,000 IU retinyl palmitate produced about 28% more lung cancers, weighted incidence RR 1.36 (95% CI 1.07 to 1.73, P = .01) with mortality RR 1.59, in smokers and asbestos-exposed workers (CARET, Omenn et al., J Natl Cancer Inst 1996;88:1550-9, PMID 8901853). CARET cannot be attributed to beta-carotene alone, since it co-administered a large preformed retinol dose. Third, overloading on carrots produces carotenodermia, a harmless orange discolouration, rather than hypervitaminosis A, because the conversion step is regulated. Eating carrots and taking 20 mg of beta-carotene are two different interventions. Several everyday sources are absent from the table only because no figure was available to tabulate: dried parsley, basil and coriander, paprika, fresh apricots, persimmon, papaya, sweet red pepper, spring greens and collards, watercress, Swiss chard and romaine lettuce, all of which outrank the baby spinach originally chosen as the green exemplar.
- **Absorption:** Beta-carotene in raw carrot and raw spinach is locked in chromoplasts behind intact plant cell walls, so absorption from raw vegetables is low; chopping, pureeing, juicing, cooking and eating with fat all raise it, and cooking does not destroy it. The formal expression of this is the 12:1 food against 2:1 supplement-in-oil conversion factor (IOM 2001, DOI 10.17226/10026). One retention argument from the original entry has to be withdrawn: boiled carrot at 9,616 µg/100 g against raw carrot at 8,285 now compares a CoFID value with a USDA one, so it cannot be read as a within-dataset cooking effect. Conversion efficiency varies substantially between individuals through BCO1 genotype and vitamin A status, which is why plasma responses to the same carrot intake differ several-fold between people (PMID 31013870, a review stretched somewhat to cover the genotype point). Spinach needs its own correction: it is not the weak green the submission made it, since USDA raw spinach at 5,626 µg/100 g is about two thirds of raw carrot rather than under a fifth, and the honest caution about it is the oxalate load for people managing kidney stones.
- **Does the body make it:** Humans synthesise no carotenoids whatsoever, so everything in the body reflects intake and there is no deficiency state for beta-carotene as such. The recovered set also carries a PARTIAL label for vitamin A from beta-carotene, and that PARTIAL describes the conversion of dietary beta-carotene into retinol rather than any synthesis of the carotenoid, so the two labels sit on different things and do not conflict. On this axis a beta-carotene supplement has no shortfall to correct, and it stands or falls on trial results for whatever endpoint is claimed.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Humans have no carotenoid biosynthetic pathway and no homologue of any step in the algal, plant and microbial routes. Human enzymology is cleavage only, converting dietary provitamin A carotenoids into retinoids.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/day. Humans have no carotenoid biosynthetic pathway.
- **Still matters when:** As a provitamin A source where vitamin A intake is low, which is a dietary argument rather than a synthesis one. High-dose beta-carotene sits in the carotenoid class the recovered prose says to hold during active cytotoxic therapy: in SWOG S0221, antioxidant supplement use (vitamins A, C, E, carotenoids, CoQ10) before and during treatment was associated with higher recurrence (adjusted HR 1.41, 95% CI 0.98 to 2.04). Otherwise NEVER ON THIS AXIS, since with no requirement and no deficiency state, dose selection is entirely empirical.

*Refs. ATBC PMID 8127329; CARET PMID 8602180; Cochrane Bjelakovic PMID 22419320; USPSTF PMID 35727271*

### Vitamin E at or above 400 IU/d

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** No effective preventive dose exists. 400 IU/d was the harmful dose. ATBC low-dose 50 mg/d produced neither benefit nor lung cancer harm.
- **Who it is for:** Documented deficiency, which in practice means fat malabsorption: abetalipoproteinaemia, cholestatic liver disease, cystic fibrosis, short bowel.

**Pros**

- Ataxia with vitamin E deficiency requires high-dose replacement, and that is a genuine indication.
- There is a narrow niche in biopsy-proven non-diabetic non-alcoholic steatohepatitis, where 800 IU/d improved histology in the PIVENS trial. That is a hepatology decision, not a self-care one.

**Cons**

- A dose-response meta-analysis of 19 trials and 135,967 participants found high-dose trials produced 39 additional deaths per 10,000 persons (95% CI 3 to 74, P=0.035). Low-dose trials showed -16 per 10,000.
- SELECT (n=34,887 men): 400 IU/d of synthetic all-rac-alpha-tocopheryl acetate raised prostate cancer incidence by 17%, HR 1.17 (99% CI 1.004-1.36, P=0.008). The excess emerged in extended follow-up after supplementation had already stopped.
- Haemorrhagic stroke RR 1.22 (1.00-1.48) across 9 trials and 118,765 participants, which is one additional haemorrhagic stroke per 1,250 people treated.
- Contraindicated with warfarin, direct oral anticoagulants, aspirin, clopidogrel and other antiplatelets, because impaired coagulation is the endpoint EFSA used to set its limit.
- USPSTF grade D. Note the limits diverge sharply: 1,000 mg/d supplemental alpha-tocopherol in the US, but 300 mg/d from ALL sources in the EU.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

The body's main fat-soluble antioxidant, protecting cell membranes and nerves from oxidative damage.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** Alpha-tocopherol is the only form of vitamin E the body retains. After absorption, hepatic alpha-tocopherol transfer protein (TTPA) selectively loads alpha-tocopherol onto VLDL for export to tissues, while the other tocopherols and the tocotrienols are metabolised and excreted. Its established function is to break the chain reaction of lipid peroxidation in the polyunsaturated fatty acids of cell membranes and lipoproteins, donating a hydrogen atom to lipid peroxyl radicals and then being regenerated by ascorbate. The tissues that suffer first without it are peripheral nerve axons, the posterior columns and cerebellum, the retina, and the erythrocyte membrane. Converting the dose properly (1 mg alpha-tocopherol = 1.49 IU natural-source RRR, 2.22 IU synthetic all-rac), 400 IU is 268 mg of natural-source or 180 mg of synthetic alpha-tocopherol, roughly 12 to 18 times the adult RDA of 15 mg/d depending on which form the product uses, so at that level it is being used pharmacologically rather than to correct any dietary shortfall.
- **Deficiency disease:** No classic single-name disease. The recognised syndrome is vitamin E deficiency ataxia, a spinocerebellar degeneration with peripheral neuropathy and pigmentary retinopathy; in preterm infants it presents as haemolytic anaemia of prematurity. Ataxia with isolated vitamin E deficiency (AVED) produces the same clinical picture but is an inherited TTPA gene defect, not a dietary deficiency.
- **First signs:** Plasma alpha-tocopherol below roughly 12 micromol/L, or a low tocopherol-to-total-lipid ratio, precedes symptoms by months to years and is the practical marker. The first clinical signs are loss of ankle reflexes and reduced vibration and joint position sense in the feet, sometimes with unsteady walking in the dark. In preterm infants, a haemolytic anaemia with raised reticulocytes.
- **Untreated:** Untreated, it progresses to a combined spinocerebellar and sensory picture: ataxia from posterior column degeneration, dysarthria, ophthalmoplegia, head titubation, proximal weakness, and pigmentary retinopathy with declining visual acuity. The endpoint is a person who can no longer walk unaided, with fixed sensory ataxia and neuropathy.
- **Does it reverse:** High-dose repletion reliably halts progression, and when started early (within roughly a year of symptom onset, or before symptoms in a child with known malabsorption) much of the neuropathy recovers. Long-standing posterior column and cerebellar degeneration and established retinopathy usually do not fully resolve, so treatment prevents more than it restores.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Almost exclusively people who cannot absorb fat: cystic fibrosis, cholestatic liver disease and biliary atresia, short bowel syndrome, chronic pancreatitis, abetalipoproteinaemia and homozygous hypobetalipoproteinaemia. Preterm and very low birth weight infants. Separately, people with the inherited TTPA defect (AVED), which is a genetic transport disorder rather than a consequence of diet. In an adult with normal fat absorption, eating too little vitamin E essentially does not produce clinical deficiency. At the 400 IU/d dose this entry names, the established direction of effect is harm rather than repletion: in SELECT, 400 IU/d of alpha-tocopherol raised prostate cancer incidence in healthy men (HR 1.17, 99% CI 1.004-1.36; PMID 21990298).
- **How common:** NOT ESTABLISHED for frank clinical deficiency, which is rare enough in the general population that no meaningful prevalence figure exists. The widely quoted claim that over 90% of Americans fail to meet the estimated average requirement for alpha-tocopherol is an intake statistic, not a deficiency rate, and no corresponding burden of neurological disease accompanies it (doi:10.3945/an.114.006254). Biochemical deficiency is common in untreated cystic fibrosis and cholestatic liver disease.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Alpha-tocopherol is abundant in ordinary foods: seed and nut oils, sunflower seeds, almonds, hazelnuts, paprika and wheatgerm all supply meaningful amounts, and a normal diet covers the 15 mg/day RDA without effort. This is the clearest case in the group where the two questions come apart, because the nutrient is genuinely rich in food while the dose that defines this item is not obtainable from any diet. The original single RICH label sat directly above the entry's own sentence saying the dose 'cannot be reached by eating', which is why the two answers are now separated.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Replaces the submission's 133.0 mg/100 g (CoFID 17-048), which ran 12% low |

Replaces the submission's Hass-specific 1.74 mg/100 g, which ran 19% low |

- **To match the dose:** 400 IU of natural vitamin E is 268 mg of RRR-alpha-tocopherol. Reaching that every day from food means about 762 g of sunflower seeds (roughly 4,450 kcal), or 1.05 kg of almonds, or 179 g of wheatgerm oil (about 13 tablespoons of neat oil), or 550 g of sunflower oil at the CoFID value and 654 g at USDA's lower one. If the capsule is synthetic all-rac (180 mg for 400 IU), it is still 512 g of sunflower seeds or about 8.9 tablespoons of wheatgerm oil daily. Habitual intakes cluster around 5 to 20 mg/day, so 400 IU sits one to two orders of magnitude above anything eating produces. No dietary pattern gets there.
- **Food vs supplement:** Most 400 IU capsules contain all-rac-alpha-tocopheryl acetate, a synthetic mixture of eight stereoisomers of which only one eighth is the RRR form that human alpha-tocopherol transfer protein preferentially retains. Food contains only RRR, which is exactly why the label conversions differ (1 IU = 0.67 mg natural against 0.45 mg synthetic). Food also never delivers alpha-tocopherol in isolation: sunflower oil carries 48.7 mg alpha with 5.1 mg gamma per 100 g, rapeseed oil carries 18.4 mg alpha with 38.0 mg gamma (CoFID 17-045 and 17-041, both unconfirmed), red palm oil is tocotrienol-rich, and nuts add tocotrienols too. High-dose alpha-tocopherol actively removes part of what food supplies: 400 IU/day of RRR-alpha-tocopheryl acetate cut serum gamma-tocopherol by a median 58% (95% CI 51 to 66%) and reduced detectable delta-tocopherol in a two-month randomised placebo-controlled trial of 184 adult nonsmokers (Huang HY and Appel LJ, J Nutr 2003;133:3137-40, PMID 14519797, all elements confirmed). A 400 IU capsule is therefore something other than a concentrated handful of sunflower seeds; it shifts the balance of vitamin E forms in blood in a direction food never does.
- **Absorption:** Alpha-tocopherol needs dietary fat and bile for absorption, which nuts and oils supply intrinsically, so food forms are absorbed efficiently in ordinary meals. Fractional absorption falls as the single dose rises, and hepatic alpha-TTP selectively retains only the 2R stereoisomers, so the 2S half of a synthetic all-rac dose is largely excreted. The useful fraction of a 400 IU synthetic capsule is therefore well below its stated IU, plasma alpha-tocopherol does not scale linearly with dose, and the displacement of gamma- and delta-tocopherol documented by Huang et al. (PMID 14519797) does.
- **Does the body make it:** Alpha-tocopherol is entirely dietary: humans carry no tocopherol or tocotrienol biosynthetic pathway, and human enzymology handles absorption with dietary fat, hepatic selection of the alpha-tocopherol form and lipoprotein transport only. NONE here does imply a requirement, and a deficiency state exists, described in the recovered prose in fat malabsorption and abetalipoproteinaemia. The axis therefore supports correcting a documented shortfall and gives no argument for 400 IU/d and above, which is a pharmacological antioxidant dose to be judged on its own trial and risk record.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Humans have no tocopherol biosynthetic pathway; tocopherols and tocotrienols are made by plants and other photosynthetic organisms. Human handling covers intestinal absorption with dietary fat, hepatic selection of the alpha-tocopherol form and lipoprotein transport, with no synthetic step at any point.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/day synthesised, since no human biosynthetic pathway exists. The 400 IU/d in the name is a supplement dose; the recovered prose quotes no dietary reference value for vitamin E, so none is asserted here.
- **Still matters when:** Documented vitamin E deficiency, which the recovered prose ties to fat malabsorption and abetalipoproteinaemia. During active cytotoxic chemotherapy the default is to hold high-dose antioxidant supplements: in SWOG S0221, self-reported use of any antioxidant supplement (vitamins A, C, E, carotenoids, CoQ10) both before and during treatment was associated with a higher recurrence rate (adjusted HR 1.41, 95% CI 0.98 to 2.04). For 400 IU/d and above in a well-nourished person, NEVER ON THIS AXIS.

*Refs. SELECT Klein PMID 21990298; Miller PMID 15537682; Schurks BMJ PMID 21051774; USPSTF PMID 35727271; EFSA J 2024 PMID 39099617*

### Selenium at 200 µg/d

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** RDA is 55 µg/d and food intake meets it in most Western diets. 200 µg/d is the dose used in the failed and harmful trials.
- **Who it is for:** People with genuinely low selenium status: low-soil regions, long-term parenteral nutrition, severe malabsorption.

**Pros**

- It prevents Keshan disease, a selenium-deficiency cardiomyopathy, in genuinely deficient populations. That is a real and important indication.
- There is a Grade B signal for mild Graves orbitopathy in selenium-suboptimal European populations at 200 µg/d for 6 months, resting on a single 159-patient trial.

**Cons**

- Cochrane, 10 randomised trials and 27,232 participants: any cancer RR 1.01 (0.93-1.10) from the low-risk-of-bias studies. That is HIGH-certainty evidence of NO benefit. The cancer-prevention reputation rests on a secondary endpoint of a 1996 skin cancer trial that was not designed to test it.
- SELECT (n=35,533 men, 200 µg/d L-selenomethionine) found prostate cancer HR 1.09 (99% CI 0.93-1.27). No preventive effect, and the trial stopped early for futility.
- Incident type 2 diabetes hazard ratio 1.55 (1.03-2.33) over a mean 7.7 years at 200 µg/d, with excess risk concentrated in those already in the highest tertile of baseline plasma selenium. This harm appears at HALF the US upper limit.
- The therapeutic window is narrow. EFSA lowered its upper limit to 255 µg/d in 2023 with alopecia as the critical endpoint, and derived its lowest-observed-adverse-effect level of 330 µg/d from the SELECT trial itself.
- Stacking is how people get there: a 200 µg tablet on top of a multivitamin containing 55-70 µg plus dietary intake.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Selenium builds the enzymes that mop up oxidative damage and switch on thyroid hormone.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** Selenium is incorporated as the amino acid selenocysteine into about 25 human selenoproteins. The glutathione peroxidases (GPX1 to GPX4) reduce hydrogen peroxide and lipid hydroperoxides, GPX4 specifically preventing ferroptotic membrane damage. Thioredoxin reductases maintain cellular redox state and support DNA synthesis. The iodothyronine deiodinases convert T4 to active T3, which links selenium status to thyroid function. Selenoprotein P is the plasma transport form and the best status marker. The dose in this entry matters. Plasma glutathione peroxidase (GPX3) plateaus near 55 micrograms per day, which is what the RDA is built on. Selenoprotein P plateaus somewhere in the range of roughly 50 to 105 micrograms per day depending on baseline status: about 49 micrograms per day in selenium-deficient Chinese subjects, against about 105 micrograms per day of total intake in UK adults (a 50 microgram supplement on top of roughly 55 micrograms habitual). Either way, 200 micrograms per day supplies three to four times the adult RDA and produces no further enzyme activity in someone already replete, while sitting halfway to the tolerable upper limit of 400 micrograms per day. In the Nutritional Prevention of Cancer trial, 200 micrograms per day for a mean 7.7 years raised type 2 diabetes incidence (hazard ratio 1.55, 95% CI 1.03 to 2.33), concentrated in participants who started in the highest baseline selenium tertile (hazard ratio 2.70).
- **Deficiency disease:** Keshan disease, an endemic cardiomyopathy. Kashin-Beck disease, an endemic osteoarthropathy, occurs in the same low-selenium regions and is associated with, though not fully explained by, selenium deficiency.
- **First signs:** Plasma selenium and glutathione peroxidase activity fall with no symptoms at all. In endemic areas, subclinical ECG abnormalities and reduced ejection fraction appear before overt illness, which is how latent Keshan disease is detected on screening. Isolated cases outside endemic areas typically surface as unexplained myopathy, muscle pain, or whitened nail beds.
- **Untreated:** Keshan disease presents acutely with cardiogenic shock and arrhythmia, or chronically as congestive cardiomyopathy with cardiac enlargement and multifocal myocardial necrosis and fibrosis. It classically strikes children and women of childbearing age in low-soil-selenium areas and is fatal without treatment. A coxsackievirus co-factor is thought to trigger the acute form in a selenium-depleted host. Kashin-Beck disease destroys growth-plate and articular cartilage, producing shortened fingers, deformed ankles, knees, wrists and elbows, and permanently limited movement.
- **Does it reverse:** Early Keshan disease responds well to selenium replacement, and heart failure can improve substantially or resolve if caught before the myocardium is remodelled. Once dilated cardiomyopathy with established fibrosis has developed, the myocardial damage does not fully reverse and outcomes are poor. Kashin-Beck cartilage necrosis and the resulting joint deformity are permanent. Prophylactic sodium selenite in endemic Chinese counties prevented new cases, which is the clearest evidence the causal direction is right.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Residents of low-selenium soil belts, chiefly the diagonal band across China from the northeast to the southwest, plus parts of Siberia. Long-term parenteral nutrition without added selenium. Severe malabsorption, short bowel, and cystic fibrosis. Dialysis patients. Dietary selenium deficiency is not a realistic concern for anyone eating a mixed diet in North America, where soil selenium is high; two Brazil nuts alone exceed the adult RDA.
- **How common:** NOT ESTABLISHED as a current global figure. Keshan disease was endemic across a defined low-selenium region of China and has been reduced to near-zero incidence since selenite supplementation and food-supply changes began in the 1970s. Dietary selenium deficiency is essentially absent in the United States, where median intake already exceeds the RDA from food alone.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Selenium is genuinely abundant in ordinary food, and offal and marine fish carry it most reliably: braised pork kidney is 312 ug per 100 g, braised lamb kidney 219 ug, cooked yellowfin tuna 108 ug and braised lamb's liver 111 ug. Brazil nuts are the richest known food source at a USDA average of 1,920 ug/100 g, about 96 ug in a single 5 g kernel, which makes them the one food where a supplement dose is met by two nuts, and that is a hazard rather than a reassurance because their selenium is set by the soil the tree grew in and varies more than thirty-fold between Amazon regions. Reference points used in the table: EFSA AI 70 ug/d for adults, and the EFSA tolerable upper intake level of 255 ug/d for adult men and women including pregnancy and lactation, set from alopecia as the critical endpoint with a LOAEL of 330 ug/d taken from the SELECT trial and an uncertainty factor of 1.3 (EFSA Journal 2023;21(1):e07704, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2023.7704; AI from EFSA, Dietary Reference Values for nutrients Summary report, doi 10.2903/sp.efsa.2017.e15121, Tables 5 and 7). The 200 ug/d dose comes from two landmark trials: selenised yeast in the Nutritional Prevention of Cancer trial and 200 ug/d L-selenomethionine in SELECT.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Answers the reliable-dose question better than the fresh yellowfin steak: a store-cupboard product with a stable value. Drained tin weight is a conventional 100 g rather than a USDA measure. |

Replaces the raw record (171978, 19.7 ug/100 g), which halved the figure while the zinc item used the cooked and canned records; canned is 35.8 ug/100 g (171981). |

Like all plant selenium this tracks the soil the wheat grew in; the value is North American. Portion conventional, per-portion derived. |

- **To match the dose:** There is almost no gap, and that is the finding. 200 ug/d is met by 2.1 average Brazil nuts (10 g), or 64 g of braised pork kidney, or 91 g of braised lamb kidney, or 185 g of cooked yellowfin tuna, or 180 g of braised lamb's liver, or 223 g of cooked blue mussels, or 243 g of simmered chicken liver, or 263 g of canned light tuna in oil, or 532 g of cooked cod, or 4 tins of sardines, or 13 eggs. Kidney and Brazil nuts overshoot in one normal portion: an 85 g portion of braised pork kidney is 265 ug, already 133 percent of the supplement dose and above the EFSA upper level on its own, and an 85 g portion of braised lamb kidney is 186 ug, 93 percent of the dose. Because the dose is so easy to reach, the useful calculation runs the other way. The EFSA AI is 70 ug/d and the EFSA upper level is 255 ug/d, a working margin of about 185 ug. A person on a 200 ug/d supplement plus an ordinary diet is at roughly 270 ug and already over the upper level; add two average Brazil nuts and they are at about 462 ug. The UL derives from alopecia observed in SELECT at a LOAEL of 330 ug/d, so this is an observed human effect at achievable intakes rather than a theoretical ceiling (DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2023.7704). Selenium is the case where food easily matches the supplement, and the honest advice is to pick one route.
- **Food vs supplement:** Brazil nuts are the textbook answer and a genuinely bad way to dose selenium deliberately, because the dose is unknowable at the point of eating. Median selenium varied from 2.07 mg/kg in Mato Grosso state to 68.15 mg/kg in Amazonas, which the authors attribute to soil selenium, soil pH and texture, location and genotype (PMID 28923728). On the 5 g kernel used throughout this item's table, one nut therefore supplies about 15 percent to about 490 percent of the 70 ug adult AI. The paper's own '11 percent to 288 percent' phrasing implies a 3.0 to 3.7 g kernel and cannot stand alongside a 5 g table, so the figures have been put on one basis. Nuts from the two extremes are visually identical, are not labelled by state of origin, and are routinely blended, so 'two Brazil nuts a day' is a dose ranging from about 20 ug to about 680 ug depending on a fact the eater cannot access, spanning deficient to well over the upper level. On form equivalence, selenium is unusual in a helpful way: the dominant species in Brazil nuts and cereals is selenomethionine, the same species used as the supplement in SELECT, so food and that supplement are pharmacologically comparable (PMID 18346307). But 200 ug of selenium is not one thing. The selenised yeast used in the earlier NPC trial was a poorly speciated mixture whose delivered selenium was variable, and the two trials produced opposite results, so the number 200 ug carries no single meaning across the literature (PMID 18346308, PMID 18346307). Finally, the case for supplementing collapses in a selenium-replete person: SELECT tested 200 ug/d L-selenomethionine in selenium-replete men, found no protection against prostate or other major cancers, and produced the alopecia signal that now defines the UL.
- **Absorption:** Selenium bioavailability depends on chemical species more than on food matrix. Selenomethionine, the form in Brazil nuts, cereals and meat, is absorbed almost completely and is incorporated non-specifically into body proteins as a storage pool, which is why it raises plasma selenium readily and why its kinetics differ from inorganic selenite and selenate (PMID 18346307, PMID 28412747). That has a practical consequence the composition tables hide: the selenium content of any plant food is a proxy for the soil it grew in rather than a property of the species. The USDA wholemeal bread figure of 25.7 ug/100 g and the wheat germ figure of 79.2 ug/100 g both reflect North American wheat grown on selenium-rich soils. European and UK wheat grows on selenium-poor soils, so the same bread and the same wheat germ cannot be assumed to deliver the same selenium, and the wide between-country variation in intake driven by exactly this mechanism is the central theme of the food-chain intake review (PMID 18346308). Animal foods hold their values better across countries, because livestock feed is commonly selenium-supplemented, which is why kidney, liver, tuna, mussels and eggs travel better than bread or nuts do. Ranking for a reader who wants reliable selenium without a variable dose: kidney and liver first, then marine fish and shellfish including canned tuna and mussels, eggs as a steady background contributor, and Brazil nuts last precisely because they are the richest source.
- **Does the body make it:** Selenium is chemical element 34, so none of it can be made, although its functional form is partly homemade: the body builds selenocysteine on its own tRNA and inserts it at recoded UGA codons, using selenium supplied entirely by diet. At 200 µg/d the limiting issue is saturation, since selenoprotein P was optimised at an estimated intake of 49 µg/d in 98 healthy Chinese adults whose baseline intake was only 14 µg/d, and 75 µg Se/d as l-selenomethionine is postulated rather than measured to allow full selenoprotein expression in US residents, so the extra 125 to 150 µg buys no further selenoprotein. Supplementing at this dose therefore makes sense only for documented deficiency, and above the plateau the dose is pharmacological, with type 2 diabetes incidence raised in the Nutritional Prevention of Cancer trial (overall hazard ratio 1.55) and high-grade prostate cancer in SELECT.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for the element. For its functional form: dietary selenomethionine or selenite is reduced to selenide, SEPHS2 (with SEPHS1) makes selenophosphate, seryl-tRNA synthetase charges tRNA[Ser]Sec with serine, PSTK (phosphoseryl-tRNA kinase) phosphorylates it, SEPSECS converts O-phosphoseryl-tRNA[Ser]Sec to Sec-tRNA[Ser]Sec, and the SECIS-dependent recoding machinery (SECISBP2 with the dedicated elongation factor EEFSEC) inserts selenocysteine at UGA codons. Products include GPX1-4 and GPX6, TXNRD1-3, the deiodinases DIO1-3 and SELENOP as the plasma transport form.
- **Amount made:** 0 µg/d of the element. Mass of selenocysteine synthesised per day: NOT ESTABLISHED. Reference points: RDA 55 µg/d; selenoprotein P optimised at an estimated intake of about 49 µg/d in selenium-deficient Chinese adults (baseline 14 µg/d), about 75 µg/d postulated for full selenoprotein expression in US residents; UL 400 µg/d, based on selenosis as the critical adverse effect.
- **Still matters when:** Justified only by low intake or loss: selenium-poor soil regions (historic Keshan and Kashin-Beck disease areas, parts of China, historically Finland), long-term parenteral nutrition without selenium, severe malabsorption, some critical-illness protocols under clinician direction. In all of those, doses near 50 to 75 µg/d cover the requirement, so 200 µg/d sits above the useful range for repletion and carries a documented metabolic and oncological cost in the already-replete: diabetes incidence in NPC and high-grade prostate cancer in SELECT.

*Refs. Cochrane Vinceti PMID 29376219; SELECT PMID 19066370 and PMID 21990298; Stranges PMID 17620655; EFSA 2023*

### Preformed vitamin A at or above 10,000 IU

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** RDA 900 µg RAE for men and 700 µg for women. The upper limit is 3,000 µg/d, which is exactly 10,000 IU.
- **Who it is for:** Clinical vitamin A deficiency in low-income settings, fat malabsorption, cystic fibrosis. In replete Western adults, nobody.

**Pros**

- WHO supplementation saves lives in vitamin-A-deficient children, particularly in measles. That is one of the highest-value public health interventions in existence, and it is not what an over-the-counter 10,000 IU capsule is for.

**Cons**

- CARET showed net harm: 25,000 IU/d of retinyl palmitate plus 30 mg beta-carotene gave all-cause death RR 1.17 (1.03-1.33) and lung cancer death RR 1.46 (1.07-2.00).
- Over-the-counter products at 10,000 IU/d sit exactly AT the upper limit, leaving zero headroom for diet, liver consumption or a second supplement.
- It is teratogenic. Preformed vitamin A above the upper limit in pregnancy is a hard contraindication, and prenatal products should use beta-carotene rather than retinol.
- Unlike beta-carotene, preformed vitamin A is not subject to feedback regulation on absorption, so chronic excess accumulates and causes hepatotoxicity, intracranial hypertension and bone loss.
- Contraindicated in liver disease of any kind, since retinol is stored in and toxic to the liver.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Makes the eye's dim-light pigment and keeps skin, gut and airway linings healthy.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** Retinol is oxidised to 11-cis-retinal, which binds opsin to form rhodopsin, the photopigment of the rod cells that makes vision in dim light possible. By a separate route it is oxidised to all-trans and 9-cis retinoic acid, ligands for the nuclear RAR and RXR receptors, which regulate transcription of genes controlling epithelial differentiation (goblet cells and mucin production in the conjunctiva, cornea, respiratory tract and gut), embryonic patterning, haematopoiesis, and both innate and adaptive immunity including gut-homing of T lymphocytes. Preformed retinol from liver, dairy, eggs and supplements is absorbed efficiently and stored in hepatic stellate cells, which buffer plasma concentrations for months. 10,000 IU is about 3,000 micrograms retinol activity equivalents, roughly four times the adult RDA and at the tolerable upper intake level, so at this dose the clinically relevant question is excess rather than shortfall.
- **Deficiency disease:** Xerophthalmia, the spectrum running from night blindness (nyctalopia) through conjunctival and corneal xerosis to keratomalacia.
- **First signs:** Impaired dark adaptation, then frank night blindness: trouble seeing after dusk or in a dimly lit room, which in a small child shows as reluctance to move about after dark. Conjunctival xerosis and Bitot spots, foamy grey triangular patches of keratinised conjunctiva, follow. Increased severity and duration of measles, diarrhoea and respiratory infection often precede any eye sign. Serum retinol below 0.70 micromol/L is the biochemical marker, though it falls only once liver stores are largely gone.
- **Untreated:** Corneal xerosis progresses to punctate keratopathy, then corneal ulceration, then keratomalacia, in which the corneal stroma liquefies over a few days and frequently perforates. The eye is structurally destroyed and blindness is permanent. Deficiency also substantially raises child mortality from measles, diarrhoeal disease and respiratory infection, and roughly half of the children who go blind from it die within twelve months.
- **Does it reverse:** Night blindness, conjunctival xerosis and Bitot spots reverse within days to weeks of high-dose retinol. Once the cornea ulcerates or melts, the loss is structural and permanent, and vitamin A deficiency remains a leading cause of preventable childhood blindness worldwide. The window between the fully reversible and the irreversible stage can be only a few days in a sick, depleted child, which is why treatment is given on clinical suspicion without waiting for a retinol assay.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Preschool children and pregnant and lactating women in low-income settings whose diets contain little animal-source food or provitamin A vegetables, particularly where measles and diarrhoeal disease are common. In high-income countries deficiency is rare and almost always secondary to disease: cystic fibrosis, cholestatic liver disease, short bowel syndrome, pancreatic insufficiency and bariatric surgery. It also occurs with extremely restricted eating, including the food selectivity seen in some autistic children, where xerophthalmia has been reported.
- **How common:** WHO estimated that for 1995-2005, 190 million preschool-age children (33.3% of that age group in at-risk populations) and 19.1 million pregnant women had serum retinol below 0.70 micromol/L, with about 5.2 million preschool children affected by night blindness (WHO, Global prevalence of vitamin A deficiency in populations at risk 1995-2005, Geneva 2009). Deficiency in high-income populations is rare and disease-related rather than dietary; no useful population prevalence figure exists for those settings.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** Low
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Liver is far more concentrated in preformed retinol than the supplement: 10,000 IU is 3,000 µg retinol, and every common liver in the UK tables carries 10,500 to 25,200 µg per 100 g. Hard cheese, butter, fortified fat spreads and egg yolk supply an everyday retinol baseline of tens to about a hundred micrograms per portion on top of that. This is the one item in the group where the food beats the pill, which is why liver carries a public-health warning rather than a recommendation.

| Liver, pig, stewed *[unconfirmed]* | 22,600 µg retinol (75,300 IU) | 1 serving (100 g) | 22,600 µg (75,300 IU) | 753% of a 10,000 IU dose; 3,229% of the 700 µg RNI | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 18-418 (18 samples) |
| Liver, lamb, fried in corn oil *[unconfirmed]* | 19,700 µg retinol (65,700 IU) | 1 serving (100 g) | 19,700 µg (65,700 IU) | 657% of a 10,000 IU dose; 2,814% of the 700 µg RNI | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 18-494 (10 samples) |
| Liver, ox, stewed *[unconfirmed]* | 17,300 µg retinol (57,700 IU) | 1 serving (100 g) | 17,300 µg (57,700 IU) | 577% of a 10,000 IU dose; 2,471% of the 700 µg RNI | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 18-416 (18 samples) |
| Liver, chicken, fried in corn oil *[unconfirmed]* | 10,500 µg retinol (35,000 IU) | 1 serving (100 g) | 10,500 µg (35,000 IU) | 350% of a 10,000 IU dose; 1,500% of the 700 µg RNI | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 18-412 (10 samples) |
| Pate, liver *[unconfirmed]* | 7,300 µg retinol (24,300 IU) | 1 serving on toast (40 g) | 2,920 µg (9,730 IU) | 97% of a 10,000 IU dose from one portion; 417% of the 700 µg RNI | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 19-317 (20 samples including canned) |
| Cod liver oil *[unconfirmed]* | 18,000 µg retinol (60,000 IU) | 1 teaspoon (4.6 g) | 828 µg (2,760 IU) | 28% of a 10,000 IU dose per teaspoon; a tablespoon (13.6 g) gives 2,448 µg (8,160 IU), 82% | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 17-488 (mean of 20 samples) |
| Cheddar, hard cheese *[unconfirmed]* | about 325 µg retinol (1,083 IU) | 1 matchbox piece (30 g) | 98 µg (325 IU) | 3.3% of a 10,000 IU dose; 14% of the 700 µg RNI | Figure as given in the verification report's missing-foods list |
| Margarine and fat spread, fortified *[unconfirmed]* | about 800 to 900 µg retinol (2,670 to 3,000 IU) | 1 pat (10 g) | 80 to 90 µg (267 to 300 IU) | 2.7% to 3.0% of a 10,000 IU dose; 11% to 13% of the 700 µg RNI | Range as given in the verification report's missing-foods list |
| Butter, salted | 671 µg retinol (2,237 IU); USDA also gives 684 µg RAE/100 g | 1 pat (10 g) | 67 µg (224 IU) | 2.2% of a 10,000 IU dose; 10% of the 700 µg RNI | USDA salted butter, 33.55 µg retinol per 5 g = 671 µg/100 g, via FDC mirror. Replaces the submission's 958 µg/100 g, which ran 43% high, and the per-pat figures of 96 µg / 319 IU / 14% |
| Egg yolk, chicken, raw | 371 µg retinol (1,237 IU) | 1 large yolk (17 g) | 63 µg (210 IU) | 2.1% of a 10,000 IU dose; 9% of the 700 µg RNI | USDA raw egg yolk, 63.07 µg retinol per 17 g = 371 µg/100 g, via FDC mirror. Replaces the submission's 447 µg/100 g, which ran 17% high, and the per-yolk figures of 76 µg / 253 IU / 11% |

- **To match the dose:** There is no gap to close. About 12 g of fried calf liver supplies the full 3,000 µg (10,000 IU) of a high-dose retinol capsule, and a normal 100 g portion of any liver delivers 3.5 to 8.4 times that dose in one sitting. A 40 g serving of liver pate is functionally a 10,000 IU tablet at 2,920 µg (9,730 IU). A tablespoon of cod liver oil is 2,448 µg (8,160 IU). Anyone eating liver weekly is already taking supraphysiological preformed vitamin A intermittently, and a 10,000 IU supplement on top compounds a load the body stores rather than clears. For scale, the US tolerable upper intake level for preformed vitamin A is 3,000 µg/day, which is the supplement dose itself, against a UK RNI of 700 µg for men and 600 µg for women.
- **Food vs supplement:** The retinol in liver is the same molecule as the retinyl palmitate or acetate in capsules, absorbed by the same route and stored in the liver the same way, so the usual reassurance that food forms are safer does not apply here. Pregnancy is the sharp edge: UK advice is that people who are pregnant or who might become pregnant should avoid liver and liver products including pate, and should avoid supplements containing vitamin A including fish liver oils, because excess retinol is teratogenic (NHS pregnancy vitamins guidance). Cod liver oil is the common trap, bought for omega-3 or vitamin D and delivering 60,000 IU of retinol per 100 g, and the same trap has sharper versions that are eaten as ordinary food rather than dosed: canned cod liver (Icelandic and Polish tinned), which carries retinol in the cod-liver-oil range, and halibut liver oil, which is more concentrated again and sold in the same aisle. Liver is also eaten unnoticed as liver sausage, liverwurst, haggis, foie gras, and goose and duck liver, all in the same range as the livers tabulated. Outside pregnancy, chronic preformed retinol above the 3,000 µg/day UL is associated with reduced bone mineral density and fracture risk, which is why UK advice is to eat liver no more than once a week. Beta-carotene-rich foods are the safe substitute precisely because their conversion is regulated.
- **Absorption:** Retinyl esters from liver and from supplements are absorbed at high efficiency and, critically, absorption is not down-regulated when stores are full, so intake accumulates in hepatic stellate cells over weeks and months. Carotenoid absorption works the opposite way, feedback-limited through retinoic-acid induction of the intestinal transcription factor ISX and consequent repression of SR-B1 and BCO1 (reviewed in Reboul E, Nutrients 2019;11(4):838, PMID 31013870, DOI 10.3390/nu11040838; the feedback loop itself originates with Lobo et al. and Widjaja-Adhi et al.). Cooking concentrates liver retinol rather than destroying it, through water loss: CoFID shows raw lamb liver at 17,300 µg/100 g rising to 19,700 fried, and raw ox liver at 14,200 rising to 17,300 stewed (codes 18-413, 18-494, 18-415, 18-416, all unconfirmed along with the rest of the CoFID series).
- **Does the body make it:** Retinol cannot be built from scratch at all, because human cells only cleave dietary provitamin A carotenoids to make it and there is no de novo retinoid pathway. NONE means required here, so ordinary vitamin A intake has a real target and deficiency is a genuine clinical state. The axis says nothing for 10,000 IU and above of preformed retinol, where the retinol load itself is the hazard, and high retinol doses are specifically to be avoided in pregnancy because of teratogenicity.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for synthesis. Human cells only cleave dietary provitamin A carotenoids to retinoids; the remaining human enzymology handles absorption, storage and interconversion of dietary retinoids, with no synthetic step.
- **Amount made:** 0 IU/day synthesised, since humans have no retinoid biosynthetic pathway. The 10,000 IU in the name is a supplement dose; the recovered prose quotes no dietary reference value for preformed vitamin A, so none is asserted here.
- **Still matters when:** Documented vitamin A deficiency and low-intake settings where a preformed or provitamin A source is needed. Not justified as a routine high-dose retinol source, and specifically to be avoided at high retinol doses in pregnancy because of teratogenicity, which is the recovered reason cod liver oil is a poor carrier and separate D3 plus fish or algal oil is the cleaner choice. High-dose vitamin A also sits in the antioxidant class the recovered prose says to hold during active cytotoxic therapy (SWOG S0221, adjusted HR 1.41, 95% CI 0.98 to 2.04).

*Refs. CARET PMID 8602180; Cochrane PMID 22419320; IOM DRI NBK56068*

### High-dose vitamin B6

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** RDA is 1.3 mg/d. EFSA set the upper limit at 12 mg/d in 2023. The US IOM figure is still 100 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** People on isoniazid, cycloserine, hydralazine or penicillamine, where B6 is protective against drug-induced neuropathy. Pregnant women with nausea and vomiting, at obstetric direction.

**Pros**

- It genuinely prevents isoniazid-induced peripheral neuropathy at 10-50 mg/d, which is a real and important indication.
- Pyridoxine with doxylamine is a first-line option for nausea and vomiting of pregnancy, ahead of antiemetics.

**Cons**

- This is the clearest case in this document where the product category actively causes the harm it claims to prevent. Nerve support, neuropathy and PMS formulas routinely deliver 50-100 mg of B6 to people with no deficiency, which is four to eight times the current European upper limit.
- Sensory peripheral neuropathy is the dose- and duration-dependent toxicity: numbness, burning, ataxia and loss of proprioception. EFSA set its 12 mg/d limit on exactly this, from a reference point of 50 mg/d in case reports.
- Choosing pyridoxal-5-phosphate (P5P) at a premium does NOT prevent it. The toxicity is not form-specific.
- Audit your total intake. Anyone on a B-complex, a fortified energy drink and a nerve or stress formula can easily stack past 100 mg without noticing.
- An observational safety signal exists: in men, a 10-year average intake above 20 mg/d from individual supplements gave lung cancer HR 1.82 (1.25-2.65). Confounding by smoking is possible, but the direction is not reassuring.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Vitamin B6 powers amino acid enzymes; at high doses it damages sensory nerves.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Fully reversible
- **In detail:** The active form, pyridoxal 5'-phosphate, is the cofactor for well over 100 enzymes, most of them in amino acid metabolism: transaminases, the decarboxylases that make serotonin, dopamine, GABA and histamine, the first committed step of haem synthesis (ALA synthase), glycogen phosphorylase, and cystathionine beta-synthase and gamma-lyase, which dispose of homocysteine by transsulphuration. Requirement rises with protein intake. Above the point where these enzymes are saturated, extra B6 adds no function. High-dose products (typically 50 to 500 mg/day, sold for premenstrual symptoms, carpal tunnel syndrome, nausea in pregnancy, or hidden inside B-complex and energy formulations) instead produce a dose-dependent and duration-dependent sensory neuropathy, with the dorsal root ganglion as the target. On toxicity, which is a separate question from the deficiency rated below: because the injury is to the sensory nerve cell bodies in the dorsal root ganglion rather than to the axon alone, recovery after stopping the supplement is often incomplete. Numbness and impaired position sense usually improve over several months, sometimes substantially, but residual deficit persists in some people (Schaumburg 1983). Time to onset is inversely related to dose, so grams per day can do it in weeks while tens of milligrams may take years, which is why EFSA applied an uncertainty factor of 4 to a 50 mg/day reference point when setting the 12 mg/day adult upper level in 2023.
- **Deficiency disease:** Pyridoxine deficiency: seborrhoeic dermatitis with cheilosis and glossitis, hypochromic microcytic anaemia, and seizures in infants; nearly always secondary to isoniazid, cycloserine, hydralazine or penicillamine, alcohol dependence, dialysis or malabsorption.
- **First signs:** Plasma pyridoxal 5'-phosphate below about 20 nmol/L, usually with no symptoms at all. When symptoms appear they are mucocutaneous and non-specific: angular cheilitis, glossitis, seborrhoeic dermatitis around the eyes, nose and mouth, plus irritability and low mood. Homocysteine may be mildly raised.
- **Untreated:** Hypochromic microcytic anaemia from impaired haem synthesis (distinct from X-linked ALAS2 pyridoxine-responsive sideroblastic anaemia, which is genetic, not dietary), and a peripheral sensory neuropathy. In infants, deficiency causes seizures through failure of GABA synthesis. Isolated severe deficiency is rare and is nearly always secondary: isoniazid, cycloserine, hydralazine or penicillamine treatment, alcohol dependence, dialysis, or malabsorption. Note that both deficiency and excess of B6 produce peripheral neuropathy, so neuropathy in a supplement user is at least as likely to be caused by the supplement as corrected by it.
- **Does it reverse:** This field rates recovery from the deficiency, and B6 deficiency corrects fully within weeks on modest repletion, with no residual deficit: the mucocutaneous signs clear, the anaemia resolves, and pyridoxine-responsive infantile seizures stop once the vitamin is given. Recovery from high-dose B6 neuropathy is a different question with a different answer (often incomplete) and is set out in the function description above, not here.
- **Who becomes deficient:** For deficiency: people taking isoniazid, cycloserine, hydralazine or penicillamine; alcohol dependence; dialysis; malabsorption. Lower plasma PLP is also seen in older adults, smokers, and current or former oral contraceptive users. For toxicity: anyone taking a high-dose B6 or B-complex product for months to years, often without realising the dose, since a single tablet can exceed the European upper level several-fold.
- **How common:** Around 23 to 27% of US adults not taking supplements had plasma PLP below 20 nmol/L in NHANES 2003 to 2004, mostly biochemical rather than symptomatic (PMID 18469270). On the toxicity side, EFSA set the adult tolerable upper intake at 12 mg/day in 2023, derived from a 50 mg/day reference point for peripheral neuropathy with an uncertainty factor of 4 (doi:10.2903/j.efsa.2023.8006).

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Vitamin B6 itself is abundant and widely distributed, with fish, organ meat, poultry, potatoes, bananas, seeds, nuts and fortified cereals all carrying useful amounts, and dietary deficiency uncommon in people eating normally. The marketed high-dose object is a different thing: pyridoxine hydrochloride, a manufactured crystalline salt sold at 50, 100 and sometimes 250-500 mg per tablet, which is 40 to 400 times the 1.3 mg adult RDA and has no dietary counterpart at that concentration. Both statements are true at once, which is why the label is split here: the molecule is RICH in food (so RICH stands rather than being reclassified as synthetic), while the product as sold is unreachable by eating, which is what dose_from_food now records. All percentages below use the 1.3 mg RDA for ages 19-50; over 50 the RDA rises to 1.7 mg for men and 1.5 mg for women, so these percentages overstate the contribution for older adults.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Salmon, Atlantic, farmed, cooked, dry heat | 0.647 mg | half fillet (178 g) | 1.152 mg | 89% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 1.2% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175168, SR Legacy |
| Yellowfin tuna, fresh, raw | 0.933 mg | 3 oz (85 g) | 0.793 mg | 61% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.79% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175159, SR Legacy. Raw basis, stated as raw: cooking costs B6, so where a cooked composition entry exists it should be preferred over applying a retention factor to this figure. |
| Beef liver, cooked, braised | 1.02 mg | 1 slice (68 g) | 0.694 mg | 53% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.69% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168626, SR Legacy |
| Potatoes, baked, flesh and skin, without salt | 0.311 mg | 1 medium potato (173 g) | 0.538 mg | 41% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.54% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170093, SR Legacy |
| Chicken breast, meat only, cooked, roasted | 0.600 mg | half breast, bone and skin removed (86 g) | 0.516 mg | 40% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.52% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171477, SR Legacy |
| Instant oatmeal, flavoured, dry, B6-fortified (QUAKER maple and brown sugar) | 1.20 mg (fortification, not intrinsic) | 1 packet (43 g) | 0.516 mg | 40% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.52% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173907 |
| Bananas, raw | 0.367 mg | 1 medium, 7 to 7-7/8 inch (118 g) | 0.433 mg | 33% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.43% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173944, SR Legacy |
| Sunflower seed kernels, dried | 1.34 mg | 1 oz (28.35 g) | 0.380 mg | 29% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.38% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170562 for the 1.34 mg/100 g figure as verified in the verification report; the 1 oz (28.35 g) portion basis is stated here rather than taken from a USDA household measure. Richer per 100 g than pistachios, and behind them per portion only because the portion is the same size. |
| Pistachio nuts, dry roasted, without salt | 1.12 mg (analytical range 0.484-1.80 across 13 data points) | 1 oz, 49 kernels (28.35 g) | 0.317 mg | 24% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.32% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170185, SR Legacy (13 data points, min 0.484, max 1.80) |
| Garlic, raw | 1.24 mg | 1 clove (3 g) | 0.0372 mg | 2.9% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.04% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169230 for the 1.24 mg/100 g figure as verified in the verification report; the 1 clove (3 g) portion basis is stated here rather than taken from a USDA household measure. Listed to show that per-100 g richness can vanish at the realistic portion: garlic outranks pistachios per 100 g and comes last per clove. |

- **To match the dose:** A 100 mg pyridoxine tablet is 77 times the RDA. Matching it by eating, in one day, takes 7.46 kg of dried sunflower kernels, 8.93 kg of pistachios, 9.80 kg of braised beef liver, 10.7 kg of raw yellowfin tuna, 16.7 kg of roasted chicken breast, or 27.2 kg of banana, about 231 medium bananas. A 50 mg tablet still needs 4.46 kg of pistachios or 116 bananas. Even the regulatory ceiling sits outside dietary reach: EFSA's 2023 tolerable upper intake level of 12 mg/day for adults corresponds to about 1.07 kg of pistachios or 3.27 kg of banana, roughly 28 bananas, every single day. Nobody eats any of that. Ordinary diets deliver a few mg per day, an order of magnitude below the UL and two to three orders below the supplement, and that is the mechanical reason dietary B6 has never produced the sensory neuropathy that supplements produce: no food is concentrated enough to approach the toxic range. The syndrome was described in seven adults taking megadose pyridoxine (Schaumburg H et al., 'Sensory neuropathy from pyridoxine abuse. A new megavitamin syndrome', N Engl J Med 1983;309:445-8, PMID 6308447), all of whom improved after withdrawal; the widely quoted 2-6 g/day range is secondary-literature summary that does not appear in the abstract, so treat it as unconfirmed and source any dose figure to the full text or to EFSA's case tabulation. Cases have since been reported at far lower supplemental doses, and EFSA cut the adult UL from the SCF's 25 mg/day to 12 mg/day on peripheral neuropathy grounds, from a 50 mg/day reference point with an uncertainty factor of 4 (EFSA NDA Panel, EFSA J 2023;21:e08006, PMID 37207271, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2023.8006). Not one case has been attributed to food B6.
- **Food vs supplement:** This is a genuine case where the food is safe and the concentrated form is not, and the reason is dose plus kinetics rather than any exotic compound. Food B6 arrives as pyridoxal phosphate and pyridoxamine phosphate bound in the matrix, plus glycosylated pyridoxine in plant foods, released and absorbed gradually across a meal. A tablet delivers a bolus of free pyridoxine. The accepted mechanism for the neuropathy is that free pyridoxine at high concentration outruns conversion to pyridoxal 5-phosphate and acts at PLP-dependent sites in the dorsal root ganglion, which lacks a blood-nerve barrier. Pyridoxal-5-phosphate supplements are marketed as the safe alternative on the strength of that mechanism, but the human evidence is thin and EFSA applies its 12 mg/day UL to total B6 regardless of form, so treating P5P as exempt has no support. One practical consequence: because B6 is added to energy drinks, fortified cereals and B-complex products, people accumulate supplemental doses without intending to, and the UL is easy to cross by stacking products while eating a diet that contributes almost nothing to the risk.
- **Absorption:** Roughly 75% of dietary B6 is absorbed. Plant B6 is partly pyridoxine-5'-beta-D-glucoside, which is about half as available as free pyridoxine and can act as a weak antagonist: a diet supplying 27% of its B6 as PNG lowered plasma total B6 and red cell PLP in women, an effect the authors quantified as a 15-18% loss of total B6 intake (Hansen, Leklem and Miller, J Nutr 1996;126:2512-8, PMID 8857512). That is a real discount on high-PNG plant diets and a modest one, so it should not be stretched into a claim that plant-food B6 numbers are broadly unreliable. Cooking removes more. USDA Table of Nutrient Retention Factors Release 6 (2007) gives B6 retention of 50% for roasted beef (code 0601), 35% for beef braised or simmered without the drippings (0651/0653), 45% for liver simmered without drippings (1152), 80% for roasted chicken (0805), 50% for legumes boiled 2-2.5 h (0541), 65% for legumes boiled 45-75 min and drained (0521) and 85% for greens boiled in water to cover (3005). A raw-basis figure such as the 0.933 mg/100 g for raw tuna is not what lands on the plate.
- **Does the body make it:** Humans have no vitamin B6 biosynthetic pathway; human enzymology only absorbs the dietary vitamers, interconverts them and phosphorylates them to pyridoxal 5-phosphate, the cofactor form. NONE means required, and the recovered prose names seizures and dermatitis as the deficiency picture, so ordinary B6 intake has a real target. The axis supports correcting a shortfall and gives no argument for the high-dose part, which is pharmacological and has to be judged on its own trial and safety record.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for synthesis. Human enzymology handles absorption, interconversion of the dietary vitamers and phosphorylation to pyridoxal 5-phosphate, which then serves as cofactor for kynureninase in the tryptophan to niacin equivalents route and for CBS in the transsulfuration of homocysteine.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/day synthesised, since no human pathway exists. The recovered prose quotes no B6 reference intake figure, so none is asserted here; the comparison it does supply is functional, since B6 deficiency breaks the kynurenine conversion and produces functional niacin deficiency.
- **Still matters when:** Documented B6 deficiency, where seizures and dermatitis are the described picture; isoniazid therapy, which antagonises B6; and riboflavin or B6 deficiency states that break the kynurenine route to niacin equivalents, producing functional niacin deficiency. For the high-dose part specifically, NEVER ON THIS AXIS, since nothing about endogenous synthesis speaks to doses above requirement.

*Refs. EFSA PMID 37207271; Cochrane Matthews PMID 26348534; Brasky JCO PMID 28829668*

### Green tea extract (EGCG)

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** Commercial products commonly deliver 250-500 mg EGCG/d. EFSA found significant transaminase elevation at or above 800 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody for the marketed indications. Drinking green tea is fine and is a different exposure.

**Pros**

- Green tea as a beverage has no meaningful hepatotoxicity signal and is a perfectly reasonable drink.

**Cons**

- EFSA was unable to identify ANY dose of EGCG from green tea extracts that can be considered safe, because catechin composition varies with cultivar, growing conditions and extraction method. That is an unusual regulatory conclusion.
- NIH LiverTox documents more than 100 published cases of clinically apparent liver injury: median latency 42 days, 95% hepatocellular, severe in 35%, and transplantation in 8%, with fatal acute liver failure reported.
- HLA-B*35:01 was present in 72% of green tea liver-injury cases versus 11-15% of controls, and essentially nobody is tested for it.
- The weight-loss claim, which is why most of it is sold, has clinically trivial effect sizes where measurable.
- It was the second most used of six hepatotoxic botanicals in US national survey data. Avoid entirely with pre-existing liver disease, raised transaminases, or another hepatotoxic agent such as methotrexate, isoniazid, high-dose paracetamol or alcohol.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A tea polyphenol the body does not need; concentrated extracts can injure the liver.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Epigallocatechin-3-gallate is the dominant catechin of green tea. It is poorly absorbed, extensively methylated, glucuronidated and sulfated, and degraded by gut microbiota, leaving free plasma EGCG in the sub-micromolar range. Proposed actions include inhibition of catechol-O-methyltransferase, modest effects on fat oxidation and binding to the 67 kDa laminin receptor, none of which establish any physiological requirement. The important safety fact runs the other way: concentrated extracts, especially taken while fasting, cause idiosyncratic hepatocellular injury, with published cases spanning intakes of roughly 140 to 1000 mg EGCG per day and a strong association with the HLA-B*35:01 allele. Brewed tea as a beverage is not implicated at these concentrations.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Green tea, matcha, white tea and gunpowder green tea all deliver EGCG at tens to over a hundred milligrams per cup or bowl from ordinary shop-bought products, so this is the one entry in the set where a normal beverage reaches the doses used in supplement trials. EFSA's exposure assessment puts mean adult intake at 86 to 321 mg EGCG/day across dietary surveys and high-level (95th percentile) intake at 308 to 866 mg/day, so the top of habitual tea drinking brushes the 800 mg supplement threshold. Oolong is often grouped in here, but no measured per-cup figure survived verification, so it gets no row; bottled ready-to-drink green tea is the commercially dominant form and is far weaker than anything listed, and it also lacks a verified figure here.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Green tea, brewed infusion (traditional preparation) | 70.2 mg EGCG per 100 mL (mean of 100 brewed samples from 12 references; range 2.3 to 203 mg/100 mL) | 1 cup (250 mL) | 176 mg EGCG at the mean (range 6 to 508 mg depending on leaf quantity, water temperature and steeping time) | 22% of an 800 mg extract dose per cup at the mean strength | EFSA ANS Panel, Scientific opinion on the safety of green tea catechins, EFSA Journal 2018;16(4):5239, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2018.5239, Table 2 (PMC7009618). Full text verified, including the sample count |
| White tea, dry leaf *[unconfirmed]* | 5,230 to 9,490 mg EGCG per 100 g dry leaf (5.23 to 9.49 g/100 g), overlapping green tea at 4,400 to 9,600 mg/100 g | 2 g of leaf, the amount used for one cup | 105 to 190 mg EGCG present in the leaf; the infusion delivers part of it | 13 to 24% of an 800 mg dose | Abiri B et al., Tea's anti-obesity properties, cardiometabolic health-promoting potentials, bioactive compounds, and adverse effects: a review focusing on white and green teas, Food Sci Nutr 2023;11(10):5818-36, PMID 37823174, DOI 10.1002/fsn3.3595 |
| Green tea, dry leaf | 7,116 mg EGCG per 100 g dry leaf (mean; range 1,600 to 20,320 mg/100 g, n=68) | 2 g of leaf, the amount used for one cup | 142 mg EGCG present in the leaf at the mean, of which brewing transfers a variable fraction | 18% of an 800 mg dose | EFSA ANS Panel, EFSA Journal 2018;16(4):5239, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2018.5239, Table 2 (verified) |
| Gunpowder green tea, dry leaf (highest-EGCG product measured in the commercial survey) | 7,022 mg EGCG per 100 g dry leaf (70.22 mg/g), the highest of 15 commercial products and above both matcha grades; the survey range across commercial green teas was 23.29 to 70.22 mg/g | 2 g of leaf, the amount used for one cup | 140 mg EGCG present in the leaf, of which brewing transfers a variable fraction | 18% of an 800 mg dose | Catechin Composition of Commercially-Available Green Teas, Plant Foods Hum Nutr 2023, PMID 37923855, PMC10665233 (15 commercial products, HPLC) |
| Matcha powder (whisked, whole leaf consumed) | 5,053 to 5,657 mg EGCG per 100 g powder (culinary matcha 50.53 +/- 2.41 mg/g, ceremonial matcha 56.57 +/- 2.19 mg/g; individual ceremonial samples 49.03 to 69.73 mg/g) | 1 bowl (2 g powder) | 101 to 113 mg EGCG, all of it consumed because the whole leaf is drunk (98 to 139 mg across the individual ceremonial samples) | 13 to 14% of an 800 mg dose per bowl | Catechin Composition of Commercially-Available Green Teas, Plant Foods Hum Nutr 2023, PMID 37923855, PMC10665233 (15 commercial products, HPLC). The previously cited Kolackova T et al., Antioxidants 2022;11(5):889, PMID 35624753 contains no EGCG value at all and has been dropped as the source |

- **To match the dose:** 800 mg EGCG, the dose at which supplements produce measurable liver enzyme rises, is about 4 to 5 cups of green tea at EFSA's mean brewed concentration of 70.2 mg/100 mL, or 10 to 16 cups of more weakly brewed retail tea, or about 7 to 8 bowls of matcha at 2 g each. EFSA's high-level consumers already reach 308 to 866 mg/day from infusions alone, so the top of that range is at the supplement dose. What differs is the vehicle: the tea drinker's 800 mg arrives spread over a day in two to three litres of water, usually with food, while the supplement delivers it as a single fasted bolus.
- **Food vs supplement:** State this plainly: the beverage carries no meaningful liver signal, the extract does. EFSA concluded that intake at or above 800 mg EGCG/day taken as a food supplement causes a statistically significant increase in serum transaminases, while catechins from green tea infusion prepared in the traditional way, and reconstituted drinks of equivalent composition, are in general considered safe. Same molecule, different product. Risk concentrates in high-dose, single-bolus, fasted dosing of concentrated extracts, and Commission Regulation (EU) 2022/2340 now prohibits supplements delivering 800 mg or more EGCG per day in the EU. Green tea extract is also the ingredient behind a large share of liver injury cases attributed to weight-loss and fat-burner blends, which is a warning about the extract rather than about tea.
- **Absorption:** Systemic bioavailability of EGCG is low, on the order of 0.1 to 1% of an oral dose as the unchanged compound, with heavy glucuronidation, sulfation and methylation plus colonic degradation. The critical modifier is food: fasted dosing raises EGCG Cmax severalfold compared with the same dose taken with a meal, and fasted administration is both the way extracts are typically taken and the condition associated with hepatotoxicity. Milk and food proteins bind catechins and blunt peak levels, so tea with milk is pharmacologically not tea on an empty stomach. Brewing extracts only part of the leaf's EGCG whereas matcha delivers the whole leaf. The corrected numbers support the point that matcha is no many-fold EGCG upgrade per gram of leaf: matcha measures 50.53 to 56.57 mg/g against gunpowder green tea at 70.22 mg/g in the same 15-product HPLC survey, which found no statistically significant EGCG difference across tea types.
- **Does the body make it:** EGCG is a galloylated flavan-3-ol built in the tea plant by the shikimate and phenylpropanoid pathways plus chalcone synthase, with a dedicated epicatechin galloyltransferase adding the gallate group. Humans have none of those enzymes, starting with phenylalanine ammonia-lyase, so intake is the only source and no deficiency exists. On this axis EGCG is optional by definition, which shifts the whole question onto trial benefit and onto safety, where concentrated catechin extracts carry a documented liver injury signal that brewed tea does not.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Camellia sinensis route: phenylalanine through phenylalanine ammonia-lyase to 4-coumaroyl-CoA, chalcone synthase and the flavonoid branch to epigallocatechin, then galloylation by ECGT using galloyl-1-O-beta-D-glucose as donor.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/day. Humans have no flavonoid biosynthetic pathway.
- **Still matters when:** Only trial-supported endpoints. Dose and form are the safety-relevant variables, and there are usable thresholds: EFSA concluded that 800 mg EGCG/day or more taken as a food supplement produces a statistically significant rise in serum transaminases, with no evidence of hepatotoxicity below that level for up to 12 months, while catechins from traditionally brewed green tea infusion are generally considered safe (EFSA Journal 2018;16(4):5239, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2018.5239). The USP assessment of case reports concluded that concentrated green tea extract can cause hepatotoxicity, particularly when taken on an empty stomach, and recommended a label caution. Both support cautious use rather than routine use.

*Refs. EFSA J 2018;16(4):5239; NIH LiverTox NBK547925; Likhitsup PMID 39102266*

### Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera)

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 300-600 mg/d of root extract standardised to about 5% withanolides. Liver injury occurred at ordinary retail doses.
- **Who it is for:** No population with a demonstrated clinical need. Anyone with liver disease should be steered away entirely.

**Pros**

- There is a real but small and fragile efficacy signal: pooled Perceived Stress Scale -4.72 (-8.45 to -0.99), Hamilton Anxiety Scale -2.19 (-3.83 to -0.55) and serum cortisol -2.58 across 9 randomised trials and 558 patients.
- A sleep meta-analysis of 5 trials and 400 participants found SMD -0.59, with larger effects at 600 mg/d or more over 8 weeks or longer.

**Cons**

- Hepatotoxicity is documented in a prospective case series from Iceland and the US DILIN network: jaundice with nausea, lethargy and pruritus after a latency of 2 to 12 weeks, cholestatic or mixed injury, symptoms lasting 5 to 20 weeks.
- In an Indian case series, 5 of 23 patients had pre-existing chronic liver disease, 3 developed acute-on-chronic liver failure and ALL 3 DIED. Pre-existing liver disease is the hard contraindication.
- Denmark banned it in food supplements in 2023 after its national food institute concluded no safe intake level could be established.
- It raises T4 and can precipitate thyrotoxicosis or require a levothyroxine dose reduction. Published cases exist.
- Avoid in pregnancy (traditional abortifacient use), in autoimmune disease and in transplant recipients (immunostimulant effects), and with sedatives (additive).
- The trials are small, short, mostly single-centre and frequently manufacturer-sponsored, with subjective questionnaire endpoints and no hard endpoint anywhere in the literature.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

A root extract taken for stress; it plays no part in normal human physiology.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Withania somnifera root contains withanolides (principally withaferin A and withanolide A) together with sitoindosides, and commercial extracts are standardised to withanolide content. Randomised trials running 8 to 12 weeks report modest reductions in self-rated stress scales and in morning serum cortisol, while the proposed HPA axis and GABA-A receptor mechanisms rest largely on animal and in vitro work. Nothing in the human body synthesises withanolides and no metabolic pathway requires them, so this is a pharmacological exposure rather than a nutritional one. Liver injury is a documented harm: a case series from Iceland and the US Drug-Induced Liver Injury Network described five patients (3 male, 2 female, mean age 43, range 21 to 62) who became jaundiced 2 to 12 weeks after starting ashwagandha, with cholestatic or mixed injury (R ratios 1.4 to 3.3) and hyperbilirubinaemia persisting 5 to 20 weeks. Liver tests normalised within 1 to 5 months in four of the five and the fifth was lost to follow-up; no patient developed hepatic failure and there were no deaths or transplants in the series.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Not food
- **Dose from food:** Out of reach
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Ashwagandha is the root, sometimes root plus leaf, of Withania somnifera, a small shrub in the Solanaceae. The root is a medicinal drug rather than a dietary item: bitter, taken as a churna or a decoction in milk, and present in no cuisine as an ingredient. The active steroidal lactones, withanolides such as withaferin A, withanolide A and withanone, occur in Withania and a few related genera and are absent from the food supply. The nightshades people actually eat, tomato, potato, aubergine, sweet and chilli peppers, contain a different class of steroidal compounds (glycoalkaloids such as tomatine and solanine), so botanical relatedness buys nothing here. A related species, Withania coagulans, has fruit used regionally as a vegetarian milk-clotting agent, which is a processing aid rather than a dietary source of withanolides.

- **To match the dose:** Trials use 300-600 mg/day of standardised root extract. KSM-66 is a root-only extract standardised to a minimum 5% withanolides, Sensoril is a root-and-leaf extract at 10%, and Shoden is standardised to 35% withanolide glycosides, so a typical daily dose is roughly 15-60 mg of withanolides. Traditional practice uses 3-6 g/day of dried root powder to reach a comparable place. No food quantity supplies this at any size, because no food plant contains withanolides. Quoting an equivalent weight of an ordinary food would require inventing a content value, so the honest answer is that the dietary route does not exist.
- **Food vs supplement:** Plant part matters and so does the regulator's view. Root-only preparations have a better documented safety profile than preparations including leaf and aerial parts, which are richer in withaferin A, the most cytotoxic of the withanolides. Ashwagandha is banned in food supplements in Denmark, the Dutch national institute RIVM has published a risk assessment of Withania preparations (RIVM report 2024-0029), French authorities have advised against use in specific groups, the UK Food Standards Agency has logged rising incident reports and its committee reviewed associations with thyroid toxicity, hypoglycaemia and liver toxicity, and Australia's TGA issued a medicine safety update in July 2026 on continuing reports of ashwagandha-associated liver injury judged to be idiosyncratic. KSM-66's milk-based extraction is a marketing distinction rather than a safety one. Withanolide content also varies with chemotype, cultivation and plant part, so two products at the same milligram dose can differ several-fold in the compounds that matter.
- **Absorption:** Human pharmacokinetic data are thin and no standard bioavailability figure can be quoted with confidence. Withanolides are lipophilic and poorly water-soluble, which is why traditional preparation uses milk or ghee and why modern products add phospholipid or cyclodextrin carriers. Withanolide glycosides (withanosides) are more water-soluble and are the markers Shoden standardises on, so percent withanolides on two labels may refer to chemically different pools measured by different assays. Because there is no food form there is no food-versus-supplement absorption comparison to make; the meaningful comparison is between extract types, and it is not standardised.
- **Does the body make it:** The active constituents are withanolides, steroidal lactones such as withaferin A and withanolide A, built in the plant from mevalonate-derived sterols. Humans make sterols but not withanolides, and there is no human enzyme that produces the withanolide lactone ring. Nothing about ashwagandha can be framed as replacing something the body has run short of.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Withanolide biosynthesis proceeds in Withania somnifera from 24-methylenecholesterol through plant cytochrome P450 oxidations; absent from human tissue.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway)
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis. Screen on trial quality for the claimed indication (stress, sleep, testosterone), extract standardisation to withanolide content, and reported hepatotoxicity and thyroid effects.

*Refs. Bjornsson Liver Int PMID 31991029; Arumugam PMID 39348746; Cheah PMID 34559859; DTU Denmark 2020*

### Weight-loss, fat-burner and energy blends

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Garcinia products commonly supply 500-1,500 mg of extract. No effective dose has been demonstrated for clinically meaningful weight loss.
- **Who it is for:** No identifiable group. This is the worst-behaving corner of the entire supplement market.

**Pros**

- None identified. The efficacy literature does not support clinically meaningful weight loss from these products.

**Cons**
