- Weight-loss and energy products account for 71.8% (67.6-76.1) of the supplement-related emergency department visits presenting with cardiac symptoms, out of an estimated 23,005 visits per year in the US.
- Adults aged 20-34 accounted for 28.0% of all such visits, so the demographic bearing the harm is young and otherwise healthy.
- The FDA acted against Hydroxycut in 2009 after hepatotoxicity reports including a death. NIH LiverTox maintains a dedicated monograph for Garcinia cambogia acute liver injury including fulminant hepatic failure.
- These are proprietary blends, and 40.9% of all adulterated supplements in the FDA database are weight-loss products. Undeclared sibutramine, withdrawn from all major markets in 2010, and prohibited stimulants such as DMAA, deterenol, octodrine, oxilofrine and phenpromethamine are repeatedly found.
- Serotonin syndrome has been reported with garcinia in combination with SSRIs, SNRIs and MAOIs. Contraindicated in liver disease, arrhythmia and hypertension.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Stimulant and herbal blends for fat loss; adulteration with withdrawn drugs is common.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Typical declared ingredients are caffeine, green tea catechins, synephrine, yohimbine, garcinia and undisclosed stimulant analogues, often behind a proprietary blend label that conceals individual doses. Weight-loss products accounted for 317 of the 776 supplements associated with US FDA warnings from 2007 to 2016, and within them 84.9% contained sibutramine, an appetite suppressant suspended in the EU in January 2010 and withdrawn in the US in October 2010 over cardiovascular events, though it remained on the market in some countries afterwards; 23.7% contained the laxative phenolphthalein, 6.3% contained sibutramine analogues and 5.4% contained fluoxetine. Non-bodybuilding herbal and dietary supplements as a class carry the worst liver outcomes in the US Drug-Induced Liver Injury Network: among DILIN-enrolled liver-injury cases, death or liver transplantation occurred in 13% of the 85 non-bodybuilding supplement cases against 3% for conventional medications. That group is heterogeneous, covering weight-loss along with energy, joint, immune and mixed products, and the figure describes outcomes among adjudicated cases already in a hepatology referral network rather than the risk of taking such a product. This is a commercial product category built on stimulants and adulterants; there is no nutrient in it and nothing a person can be deficient in.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Partly
- **Food data confidence:** Medium
- **Body makes it:** n/a

This category holds four different answers and the earlier single TRACE label hid the most important one. Caffeine is RICH and is the only component with a consistent thermogenic effect, available from coffee and tea by the cup. p-Synephrine is RICH as well: citrus juices carry up to 25 to 40 mg per quarter litre and Seville orange juice about 13 mg per 240 mL glass, which sits inside the clinical dose range, so the earlier NOT_FOOD label for bitter orange was wrong. Garcinia gummi-gutta rind (kodampuli, Malabar tamarind) is a genuine culinary souring agent in Kerala and parts of Southeast Asia, used in fish curry and asam laksa, and carries hydroxycitric acid at 10 to 30% of dry weight. The remainder splits differently: raspberry ketone is TRACE (up to 4.3 mg/kg in raspberries against 100 to 1,400 mg/day sold), green coffee chlorogenic acids come from unroasted beans that nobody eats, yohimbe bark and Coleus forskohlii root are NOT_FOOD, and higenamine and DMAA are synthetic with no food origin at all.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Garcinia gummi-gutta dried fruit rind (kodampuli / Malabar tamarind), culinary souring agent *[unconfirmed]* | 10 to 30 g hydroxycitric acid per 100 g of dried pericarp (10 to 30% w/w of dry weight, the commonly quoted range). Commercial extracts are standardised to 40% HCA or more | about 3 g of dried rind, a couple of pieces as used to sour a pot of fish curry for four | 300 to 900 mg HCA in the pot, about 75 to 225 mg per person, and less again because HCA transfers only partly from rind into broth and the rind is discarded rather than eaten | about 8 to 25% of the 900 mg top supplement dose per person; the whole pot spans the 72 to 900 mg HCA range delivered by supplements | van Breemen RB, Roe AL, Akhtar N, Hepatotoxicity of dietary supplements containing Garcinia gummi-gutta (L.) N. Robson, Pharmaceutical Biology 2025;63(1):912-24, PMID 41262061, DOI 10.1080/13880209.2025.2591467 |
| Seville (bitter) orange juice, and citrus juice generally | about 5.7 mg p-synephrine per 100 mL for Seville orange juice (measured at roughly 57 ug/mL); citrus juices generally are reported at up to 25 to 40 mg per quarter litre, i.e. 10 to 16 mg/100 mL | 1 glass (240 mL, 8 fl oz) | about 13 mg p-synephrine from Seville orange juice; up to 25 to 40 mg per 250 mL across citrus juices | sits inside the 10 to 53 mg/day p-synephrine range used in the pooled clinical studies (individual study doses 5.5 to 80 mg), so one glass is dose-equivalent to a capsule | Stohs SJ et al., A review of the human clinical studies involving Citrus aurantium (bitter orange) and p-synephrine, Int J Med Sci 2012, PMC3444973. Figures taken from the verification report's authoritative check rather than re-read here, so treat the per-100 mL conversion as indicative |
| Coffee, brewed filter (Arabica, roasted) | 30 mg caffeine per 100 mL. The chlorogenic acid bean percentages previously given here (0.9 to 1.9 g/100 g roasted against 5.4 g/100 g green) are withdrawn: the cited study measured coffee solutions, reporting green coffee highest in chlorogenic acid at 543.23 mg/L and medium roast highest in caffeine at 203.63 mg/L, and gives no per-bean values | 1 cup (150 mL) | 45 mg caffeine per cup; espresso gives 112 mg per 40 mL and instant coffee 54 mg per 150 mL. A 240 mL US/UK mug is roughly 95 mg on USDA figures of about 40 mg/100 g | 23% of a 200 mg pre-workout caffeine dose per 150 mL cup, and about 48% for a 240 mL mug | Caffeine: Rocha PLA, Lima ALC, Saunders B, Reis CEG, Development of a caffeine content table for foods, drinks, medications and supplements typically consumed by the Brazilian population, Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, PMID 36297100, PMC9608580 (table verified to the digit). Mug figure from USDA FoodData Central for brewed coffee. Chlorogenic acid: Awwad S, Issa R, Alnsour L, Albals D, Al-Momani I, Molecules 2021;26(24):7502, PMID 34946584, which reports solution concentrations only; green Arabica beans are usually quoted at roughly 4 to 8% chlorogenic acids by dry weight, an indicative literature range with no page-level source given here |
| Green tea, brewed (the EGCG and caffeine component of most fat-burner blends) | 20 mg caffeine per 100 mL; for EGCG see the green tea extract entry (70.2 mg/100 mL brewed) | 1 cup (200 mL) | 40 mg caffeine, plus roughly 140 mg EGCG at EFSA's mean brewed concentration | 20% of a 200 mg caffeine dose per cup | Rocha PLA et al., Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, PMID 36297100, PMC9608580 (green tea infused, 20 mg/100 mL and 40 mg per 200 mL cup, verified); EGCG from EFSA Journal 2018;16(4):5239, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2018.5239 |
| Raspberries, raw | up to 0.43 mg raspberry ketone per 100 g (up to 4.3 mg/kg); published values for raspberries generally cluster around 1 to 4 mg/kg | 1 cup / small punnet (125 g) | up to 0.54 mg at the highest reported natural concentration, and typically far less | up to 0.5% of a 100 mg supplement dose | Bredsdorff L et al., Raspberry ketone in food supplements: high intake, few toxicity data, a cause for safety concern?, Regul Toxicol Pharmacol 2015;73(1):196-200, PMID 26160596, DOI 10.1016/j.yrtph.2015.06.022 (abstract verified: natural occurrence up to 4.3 mg/kg, recommended intake 100 to 1,400 mg/day, 100 mg is 56 times the 1,800 ug/day threshold of toxicological concern for Class 1 substances). The lower bound of 0.001 mg/kg previously stated does not appear in the paper and has been removed |

- **To match the dose:** Component by component. Caffeine: 200 mg is four to five 150 mL cups of filter coffee on the cited Brazilian table, or about two 240 mL US/UK mugs at roughly 95 mg each, or five cups of green tea, so this one is trivially reachable (guarana powder, yerba mate, cocoa and energy drinks belong in the same picture and appear in the same cited caffeine table, but no per-portion figure was verified here, so they get no row). p-Synephrine: a single 240 mL glass of Seville orange juice at about 13 mg already sits inside the 10 to 53 mg/day range used in the pooled overweight and obese subjects, whose individual study doses spanned 5.5 to 80 mg. Garcinia: 3 g of dried rind souring a pot for four carries 300 to 900 mg HCA at 10 to 30% w/w, about 75 to 225 mg per person against supplement doses of 72 to 900 mg HCA, and less again because HCA transfers only partly into the broth and the rind is fished out rather than eaten, so culinary use lands in the lower middle of the supplement range rather than at its top. Raspberry ketone: 100 mg, the smallest common dose, is about 23 kg of fresh raspberries at the highest natural concentration of 4.3 mg/kg, and the 1,400 mg top dose about 326 kg, so it is unreachable by three orders of magnitude, and the safety review notes even the lowest recommended dose is about 56 times the threshold of toxicological concern for its structural class. Green coffee: extracts standardised to 45 to 50% chlorogenic acids at 400 to 1,600 mg/day supply roughly 200 to 800 mg CGA, and the concentrated dose exists only because green beans are inedible; the cups-of-coffee comparison cannot be stated here, because the bean percentages it rested on were misattributed and have been withdrawn.
- **Food vs supplement:** The governing pattern in this category is that the food is safe while the concentrate is not, and Garcinia is the cleanest illustration. Centuries of culinary use of the dried rind as a souring agent in South and Southeast Asian cooking carry no liver-injury signal, while concentrated HCA supplements have produced 34 published hepatotoxicity case reports including nine liver transplants and one death, plus more than 200 adverse events of liver injury and 93 international case reports reviewed by Health Canada, with 17 cases meeting CIOMS/RUCAM causality. The injury looks idiosyncratic rather than dose-dependent, with a proposed HLA-B*35:01 association, so the absence of harm from culinary use says something about the product form rather than about a lower dose, and on corrected arithmetic the culinary serving delivers the middle of the supplement HCA range rather than the top of it. The same shape repeats across the category: green tea extract in fat-burner blends against drinking tea (see that entry), and bitter orange p-synephrine stacked with caffeine and linked to cardiovascular events, where the honest food comparison is a glass of citrus juice carrying a similar p-synephrine dose without the stimulant stack. Yohimbe bark extract has no edible counterpart at all, and raspberry ketone at food flavouring levels of milligrams per kilogram bears no relation to 100 to 1,400 mg/day capsules with essentially no human toxicology. Add the labelling problem: proprietary blends routinely under-declare caffeine and stack multiple stimulants, so the only reliable comparison available is to count your caffeine in cups and treat the rest of the label as unquantified.
- **Absorption:** Caffeine is the outlier and the reason these blends do anything: it is almost completely absorbed, peaks in 30 to 60 minutes, and behaves much the same from coffee as from a tablet, with coffee's chlorogenic acids only modestly slowing absorption. Chlorogenic acids themselves are poorly bioavailable, with roughly a third absorbed in the small intestine and the remainder extensively metabolised by colonic microbiota into hydroxycinnamic acid derivatives, so the milligrams of CGA printed on a green coffee label map badly onto systemic exposure. Raspberry ketone is rapidly conjugated and cleared, which is one reason the human evidence for it is close to non-existent. Hydroxycitric acid absorption from Garcinia is incomplete and depends on the salt form (calcium, potassium or magnesium salts differ in solubility), which helps explain why the pooled human weight-loss effect is at best around 0.9 kg over placebo, despite doses in the same order as what a serving of Kerala fish curry supplies.
- **Does the body make it:** This is a blended product category whose actual contents are not a defined substance, so the axis label is NA and the split has to be stated: caffeine, synephrine, green tea catechins and yohimbine are plant compounds with no human biosynthetic route and read NONE, while the same blends routinely include L-carnitine, which is endogenously synthesised and CONDITIONAL in its own right, and sometimes CLA, whose c9,t11 isomer has a partial human route. The axis is also the wrong screen for this category, where the recurring analytical finding is undeclared pharmaceuticals, including the withdrawn anorectic sibutramine and banned stimulants, in products marketed as herbal slimmers.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for the botanical stimulants and catechins. L-carnitine is synthesised in four steps from protein-derived trimethyllysine: TMLHE hydroxylates it to hydroxytrimethyllysine, aldolytic cleavage gives 4-trimethylaminobutyraldehyde, oxidation gives gamma-butyrobetaine, then BBOX1 (gamma-butyrobetaine dioxygenase) makes carnitine; BBOX1 is expressed in liver, kidney and brain and not in skeletal or cardiac muscle, which therefore import carnitine.
- **Amount made:** 0 for caffeine, synephrine, catechins and yohimbine. Endogenous L-carnitine synthesis is about 1.2 micromol/kg body weight/day, that is 84 micromol/day and about 13.5 mg/day for a 70 kg adult at MW 161.2, measured in strict vegetarians; in omnivores most body carnitine comes from the diet.
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis for the stimulants and catechins. Screen on undeclared sibutramine and stimulants, cumulative stimulant load on blood pressure and heart rhythm, herb-induced liver injury, and the small and short-lived weight effects even when the labelled ingredients are all that is present. For the carnitine content, diet already supplies most body carnitine in omnivores, so it adds little there, while in strict vegetarians and in the deficiency states listed in the carnitine row it is more than filler.

*Refs. Geller NEJM PMID 26465986; Tucker PMID 30646238; LiverTox NBK548087; Cohen Clin Toxicol 2021*

### Bodybuilding and test-booster products

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Not applicable. Dosing is label-driven and the label does not reflect the contents.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody. Any muscle gain is attributable to undeclared controlled substances the user did not consent to take.

**Pros**

- None. There is no hype-versus-reality gap to argue about, because the products contain drugs they do not declare.

**Cons**

- The DILIN prospective series enrolled 44 men, mean age 33, median latency 73 days of use. 100% were jaundiced, 84% had pruritus, 71% required hospitalisation, and liver biopsy in 59% showed mild hepatitis with profound cholestasis.
- Chemical analysis of the implicated products repeatedly finds anabolic androgenic steroids not declared on the label. These are controlled substances.
- Of 44 products sold as SARMs, only 23 (52%) contained any SARM, 17 (39%) contained a different unapproved drug, 4 (9%) contained no active compound at all, and only 18 (41%) had accurate labels.
- Muscle building accounted for 11.9% of adulterated products in the FDA database, and bodybuilding products caused 45 of 130 herbal and dietary supplement liver injury cases in the DILIN cohort.
- ABCB11 gene variants were over-represented in cases versus ethnically matched controls, so some individuals are genetically far more vulnerable and have no way to know.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Muscle and testosterone products; nearly nine in ten flagged ones contain hidden synthetic steroids.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** These products promise anabolic effect from Tribulus terrestris, D-aspartic acid, fenugreek, boron and similar botanicals, none of which raises serum testosterone in men with normal gonadal function at the doses supplied. Of 92 muscle-building supplements associated with US FDA warnings between 2007 and 2016, 89.1% contained synthetic steroids or steroid-like compounds that were not declared on the label. In the US Drug-Induced Liver Injury Network, 45 of 130 supplement-related liver injuries came from bodybuilding products, causing prolonged cholestatic jaundice in young men that lasted weeks to months, though without deaths or transplants in that series. Liver injury attributed to herbal and dietary supplements rose from 7% to 20% of all DILIN cases between 2004 and 2013, and this category is a substantial part of that rise. It is a product class with no nutritional content, so no deficiency state exists.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** None
- **Dose from food:** No dose
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** n/a

There is no food source, because the active ingredients are undeclared anabolic androgenic steroids: manufactured pharmaceuticals and controlled substances. The label ingredients are largely decorative. Chemical analysis of the products implicated in liver injury repeatedly finds steroids the label does not mention.

- **To match the dose:** Not applicable. You cannot eat your way to an undeclared steroid dose, and you should not want to.
- **Food vs supplement:** The legitimate food-adjacent route to the advertised outcome is dull and well evidenced: enough total protein to reach about 1.6 g/kg/day, and creatine monohydrate. Both have their own entries with food tables. Neither requires buying a proprietary blend.
- **Absorption:** NONE. This entry is a product category rather than a single substance, so there is no single absorption comparison to make.
- **Does the body make it:** NA because this is a commercial product category whose contents are not a defined substance, so the axis cannot be applied to the product itself; the recovered classification for its parts is FULL in healthy young men, CONDITIONAL in ageing, hypogonadism and HPG suppression, and NONE for the plant ingredients. A healthy man's testes already make about 7 mg of testosterone a day, output pinned where it is by LH feedback through the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, so in a eugonadal man there is no idle synthetic capacity for a capsule to unlock, and the CONDITIONAL tier exists because total testosterone declines roughly 1 percent a year from midlife and the body demonstrably does not make enough in primary or secondary hypogonadism. Tribulus, fenugreek and ashwagandha withanolides score NONE with no requirement and no deficiency state attached, and the compounds that reliably move testosterone are drugs (anabolic steroids, SARMs, hCG, aromatase inhibitors) that work by overriding or suppressing that feedback loop, so no case for these products can be built on this axis.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE, since the category is not a defined substance and no animal synthesises the plant ingredients. The endogenous process these products claim to raise is testicular steroidogenesis: cholesterol is imported into the mitochondrion by StAR, the rate-limiting step, cleaved to pregnenolone by CYP11A1, the rate-limiting enzymatic conversion, then processed by 3-beta-HSD type 2 (HSD3B2) and CYP17A1 to androstenedione and reduced to testosterone by 17-beta-HSD type 3 (HSD17B3) in LH-driven Leydig cells; human Leydig cells favour the delta-5 route (pregnenolone, 17-hydroxypregnenolone, DHEA, androstenedione or androstenediol, testosterone) because the 17,20-lyase activity of human CYP17A1 is poor on delta-4 substrates. Smaller contributions come from the adrenal cortex (DHEA, DHEA-S, androstenedione) and from peripheral conversion.
- **Amount made:** About 7 mg/day of testosterone in healthy adult men (NCBI Bookshelf NBK279031, Endotext), alongside smaller amounts of weaker androgens such as androstenedione and DHEA; the testes are the dominant source with small adrenal and peripheral contributions, and NBK279031 does not quantify that split, so the often-quoted over 95 percent figure is not used here. 0 mg/day for the plant ingredients, none of which any animal synthesises. No synthesis figure applies to the product category itself.
- **Still matters when:** Diagnosed hypogonadism, which is treated with prescription testosterone and clinician monitoring rather than with a booster; correcting a measured deficiency of a real cofactor such as vitamin D or zinc; and repairing the inputs that suppress the axis, meaning sleep, energy availability, alcohol and body fat. An analysis of best-selling online testosterone-boosting supplements found most ingredient claims unsupported by human data, with some products carrying very large vitamin and mineral doses (PMID 30621919, J Sex Med 2019;16:203-12), and the concrete consumer risk is adulteration, since over-the-counter boosters are among the products most often found spiked with SARMs, aromatase inhibitors or actual steroids, which do move testosterone and do suppress the axis. For these products themselves: NEVER ON THIS AXIS.

*Refs. Stolz PMID 30934130; Navarro DILIN PMID 25043597; Van Wagoner JAMA PMID 29183075; Geller PMID 26465986*

### Sexual-enhancement products

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** Not applicable. Undeclared pharmaceutical doses are present at pharmacological levels.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody, from any retailer, at any price.

**Pros**

- None. The only defensible use of this evidence is categorical avoidance.

**Cons**

- Sexual enhancement is the single largest adulterated category: 353 of 776 products (45.5%) in the FDA tainted-products database, from 146 different companies.
- The adulterants are PDE5 inhibitors, chiefly sildenafil and structural analogues designed to evade detection. Undeclared sildenafil taken by someone on nitrates for angina is a route to catastrophic hypotension in a patient who does not know they took a PDE5 inhibitor.
- Enforcement does not work on this category. Of 27 FDA-recalled supplements still purchasable and re-analysed a mean 34.3 months after recall, 18 (66.7%) still contained banned drugs and 63% contained the same adulterant the FDA had already ordered removed.
- Tucker found 157 products (20.2%) contained more than one unapproved ingredient, and on resampling 19 of 28 repeatedly flagged products (67.9%) had merely switched to a different adulterant.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Pills sold for erections; a large share of the products FDA has flagged contain undeclared PDE5 inhibitors.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** Marketed as herbal or all-natural, these products routinely contain undeclared PDE5 inhibitors. Of 776 dietary supplements associated with US FDA warnings between 2007 and 2016, 353 (45.5%) were sexual enhancement products, and within that group sildenafil was identified in 47.0%, sildenafil analogues in 38.0%, tadalafil in 20.4% and the antidepressant dapoxetine in 4.0%. The botanicals actually named on the label (Panax ginseng, Tribulus terrestris, Epimedium, yohimbe, maca) have no established effect on erectile function at the quantities supplied, so any felt effect points to the hidden pharmaceutical. The concrete danger is a vasodilator taken unknowingly alongside nitrates or in the presence of cardiac disease, since the user believes the product is herbal and does not mention it to a clinician. As a product category there is no nutrient here and no deficiency state.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** None
- **Dose from food:** No dose
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

There is no food source. The active ingredients are undeclared PDE5 inhibitors, chiefly sildenafil and structural analogues designed to evade laboratory detection. These are prescription pharmaceuticals, not nutrients, and they are present because the product would otherwise do nothing.

- **To match the dose:** Not applicable. No quantity of any food contains a pharmaceutical PDE5 inhibitor.
- **Food vs supplement:** The danger runs the other way from a normal form caveat. Here the product contains more than the label admits rather than less. Someone taking nitrates for angina who swallows an undeclared PDE5 inhibitor can reach catastrophic hypotension without knowing what they took.
- **Absorption:** NONE. This entry is a product category rather than a single substance, so there is no single absorption comparison to make.
- **Does the body make it:** These are plant materials, mainly yohimbe bark, Epimedium, maca and Tribulus, so nothing in them is endogenous and the synthesis axis cannot screen them at all; the endogenous members once grouped here, L-citrulline, L-arginine and DHEA, sit in their own rows. The screening question that does discriminate is adulteration: analytical surveys of herbal sexual enhancers repeatedly recover undeclared synthetic PDE5 inhibitors, including sildenafil and tadalafil and unlicensed structural analogues. That makes an unregulated capsule an undisclosed prescription drug, with the nitrate interaction and cardiac risk that go with it.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Plant alkaloid, prenylflavonoid and saponin pathways; no human synthesis of yohimbine, icariin or the declared or undeclared PDE5 inhibitors.
- **Amount made:** 0 (no human biosynthetic pathway)
- **Still matters when:** Nothing on this axis. Screen on documented adulteration with undeclared PDE5 inhibitors and their analogues, contraindication with nitrates and alpha-blockers, yohimbine's pressor and anxiogenic effects, and the fact that erectile dysfunction is a cardiovascular warning sign that warrants clinical assessment.

*Refs. Tucker PMID 30646238; Cohen JAMA 2014; FDA CDER tainted products database*

### High-dose folic acid plus B12 for cardiovascular prevention

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** The Norwegian trials used folic acid 0.8 mg/d plus B12 0.4 mg/d, with or without B6 40 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody for cardiovascular prevention. Periconceptional folic acid at 400 µg is a completely different indication and should not be conflated with this.

**Pros**

- B vitamins do reliably lower homocysteine, which was the mechanistic rationale. The mechanism worked. The clinical benefit did not follow.

**Cons**

- The homocysteine hypothesis is dead as a cardiovascular target. HOPE-2 (n=5,522, 5 years) lowered homocysteine with no effect on major vascular events (RR 0.95, 0.84-1.07). SEARCH (n=12,064, 6.7 years) cut it 28% with RR 1.04 (0.97-1.12) for major vascular events.
- There is a positive cancer harm signal. Pooled Norwegian trials in 6,837 patients with ischaemic heart disease: cancer incidence HR 1.21 (1.03-1.41), cancer mortality HR 1.38 (1.07-1.79), and all-cause mortality also raised.
- Anyone with a history of cancer or at elevated cancer risk should not take high-dose folic acid for cardiovascular indications.
- Vitamin B12 use before and during chemotherapy was associated with worse disease-free survival (HR 1.83, 1.15-2.92) and worse overall survival (HR 2.04, 1.22-3.40) in a prospective study nested in a phase 3 trial.
- The upper limit for synthetic folic acid is 1,000 µg/d, and it was set to prevent masking of B12 deficiency neuropathy.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Lowers homocysteine in the blood, which turns out not to prevent heart attacks.

- **If you lack it:** Mixed product
- **In detail:** Folic acid with B12, often with B6 added, reliably lowers plasma total homocysteine by roughly a quarter, because both vitamins feed the methionine synthase step that converts homocysteine back to methionine. The rationale was observational: raised homocysteine tracks with vascular disease and stroke. Randomised trials separated the biomarker from the disease. The Cochrane review included 15 trials and 71,422 participants in total, with each outcome pooled from a subset of those trials: no effect on myocardial infarction (7.1% versus 6.0%, RR 1.02, 95% CI 0.95 to 1.10; 12 trials, 46,699 participants) and none on death from any cause (11.7% versus 12.3%, RR 1.01, 95% CI 0.96 to 1.06; 11 trials, 44,817 participants), with a small reduction in stroke (4.3% versus 5.1%, RR 0.90, 95% CI 0.82 to 0.99; 10 trials, 44,224 participants) driven largely by trials in populations without folic acid fortification and with low baseline folate. One safety point carries over from the components: high-dose folic acid given without first checking B12 status can normalise the anaemia of B12 deficiency while the spinal cord damage continues undetected, so B12 status should be established before folate is given at these doses.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** No dose
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Both nutrients are abundant in ordinary food, so see the folic acid and vitamin B12 entries for the food tables. The distinction that matters here is the dose and the purpose. Food folate and food B12 at dietary levels carry none of the cancer signal seen in the Norwegian trials, which used 0.8 mg/d of synthetic folic acid plus 0.4 mg/d of B12 for a cardiovascular indication.

- **To match the dose:** Not applicable as a target. There is no food quantity to aim for, because the trials that produced the cancer signal were testing a supplemental dose for a purpose that does not work. The dietary answer is to meet the reference intake from food and stop there.
- **Food vs supplement:** This is the clearest case in the document where the food and the supplement diverge on outcome rather than on amount. Periconceptional folic acid at 400 µg remains the strongest supplement result in existence; the same molecule at twice the dose given to cardiac patients raised cancer incidence. Same compound, different dose, different population, opposite conclusion.
- **Absorption:** NONE. This entry is a product category rather than a single substance, so there is no single absorption comparison to make.
- **Does the body make it:** Both components are NONE on this axis: humans have no folate and no cobalamin biosynthetic pathway, so each has a real target whenever intake or absorption fails, and the colonic bacterial output of either counts as exogenous supply, with B12 not even absorbable at the site where bacteria make it. That settles nothing about the cardiovascular claim, which rests on lowering homocysteine, a surrogate in the methionine cycle, and the recovered prose carries no cardiovascular outcome data for this combination. Use the axis to confirm the two components are genuinely required nutrients, then judge the prevention claim on trial evidence, which this dataset does not supply.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE for synthesis of either component. The shared biochemistry is the methionine cycle: methyl transfer yields S-adenosylhomocysteine, hydrolysed by AHCY to homocysteine, which MTR remethylates using 5-methyl-THF and vitamin B12, or BHMT remethylates using betaine, or CBS commits to transsulfuration with vitamin B6. Folate enters on PCFT (SLC46A1) and the reduced folate carrier; B12 enters by the haptocorrin, intrinsic factor, cubilin and amnionless route in the ileum, with transcobalamin transport in blood and CD320 uptake by tissues.
- **Amount made:** 0 synthesised for both components. Reference points: folate RDA 400 µg DFE/d and vitamin B12 RDA 2.4 µg/d, against high-dose products that sit well above both. Colonic microbial folate contribution NOT ESTABLISHED in µg/d; absorbable colonic B12 effectively 0 µg/d.
- **Still matters when:** Correcting documented folate or B12 deficiency, and periconceptional folic acid 400 micrograms/day for neural tube defect prevention, which has outcome evidence. Also the standard B12 settings: vegans, pernicious anaemia, gastrectomy, ileal resection, metformin, long-term proton pump inhibitors and atrophic gastritis. For cardiovascular prevention specifically, NEVER ON THIS AXIS; folate, B12 and B6 status is what is actually correctable in the methylation cycle, and a raised homocysteine is a reason to check that status.

*Refs. Ebbing JAMA 2009 PMID 19920236; HOPE-2; SEARCH; Ambrosone JCO PMID 31855498; USPSTF PMID 35727271*

### Antioxidants during chemotherapy

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Not applicable. No dose is recommended and the concern is temporal rather than numeric.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody. Documented deficiency should be corrected under oncology supervision, with timing discussed against the chemotherapy schedule.

**Pros**

- A plain reference-intake-level multivitamin showed no adverse association in the same dataset, so ordinary nutritional support is not the concern here.

**Cons**

- The theoretical objection is direct and mechanistic: several cytotoxic classes including anthracyclines, platinum agents and alkylating agents work partly through oxidative damage, and radiotherapy works through free radical generation. Supplementing antioxidants may blunt the treatment.
- SWOG S0221 (n=1,134 women with high-risk early breast cancer on cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin and paclitaxel): any antioxidant (vitamins A, C, E, carotenoids, CoQ10) taken both before and during chemotherapy gave recurrence adjusted HR 1.41 (0.98-2.04, P=0.06).
- In the same study, vitamin B12 taken before and during chemotherapy gave disease-free survival HR 1.83 (1.15-2.92) and overall survival HR 2.04 (1.22-3.40). Iron was also associated with worse outcomes.
- SELECT establishes in a randomised setting that high-dose antioxidant supplementation can INCREASE cancer incidence: vitamin E 400 IU/d raised prostate cancer HR 1.17 in 34,887 men.
- Honest caveat: SWOG S0221 is observational within a randomised trial, and the headline antioxidant hazard ratio crossed 1 with P=0.06. Nobody should overstate it. But cancer patients are the most aggressively targeted and least protected supplement consumers, and support your body through chemo is sold with no evidence and a plausible mechanism of harm.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Not a nutrient. This is the practice of taking antioxidant pills while on chemotherapy.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** This entry is a clinical scenario rather than a substance, and there is no antioxidant deficiency to correct. Anthracyclines, platinum agents, alkylating agents and radiotherapy kill tumour cells partly through reactive oxygen species and oxidative DNA damage, so high-dose antioxidants taken concurrently could in principle shield the tumour along with normal tissue, and tumour cells themselves often depend on antioxidant defences to survive. In the largest prospective dataset, 1,134 women in the SWOG S0221 adjuvant breast cancer trial, self-reported use of any antioxidant supplement (vitamin A, C or E, carotenoids or coenzyme Q10) both before and during chemotherapy was associated with a borderline increase in recurrence (adjusted hazard ratio 1.41, 95% CI 0.98 to 2.04, P = .06) and in death (adjusted hazard ratio 1.40, 95% CI 0.90 to 2.18, P = .14), with separately significant signals for vitamin B12 and for iron. These associations are observational within a randomised trial cohort and both antioxidant confidence intervals cross 1, so this is a well-founded caution rather than proof of harm, and it underpins oncology advice to avoid supplements other than a standard multivitamin during treatment unless a specific deficiency has been diagnosed.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** No dose
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Partly

Vitamins A, C and E, carotenoids and CoQ10 are all present in ordinary food, and each has its own food table elsewhere in this document. Food is not the concern here. The concern is supplemental doses taken alongside cytotoxic treatment.

- **To match the dose:** Not applicable, and deliberately so. Eating fruit and vegetables during chemotherapy is not the exposure that SWOG S0221 measured. That study looked at supplement use before and during treatment, at doses far above what food supplies.
- **Food vs supplement:** The gap between food and supplement is the whole point. A portion of berries and a 1,000 mg vitamin C capsule are not the same intervention. Nobody has suggested that dietary antioxidant intake during treatment is harmful, and no oncology body recommends restricting fruit and vegetables. Any change to supplements during cancer treatment belongs with the treating oncologist.
- **Absorption:** NONE. This entry is a product category rather than a single substance, so there is no single absorption comparison to make.
- **Does the body make it:** The label has to be PARTIAL, because the class as scoped here (vitamins A, C and E, carotenoids, CoQ10) contains nutrients the body cannot make at all: humans carry a non-functional GULO (L-gulonolactone oxidase) pseudogene and synthesise zero ascorbate (PMID 8175804), and alpha-tocopherol is entirely dietary, while CoQ10 is fully endogenous, which is the split inside the category. The enzymatic arm is endogenous but nutrient-dependent: every glutathione peroxidase and thioredoxin reductase is a selenoprotein requiring dietary selenium, and glutathione regeneration needs NADPH supplied through G6PD and the pentose phosphate pathway, on top of the substantial system the body runs by itself (millimolar intracellular glutathione, superoxide dismutases 1 to 3, catalase, glutathione peroxidases, peroxiredoxins and thioredoxins). For supplementing this means the endogenous defences are already running during treatment, so the default during active cytotoxic therapy is to hold high-dose antioxidant supplements unless a documented deficiency or the oncology team says otherwise.
- **Pathway:** Glutathione: glutamate + cysteine to gamma-glutamylcysteine by GCLC/GCLM, then GSH by glutathione synthetase, in the cytosol of all cells with liver as the main exporter; regenerated by glutathione reductase using NADPH from the pentose phosphate pathway, with G6PD as the entry enzyme, so G6PD deficiency limits the regenerating arm. Enzymatic arm: SOD1/SOD2/SOD3, CAT, GPX1-8, PRDX and TXN systems, with the glutathione peroxidases and thioredoxin reductases dependent on dietary selenium. NOT APPLICABLE for the dietary members of the class: there is no human pathway to ascorbate (GULO pseudogene), to alpha-tocopherol or to carotenoids.
- **Amount made:** Whole-blood glutathione synthesis 747 +/- 216 micromol/L/day with a fractional synthesis rate of 0.65 +/- 0.13 per day and whole-blood GSH of 1,142 +/- 243 micromol/L in 7 healthy adult men on an adequate diet, falling to 579 +/- 135 micromol/L/day on a sulfur amino acid free diet. Two limits on reading that figure: it covers only the glutathione arm, and the dietary members of the class have a synthesis rate of 0 mg/day, so no single number describes the category.
- **Still matters when:** Documented deficiency of the dietary antioxidant vitamins (vitamin C in scurvy, vitamin E in fat malabsorption or abetalipoproteinaemia), documented selenium deficiency affecting the selenoprotein arm, and supportive care specifically ordered by the treating oncology team. Otherwise the default during active cytotoxic therapy is to hold high-dose antioxidant supplements. In SWOG S0221 (self-reported supplement use observed within a randomised trial, so not randomised evidence), use of any antioxidant supplement (vitamins A, C and E, carotenoids, CoQ10) both before and during treatment was associated with a higher recurrence rate, adjusted HR 1.41, 95% CI 0.98 to 2.04.

*Refs. Ambrosone SWOG S0221 JCO PMID 31855498; SELECT PMID 21990298; Cochrane PMID 22419320*

### Probiotics in critical illness

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** Not applicable. The relevant ceiling is a population restriction rather than a milligram figure.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody in critical illness. See the strain-specific entry for the two indications where probiotics do work.

**Pros**

- None in this population. In non-critically-ill patients there are two narrow indications with real evidence, covered separately.

**Cons**

- PROPATRIA is the most important negative trial in this entire field. In 296 patients with predicted severe acute pancreatitis given a multispecies synbiotic enterally twice daily for 28 days: mortality 24 of 152 (16%) versus 9 of 144 (6%), RR 2.53 (1.22-5.25). Infectious complications were not reduced (RR 1.06).
- Nine cases of bowel ischaemia occurred, eight of them fatal, versus zero on placebo. The plausible mechanism is that fermentable substrate plus bacteria in a gut with compromised splanchnic perfusion increases local oxygen demand.
- The FDA warned providers after a preterm infant died of probiotic-associated Bifidobacterium sepsis, and cases of severe or fatal infection in premature infants receiving probiotics are documented.
- Saccharomyces boulardii fungaemia is documented in ICU patients with central venous catheters, which is the dominant risk factor.
- Absolute contraindications: predicted severe acute pancreatitis, multi-organ failure, central venous catheter, neutropenia, severe immunocompromise, and preterm infants outside a supervised NICU protocol.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Giving live bacteria to intensive care patients; large trials show no benefit and real harm.

- **If you lack it:** No deficiency state exists
- **In detail:** The rationale was that critical illness collapses gut microbial diversity and promotes translocation of pathogens across a compromised barrier, so restoring commensals might prevent ventilator-associated pneumonia and sepsis. PROSPECT randomised 2650 mechanically ventilated ICU patients across 44 units to Lacticaseibacillus rhamnosus GG or placebo and found ventilator-associated pneumonia in 21.9% versus 21.3% (hazard ratio 1.03, 95% CI 0.87 to 1.22), with no benefit on any secondary outcome. PROPATRIA, in 298 patients with predicted severe acute pancreatitis, found mortality of 16% on a multispecies probiotic against 6% on placebo (relative risk 2.53, 95% CI 1.22 to 5.25), with nine cases of bowel ischaemia in the probiotic arm and none on placebo, eight of them fatal; that trial has carried an Expression of Concern from The Lancet since March 2010, issued after a Dutch investigation by the IGZ, CCMO and VWA into its design, approval and conduct, and it has not been retracted. Probiotic bacteraemia and Saccharomyces fungaemia are separately documented in patients with central venous catheters or a breached gut barrier, which is why this is a setting where a live organism behaves as a hazard rather than a supplement.
- *There is no deficiency state for this. It is not an essential nutrient, so being short of it is not a thing that can happen.*

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** No dose
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** n/a

Live microorganisms are abundant in fermented foods, and the counts are given in the probiotics entry. That is not the relevant question for this item, because the population is patients in intensive care.

- **To match the dose:** Not applicable, and the direction is reversed. There is no food target to reach. The same caution that applies to probiotic capsules applies to fermented foods in these patients, and enteral feeding decisions in intensive care are made by the clinical team.
- **Food vs supplement:** Fermented foods are a reasonable habit for healthy people and are not a substitute for a named strain at a defined dose. In critical illness, compromised gut perfusion and mucosal barrier injury are what make live organisms hazardous, and that hazard does not care whether the organism arrived in a capsule or in a spoonful of yoghurt.
- **Absorption:** NONE. This entry is a product category rather than a single substance, so there is no single absorption comparison to make.
- **Does the body make it:** These are living organisms, so the synthesis axis does not apply, and in the ICU the argument runs the other way entirely. The largest randomised trial found no reduction in ventilator-associated pneumonia with Lactobacillus rhamnosus GG, and genomic work traced bloodstream isolates in critically ill patients back to the probiotic capsules they had been given. A compromised gut barrier converts a harmless commensal into an exposure, so the absence of endogenous synthesis is no argument for adding one.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE. Microorganisms are acquired, not synthesised.
- **Amount made:** 0 (human cells cannot synthesise a microorganism)
- **Still matters when:** Not on this axis, and arguably not at all outside a trial protocol in critical illness: probiotic-strain bacteraemia has been genomically confirmed in ICU patients, and the largest RCT showed no benefit for ventilator-associated pneumonia. Central lines, mucosal injury and immunosuppression are the reasons to withhold rather than to dose.

*Refs. PROPATRIA Besselink Lancet PMID 18279948; NCCIH; case report PMID 41476631*

### Calcium supplements on top of an adequate diet

**Verdict:** Avoid &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** The trials used at least 500 mg/d elemental calcium. Adult reference intake is 1,000-1,200 mg/d TOTAL from all sources.
- **Who it is for:** People with genuinely low dietary calcium, below about 600-700 mg/d, particularly institutionalised elderly, and patients on long-term glucocorticoids.

**Pros**

- With vitamin D in frail, calcium-and-D-deplete institutionalised elderly, it cuts hip fracture: RR 0.84 (0.74-0.96) across 9 trials and 49,853 people. That population is real and the effect is real.
- Correcting a genuine dietary shortfall is sensible, and food sources are preferable where possible.

**Cons**

- It does very little for fractures in ordinary community-dwelling adults. WHI, the largest trial ever run (n=36,282 postmenopausal women, 1,000 mg calcium plus 400 IU vitamin D, 7 years) gained 1.06% hip bone density but produced hip fracture HR 0.88 (0.72-1.08) and spine 0.90 (0.74-1.10). Both null.
- There is a cardiovascular harm signal. Bolland pooled 15 placebo-controlled trials of calcium at least 500 mg/d without vitamin D: patient-level myocardial infarction HR 1.31 (1.02-1.67, P=0.035) across 5 studies and 8,151 participants. A separate WHI reanalysis plus updated meta-analysis gave myocardial infarction RR 1.24 (1.07-1.45, P=0.004).
- Kidney stones increased significantly in WHI, HR 1.17 (1.02-1.34). This is the clearest confirmed harm in that trial.
- Supplementing on top of adequate dietary intake gets you the possible cardiovascular downside and no bone upside. Supplement the shortfall only.
- Absorption falls above about 500 mg per dose, so split doses. Chelates levothyroxine, bisphosphonates, tetracyclines and fluoroquinolones. Avoid in hypercalcaemia, primary hyperparathyroidism, sarcoidosis and a history of calcium-containing stones.

**What it does, and what happens without it**

Builds bone mineral and carries the signals for muscle contraction, nerve firing and clotting.

- **If you lack it:** Named deficiency disease
- **Reversible:** Partly reversible
- **In detail:** About 99% of body calcium is hydroxyapatite crystal in bone and teeth, serving a structural role. The remaining 1% is the cell's universal second messenger: it triggers excitation-contraction coupling in muscle, neurotransmitter release at synapses, and several steps of the coagulation cascade. Serum ionised calcium is defended within a very narrow range by parathyroid hormone, calcitriol and bone resorption, so a low dietary intake does not lower blood calcium. It is paid for out of the skeleton instead. That regulation is the key to this particular entry. Fractional absorption falls as intake rises, so in a person already meeting requirements from food, additional supplemental calcium contributes little to bone mineral and the surplus is largely excreted. What a supplement does add that food does not is a large absorptive bolus, and that is the context for the constipation, the increased kidney stone risk seen in the Women's Health Initiative, and the contested cardiovascular signal.
- **Deficiency disease:** Calcium-deficiency rickets in children and osteomalacia in adults, with nutritional secondary hyperparathyroidism. This is a disease of genuinely low intake and has no bearing on whether someone eating an adequate diet adds a supplement.
- **First signs:** Nothing a person notices. Biochemically, parathyroid hormone rises while serum calcium stays normal, urinary calcium falls, and bone turnover markers climb. The skeleton is being drawn on silently, which is why the early phase is only ever detected on testing done for another reason.
- **Untreated:** In children, rickets with bowed legs, widened wrists and costochondral junctions, bone pain and delayed growth. In adults, osteomalacia with diffuse bone pain, proximal muscle weakness and pseudofractures, and over decades accelerated bone loss with increased fragility fracture risk. Symptomatic hypocalcaemia with tetany and paraesthesiae comes from parathyroid or vitamin D failure rather than from dietary shortfall alone, because the skeleton buffers intake so effectively.
- **Does it reverse:** Rickets and osteomalacia heal with calcium repletion (plus vitamin D where that is also deficient), and bone mineral density largely restores. Two things do not fully recover: skeletal deformity already established in a growing child, along with the height lost to it, and peak bone mass never accrued during adolescence, which sets the lifetime baseline and cannot be rebuilt later by supplementing.
- **Who becomes deficient:** Children on low-calcium, high-phytate diets in parts of sub-Saharan Africa and South Asia, where dietary intakes around 200 mg/day have been documented. Adults with no dairy and no fortified alternatives. Malabsorption, post-gastrectomy states, long-term corticosteroid use, and untreated coeliac disease. The population this entry describes, someone whose diet already meets requirements, is not at risk by definition, which is the whole point.
- **How common:** NOT ESTABLISHED as a global figure for calcium-specific deficiency, because it is difficult to separate from vitamin D deficiency in survey data. Regionally it is well documented: median dietary calcium intake in Nigerian children with nutritional rickets was about 203 mg/day, against 196 mg/day in controls (P=0.64), on a requirement of roughly 700 to 1,300 mg/day depending on age. Calcium alone outperformed vitamin D alone in treating them, with 61% on calcium and 58% on the combination reaching the combined endpoint against 19% on vitamin D alone.

**Food and the body**

- **In food:** Rich
- **Dose from food:** Reachable
- **Food data confidence:** High
- **Body makes it:** Cannot

Calcium is among the easiest nutrients to get from ordinary food. Dairy, calcium-set tofu, hard cheeses, tinned fish eaten with the bones, whole small fish, fortified plant milks and fortified juice each deliver 200 to 860 mg per normal portion, and low-oxalate brassicas add 150 to 270 mg per cooked cup with fractional absorption in the same band as milk. In the UK, non-wholemeal flour is fortified with calcium carbonate by law, so bread is one of the largest single contributors to national intake at about 50 mg per slice. The adult reference intake of 1,000 mg/day (1,200 mg for women 51+, Institute of Medicine 2011) is reachable in three or four food choices, and this entry concerns adding a supplement to a diet that already meets that target, which is a different question from correcting a real deficit.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Tofu, raw, firm, prepared with calcium sulfate | 683 mg calcium | 1/2 cup (126 g) | 861 mg | 86% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172475, including the 0.5 cup = 126 g portion. Absorption 39-49% at a 200-250 mg load |
| Whitebait, coated in flour, fried (whole small fish, bones included) | 860 mg calcium | 80 g starter portion | 688 mg | 69% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | CoFID 16-231. Added on verification as the whole-small-fish archetype, richer than either canned fish below. The 80 g portion is a conventional serving weight rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Instant hot oat cereal, fortified, dry | 1,336 mg calcium | 30 g sachet | 401 mg | 40% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | CoFID 11-792. Added on verification. All of it is added mineral. The 30 g sachet is a conventional weight rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Dried skimmed milk, fortified | 1,280 mg calcium | 30 g powder, about a 250 ml glass reconstituted | 384 mg | 38% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | CoFID 12-521. Added on verification. The 30 g powder weight is a conventional reconstitution rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Parmesan and other hard cheeses | 1,184 mg calcium | 30 g piece or grated over a dish | 355 mg | 36% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170848; CoFID 12-526 Parmesan gives 1,025 mg/100 g, Emmental 1,025 (12-493) and Gruyere 950 (12-164). Added on verification: a 30 g piece of Parmesan beats the 1 oz Cheddar slice below by 156 mg, and hard cheeses were the largest omission on the original list |
| Sardines, Atlantic, canned in oil, drained solids with bone | 382 mg calcium | 1 small can (3.75 oz, 92 g) | 351 mg | 35% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175139, including the 92 g can; CoFID 16-440 gives 500 mg/100 g for sardines in olive oil. The bones are the calcium, so boneless fillets do not count. The nearest CoFID contrast, 16-438 at 63 mg/100 g, is grilled sardine flesh rather than canned, so that comparison spans a change of preparation as well as the removal of bones |
| Calcium-fortified orange juice | 140 mg calcium | 1 cup (249 g) | 349 mg | 35% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169920 ('with added calcium'; no added vitamin D in this entry). Unfortified chilled orange juice is 11 mg/100 g (FDC 169100), so the entire figure is added mineral |
| Yogurt, plain, low fat | 183 mg calcium | 1 pot (6 oz, 170 g) | 311 mg | 31% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170886, including the 170 g container; CoFID 12-184 whole milk plain yogurt gives 200 mg/100 g. Fractional absorption 24.2% at a 523 mg load |
| Fortified soy drink (with added calcium, vitamins A and D) | 123 mg calcium | 1 cup (243 g) | 299 mg | 30% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172456; CoFID 12-523 gives 130 mg/100 g. Unfortified soy drink is 25 mg/100 g (FDC 172446), so all of it is added. Calcium-carbonate-fortified soymilk absorbs as well as cow milk (21.1% against 21.7%, Zhao et al. 2005), so the fortificant is not the weak point; the sedimentation problem is |
| Milk, whole, 3.25% fat, with added vitamin D | 113 mg calcium | 1 cup / 240 ml (244 g) | 276 mg | 28% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171265; CoFID 12-596 gives 120 mg/100 g. Fractional absorption across the cited table spans 21.7-66.0% depending on calcium load (14.7 to 563 mg), typically around 30-32% at a 250 mg load |
| Collard greens, boiled, drained | 141 mg calcium | 1 cup chopped (190 g) | 268 mg | 27% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170407. Low oxalate, so absorption is in the brassica range of roughly 48-53% |
| Tofu, firm, prepared with calcium sulfate and magnesium chloride (nigari) | 201 mg calcium | 1/2 cup (126 g) | 253 mg | 25% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172448. The original submission described this as nigari-set 'rather than' calcium sulfate; USDA's own description is a dual-coagulant product using calcium sulfate and magnesium chloride together, so it cannot carry that contrast. The coagulant argument rests instead on the calcium-sulfate firm tofu above at 683 mg/100 g against MORI-NU silken extra firm at 31 mg/100 g (FDC 172462, a single branded product). The same food spans a twenty-fold range and the label has to be checked |
| Salmon, pink, canned, drained solids (bones included) | 283 mg calcium | 3 oz (85 g) | 241 mg | 24% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175175. The same fish with skin and bones removed drops to 60 mg/100 g (FDC 173724), a 4.7-fold penalty for picking the bones out. NIH ODS quotes a different canned-salmon entry at 181 mg per 3 oz, so 283 mg/100 g is specific to the ID cited |
| Jack mackerel, canned, drained solids with bones | 241 mg calcium | 3 oz (85 g) | 205 mg | 21% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175121. Added on verification. The 85 g weight is the standard 3 oz serving used for the canned salmon row above |
| Cheddar cheese | 710 mg calcium | 1 slice (1 oz, 28 g) | 199 mg | 20% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173414, including the 28 g slice; CoFID 12-346 English Cheddar gives 739 mg/100 g. Fractional absorption 37.4% at a 498 mg load |
| Turnip greens, boiled, drained | 137 mg calcium | 1 cup chopped (144 g) | 197 mg | 20% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170466 |
| Kale, boiled, drained | 150 mg calcium | 1 cup cooked (118 g) | 177 mg | 18% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169238; CoFID 13-649 curly kale boiled gives 231 mg/100 g. Caveat added on verification: NIH ODS and newer FNDDS-based entries put cooked kale nearer 94 mg per cup, so this SR Legacy figure is the high end of current data. Fractional absorption 40.9% at a 288 mg load and 52.7% at an 83 mg load |
| Bok choy / pak choi, boiled, drained | 93 mg calcium | 1 cup shredded (170 g) | 158 mg | 16% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170391. The original claim that this is the best-absorbed calcium food is withdrawn: in Heaney et al. (J Food Sci 1993;58:1378-1380) at an identical 83 mg load, bok choy stems were 51.9% and leaves 52.0% alongside kale 52.7%, broccoli 47.8% and milk 46.3%, and the authors reported the differences as absolutely small and statistically insignificant and declined to rank them |
| Almonds, whole raw kernels | 269 mg calcium | 1 oz, about 23 kernels (28.35 g) | 76 mg | 8% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170567. Added on verification, which noted almonds appeared only under magnesium despite being a legitimate calcium source |
| Tahini (sesame paste) from roasted and toasted kernels | 426 mg calcium | 1 tbsp (15 g) | 64 mg | 6% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170189; CoFID 14-847 gives 680 mg/100 g. Whole sesame seeds are 670 mg/100 g (CoFID 14-844), but a tablespoon is only 8-15 g, so the portion does the work |
| Bread, white, average (UK, flour fortified with calcium carbonate by law) | 155 mg calcium | 1 slice (32 g) | 50 mg | 5% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | CoFID 11-1145; CoFID wholemeal 11-981 is 106 mg/100 g, about 34 mg per slice, so here the white loaf beats the wholemeal one because the fortification applies to non-wholemeal flour. Added on verification: bread is one of the largest contributors to UK calcium intake and was absent from a list that otherwise uses CoFID throughout. Four slices a day is about 200 mg with no thought given to it |
| Spinach, boiled, drained (the answer that should not be given) | 136 mg calcium | 1 cup cooked (180 g) | 245 mg on the label, roughly 12 mg actually absorbed | 25% of the reference intake nominally, about 1% in reality | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168463 for composition; fractional absorption 5.1% at a 200 mg load from the Heaney and Weaver spinach work, tabulated in the Nutrients 2022 review of calcium absorption and food matrix effects (PMC8746734, PMID 35011055). Oxalate precipitates the calcium in the gut, so a cup of spinach delivers about the calcium of a tablespoon of milk. Placed last because the list is ordered by what a portion actually delivers, and on that basis this row belongs at the bottom despite a mid-table label figure |

- **To match the dose:** Calcium supplements are sold at 500 to 600 mg elemental per tablet, and the trials that generated the safety signals gave 1,000 to 1,200 mg/day on top of whatever participants were already eating. Matching 1,000 mg from food takes 3.6 cups of milk, or 141 g of Cheddar, or about 85 g of Parmesan, or one 1/2-cup serving of calcium-set tofu (861 mg) plus one cup of cooked kale (177 mg) for 1,038 mg, or 3.2 pots of low-fat yogurt (about 546 g), or two small cans of sardines plus a glass of fortified soy drink (1,001 mg). The 'three pots of yogurt' route quoted in the original submission is wrong: 3 x 311 mg is 933 mg, 93% of the target and short of it. Every one of the corrected routes is a normal day of eating, so in a person whose diet already provides 1,000 mg a 1,000 mg supplement does not fill a deficit, it doubles the intake and delivers the added half as a single soluble bolus. The supplement is additive rather than corrective, which is the whole point of this entry.
- **Food vs supplement:** The harms recorded in the trials attach to supplements, not to dietary calcium, and the proposed mechanism is the form itself. Food calcium arrives in small amounts inside a matrix across a whole meal and produces no sharp rise in serum calcium; a 500-1,000 mg dose of calcium carbonate or citrate produces a measurable postprandial spike, and that spike is the hypothesised driver of the vascular signal. The specific findings: in the Women's Health Initiative randomised trial, 36,282 postmenopausal women given 1,000 mg calcium carbonate plus 400 IU vitamin D3 daily for a mean 7 years had more clinically diagnosed urinary tract stones, hazard ratio 1.17 (95% CI 1.02-1.34), unrelated to baseline calcium intake (PMID 21525191). Bolland and colleagues' meta-analysis of calcium supplements without co-administered vitamin D reported a 27-31% relative increase in myocardial infarction (BMJ 2010, DOI 10.1136/bmj.c3691). In the 10-year MESA follow-up of 5,448 adults, supplement users had higher incident coronary artery calcification while the highest quintile of dietary calcium was associated with lower risk (Anderson et al., DOI 10.1161/JAHA.116.003815). Observational work in the same populations consistently finds higher dietary calcium associated with fewer kidney stones, the opposite direction to the supplement result. This is contested territory: a 2016 National Osteoporosis Foundation and American Society for Preventive Cardiology review concluded there was no demonstrable cardiovascular harm at total intakes up to 2,000-2,500 mg/day, and the meta-analyses have been criticised for relying on secondary endpoints and self-reported events. The defensible summary is that food calcium has no such signal attached to it in any dataset, so where the diet already reaches the target the supplement carries risk without a corresponding benefit. Two practical form points: calcium carbonate requires gastric acid and must be taken with food, so it is a poor choice for anyone on a proton pump inhibitor or with achlorhydria (calcium citrate does not have this dependency), and doses above about 500 mg saturate absorption, so a single 1,000 mg tablet is worse value than two 500 mg doses.
- **Absorption:** Fractional absorption reorders the raw milligram ranking, though less dramatically than the original submission claimed. From the tabulated studies (Nutrients 2022 review, PMC8746734; Heaney et al., J Food Sci 1993;58:1378-1380), at an identical 83 mg load: kale 52.7%, bok choy leaves 52.0%, bok choy stems 51.9%, broccoli 47.8% and cow milk 46.3%, with the authors stating the differences were absolutely small and statistically insignificant. No vegetable can be called the best-absorbed calcium food, and the superlative attached to bok choy in the original entry is refuted by the experiment it cited. Across loads of 14.7 to 563 mg the milk range is 21.7-66.0%, about 30-32% at a typical 250 mg load, so milk's upper bound exceeds every brassica figure and the claim that brassicas beat milk holds only at matched loads where the difference is not significant. Calcium-set tofu is 39.0-49.3%, Cheddar 37.4%, yogurt 24.2%, calcium carbonate supplement roughly 22-24%, calcium citrate roughly 24%, and spinach 5.1%. Two conclusions survive. Low-oxalate brassicas comfortably beat the supplement forms per milligram consumed and match milk at comparable loads, so a cup of bok choy at 158 mg delivers about as much absorbed calcium as 250 ml of milk (roughly 82 mg against 83 mg). And spinach, rhubarb, beet greens and Swiss chard should be struck off calcium lists entirely, since oxalate precipitates the calcium in the gut and the food is close to useless for this purpose despite good label numbers. On fortified plant milks, the carbonate fortificant performs as well as dairy calcium: Zhao, Martin and Weaver (J Nutr 2005;135(10):2379, PMID 16177199) titled their paper on the finding that calcium-carbonate-fortified soymilk is equivalent to cow milk in young women (21.1% against 21.7% at a 250 mg load), and only the tricalcium phosphate form is genuinely lower at 18.1%. The sedimentation problem is real and separate: the added salt is insoluble and settles to the bottom of the carton, so an unshaken carton delivers a fraction of the label value and the last glass delivers a dose nobody intended.
- **Does the body make it:** Calcium is chemical element 20, so none of the 1000 to 1200 g in an adult skeleton is manufactured and all of it is dietary in origin. The body defends blood calcium hard through PTH, calcitriol and bone turnover, and part of that defence is throttling absorption, since fractional intestinal absorption falls as intake rises, so a supplement added to an already adequate intake is absorbed inefficiently and largely excreted. That raised urinary calcium is the mechanism behind the excess kidney stones in the Women's Health Initiative calcium plus vitamin D trial (hazard ratio 1.17, 95% CI 1.02 to 1.34), so on an adequate diet the net account is a limited skeletal gain set against a real stone risk.
- **Pathway:** NOT APPLICABLE (chemical element). Handling only: TRPV6-mediated and paracellular intestinal absorption under calcitriol control (renal CYP27B1), with cytosolic shuttling by calbindin-D9k and basolateral export by NCX1 and PMCA1b, bone remodelling as the reservoir, and TRPV5-mediated renal reabsorption, all governed by PTH, the calcium-sensing receptor and FGF23.
- **Amount made:** 0 mg/d synthesised. Reference points: adult skeletal content 1000-1200 g; RDA 1000 mg/d for adults 19-50 and men to 70, 1200 mg/d for women 51 and over and men 71 and over; UL 2000-2500 mg/d depending on age.
- **Still matters when:** Justified when intake is genuinely short or handling is broken: habitual intake below the EAR (common in older women and dairy avoiders), osteoporosis pharmacotherapy that presumes a calcium and vitamin D floor, hypoparathyroidism, malabsorption, chronic corticosteroid therapy, chronic kidney disease under specialist direction. On an adequate diet the marginal supplement adds little and carries stone risk, so food first is the better route; where a supplement is genuinely needed, the benefit in the WHI adherent-population analysis appeared at 1000 mg/d elemental calcium plus 400 IU vitamin D3 (hip BMD +1.06%, P<0.01; hip fracture hazard ratio 0.71, 95% CI 0.52 to 0.97).

*Refs. WHI Jackson NEJM PMID 16481635; Bolland BMJ 2010 PMID 20671013; Bolland BMJ 2011 PMID 21505219; Cochrane PMID 24729336*

## 7. Doses and upper limits

| Nutrient | Reference intake | US upper limit | EU upper limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| Vitamin D | RDA 600 IU (800 IU age 71+); supplement 800-2,000 IU/d | 4,000 IU (100 µg) | 4,000 IU (100 µg) |
| Vitamin A (preformed) | 900 µg RAE men / 700 µg women | 3,000 µg (10,000 IU) | see 2024 opinion |
| Vitamin E | RDA 15 mg | 1,000 mg supplemental | **300 mg, all sources** |
| Vitamin C | 90 mg men / 75 mg women (+35 smokers) | 2,000 mg | none set (SCF 2004) |
| Vitamin K | AI 120 µg men / 90 µg women (IOM); 70 µg (EFSA) | none set | none set |
| Folate | 400 µg DFE (600 µg pregnancy) | 1,000 µg **synthetic** | 1,000 µg |
| Vitamin B12 | 2.4 µg | none (not determinable) | none |
| Vitamin B6 | 1.3 mg (1.7 men / 1.5 women over 50) | **100 mg** | **12 mg** |
| Niacin | 16 mg NE | 35 mg synthetic | 900 mg nicotinamide |
| Calcium | 1,000-1,200 mg total | 2,500 mg (2,000 mg over 51) | 2,500 mg |
| Magnesium | 310-420 mg total | **350 mg supplemental only** | **250 mg supplemental** |
| Iron | 8 mg men / 18 mg women 19-50 / 27 mg pregnancy | 45 mg | no UL; safe level 40 mg |
| Zinc | 11 mg men / 8 mg women | 40 mg elemental | 25 mg |
| Selenium | 55 µg | **400 µg** | **255 µg** |
| Iodine | 150 µg (220 pregnancy, 290 lactation) | 1,100 µg | 600 µg |
| Potassium | AI 3,400 mg men / 2,600 mg women | none set | none set |
| Omega-3 EPA+DHA | 2-4 g/d for triglycerides | none | 5 g/d combined; DHA alone 1 g/d |
| Caffeine | 3-6 mg/kg pre-exercise | not applicable | 400 mg/d; **200 mg/d pregnancy** |
| Creatine | 3-5 g/d | none set | none set |
| Protein | 1.6 g/kg/d plateau; RDA 0.8 g/kg | none set | none set |
| Fibre | AI 25-38 g/d | none set | none set |

## 8. Drug interactions

One row per drug and supplement pair, each stating who does what to whom. Use it to recognise a problem, not to solve it alone: take the bottle to a pharmacist rather than stopping a prescribed medicine.

### Do not combine

| Drug | Supplement | Direction | What happens | What to do |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **ACE inhibitors, ARBs and potassium-sparing diuretics** (ramipril, lisinopril, losartan, candesartan, spironolactone, eplerenone, amiloride) | Potassium supplements and potassium-based salt substitutes (LoSalt and similar) | Combination raises risk of harm | Blood potassium rises, which can cause muscle weakness, palpitations, and a dangerous heart rhythm with no warning symptoms beforehand. | Do not take potassium supplements or potassium-containing salt substitutes with these medicines unless a doctor has specifically prescribed them and is checking your potassium. Ask what your eGFR and potassium were at the last check, because risk is highest with reduced kidney function or older age. Get urgent help for new muscle weakness or palpitations. |
| **ACE inhibitors, ARBs and potassium-sparing diuretics** (ramipril, lisinopril, losartan, candesartan, spironolactone, eplerenone, amiloride) | Potassium supplements, potassium-based salt substitutes (LoSalt and similar), and potassium-containing electrolyte, rehydration or sports powders and coconut water concentrates | Combination raises risk of harm | Blood potassium rises, which can cause muscle weakness, palpitations, and a dangerous heart rhythm with no warning symptoms beforehand. | Do not take potassium supplements or potassium-containing salt substitutes with these medicines unless a doctor has specifically prescribed them and is checking your potassium. Get urgent help for new muscle weakness or palpitations. |
| **Anticonvulsants** (carbamazepine, lamotrigine, phenytoin) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | Drug levels can fall and seizure control can be lost; equally, stopping St John's wort after months can push levels up again. | Do not start St John's wort if you take an anticonvulsant. If you already take it, do not stop abruptly either: tell the epilepsy team, ask for a drug level 2 to 4 weeks around any change, and do not adjust the anticonvulsant yourself. |
| **Antiretrovirals** (protease inhibitors (darunavir, atazanavir), NNRTIs (efavirenz, rilpivirine)) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | Drug levels fall sharply, the virus rebounds, and the virus can become resistant so that the whole regimen stops working permanently. | Never take St John's wort with HIV medicines. If you have taken any, tell the HIV clinic so a viral load can be checked; do not stop the antiretrovirals. |
| **Antiretrovirals (raltegravir)** (raltegravir 400 mg twice daily, raltegravir 1200 mg once daily) | Aluminium and/or magnesium containing antacids | Supplement blocks the drug | Raltegravir exposure falls enough to risk viral rebound and resistance, and spacing the doses 2 hours apart does not fix it. | Do not use aluminium or magnesium containing antacids with raltegravir at all, even spaced apart, and ask the clinic for a different acid treatment. A calcium carbonate antacid may be taken with raltegravir 400 mg twice daily with no dose adjustment, but that exception does not extend to the 1200 mg once-daily regimen. |
| **Benzodiazepines and Z-drugs** (diazepam, lorazepam, alprazolam, temazepam, zopiclone, zolpidem) | Kava (Piper methysticum) | Supplement strengthens the drug | Heavy sedation, disorientation and loss of coordination; one published case progressed to coma. Kava separately causes serious liver injury. | Do not take kava with diazepam, lorazepam, zopiclone or zolpidem. Kava is restricted or banned in several countries because of liver failure cases, so do not use it at all without medical advice. |
| **Calcineurin inhibitors** (ciclosporin, tacrolimus) | Berberine | Supplement strengthens the drug | Drug levels climb, bringing tremor, headache, high blood pressure and kidney damage (rising creatinine on blood tests). The graft is at risk from toxicity in the same way it is from under-dosing. | Do not add berberine without the transplant team agreeing first. If it has already been started, ask for a trough level and kidney function test within one week. |
| **Calcineurin inhibitors** (ciclosporin, tacrolimus) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | Trough drug levels fall by roughly half within about two weeks and the transplanted organ can be rejected. Rejection may show as fever, pain over the graft, less urine or rising creatinine. | Never take St John's wort after a transplant. If you have taken any, contact the transplant team the same day and ask for an urgent trough level; do not adjust your own dose. |
| **Combined oral contraceptives and progestogen-only pills** (ethinylestradiol with levonorgestrel or desogestrel, desogestrel POP) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | Breakthrough bleeding and, more importantly, contraceptive failure and unplanned pregnancy. | Do not rely on the pill while taking St John's wort. Use condoms as well, or switch to a method that is not affected such as a copper coil or the injection, for the whole time you take it and for 4 weeks after stopping. |
| **Cytotoxic chemotherapy** (cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin, cisplatin, paclitaxel, and concurrent radiotherapy) | High-dose antioxidants (vitamin C 1 g or more, vitamin E, beta-carotene, N-acetylcysteine) | Supplement weakens the drug | Treatment may work less well: the tumour is less well controlled and cancer is more likely to come back. Nothing feels different at the time. | Do not take antioxidant supplements above the normal daily requirement during chemotherapy or radiotherapy. Bring every bottle to the oncology team. There is no established safe washout schedule between cycles, so ask them what to do rather than assuming a gap between cycles makes it safe. |
| **Cytotoxic chemotherapy and oral anticancer drugs** (irinotecan, docetaxel, imatinib, etoposide) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | Active drug levels drop, by roughly 40 percent for irinotecan and imatinib and less for docetaxel, so the treatment is effectively underdosed and the cancer is less well controlled. | Stop St John's wort at least 2 weeks before chemotherapy starts and tell the oncology team you were taking it, because the planned dose may need reviewing. |
| **Direct oral anticoagulants** (apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban, dabigatran) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | Drug levels fall and the protection against stroke or clot is lost silently, because there is no routine blood test for these drugs. | Do not take St John's wort with a DOAC. Ask the prescriber for a different treatment for low mood, and tell them if you have already been taking it. |
| **High-dose antifolate chemotherapy** (high-dose methotrexate (oncology dosing), pralatrexate) | Self-supplemented folic acid or folinic acid (including in multivitamins) | Supplement weakens the drug | The antifolate effect the treatment depends on is blunted, so the cancer is less well controlled. Nothing feels different at the time. | Do not self-supplement folic or folinic acid during high-dose antifolate chemotherapy, and hand over any multivitamin for review. The opposite rule applies to low-dose weekly methotrexate for rheumatoid arthritis or psoriasis, where folic acid is deliberately prescribed to reduce side effects: follow the prescription there, and never stop prescribed folate on the basis of this row. |
| **Immunosuppressants** (ciclosporin, tacrolimus, mycophenolate, azathioprine, prednisolone) | Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera) | Supplement weakens the drug | In theory the immune suppression the transplant or autoimmune treatment depends on is opposed, risking rejection or a disease flare. Nothing is felt until that happens. | Do not take ashwagandha if you are on immunosuppressants after a transplant or for an autoimmune disease, and tell the team about any you have already taken. |
| **Irreversible non-selective MAOIs** (phenelzine, tranylcypromine, isocarboxazid) | Tyramine-generating and pressor supplements (fermented soy products, yeast extract, aged protein concentrates, broad bean pod extract; L-tyrosine as a precaution) | Combination raises risk of harm | Hypertensive crisis: sudden severe throbbing headache, neck stiffness, chest pain, racing heart, and risk of stroke. | Do not take supplements containing tyramine, fermented soy, yeast extract or broad bean pod extract while on one of these drugs, and for 2 weeks after stopping, because the enzyme block is irreversible.
