Treat L-tyrosine as precautionary and ask the prescriber. Call emergency services for a sudden severe headache with a pounding heartbeat. |
| **Levodopa given without a decarboxylase inhibitor** (plain levodopa (not co-careldopa or co-beneldopa)) | High-dose vitamin B6 (pyridoxine) | Supplement weakens the drug | Parkinson's symptoms return: more stiffness, slowness and tremor, because less levodopa reaches the brain. | Do not take high-dose B6 with plain levodopa. Check whether your product is a combination (co-careldopa, co-beneldopa), in which case this matters much less, and keep B6 to the amount in a standard multivitamin. High-dose B6 also causes peripheral neuropathy in its own right. |
| **Levodopa without a peripheral decarboxylase inhibitor** (plain levodopa (no carbidopa or benserazide)) | High-dose vitamin B6 (pyridoxine, 10 mg/day or more) | Supplement weakens the drug | Parkinson's control is lost within days: stiffness, slowness and tremor return, because more of the dose is destroyed before it reaches the brain. Modern combinations with carbidopa or benserazide are not affected, since they already block the same step. | Do not take vitamin B6 supplements above the normal daily requirement with plain levodopa. If you take co-careldopa or co-beneldopa this does not apply, but still tell the Parkinson's team what you are taking. High-dose B6 also causes peripheral neuropathy in its own right. |
| **MAOIs** (phenelzine, tranylcypromine, isocarboxazid, selegiline, moclobemide) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Combination raises risk of harm | Serotonin syndrome or a hypertensive reaction: sweating, tremor, confusion, high blood pressure, fast heartbeat. | Do not combine. Leave at least 2 weeks between stopping an irreversible MAOI and starting St John's wort, and have the prescriber confirm the gap for your specific drug. |
| **MAOIs (irreversible, non-selective)** (phenelzine, tranylcypromine, isocarboxazid) | Bitter orange (Citrus aurantium, synephrine) and high-caffeine pre-workout stimulant blends | Combination raises risk of harm | Hypertensive crisis: sudden severe throbbing headache, chest pain, racing heart and stroke risk. | Do not take bitter orange, synephrine or pre-workout stimulant blends while on an MAOI, or for 2 weeks after stopping an irreversible one. Call emergency services for a sudden severe headache with a pounding heartbeat. |
| **MAOIs (irreversible, non-selective)** (phenelzine, tranylcypromine, isocarboxazid) | Broad bean (fava) pod extract, sold as a natural levodopa or dopamine source | Combination raises risk of harm | Blood pressure surges with severe headache, flushing and palpitations, sometimes with nausea, agitation or confusion. | Do not take broad bean or fava pod extract while on an MAOI, or for 2 weeks after stopping an irreversible one. Ordinary broad beans eaten as a vegetable are a much smaller load, but avoid large portions and any concentrated pod product. |
| **MAOIs (irreversible, non-selective)** (phenelzine, tranylcypromine, isocarboxazid, oral selegiline above 10 mg/day) | Preformed tyramine sources (yeast extract spreads and tablets, fermented soy such as miso, tempeh and soy sauce concentrates, aged protein concentrates) | Combination raises risk of harm | Hypertensive crisis: sudden severe throbbing headache, neck stiffness, chest pain, racing heart, and risk of stroke. | Avoid the named products by product type, because supplement labels never declare tyramine content: yeast extract spreads and tablets, fermented soy concentrates, and aged protein concentrates, while on the MAOI and for 2 weeks after stopping it. Call emergency services for a sudden severe headache with a pounding heartbeat. |
| **Narrow-margin drugs cleared by CYP3A4, CYP2C19 or P-glycoprotein (consolidated St John's wort row)** (methadone, clozapine, olanzapine, voriconazole, itraconazole, oral targeted oncology agents, plus every drug named in the St John's wort rows above) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | Any drug cleared by these routes loses effect within 1 to 2 weeks: methadone withdrawal, loss of antipsychotic or antifungal cover, or an underdosed cancer treatment. Stopping St John's wort is itself an interaction event, because levels climb again over the following 1 to 2 weeks and can reach toxicity. | Before starting or stopping St John's wort, list every prescription you take and have the prescriber or pharmacist check each one, not only the obvious ones. Expect a level check or a clinical review 1 to 2 weeks after either change for any narrow-margin drug. This is the row that resolves the apparent conflict between the do-not-stop-abruptly advice in the anticoagulant rows and the stop-2-weeks-before advice in the chemotherapy row: both follow from the same 1 to 2 week de-induction window. |
| **Oral retinoids** (isotretinoin, acitretin, alitretinoin) | High-dose vitamin A / retinol (including stacked multivitamins and cod liver oil) | Combination raises risk of harm | Additive vitamin A toxicity: headache with blurred or double vision from raised pressure around the brain, dry cracked lips and skin, hair loss, bone and joint pain, and abnormal liver blood tests. | Take no vitamin A or retinol supplement and no cod liver oil while on isotretinoin or acitretin, and check that no multivitamin you use contains retinol. Report headache with visual changes the same day. |
| **Oral retinoids** (isotretinoin, acitretin, alitretinoin) | Vitamin A (retinol or retinyl palmitate supplements, cod liver oil, high-dose multivitamins) | Combination raises risk of harm | Additive vitamin A toxicity: dry cracked lips and skin, nosebleeds, headache, joint and bone pain, hair loss, raised liver enzymes and raised triglycerides. Severe cases include raised intracranial pressure with headache and visual changes. | Do not take vitamin A supplements, cod liver oil or high-dose multivitamins containing retinol while on isotretinoin or acitretin, and check labels for retinol and retinyl palmitate. Report severe headache, visual changes or worsening bone and joint pain. |
| **Organic nitrates and nicorandil** (glyceryl trinitrate spray, isosorbide mononitrate, nicorandil) | Unlicensed herbal sexual-enhancement products (frequently adulterated with undeclared sildenafil or tadalafil) | Combination raises risk of harm | Profound and prolonged collapse of blood pressure: fainting, heart attack or death. The label gives no warning, because the drug in the product is undeclared. | Never take an unlicensed herbal erectile or sexual-performance product if you use any nitrate or nicorandil. If you want treatment for erectile dysfunction, ask the prescriber, who can check it against your nitrate. Call emergency services for collapse or chest pain after taking one. |
| **PDE5 inhibitors** (sildenafil, tadalafil, vardenafil, avanafil) | Yohimbine (and unlicensed herbal erectile products) | Combination raises risk of harm | Unpredictable blood pressure swings, racing heart, anxiety and tremor; in people with heart disease this can precipitate chest pain. | Do not take yohimbine or herbal erectile supplements with sildenafil or tadalafil. Assume unlicensed herbal sex supplements may already contain undeclared sildenafil, which makes an accidental double dose likely. |
| **Proteasome inhibitors** (bortezomib) | Green tea extract / EGCG | Supplement weakens the drug | The antitumour effect is blunted or abolished, with nothing noticeable to the patient at the time. | Do not take green tea extract or EGCG supplements while on bortezomib, and tell the oncology team about any you have been taking. |
| **SSRIs and other serotonergic drugs** (sertraline, fluoxetine, citalopram, venlafaxine, tramadol) | 5-HTP (5-hydroxytryptophan) | Combination raises risk of harm | Potential serotonin syndrome: restlessness, sweating, tremor, diarrhoea, fast heartbeat and confusion. | Do not take 5-HTP alongside an antidepressant. If you are already taking both, stop the 5-HTP and tell the prescriber; get urgent help if you develop shivering, twitching or confusion. |
| **SSRIs and other serotonergic drugs** (fluoxetine, sertraline, paroxetine, venlafaxine) | L-tryptophan | Combination raises risk of harm | Agitation, restlessness, nausea, sweating and poor sleep, up to full serotonin syndrome. | Do not add tryptophan to an antidepressant unless a specialist prescribes it and reviews you; buy nothing labelled as a sleep or mood amino acid without checking the ingredient list against your prescription. |
| **SSRIs and other serotonergic drugs** (sertraline, fluoxetine, citalopram, venlafaxine, duloxetine, tramadol, triptans) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Combination raises risk of harm | Serotonin syndrome: agitation, sweating, shivering, muscle twitching, diarrhoea, fast heartbeat and confusion, sometimes within hours of the first combined dose. | Never take the two together. If you want to switch in either direction, ask the prescriber to plan a washout gap first. Get urgent medical help for shivering, sweating, twitching muscles and confusion. |
| **Statins** (atorvastatin, simvastatin, rosuvastatin, pravastatin) | Red yeast rice (Monascus purpureus, monacolin K) | Combination raises risk of harm | Effectively a double statin dose: muscle pain and weakness, and rarely rhabdomyolysis with dark urine and kidney failure, plus liver injury. | Never take red yeast rice alongside a prescribed statin. Stop it and contact a doctor the same day if you get muscle pain or weakness, or dark cola-coloured urine. |
| **Tamoxifen** (tamoxifen) | Curcumin supplements (more marked with piperine) | Supplement weakens the drug | Levels of endoxifen, the active metabolite that does the anticancer work, fall, so the treatment is weaker with nothing noticeable at the time. | Do not take curcumin supplements while on tamoxifen, and bring any turmeric product to the oncology team for review. Cooking with turmeric is not the issue. |
| **Thyroid and thyroid-active drugs** (levothyroxine, carbimazole, propylthiouracil, amiodarone, lithium) | Iodine-containing supplements (kelp, bladderwrack, seaweed powders, high-dose potassium iodide, some multivitamins) | Combination raises risk of harm | Thyroid function swings in either direction: an iodine load can cause hypothyroidism (tiredness, weight gain, cold intolerance) or thyrotoxicosis (palpitations, weight loss, tremor), and it destabilises a levothyroxine or antithyroid dose that was previously steady. Risk is higher with amiodarone, which already delivers a large iodine load, and with lithium, which itself blocks thyroid hormone release. | Do not take kelp, seaweed or iodine supplements if you take levothyroxine, carbimazole, propylthiouracil, amiodarone or lithium. If you have already been taking them, stop and ask for a TSH and free T4 check; do not change the prescribed dose yourself. |
| **Thyroid drugs** (levothyroxine, carbimazole, propylthiouracil) | Iodine and kelp or seaweed supplements (including greens powders and multivitamins with iodine) | Combination raises risk of harm | Thyroid control is destabilised in either direction: a large iodine load can trigger overactivity (palpitations, tremor, weight loss) or block hormone output and cause underactivity, so dose titration stops working. | Do not take iodine or kelp supplements if you take levothyroxine, carbimazole or propylthiouracil. Check multivitamins, greens powders and seaweed products for iodine content, and ask for a thyroid function test if you have been taking any. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol) | Danshen (Salvia miltiorrhiza) and dong quai (Angelica sinensis), including Chinese herbal mixtures containing them | Supplement strengthens the drug | The INR rises sharply, in reported cases above 5, with bruising, nosebleeds or serious internal bleeding. Because these herbs are usually taken inside multi-herb prescriptions, the trigger is often not obvious to the patient. | Do not take danshen or dong quai, or Chinese herbal mixtures containing them, while on warfarin. If you have taken any, tell the anticoagulant clinic and ask for an INR check now, and bring the packaging or the prescription list so the ingredients can be identified. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol) | Danshen (Salvia miltiorrhiza) and dong quai (Angelica sinensis), including multi-herb Chinese formulations containing either | Supplement strengthens the drug | The INR climbs, sometimes markedly, with bruising, nosebleeds or serious bleeding. | Do not take danshen or dong quai with warfarin, and check the ingredient list of any Chinese herbal formulation, which often contains one of them. If you have taken either, ask for an INR check now. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol, phenindione) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | Within one to two weeks the INR drops and clot protection is lost. Stopping St John's wort later swings the INR the other way and can cause bleeding. | Do not start St John's wort while on warfarin. If you are already taking it, do not stop abruptly either; tell the anticoagulant clinic so the INR is checked before and after the change. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol) | Turmeric / curcumin supplements (as distinct from turmeric used as a cooking spice) | Supplement strengthens the drug | The INR rises, with bruising, nosebleeds or heavier periods. Curcumin products also carry a hepatotoxicity signal of their own. | Do not take turmeric or curcumin capsules while on warfarin. Turmeric used as a spice in cooking is not the problem. If you have been taking capsules, tell the anticoagulant clinic and ask for an INR check. |

### Only with monitoring

| Drug | Supplement | Direction | What happens | What to do |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **ACE inhibitors and ARBs** (ramipril, lisinopril, losartan, candesartan) | Liquorice root / glycyrrhizin (supplements, teas, liquorice confectionery) | Supplement weakens the drug | Blood pressure creeps back up despite the tablets, with ankle swelling, and potassium falls causing cramps and weakness. | Stop liquorice-containing supplements, teas and sweets if your blood pressure is rising. If you keep using them, ask for a blood pressure and potassium check within 2 to 4 weeks. |
| **Anticoagulants and antiplatelets** (warfarin, apixaban, rivaroxaban, aspirin, clopidogrel) | Fibrinolytic enzyme supplements (nattokinase, serrapeptase, lumbrokinase) | Combination raises risk of harm | Extra bruising and bleeding, with case reports of serious bleeds including into the brain and the eye. Nattokinase products also vary widely in strength, and some carry vitamin K2 (MK-7) from the fermentation, which pushes the INR the other way on warfarin. | Do not combine fibrinolytic enzyme supplements with an anticoagulant or antiplatelet without asking the prescriber. If you take a nattokinase product on warfarin, check whether the label declares MK-7 and ask for an INR check. |
| **Anticonvulsants** (phenytoin, valproate, carbamazepine, lamotrigine) | Ginkgo biloba (leaf extract; ginkgo seeds are worse) | Supplement weakens the drug | Breakthrough seizures despite taking the usual dose, with drug levels found below the treatment range. One published case was fatal, although that person was taking many other herbal products at the same time. | Tell the neurology team before you start ginkgo, and if you already take it ask for an anticonvulsant level check. Avoid ginkgo seeds entirely. Never stop your anticonvulsant yourself. |
| **Anticonvulsants (clobazam and valproate specifically)** (clobazam, valproate) | Cannabidiol (CBD oils and capsules sold as supplements) | Supplement strengthens the drug | With clobazam, heavy sedation and unsteadiness as the active metabolite builds up. With valproate, liver enzymes (ALT and AST) rise, sometimes several times above normal, with no symptoms at first. | Tell the epilepsy team before using any CBD product with clobazam or valproate. Ask for liver blood tests before starting and at about 1 and 3 months if you take valproate, and report drowsiness, unsteadiness, vomiting or yellow eyes. |
| **Anticonvulsants (phenytoin specifically)** (phenytoin) | Folic acid | Supplement weakens the drug | Phenytoin blood levels fall after folate is replaced, and seizures can return in someone who was previously stable. | Do not start or stop folic acid on your own while taking phenytoin. If you need it, for example when planning a pregnancy, ask for a phenytoin level 2 to 4 weeks after starting. |
| **Antihypertensives** (amlodipine, ramipril, losartan, bisoprolol, indapamide) | Bitter orange (synephrine), high-dose caffeine and pre-workout stimulant blends | Supplement weakens the drug | Blood pressure and heart rate rise again despite the tablets, sometimes with palpitations, so readings look worse with no change in medication. | Stop pre-workout and bitter orange products if your blood pressure is drifting up, and take a week of home readings before and after so the prescriber can see the difference. Keep caffeine intake steady rather than swinging. |
| **Antiplatelet prodrug (clopidogrel)** (clopidogrel) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement strengthens the drug | This pair runs opposite to every other St John's wort entry here: platelet inhibition becomes stronger, so bruising and bleeding are more likely, and people who responded poorly to clopidogrel can become full responders. | Do not start or stop St John's wort without telling the prescriber, because both directions change how much clopidogrel is activated. Report unusual bruising or bleeding. |
| **Antiplatelets** (aspirin, clopidogrel, ticagrelor, prasugrel) | Concentrated garlic (allicin or aged garlic capsules) | Combination raises risk of harm | Longer bleeding from cuts and more bruising; the main documented harm is unexpected bleeding around surgery. | Garlic in cooking is fine. Stop concentrated garlic capsules 7 to 10 days before any operation or dental extraction and tell the surgeon or dentist you were taking them. |
| **Antiplatelets** (aspirin, clopidogrel, ticagrelor, prasugrel, dipyridamole) | Ginkgo biloba (leaf extract) | Combination raises risk of harm | Reported harms are bruising and, rarely, bleeding inside the skull or eye. Nothing shows on routine blood tests. | Do not start ginkgo while on aspirin or clopidogrel without asking the prescriber. Report bleeding that takes longer than usual to stop, and stop ginkgo at least 36 hours before planned surgery, which is the standard perioperative interval for ginkgo (the 7 day interval applies to garlic and ginseng). |
| **Antiretrovirals** (saquinavir, darunavir, other protease inhibitors) | Concentrated garlic capsules (aged garlic extract, allicin) | Supplement weakens the drug | Drug levels can drop by about half, so the virus is less well suppressed and resistance becomes more likely. Unboosted protease inhibitor regimens are the ones most at risk. | Garlic used in cooking is fine. Do not take concentrated garlic capsules with HIV medicines without asking the clinic, and ask for a viral load check if you have been taking them. |
| **Benzodiazepines and Z-drugs** (diazepam, lorazepam, temazepam, zopiclone, zolpidem) | Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera) | Supplement strengthens the drug | More sedation than the prescribed dose alone gives, with next-morning grogginess and unsteadiness. Ashwagandha also carries its own liver injury signal, so jaundice or dark urine needs same-day advice. | Ask the prescriber before combining ashwagandha with a sedative, do not drive the next morning until you know the effect, and stop it if you feel unusually drowsy or unsteady. Seek advice for jaundice, dark urine or pain under the right ribs. |
| **Benzodiazepines and Z-drugs** (temazepam, diazepam, zopiclone, zolpidem) | Melatonin | Supplement strengthens the drug | More sedation and next-morning grogginess, with slower reactions and a higher chance of falls, especially in people over 65. | Take melatonin no more than 1 hour before bed, do not drive or use machinery the next morning until you know how the combination affects you, and tell the prescriber, particularly if you are over 65 or have fallen before. |
| **CYP3A4-dependent statins** (simvastatin, atorvastatin, lovastatin) | Berberine | Supplement strengthens the drug | Statin blood levels rise, which brings muscle pain and weakness and, rarely, rhabdomyolysis with dark urine. | Tell the prescriber before combining berberine with simvastatin or atorvastatin, and report muscle pain, weakness or dark urine. Pravastatin and rosuvastatin do not depend on CYP3A4, so ask whether a switch makes sense if you want to keep taking berberine. |
| **Corticosteroids** (prednisolone, hydrocortisone, dexamethasone) | Liquorice root / glycyrrhizin (supplements, teas, confectionery) | Supplement strengthens the drug | Steroid effects are amplified: more fluid retention and puffiness, higher blood pressure, higher blood glucose, and low potassium with cramps and weakness. | Stop liquorice supplements, teas and heavy liquorice sweet intake while on steroids. If you keep using them, ask for a blood pressure and potassium check within 2 to 4 weeks, and report ankle swelling, cramps or muscle weakness. |
| **Digoxin** (digoxin) | High-dose vitamin D (with or without calcium) | Combination raises risk of harm | If blood calcium rises, digoxin becomes more arrhythmogenic: palpitations, a slow or irregular pulse, nausea and confusion. | Stay within the usual vitamin D dose (typically 400 to 1000 IU a day) unless a doctor prescribes more and checks your blood calcium. Report palpitations or an irregular pulse. |
| **Digoxin** (digoxin) | Liquorice root / glycyrrhizin (supplements, teas, confectionery) | Combination raises risk of harm | Digoxin toxicity at an unchanged dose: nausea, loss of appetite, yellow-green visual haloes, a slow or irregular pulse and confusion. | Avoid liquorice supplements and heavy liquorice sweet consumption. If you have been using them, ask for a potassium and digoxin check, and report nausea, visual haloes or a slow pulse the same day. |
| **Digoxin** (digoxin) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | Digoxin levels fall by around a quarter, so heart failure symptoms or a fast irregular pulse can return. | Do not start or stop St John's wort without telling the prescriber, and ask for a digoxin level 1 to 2 weeks after any change, because stopping it can push levels up towards toxicity. |
| **Direct oral anticoagulants** (apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban, dabigatran) | Concentrated garlic (allicin or aged garlic capsules) | Combination raises risk of harm | Longer bleeding from cuts and more bruising, most clearly around surgery or dental work. | Garlic in cooking is fine. Stop concentrated garlic capsules 7 to 10 days before any operation or dental extraction, tell the surgeon or dentist you were taking them, and do not stop the DOAC yourself. |
| **Direct oral anticoagulants** (apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban, dabigatran) | Fish oil / omega-3 (EPA and DHA) | Combination raises risk of harm | Slightly more bruising and minor bleeding on top of an already thinned blood; no test will show it. | Stay at 1 g a day or less of combined EPA plus DHA unless the prescriber agrees to more; a 1 g fish oil capsule usually contains only about 300 mg of EPA plus DHA, so read the label. Seek urgent advice for black stools, blood in urine, or bleeding that will not stop after 10 minutes of pressure. |
| **Direct oral anticoagulants** (apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban, dabigatran) | Ginkgo biloba (leaf extract) | Combination raises risk of harm | Bruising and minor bleeding on top of an already thinned blood, with no INR or other routine test to pick it up. High-dose vitamin E behaves the same way, and both are often assumed to matter only with warfarin. | Tell the prescriber before starting ginkgo with a DOAC. Report bruising or bleeding that will not stop, seek urgent advice for black stools or blood in urine, and stop ginkgo at least 36 hours before planned surgery. |
| **Insulin and sulfonylureas** (insulin, gliclazide, glimepiride, glipizide) | Alpha-lipoic acid | Supplement strengthens the drug | Extra glucose lowering with hypoglycaemia: sweating, tremor, palpitations and confusion, most often a few hours after a dose. | Ask the prescriber before starting it, monitor glucose closely for the first 2 weeks, and expect that the insulin or sulfonylurea dose may need lowering. |
| **Insulin and sulfonylureas** (insulin glargine, insulin aspart, gliclazide, glimepiride, glipizide) | Chromium (chromium picolinate) | Supplement strengthens the drug | Blood glucose can fall too low: shakiness, sweating, hunger, confusion, and in severe cases loss of consciousness. | Test your blood glucose more often for 2 weeks after starting, tell the diabetes team, and carry fast-acting glucose. The insulin or gliclazide dose may need reducing. |
| **Insulin, sulfonylureas and metformin** (insulin, gliclazide, glimepiride, metformin) | Niacin (nicotinic acid, 1 g/day or more) | Supplement weakens the drug | Blood glucose and HbA1c drift upwards despite unchanged diabetes treatment, and people without diabetes can cross into it. | Do not take gram doses of niacin for cholesterol if you have diabetes unless the prescriber agrees, and check glucose more often for the first 4 to 8 weeks. Nicotinamide and the small amounts in a multivitamin are not the issue. |
| **Levothyroxine** (levothyroxine, liothyronine) | Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera) | Supplement strengthens the drug | Thyroid hormone levels can rise on an unchanged levothyroxine dose, giving palpitations, tremor, sweating, weight loss and anxiety; published cases include frank thyrotoxicosis. Ashwagandha also carries its own liver injury signal. | Tell the prescriber if you take ashwagandha with levothyroxine and ask for a TSH and free T4 check. Stop it and seek advice for palpitations, tremor or weight loss, and also for jaundice, dark urine or pain under the right ribs. |
| **Levothyroxine and antithyroid drugs** (levothyroxine, carbimazole, propylthiouracil) | Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera) | Supplement strengthens the drug | Thyroid hormone effect adds up: palpitations, tremor, weight loss and heat intolerance, with a suppressed TSH on the next blood test. Thyrotoxicosis has been reported. | Tell the prescriber before taking ashwagandha with any thyroid medicine, and ask for TSH and free T4 6 to 8 weeks after starting or stopping it. Stop it and seek advice for palpitations, tremor or unexplained weight loss. It also carries a liver injury signal. |
| **Lithium** (lithium carbonate, lithium citrate) | Caffeine supplements (caffeine tablets, guarana, pre-workout powders) | Supplement weakens the drug | Lithium levels fall while caffeine intake is high, so symptoms can return; the bigger danger is stopping caffeine suddenly, which pushes levels up towards toxicity (tremor, vomiting, unsteadiness, confusion). | Keep your caffeine intake roughly the same every day and tell the prescriber before you start or stop caffeine supplements, so a lithium level can be checked. Seek urgent help for coarse tremor, vomiting or unsteadiness. |
| **Loop diuretics** (furosemide, bumetanide, torasemide) | Liquorice root / glycyrrhizin | Combination raises risk of harm | Potassium falls further than the diuretic alone would cause: cramps, muscle weakness, palpitations, and rarely muscle breakdown or a dangerous rhythm. If you also take digoxin, the low potassium raises the risk of digoxin toxicity. | Stop liquorice supplements and cut down liquorice sweets. Ask for a potassium blood test if you have been taking them, and report cramps, unusual muscle weakness or palpitations. |
| **Loop diuretics** (furosemide, bumetanide, torasemide) | Thiamine (vitamin B1) | Drug depletes the supplement | Thiamine stores fall, which can worsen heart failure symptoms and cause tiredness, leg numbness or unsteadiness. The loss is through the urine, so gut absorption is not the problem. | If you take high-dose furosemide long term, ask the prescriber whether a thiamine supplement or a thiamine level is warranted, especially if you drink alcohol or eat poorly. |
| **MAOIs** (phenelzine, tranylcypromine, isocarboxazid, moclobemide, selegiline) | L-tyrosine supplements | Combination raises risk of harm | In theory blood pressure could rise with headache and palpitations, but L-tyrosine has not been an established cause of hypertensive crisis. | Tell the prescriber before taking L-tyrosine on an MAOI and check your blood pressure during the first week. Treat this as a lower-grade caution than the yeast extract and fermented soy restriction, which is the real one. |
| **Metformin** (metformin) | Berberine | Supplement strengthens the drug | Blood glucose drops further than expected. Metformin on its own rarely causes hypoglycaemia, so the shakiness, sweating, hunger and dizziness picture belongs mainly to people also taking a sulfonylurea or insulin. | Test your own blood glucose more often for the first 2 weeks and tell the diabetes team you have started it. Do not combine berberine with metformin plus a sulfonylurea or insulin without medical advice. |
| **Metformin** (metformin, metformin modified release) | Vitamin B12 (cyanocobalamin) | Drug blocks the supplement | Body stores of B12 fall over years, causing tiredness, sore tongue and numbness or pins and needles in the hands and feet; the blood count may show large red cells. | If you have taken metformin for several years, or you have numbness or tingling in hands or feet, ask for a vitamin B12 blood test. Do not stop metformin; oral or sublingual B12 replacement usually fixes it. |
| **Narrow-margin drugs cleared by CYP3A4 or UGT** (tacrolimus, ciclosporin, midazolam, carbamazepine, simvastatin, many oral oncology drugs) | Curcumin with piperine (black pepper extract; sold as high absorption or bioavailability enhanced turmeric) | Supplement strengthens the drug | Blood levels of the prescribed drug rise, so its dose-related side effects appear even though the dose has not changed. | Check whether your turmeric product lists piperine or black pepper extract, and tell the prescriber before starting it if you take any narrow-margin medicine. Do not treat a high absorption formula as interchangeable with kitchen turmeric. |
| **Organic nitrates** (glyceryl trinitrate spray, isosorbide mononitrate, isosorbide dinitrate) | N-acetylcysteine (NAC) | Supplement strengthens the drug | Blood pressure falls further and headache is worse than with the nitrate alone, with dizziness on standing and a risk of fainting. | Tell the prescriber before combining them, stand up slowly, and stop the NAC if you get headache or light-headedness. Do not use NAC as a self-treatment for nitrate tolerance. |
| **PDE5 inhibitors** (sildenafil, tadalafil, vardenafil) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | The tablet simply stops working as well, which is often mistaken for the condition worsening. | If the tablet has stopped working and you take St John's wort, tell the prescriber rather than doubling the dose yourself. |
| **Proton pump inhibitors** (omeprazole, lansoprazole, pantoprazole, esomeprazole) | Iron (oral ferrous salts) | Drug blocks the supplement | Iron tablets work poorly: haemoglobin and ferritin fail to rise, and the tiredness and breathlessness of anaemia persist. | Recheck haemoglobin and ferritin after 3 months. If levels are not rising, ask about an iron infusion or whether the PPI is still needed. Taking iron with orange juice is often suggested on the grounds that acid aids absorption, but a randomised trial found added vitamin C made no difference, so treat that step as optional. |
| **Proton pump inhibitors** (omeprazole, lansoprazole, pantoprazole, esomeprazole) | Magnesium | Drug blocks the supplement | Blood magnesium falls, causing cramps, tremor, tiredness, palpitations, and in severe cases fits. Oral magnesium often fails to correct it while the PPI continues. | If you have been on a PPI for over a year, ask for a magnesium blood test. Report cramps, tremor or palpitations, and ask whether the PPI can be reduced or stopped, since supplements alone may not fix it. |
| **Proton pump inhibitors** (omeprazole, lansoprazole, pantoprazole, esomeprazole) | Vitamin B12 (cyanocobalamin) | Drug blocks the supplement | Over years, B12 falls: tiredness, sore tongue, pins and needles in hands or feet, and low mood or poor memory. | If you have taken a PPI for 2 years or more, ask for a B12 blood test. Sublingual or higher-dose oral B12 works, because it does not need stomach acid to be freed from food. |
| **Reversible MAO-A and selective MAO-B inhibitors** (moclobemide, oral selegiline up to 10 mg/day, transdermal selegiline 6 mg/24 h) | Tyramine-containing foods and supplements (yeast extract, fermented soy) | Combination raises risk of harm | A blood pressure rise is possible but uncommon at these doses and formulations, and the full MAOI diet is not required. | No general tyramine restriction is needed. On moclobemide, take doses after food and avoid a single large tyramine load such as a jar of yeast extract or a large helping of aged cheese. On selegiline, stay within 10 mg a day orally or the 6 mg/24 h patch, because higher doses lose MAO-B selectivity and the full restriction then applies. Report a sudden severe headache with palpitations. |
| **Reversible MAO-A inhibitor and MAO-B selective agents** (moclobemide, oral selegiline 10 mg/day, selegiline transdermal 6 mg/24 h and 9 mg/24 h) | Tyramine or tyrosine containing supplements (L-tyrosine, fermented soy, yeast extract, broad bean pod extract) | Combination raises risk of harm | At licensed doses these drugs do not require tyramine restriction and a hypertensive reaction is unlikely. The risk appears as the dose rises: oral selegiline loses MAO-B selectivity at higher doses, and the 12 mg/24 h patch does carry a dietary restriction while 6 and 9 mg/24 h do not. | No tyramine or cheese restriction is needed at standard moclobemide doses or at oral selegiline 10 mg a day. Keep to the prescribed dose, and check with the prescriber before large tyrosine or fermented-supplement doses, or if the selegiline dose or patch strength goes up. Moclobemide washout is roughly 24 hours, so the 2 week rule for irreversible MAOIs does not apply here. |
| **SSRIs (fluvoxamine specifically)** (fluvoxamine) | Melatonin | Drug strengthens the supplement | Melatonin blood levels rise many-fold on the same dose, giving heavy sedation, a hangover feeling and slow reactions the next morning. | Avoid melatonin with fluvoxamine, or use the smallest dose (0.5 to 1 mg) only if the prescriber agrees. Do not drive or use machinery the next morning until you know how the combination affects you. |
| **Statins** (simvastatin, atorvastatin) | Berberine | Combination raises risk of harm | Statin levels rise, so muscle pain and weakness become more likely, with a rise in creatine kinase on blood tests. | Tell the prescriber before adding berberine to simvastatin or atorvastatin, and report muscle pain or weakness. Pravastatin and rosuvastatin are less affected because they do not depend on CYP3A4. |
| **Statins** (simvastatin, atorvastatin, lovastatin) | Niacin (nicotinic acid, 1 g/day or more) | Combination raises risk of harm | Muscle aches and weakness become more likely, with a rise in creatine kinase on blood tests; flushing and itching are separate nuisance effects. | Do not take gram doses of niacin with a statin unless the prescriber agrees and checks a creatine kinase level. Report muscle pain or weakness. No-flush inositol hexanicotinate is not a proven safe substitute. |
| **Statins** (simvastatin, atorvastatin (not pravastatin or rosuvastatin)) | St John's wort (Hypericum perforatum) | Supplement weakens the drug | The cholesterol-lowering benefit shrinks: LDL cholesterol creeps back up on the next blood test, with no symptoms in the meantime. | Tell the prescriber if you take St John's wort so cholesterol is rechecked, and ask whether switching to pravastatin or rosuvastatin would avoid the problem. |
| **Thiazide and thiazide-like diuretics** (bendroflumethiazide, hydrochlorothiazide, indapamide, chlortalidone) | Calcium supplements (particularly with vitamin D) | Combination raises risk of harm | Blood calcium can rise too high: nausea, constipation, excessive thirst, passing lots of urine, then confusion and kidney impairment. | Normal supplement doses are usually fine, but ask for a blood calcium test 4 to 6 weeks after starting the combination, and report nausea, constipation, thirst or confusion. |
| **Thiazide and thiazide-like diuretics** (bendroflumethiazide, hydrochlorothiazide, indapamide) | Liquorice root / glycyrrhizin | Combination raises risk of harm | Potassium falls further than the diuretic alone would cause: cramps, muscle weakness, palpitations, and rarely muscle breakdown or a dangerous rhythm. | Stop liquorice supplements and cut down liquorice sweets. Ask for a potassium blood test if you have been taking them, and report cramps or unusual muscle weakness. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol, phenindione) | American ginseng (Panax quinquefolius; also Panax ginseng) | Supplement weakens the drug | The INR falls over about 2 weeks and clot protection is lost, with no symptoms to warn you. | Do not start ginseng while on warfarin. If you already take it, keep the dose constant, tell the anticoagulant clinic and ask for an INR check within 2 weeks. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol) | Cannabidiol (CBD oils, capsules and gummies sold as supplements) | Supplement strengthens the drug | The INR climbs over days to weeks, in published cases above 5, with bruising and bleeding. Reported patients needed the warfarin dose cut by roughly 30 percent. | Tell the anticoagulant clinic before using any CBD product, and ask for an INR check within 1 to 2 weeks of starting it, changing brand or changing dose, because product strength varies widely. Report bruising or bleeding that will not stop. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol) | Coenzyme Q10 (ubiquinone, often taken for statin muscle aches) | Supplement weakens the drug | The INR can fall, so clot protection weakens with no symptoms. Co-use is common because CoQ10 is marketed to the same patients who take statins and warfarin together. | Tell the anticoagulant clinic before starting or stopping CoQ10, keep the daily dose the same, and ask for an INR check within 1 to 2 weeks of any change. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol) | Coenzyme Q10 (ubiquinone, ubiquinol) | Supplement weakens the drug | The INR can fall, so clot protection is quietly reduced with no symptoms. Many people meet this supplement because it is marketed for statin muscle aches, so it often arrives alongside a statin rather than on its own. | Tell the anticoagulant clinic before you start or stop coenzyme Q10, keep the dose the same every day, and ask for an INR check within 1 to 2 weeks of any change. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol, phenindione) | Cranberry (juice, concentrate or capsules) | Supplement strengthens the drug | The INR can climb, sometimes sharply, with nosebleeds, heavy periods or bleeding into the gut. Large or variable intakes are the pattern linked to these reports. | Keep cranberry intake steady rather than swinging between none and a lot, and tell the anticoagulant clinic if you start or stop a cranberry product so an INR check can be arranged within 1 to 2 weeks. Large daily volumes of juice or concentrate are the pattern worth avoiding. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol, phenindione) | Fish oil / omega-3 (EPA and DHA) | Combination raises risk of harm | Bruising, nosebleeds and bleeding gums become more likely while the INR usually stays the same, so the routine blood test does not warn you. A few case reports describe an INR rise after a fish oil dose increase. | Doses up to about 1 g a day of combined EPA plus DHA are usually fine. Check the label, because a 1 g fish oil capsule typically holds only about 300 mg of EPA plus DHA. Ask the prescriber before using 3 g a day or more of EPA plus DHA, and report bruising, nosebleeds or bleeding gums rather than waiting for the next INR. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol, phenindione) | Ginkgo biloba (leaf extract) | Combination raises risk of harm | Reported bleeding events include bruising, eye bleeds and bleeding into the brain. The INR is usually unchanged, so nothing shows on the blood test. | Do not start ginkgo without telling the anticoagulant clinic. If you already take it, keep the dose the same every day and report any unusual bruising or bleeding that will not stop. An INR check cannot detect this platelet effect, so a normal INR is not reassurance. Stop ginkgo at least 36 hours before planned surgery. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol) | Glucosamine (with or without chondroitin) | Supplement strengthens the drug | The INR rises, sometimes markedly, within days to a few weeks, with bruising, nosebleeds or bleeding gums. It settles again after the glucosamine is stopped. | Do not start glucosamine while on warfarin without telling the anticoagulant clinic. If you do start it, ask for an INR check within 1 to 2 weeks, and again after you stop it. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol) | Glucosamine, with or without chondroitin | Supplement strengthens the drug | The INR rises, sometimes with bruising or bleeding. Use is common in the same age group that takes warfarin, because the supplement is sold for joint pain. | Avoid starting glucosamine while on warfarin. If you already take it, tell the anticoagulant clinic, ask for an INR check within 1 to 2 weeks, and report bruising or bleeding. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol, phenindione) | Vitamin E (alpha-tocopherol, above 400 IU/day) | Combination raises risk of harm | Easier bruising and prolonged bleeding from small cuts; at very high doses the INR itself can rise. | Stay at or below 200 to 400 IU a day, or simply the lowest dose you actually need. Tell the anticoagulant clinic if you start it and ask for an INR check 1 to 2 weeks later. Report bruising or bleeding that takes longer than usual to stop. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol, phenindione) | Vitamin E (alpha-tocopherol, above 400 IU/day; the real concern is 800 IU/day or more) | Combination raises risk of harm | Easier bruising and prolonged bleeding from small cuts; at very high doses the INR itself can rise. | Keep vitamin E at 400 IU a day or less, a dose used for 3 years in a randomised trial without a bleeding excess, and treat 800 IU a day or more as the level that needs discussion with the prescriber. Tell the anticoagulant clinic if you start it and ask for an INR check 1 to 2 weeks later. Report bruising or bleeding that takes longer than usual to stop. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol, phenindione) | Vitamin K1 (phytomenadione) and vitamin K2 (menaquinone, MK-4 and MK-7; includes vitamin D3 plus K2 combination capsules, green-leaf and greens powders, green tea, some multivitamins) | Supplement weakens the drug | The INR falls and the blood becomes less thin, so protection against clots and stroke is lost. There are no symptoms, only a lower INR on the blood test. Vitamin K2 as MK-7 acts the same way at microgram doses and stays in the blood far longer than K1, so a vitamin D3 plus K2 capsule carries the same risk even though the label never mentions vitamin K1. | Do not stop or start a vitamin K containing supplement on your own. Tell the anticoagulant clinic before any change and ask for an INR check within 1 to 2 weeks; a steady daily intake is safer than swinging between none and a lot. Read labels for menaquinone, MK-4 or MK-7 as well as for vitamin K1. |
| **Vitamin K antagonists** (warfarin, acenocoumarol, phenindione) | Vitamin K1 (phytomenadione; also green-leaf/greens powders, some multivitamins) | Supplement weakens the drug | The INR falls and the blood becomes less thin, so protection against clots and stroke is lost. There are no symptoms, only a lower INR on the blood test. | Do not stop or start a vitamin K containing supplement on your own. Tell the anticoagulant clinic before any change and ask for an INR check within 1 to 2 weeks; a steady daily intake is safer than swinging between none and a lot. |

### Fine if taken hours apart

| Drug | Supplement | Direction | What happens | What to do |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Antiretrovirals (integrase inhibitors)** (dolutegravir, bictegravir, raltegravir) | Calcium supplements (also magnesium, iron, zinc and aluminium-containing products) | Supplement blocks the drug | Much less drug is absorbed, risking viral rebound and resistance, with nothing noticeable at the time. | Take the HIV tablet 2 hours before or 6 hours after calcium, magnesium, iron or zinc supplements. Alternatively the clinic may allow you to take both together with food, so ask which option applies to your tablet. |
| **Antiretrovirals (integrase inhibitors: dolutegravir and bictegravir)** (dolutegravir, bictegravir) | Calcium, magnesium, iron, zinc and aluminium containing supplements, antacids, laxatives and sucralfate | Supplement blocks the drug | Much less drug is absorbed, risking viral rebound and resistance, with nothing noticeable at the time. | Default: take the HIV tablet 2 hours before or 6 hours after any of these products. One exception: calcium or iron supplements may be taken at the same time as dolutegravir or bictegravir if both are taken with food. Use the 2-hour and 6-hour rule until the clinic confirms the with-food option applies to your tablet. |
| **Beta blockers (nadolol specifically)** (nadolol) | Green tea and green tea extract (catechins, EGCG) | Supplement blocks the drug | Nadolol exposure falls by roughly 85 percent, so blood pressure and heart rate control are lost while the tablet is being taken exactly as prescribed. | Do not drink green tea or take green tea extract in the hours around a nadolol dose; separate them by at least 4 hours and keep intake steady rather than variable. Tell the prescriber if blood pressure control has drifted. This is specific to nadolol and is not a class effect for every beta blocker. |
| **Beta-blockers carried by intestinal OATP transporters** (nadolol, atenolol) | Green tea extract / EGCG (and strong green tea taken with the dose) | Supplement blocks the drug | Almost the whole dose can be lost, so blood pressure and heart rate control fail while the tablet is being taken exactly as prescribed. | Do not take green tea extract, and do not drink green tea, around the time of your nadolol dose; separate them by several hours and keep your intake consistent day to day. Tell the prescriber if blood pressure control has drifted. High-dose EGCG also carries a liver injury signal. |
| **Bisphosphonates** (alendronate, risedronate, ibandronate (oral)) | Calcium supplements (including calcium plus vitamin D combinations) | Supplement blocks the drug | The bisphosphonate tablet is effectively wasted, so bone protection is lost while you still believe you are treated. | Take the bisphosphonate on an empty stomach with plain tap water and stay upright: wait at least 30 minutes before food or calcium for alendronate and immediate-release risedronate, and at least 60 minutes for ibandronate. Delayed-release risedronate has its own after-breakfast instruction, so follow its leaflet. Taking calcium at a different time of day is simplest. |
| **Bisphosphonates** (alendronate, risedronate, ibandronate (oral)) | Calcium supplements (including calcium plus vitamin D combinations), and also iron, magnesium and zinc supplements and magnesium- or aluminium-containing antacids | Supplement blocks the drug | The bisphosphonate tablet is effectively wasted, so bone protection is lost while you still believe you are treated. | Take the bisphosphonate on an empty stomach with plain tap water and stay upright: wait at least 30 minutes for alendronate and risedronate, and at least 60 minutes for ibandronate, before any calcium, iron, magnesium, zinc or antacid. Taking those at a different time of day is simplest. |
| **Fluoroquinolones** (ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin, moxifloxacin, ofloxacin) | Magnesium (also zinc, iron and calcium supplements, and magnesium- or aluminium-containing antacids) | Supplement blocks the drug | Almost the whole dose can be lost, and the infection is not treated. | Take the antibiotic 2 hours before or 6 hours after magnesium, zinc, iron or calcium supplements and antacids. Never swallow them at the same time, and check multivitamins, which usually contain zinc, iron or magnesium. |
| **Fluoroquinolones** (ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin, moxifloxacin, ofloxacin) | Magnesium, calcium, iron and zinc supplements (also magnesium-, aluminium- or calcium-containing antacids) | Supplement blocks the drug | Almost the whole dose can be lost, and the infection is not treated. | Use the interval for your own drug: ciprofloxacin 2 hours before or 6 hours after; levofloxacin 2 hours either side; moxifloxacin at least 4 hours before or 8 hours after. If you are unsure which you have, use the most conservative rule, 4 hours before or 8 hours after. Never swallow them at the same time. |
| **Immunoassay-based blood tests (assay artefact, not a drug class)** (cardiac troponin, TSH, hCG, ferritin, vitamin D and other streptavidin-biotin immunoassays) | High-dose biotin (vitamin B7) | Combination raises risk of harm | ASSAY ARTEFACT rather than a drug interaction: troponin can read falsely LOW, which can hide a heart attack; other tests read falsely high or low depending on assay design. This is the higher-consequence of the two biotin entries, since the FDA has linked a death to a falsely low troponin. | Tell any clinician or phlebotomist that you take biotin, including in an emergency with chest pain, and ask for a repeat sample after stopping it or for a biotin-insensitive assay. Stop biotin at least 2 days before planned blood tests, or 3 to 7 days at 5 mg a day or more. Do not let a normal troponin reassure you if you are still taking high-dose biotin. |
| **Levodopa** (co-careldopa, co-beneldopa, levodopa) | Iron (oral ferrous salts) | Supplement blocks the drug | Less levodopa is absorbed, so off periods return: stiffness, slowness and tremor between doses. | Separate the iron tablet from every levodopa dose by at least 2 hours, and tell the prescriber if off periods have worsened since you started iron. |
| **Levodopa** (co-careldopa, co-beneldopa) | Protein, whey and branched-chain amino acid powders | Supplement blocks the drug | Doses stop working reliably: visible off periods, freezing and returning tremor within an hour of a protein shake. | Take levodopa 30 to 60 minutes before, or at least 1 to 2 hours after, any protein or amino acid powder, and spread protein evenly through the day. This is a timing problem with a timing fix, so do not cut protein out of your diet. |
| **Levodopa preparations** (co-careldopa (levodopa with carbidopa), co-beneldopa (levodopa with benserazide)) | Iron (ferrous sulfate, fumarate, gluconate; also iron in multivitamins) | Supplement blocks the drug | Much less levodopa is absorbed, so Parkinson's symptoms return between doses: stiffness, slowness, tremor and longer off periods. | Separate levodopa and iron by at least 2 hours, ideally longer, and keep the same routine every day. Tell the Parkinson's team if you start iron, because the levodopa dose will look inadequate. |
| **Levothyroxine** (levothyroxine, liothyronine) | Calcium supplements (carbonate, citrate, acetate; includes calcium-based antacids) | Supplement blocks the drug | About 20 to 25 percent less thyroid hormone is absorbed, so TSH rises and tiredness, weight gain, constipation and cold intolerance return. | Take levothyroxine with water on an empty stomach and leave at least 4 hours before any calcium supplement or calcium-containing antacid. Keep the same routine every day. |
| **Levothyroxine** (levothyroxine (liothyronine assumed by class, not studied)) | Elemental calcium supplements (carbonate, citrate, acetate; includes calcium-based antacids) | Supplement blocks the drug | About 20 to 25 percent less thyroid hormone is absorbed, so TSH rises and tiredness, weight gain, constipation and cold intolerance return. | Take levothyroxine with water on an empty stomach and leave at least 4 hours before any calcium supplement or calcium-containing antacid. Keep the same routine every day. |
| **Levothyroxine** (levothyroxine, liothyronine) | Iron (ferrous sulfate, fumarate, gluconate) | Supplement blocks the drug | Absorption falls by a variable and sometimes large amount. In a controlled study TSH rose from 1.6 to 5.4 mIU/L over 12 weeks, with return of hypothyroid symptoms. | Take levothyroxine first thing with water and take the iron at lunchtime or bedtime, at least 4 hours apart. Ask for a TSH check 6 to 8 weeks after starting or stopping iron. |
| **Levothyroxine** (levothyroxine (liothyronine assumed by class, not studied)) | Magnesium and zinc supplements (including combined mineral products and magnesium-containing antacids) | Supplement blocks the drug | Less hormone is absorbed, so TSH drifts up and tiredness, weight gain and cold intolerance return, exactly as with calcium or iron. | Leave at least 4 hours between levothyroxine and any magnesium or zinc supplement, keep the same daily routine, and ask for a TSH check 6 to 8 weeks after starting or stopping them. |
| **Levothyroxine** (levothyroxine, liothyronine) | Other gut binders: magnesium supplements, aluminium- and magnesium-containing antacids, soy protein isolate, and fibre supplements (psyllium/ispaghula, glucomannan) | Supplement blocks the drug | The same loss of absorption as with calcium and iron: TSH rises and hypothyroid symptoms return while the prescribed dose has not changed. | Take levothyroxine with water on an empty stomach and leave at least 4 hours before magnesium or aluminium antacids, soy protein powders or fibre supplements. Keep the routine identical every day and ask for a TSH check 6 to 8 weeks after any change. |
| **Levothyroxine (thyroid function monitoring)** (levothyroxine dose decisions based on TSH and free T4) | High-dose biotin (vitamin B7; hair, skin and nail products up to 20 mg) | Combination raises risk of harm | ASSAY ARTEFACT rather than a drug interaction: the blood test reads falsely low TSH and falsely high free T4, mimicking an overactive thyroid or overtreatment, so a correct dose may be wrongly cut. | Stop biotin at least 2 days before a thyroid blood test, or 3 to 7 days if you take 5 mg a day or more, and tell whoever takes the blood that you use it. Do not accept a dose change based on a test taken while you were still on biotin. |
| **Lithium** (lithium carbonate, lithium citrate) | Psyllium / ispaghula husk (bulk-forming fibre) | Supplement blocks the drug | The lithium blood level drops and mood symptoms can return, even though the dose has not changed. | Leave at least 2 hours, ideally 4, between lithium and psyllium, and keep the same routine every day. Ask for a lithium level 1 to 2 weeks after starting or stopping the fibre. The same gap is sensible for other narrow-margin tablets such as levothyroxine, carbamazepine and digoxin. |
| **Tetracyclines** (doxycycline, lymecycline, oxytetracycline) | Calcium supplements (including calcium-fortified drinks and antacids) | Supplement blocks the drug | The antibiotic is largely wasted and the infection can persist or relapse. | Take the antibiotic 2 hours before or 4 hours after calcium supplements, milk-based supplement drinks or calcium antacids. Plain water only with the tablet. |
| **Tetracyclines** (doxycycline, lymecycline, oxytetracycline, minocycline) | Calcium supplements (including calcium-fortified drinks and antacids), also zinc, magnesium and aluminium antacids | Supplement blocks the drug | How much is lost depends on the drug: tetracycline hydrochloride absorption falls by about half or more with milk or calcium, while doxycycline and minocycline are less affected, roughly 20 to 30 percent. Even that can be enough for the infection to persist or relapse. | Take the antibiotic 2 hours before or 4 hours after calcium, zinc or magnesium supplements, milk-based supplement drinks or antacids. Plain water only with the tablet. |
| **Tetracyclines** (doxycycline, lymecycline, oxytetracycline, minocycline) | Iron (ferrous sulfate, fumarate, gluconate) | Supplement blocks the drug | Much less antibiotic is absorbed, so the infection or acne fails to clear and a second course is needed. | Take the antibiotic either 2 hours before or 4 hours after any iron supplement, for the whole course. Check multivitamins too, most contain iron. |
| **Tetracyclines** (doxycycline, lymecycline, oxytetracycline, minocycline) | Iron (ferrous sulfate, fumarate, gluconate); zinc supplements chelate in the same way | Supplement blocks the drug | Much less antibiotic is absorbed, so the infection or acne fails to clear and a second course is needed. | Take the antibiotic either 2 hours before or 4 hours after any iron or zinc supplement, for the whole course. Check multivitamins too, most contain iron or zinc. |
| **Timing-sensitive oral drugs taken with bulk fibre** (levothyroxine, carbamazepine, digoxin, metformin) | Psyllium / ispaghula husk and other soluble bulk fibre | Supplement blocks the drug | Blood levels drop at an unchanged dose, so thyroid symptoms, seizure control, heart failure symptoms or blood glucose can all slip depending on the drug. | Leave at least 2 hours, ideally 4, between bulk fibre and any of these tablets, and keep the same routine daily. Where a blood level exists (levothyroxine via TSH, carbamazepine, digoxin), ask for a check 1 to 2 weeks after starting or stopping the fibre. |

### Worth knowing, no restriction

| Drug | Supplement | Direction | What happens | What to do |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Organic nitrates** (glyceryl trinitrate spray, isosorbide mononitrate, isosorbide dinitrate) | L-arginine (also L-citrulline, beetroot nitrate shots) | Combination raises risk of harm | In theory blood pressure could fall further than intended, giving dizziness, light-headedness on standing or headache. The one controlled study designed to detect exactly this did not find it. | No restriction is needed on the current evidence. If you use GTN or isosorbide and start arginine, citrulline or beetroot shots, stand up slowly for the first few days and tell the prescriber if you get dizziness, light-headedness or fainting. |

## 9. How to buy

1. **Name the deficiency or the endpoint first** If you cannot name what you are correcting, no product is the right answer. Every entry in this document that earns a Take verdict earns it for a named group with a named problem.
2. **Buy single ingredients at doses that were tested** A proprietary blend hides the dose of every component behind two words. That is also where adulteration lives: in the FDA database, 776 adulterated products came from 146 companies, concentrated in blends.
3. **Check the elemental amount, not the compound** Magnesium oxide is about 60% magnesium and magnesium citrate about 16%, so a capsule labelled 500 mg magnesium citrate delivers roughly 80 mg of magnesium. Ferrous sulfate 325 mg delivers 65 mg of iron.
4. **Know what a certification mark does not cover** All three major marks certify content and purity. None of them assesses whether the product works, and only two screen for substances banned in sport. See the table below.
5. **Treat a seller-supplied certificate as a claim, not a proof** Ask which production lot it covers, which laboratory produced it, whether that laboratory is ISO 17025 accredited, and who chose the laboratory. A certificate for a different lot proves nothing about the bottle in your hand.
6. **Avoid three categories outright** Anything sold for erections, rapid fat loss, or muscle building and testosterone. These three account for 45.5%, 40.9% and 11.9% of all adulterated supplements in the FDA database, and enforcement does not fix them: 18 of 27 recalled products still contained banned drugs when re-bought a mean 34 months later.
7. **Read the disclaimer literally** The sentence saying the claim has not been evaluated by the FDA and the product is not intended to diagnose, treat, cure or prevent any disease is the regulator speaking. It is the most informative sentence on the bottle.
8. **Tell your pharmacist what you take** Especially before surgery, before starting an anticoagulant, and during cancer treatment. Post-market surveillance is the entire safety system for supplements, and it only works if people report.

### What each certification mark covers

| Mark | Tests | Screens for | Checks efficacy | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Informed Sport | Every manufactured batch | WADA prohibited list | No | The only appropriate standard under anti-doping strict liability. |
| NSF Certified for Sport | Variable batch model | 280+ banned substances | No | Recognised by USADA, MLB and the NHL. |
| USP Verified | Off-the-shelf surveillance | Identity, potency, dissolution, contaminants | No | Does **not** screen for substances banned in sport, so a verified product can legally contain a prohibited stimulant. |

## 10. How this was built

Three research passes, each fanned out across ten or more domains, each followed by an independent checker instructed to assume a source does not exist until found. Across 428 citation checks: zero fabricated papers, zero invented PMIDs, zero wrong DOIs. 486 food figures were corrected during verification.

**What to distrust.** NIH ODS returns HTTP 403 to automated retrieval, so reference intakes come from the underlying IOM/NASEM and EFSA documents. 86 food rows are tagged unconfirmed and eight items carry a Low data-confidence badge, because USDA FoodData Central returned 404s and rate limits during part of the work. Food composition varies by more than an order of magnitude between samples for Brazil nut selenium and UV-mushroom vitamin D.

## 11. What is missing

| Missing | Why it matters | Until then |
|---|---|---|
| **Red yeast rice** | Contains monacolin K, which is chemically identical to lovastatin. It is an unlabelled statin sold without a prescription, carrying rhabdomyolysis, hepatotoxicity and statin drug-interaction risk. | Treat it as a statin: same cautions, same interactions, same monitoring. |
| **Enteric-coated peppermint oil** | Sold in the same aisle, with multiple meta-analyses in irritable bowel syndrome and a stronger effect estimate than almost any probiotic reviewed here. | If you are buying a probiotic for IBS, this has better data. |
| **Biotin** | One of the largest single-B-vitamin retail categories, sold at 5,000 to 10,000 µg against a 30 µg adequate intake, on essentially no efficacy evidence. It carries an active FDA safety communication because it interferes with streptavidin-biotin immunoassays, including troponin. | Stop it before any blood test and tell the person taking the sample. |
| **Sodium bicarbonate** | On the IOC short list of supplements with robust performance evidence, alongside caffeine, creatine, beta-alanine and nitrate, and it has its own ISSN position stand. | The one item on the IOC list this document does not cover. |
| **Urolithin A** | The flagship commercial mitophagy product, with company-run randomised trials that were not assessed here. | Expect the NAD-precursor pattern until shown otherwise: biomarker moves, endpoint does not. |
| **Lavender oil / Silexan** | A licensed anxiolytic medicine in Germany with placebo-controlled and active-comparator trials in generalised anxiety disorder, and a stronger evidence base than rhodiola, bacopa or lion mane. | Better evidenced than three botanicals this document does cover. |
| **Calcifediol** | Over-the-counter across Europe, prescription in the US. Two to three times more potent per microgram than D3 and raises serum 25(OH)D far faster. | Relevant if you are correcting a documented deficiency rather than maintaining. |
| **CBD and hemp cannabinoids** | The largest single regulatory gap: excluded from the US supplement definition by the drug-exclusion rule, and treated by the UK FSA as a novel food requiring authorisation. | Regulatory status differs by country more than for anything else here. |
